=== PAGE 7 ===
Introduction by The Rt Hon
the Baroness Hallett DBE

=== PAGE 8 ===
                                        Introduction by The Rt Hon the Baroness Hallett DBE




In April 2020, the high number of deaths of people in care homes brought into sharp focus
the devastating impact of the Covid-19 pandemic on the adult social care sector. It caused
the UK government and devolved administrations to turn their attention to the adult social
care sector, as their focus until then had very much been on the response of the healthcare
system and ‘protecting the NHS’. Given that it was well known that people receiving care
were particularly vulnerable to any respiratory disease, let alone a deadly respiratory virus
like Covid-19, that shift in focus came too late. Protective measures should have been put in
place at the start of a pandemic, not once it had already taken hold.

As has been a constant theme in the Inquiry’s reports, a major factor in the failure properly
to protect the sector was the failure in pre-pandemic planning. There was confusion about
the pandemic plans in existence at national government level, local government level and
provider level. Guidance was not tailored to the sector and appeared to ignore the realities
of providing care to those in need. Initially, there were poor data available about bed and
workforce capacity and about the number of people dying in care homes. This hampered
the ability of the four governments of the UK to understand, respond and protect people
living and working in care settings.

In the early months of the pandemic, many hospital patients were discharged to care homes
without a Covid-19 test and some homes found it extremely difficult to isolate returning or
new residents. The sector as a whole experienced difficulties in acquiring personal
protective equipment.

Visiting restrictions meant that most in-person regulatory oversight of care homes was
paused and there was, at times, limited access to medical care. The restrictions also meant
that family and friends were prevented from visiting their loved ones in care homes, causing
immense distress and harm. While visits to people at the end of life should have been
permitted, this did not always happen. This resulted in many people dying alone without the
comfort of their loved ones by their side. For many bereaved, this has led to prolonged grief,
feelings of guilt and anger.

The Covid-19 pandemic exposed and exacerbated the underlying fragility of the adult social
care workforce. It entered the pandemic with a high number of staff vacancies and staff
turnover, and care workers already working long hours for low pay and in poor working
conditions.

Notwithstanding the extraordinary lengths to which care workers went to provide care
during the pandemic, it was clear to me that for too long the adult social care sector has
been undervalued and under-recognised. This includes workers providing domiciliary care
and the many millions of unpaid carers on whom the pandemic placed increased and huge
demands. The Inquiry’s Terms of Reference do not permit me to consider wider adult social
care reform but I hope the Independent Commission on Adult Social Care, chaired by
Baroness Casey of Blackstock, will address some of these more systemic issues in England.




                                                                                                 vii

=== PAGE 9 ===
       Module 6: Care sector




       When consulting on the Terms of Reference for this Inquiry, I met with bereaved families
       from across the UK. From my very first meeting with bereaved family members, I realised
       just how important the investigation into the care sector is to many of them. Over the course
       of the Inquiry’s Module 6 public hearings in June and July 2025, many witnesses spoke
       movingly about what had happened to them and their families. I thank them, as well as
       those who shared their stories with the Inquiry’s listening exercise, Every Story Matters, and
       those who agreed to take part in the impact films, for all their contributions. Their accounts
       put a spotlight on the devastating impact that Covid-19 had on people receiving and
       providing care and their loved ones.

       I am also grateful to the material providers, the 27 Core Participants, their members and
       representatives and, of course, the Inquiry’s secretariat and legal team. Module 6 has
       gathered more than 170 written statements and more than 100,000 pages of evidence.
       All of this material has been analysed carefully to produce this Report.

       My conclusions about the impact of the Covid-19 pandemic on the adult social care sector in
       the UK demonstrate clearly why it cannot be allowed to enter the next pandemic in such an
       unprepared state, that it deserves better recognition and support, and why it is so important
       that my recommendations are implemented.




       The Rt Hon the Baroness Hallett DBE

       20 October 2026

Module 6: Care sector




      “Sheryl died, alone, from Covid. We will never know for sure whether she caught
        Covid in the care home that was supposed to keep her safe. What we do know is
        that Covid … was brought into the home by discharged hospital patients in an
        environment with little PPE [personal protective equipment] or effective infection
        control measures.”1

        Linda Dinsdale, Covid-19 Bereaved Families for Justice UK



      “I asked to be able to see my mum, but they refused. It had been 10 months, since
        I last had physical contact and now, she was gravely ill. In the last few days of her
        life, I was allowed to be outside the patio door to her room and the staff wheeled
        her bed closer to me. I stayed there for hours each day in the freezing cold, rain,
        and snow.”

      “This is an open-ended trauma, a wound that won’t close.”2

        Jane Wier-Wierzbowska, Covid-19 Bereaved Families for Justice UK



      “I was devastated and blindsided to discover that when [Dad] was most vulnerable,
        the care home/district nurses had failed to administer the fluids along with the rest
        of the care package. As a consequence of this neglect, he was dehydrated and in
        pain … There was a complete lack of support.”3

        Catherine Griffiths, Covid-19 Bereaved Families for Justice Cymru



      “I was sent a photo showing Dad talking on the phone to a family member through
        the lounge window. There is a staff member standing less than two feet away from
        Dad assisting him in using the phone, the staff member is wearing a long sleeve
        shirt buttoned at the wrist – no mask, no gloves, no apron, yet I’ve been told in
        writing that no-one is allowed in the lounge without a mask. After Dad died I
        discovered that on the day in question the staff member still had a cough which
        remained until after Dad’s death.”4

        Judith Kilbee, Scottish Covid Bereaved

=== PAGE 14 ===
Executive summary

=== PAGE 15 ===
    Module 6: Care sector




    The Covid-19 pandemic had a devastating impact on the many millions of people across the
    UK who received adult social care and an equally devastating impact on the millions more
    who provided that care, whether paid or unpaid. The impact was particularly acute in care
    homes, where there were more than 43,000 recorded deaths involving Covid-19 between
    March 2020 and July 2022; Covid-19 was the second leading cause of death in care homes
    in all four nations of the UK in 2020.

    Rhetoric such as the statement of Matt Hancock (Secretary of State for Health and Social
    Care from July 2018 to June 2021) that the UK government had tried to throw “a protective
    ring” around care homes “right from the start” does not accurately reflect the lack of
    action taken to protect care homes across the UK in March and the first half of April 2020.
    The UK government and devolved administrations knew, prior to the Covid-19 pandemic,
    of the extreme vulnerability of care home residents to respiratory viruses. Despite this, care
    homes received insufficient protection at the time they most needed it.

    In March 2020, the UK government and devolved administrations took the decision
    to expedite the discharge of patients from hospital. While expedited discharges were
    necessary to free up much-needed hospital beds, the lack of testing capacity meant that
    patients were discharged from hospital without knowing their Covid-19 status. There
    was also no vaccine and there were shortages of personal protective equipment (PPE).
    Isolation was therefore an important infection prevention and control (IPC) measure to
    stop the spread of Covid-19 in care homes. However, isolation of all patients discharged
    from hospitals to care homes was not initially advised in March 2020 in England, Wales
    and Northern Ireland. This was a significant omission. There should also have been better
    planning to accommodate residents who lived in care homes that were unable to provide
    safe isolation facilities.

    There were also other failures in relation to IPC guidance and the lack of PPE. The IPC
    guidance introduced at the beginning of the Covid-19 pandemic advised care workers to
    avoid being within two metres of the person being cared for. This guidance was wholly
    unrealistic given that most social care is delivered at close quarters. The advice therefore
    provided little or no protection for either the provider or the recipient of care.

    From mid-March 2020, care workers were advised to wear PPE. However, prior to the
    pandemic, the adult social care sector was responsible for sourcing its own PPE. Care
    providers, especially domiciliary care providers (providing care at home), therefore
    did not keep large stocks of PPE and not all providers had relationships with PPE
    wholesalers. There was also a severe shortage of PPE supplies for the adult social care
    sector, attributable to a number of factors, including the worldwide shortages of PPE and
    inadequate supplies in the UK’s PPE stockpile. Shortages of PPE supplies, especially in
    the first wave of the pandemic, led to the wholly unacceptable position that care workers
    had to work either in unsuitable or poor-quality PPE, or with no PPE at all, and so without
    the protection they needed. While the UK government and devolved administrations
    subsequently sought to help the sector access PPE with one-off emergency drops of PPE,
    and there were hotlines, portals and hubs set up to deal with requests for PPE, none of
    these steps were without problems.

2

=== PAGE 16 ===
                                                                             Executive summary




By mid to late April 2020, testing capacity in each nation allowed all hospital patients
discharged to care homes to be tested for Covid-19. Subsequently, testing of both
symptomatic care workers and residents was rolled out, followed by wider asymptomatic
testing. However, there was widespread concern that discharging patients from hospital
without a test introduced Covid-19 to care homes, leading to the deaths of many residents.
This led to a number of studies being conducted across the UK, which concluded that
admissions to care homes from hospitals were one of a number of routes of ingress for
the virus and were unlikely to have been the dominant driver of infections in care homes
in the first wave of the pandemic. The precise extent to which hospital discharges seeded
infections in care homes could not be determined, due to the lack of testing capacity in the
early months.

Other likely routes included transmission via staff and visitors. Each nation of the UK
sought to restrict staff movement between care settings, including proposals in England
to introduce legislation limiting staff movement. These restrictions presented significant
practical challenges, not least the very real potential for there to be insufficient care workers
to provide care safely. In a sector where the use of agency and bank staff is not uncommon
and low pay and poor working conditions were already prevalent, the restrictions also
had a significant adverse effect on staff income. All four governments acknowledged the
financial impact that restrictions would have on care workers and so put in place funding to
provide some recompense. However, this funding did not always filter through to front-line
care workers.

Supporting care workers to self-isolate was also a vital way to prevent the spread of
infection. There were welcome changes to sick pay in March to May 2020 in England,
Scotland and Northern Ireland which meant that sick care workers and those required
to self-isolate received (or should have received) enhanced rates of sick pay or their full
normal wage. These changes were not made in Wales until November 2020, which was
too late. Given the critical nature of their role, financial support to protect care workers from
economic hardship and to mitigate the risk of the virus being spread by staff needs to be
in place at the start of any pandemic.

The Covid-19 pandemic also saw the introduction of restrictions on the number of visitors
who could enter care homes. Visits by professionals, including healthcare workers,
dramatically reduced as UK-wide guidance strongly discouraged all but essential in-person
visits. This resulted in care workers stepping in to provide this care. By mid-March 2020,
regulatory inspections also decreased significantly as in-person routine inspections of
care settings were suspended. As acknowledged by regulators, the absence of routine
inspections (along with an absence of visits by loved ones) increased the lack of oversight
of care settings and potentially increased the risk of safeguarding concerns, abuse and
non-adherence to IPC measures going unnoticed and unreported. The regulators sought
to provide support to the care sector via check-in calls and moved to risk-based regulatory
activity focusing on higher-risk settings. Remote inspections were trialled but were not a
substitute for in-person inspections and generally were considered to be of limited use.



                                                                                                    3

=== PAGE 17 ===
    Module 6: Care sector




    Visits from families and loved ones were also restricted. These restrictions brought into
    sharp focus the difficult choices that governments and ministers were required to make.
    To allow visitors into care homes ran the risk of introducing a deadly virus to an already
    vulnerable population. However, preventing visits caused incalculable harm to residents
    and their loved ones and often resulted in a significant decline in the health and wellbeing
    of residents, especially those with complex cognitive conditions such as dementia.
    The severity of the restrictions and the length of time they were imposed had profound
    and enduring effects. As some told the Inquiry’s listening exercise, Every Story Matters,
    the restrictions left residents feeling “isolated”, “abandoned” and “alone”.1

    Attempts to provide alternatives, such as remote or garden visits, were beneficial for some
    but not all residents. It was not until the summer of 2020 that visiting restrictions started
    to ease. Each nation moved to a more localised risk-based approach to visiting, based on
    the risks visiting posed to individual care homes. With varying infection rates and different
    circumstances prevailing in care homes, it was inevitable that some care homes would
    be able to allow more visits than others. It was equally inevitable that this would cause
    frustration and upset for those residents and families where stricter visiting restrictions
    remained in place.

    The summer of 2020 also brought with it recognition that some family members played
    an enhanced caring role and provided essential care. The concept of designated visitors,
    essential carers and care partners was therefore introduced but the importance of this role
    should have been acknowledged and established sooner.

    Although visiting guidance allowed exceptions to be made for end-of-life visits, some
    families were not permitted to be with their dying loved one. This caused immense upset
    and harm.


        “My mother’s death in the care home – alone and confused about why her only child
          appeared to have abandoned her. It absolutely kills me to think about it. I felt, and
          still feel, angry, powerless and distraught.”2

         Contributor, Every Story Matters


    Adult social care workers also faced caring for many people who fell seriously ill and died.
    The pandemic accelerated the need for advance care plans (which record an individual’s
    preferences for future medical care) to be in place. This resulted in sensitive discussions
    occurring in a hurried manner or not occurring at all. There was no mandatory end-of-life
    care training for all health and social care professionals working in the adult social care
    sector in the UK before the Covid-19 pandemic. This resulted in care workers lacking the
    requisite skills to ensure that advance care plans were discussed in as thoughtful and
    compassionate a way as possible. Mandatory end-of-life care training is needed.

    1   Every Story Matters: Adult Social Care Sector, pp8, 14, 37 (INQ000587564)
    2   Every Story Matters: Adult Social Care Sector, p57 (INQ000587564)


4

=== PAGE 18 ===
                                                                           Executive summary




In common with Module 3, the Inquiry heard concerns about Do Not Attempt
Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (DNACPR) decisions. A DNACPR decision is a clinical
decision not to attempt CPR and individuals can also state their wishes in advance.
There were concerns that some decisions were made inappropriately, insensitively, without
proper consultation and in some cases wholly improperly, based on an individual’s age,
health condition or a learning disability. While the Inquiry has found no evidence of any
UK-wide or nation-specific policy encouraging or condoning the ‘blanket’ use of DNACPRs
and all four governments reiterated that every DNACPR decision should be made on an
individual basis, it bears repeating that, as with advance care planning, discussions about
DNACPR must be handled sensitively and at a pace that suits the individual.

As the Inquiry has noted in previous Reports, data gaps were a significant problem,
particularly in the early months of the pandemic. Data collection, including on the numbers
of people receiving care, the workforce and capacity in the sector, was also poor. Given
that Exercise Cygnus, a pandemic exercise, recommended the development of methods
to assess social care and surge capacity in October 2016, these data should have been
available from the start of the Covid-19 pandemic.

Differences in the way mortality data were collected and published meant that real-time
data on the number of deaths in care homes in each nation were not available until mid to
late April 2020. It was also difficult to ascertain the number of people receiving domiciliary
care who had died. The data that are available suggest that more people died at home
during the pandemic than prior to the pandemic. This increased the demand for end-
of-life care at home but healthcare professionals were not always available to provide
it. More end-of-life care therefore had to be provided by people who were untrained,
including sometimes by family and friends, without the necessary medication and without
appropriate support.

The impact on the approximately 5.8 million unpaid carers across the UK was pronounced,
as they had to provide more complex care for longer hours with less – if any – respite care.
Unpaid carers are the backbone of care for many millions of people. Some support was
made available to them during the pandemic but it came too late and problems identifying
unpaid carers made it difficult to deliver support to them.

There were at least 1.5 million care workers providing care during the pandemic, many
of whom put their lives at risk to provide care. There were, however, no centrally recorded
data on the number of care workers who died from Covid-19. This hindered the ability of the
UK government and devolved administrations to understand the severity and transmission
of the virus and its impact on the social care workforce.

In addition to working longer hours and taking on more responsibility, care workers
witnessed an increase in the number of deaths of people they were caring for. Care workers
went to extraordinary lengths to provide care and show compassion to those who were
dying. This had a profound and, in some cases, long-lasting impact on workers’ health



                                                                                                 5

=== PAGE 19 ===
Module 6: Care sector




and wellbeing and led to many workers suffering burn-out and leaving their caring role.
A manager of a domiciliary care agency said:

    “The trauma was so overwhelming that some of these remarkable individuals chose to
     leave the care profession, unable to overcome the emotional toll of what transpired.”3

The adult social care workforce entered the Covid-19 pandemic in a fragile state with a high
number of staff vacancies and staff turnover. When combined with the inevitable absences
of staff resulting from sickness, the requirement to self-isolate and burn-out, there was
an urgent need to recruit more staff. Workforce appeals and initiatives were launched to
encourage people to join the sector but these were not long-term solutions to the wider
workforce issues faced by the sector. Wider reform of the sector is not within the Inquiry’s
remit. Its long-term transformation is already being considered by Baroness Casey’s
Independent Commission on Adult Social Care in England, which is expected to report in
the summer of 2027.

The existence of workforce registers in Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland had real
and tangible benefits during the pandemic. Recently retired care professionals could
be easily identified and invited to return to work. They enabled more direct and quicker
communication with the workforce to signpost advice, guidance and support and implement
workforce training. Registers also provided ministers and other decision-makers with more
up-to-date information about the impact of their decisions. Although the introduction of a
register in England has been explored by previous UK governments, there is no workforce
register in England. The time has now come to introduce a voluntary register in England
as a way to professionalise the sector and improve the recognition and retention of the
workforce. It will also help with a future emergency response. Entering a pandemic with
a less fragile workforce is critical to the ability of the care sector to respond.

Witnesses repeatedly spoke of the need for recognition of the vital work undertaken by
the adult social care workforce. The adult social care sector has long considered itself
overlooked and undervalued by successive governments; it sees itself as the ‘poor relation’
of the UK’s healthcare systems. The response of the four governments to the Covid-19
pandemic did little, if anything, to dispel that belief.

So many of these problems, as noted by the Inquiry in other Module Reports, were
attributable to a lack of pre-pandemic planning. It was unclear whether sector-specific
pandemic plans (as opposed to plans for other emergencies, such as power outages or
cyber attacks) existed at local government or provider level. UK government and devolved
administration planning documents were either incomplete or vague about planning
requirements, provided minimal detail and used imprecise terminology. It was therefore
unclear who or which organisation was responsible for different aspects of the pandemic
response. The fragmented structure of adult social care also meant that ministers had
less oversight of the sector and fewer levers to be able to direct what should happen
in it. The action taken by the UK government and devolved administrations to try to

3   INQ000587381_0049 para 97.2


6

=== PAGE 20 ===
                                                                           Executive summary




protect workers and users of care came too late. These failures were the direct result of an
absence of proper pandemic planning for the adult social care sector. It underlines why the
recommendations in this Report must be acted upon now before the next pandemic strikes.

The adult social care sector as a whole also appeared to be something of an afterthought,
where the mantra of ‘protecting the NHS’ sometimes came at the expense of the users and
providers of care. The failures set out in this Report created environments in care settings in
which the virus could spread like wildfire.

The Report makes a number of recommendations to ensure that the UK’s adult social
care sector is better protected and better equipped to respond to the next pandemic.
In summary, the Inquiry recommends:

  • improving arrangements for pandemic planning to ensure clearer structures,
     roles and responsibilities;
  • better planning for isolation;
  • implementing financial support schemes that are ready to be rolled out to support
     adult social care staff to self-isolate or to limit staff movement;
  • publishing guidance on visiting restrictions;
  • maintaining inspection and oversight by developing protocols to enable inspections
     to continue, including, where possible, on-site inspections;
  • improving collection of data about deaths in the adult social care sector;
  • establishing mandatory end-of-life training for relevant health and social care
     workers; and
  • recognising and supporting the social care workforce in England through the
     introduction of a voluntary register.

A full list of the Inquiry’s recommendations is included in Appendix 3.

=== PAGE 21 ===
Chapter 1: Overview of the
adult social care sector

=== PAGE 22 ===
                                                             Chapter 1: Overview of the adult social care sector




Introduction
1.1.      It is estimated that about half a million people resided in adult care homes at
          the start of the coronavirus (Covid-19) pandemic and nearly 1 million people were
          supported to live in their own homes (domiciliary care). Many millions more were
          looked after by family, friends and neighbours who provided unpaid care. The adult
          social care sector also included a range of other settings, such as day centres and
          supported living accommodation. Yet there is a widely held belief that the adult
          social care sector is undervalued and under-recognised – that it is seen as the
          “Cinderella” service to healthcare systems.1

1.2.      This chapter provides an overview of the sector, focusing on care homes, domiciliary
          care and unpaid care. It examines the introduction of widespread data collection
          about people in receipt of care, the workforce and capacity in the sector during
          the pandemic. It also assesses the extent to which the sector was considered in
          pandemic planning.

1.3.      More generally, this Report will examine some of the decisions that may have
          contributed to the widely held belief that the adult social care sector was not given
          the priority it deserved. It will consider the widespread perception – particularly at
          the outset of the pandemic – that healthcare systems in the four nations of the UK
          (over which the UK government and devolved administrations individually exercise
          direct control) were prioritised at the expense of the adult social care sector. Social
          care relating to children is being explored by the Inquiry in Module 8: Children and
          young people.


The adult social care sector
1.4.      As Helen Wildbore (Director of Care Rights UK, a charity focusing on the rights
          of people in care, from 2019 to 2025) explained, the care sector is hugely diverse,
          both in the types of care settings and the types of support needed by people who
          use adult social care.2 As noted by Professor Dame Jenny Harries (Deputy Chief
          Medical Officer for England from July 2019 to March 2021), adult social care is also
          commissioned from a wide variety of independent and private providers, in sharp
          contrast to the NHS.3

consistently across sources and across the UK, and some are used interchangeably, but all mean that the service is delivered by a
provider that is independent of local authorities or Health and Social Care trusts.

=== PAGE 23 ===
     Module 6: Care sector




     Users of adult social care
     1.5.      Most adult care homes are residential homes (which provide accommodation and
               help with personal tasks) or nursing care homes (which, in addition, provide access
               to nursing care). Across the UK, as at July 2020, there were 18,075 adult care homes,
               with 537,656 available beds. The majority of care homes, both in 2020 and more
               recently in 2025, were for older people (typically defined as those aged 65 and
               over).4

     1.6.      In 2025, more than 85% of care home residents were likely to have dementia,
               whether formally diagnosed or not.5 Exact figures are not consistently collected
               about the number of adults with learning disabilities receiving adult social care,
               but support for them requires significant expenditure across the UK.6

     1.7.      There are no UK-wide data about the number of people who receive domiciliary care
               in their own homes. However, Dr Jane Townson (Chief Executive of the Homecare
               Association, the UK’s membership body for homecare providers, from May 2019)
               explained that professional providers support nearly 1 million adults across the UK
               at any given time.7 The majority of those receiving domiciliary care in the UK are
               aged 65 and over.8 As of July 2019, about 9,000 domiciliary care providers were
               registered with the Care Quality Commission in England. In May 2020, approximately
               550 domiciliary care providers were registered with Care Inspectorate Wales,
               approximately 1,400 were registered with the Care Inspectorate in Scotland,
               and 300 were registered with the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority
               in Northern Ireland.9

     1.8.      Precise data about the number of people receiving unpaid care are also not readily
               available. Data from 2021 and 2022 suggest that approximately 5.8 million people
               – usually family, friends and neighbours – provided unpaid care across the UK.

          Based on census data from 2021 in England, Wales and Northern Ireland, and from
          2022 in Scotland, this included 4.7 million people in England, 311,000 people in
          Wales, 222,000 people in Northern Ireland and 627,700 people in Scotland.
          Census data also showed that approximately 1.7 million people were providing
          significant amounts of unpaid care, at 50 hours or more per week.10


The adult social care workforce
1.9.      The workforce in the adult social care sector is also diverse. It includes, among
          others, permanent employees, agency staff, people who work across multiple
          settings, registered managers and registered nurses.

1.10.     Snapshots of the numbers of people working in adult social care across the
          four nations between 2019 and 2021 show the significant size of this workforce
          (see Table 1), although comparisons between the four nations are difficult, due to
          differences in data collection.11 Based on these figures, it is estimated that more than
          1.5 million people worked in adult social care over the course of the pandemic.

1.11.     Prior to and during the pandemic, many care workers worked on zero-hours
          contracts, meaning that they were not guaranteed any minimum working hours.
          In England in 2019/20, 24% of the adult social care workforce was employed on
          zero-hours contracts, including more than 40% of the domiciliary care workforce.12
          A 2019 report confirmed that 11% of the social care workforce in Scotland was on
          zero-hours contracts.13 In Wales, responses to a survey conducted in 2020 by the

           Welsh Institute for Health and Social Care, a health and care policy research institute,
           indicated that 22% of care workers employed by local authorities and in the private/
           independent sector and 4% of care workers in the third sector (which refers to
           charities, cooperatives, social value organisations and other not-for-profit employers
           who deliver social care services, outside the local authority and independent
           sectors) were on zero-hours contracts.14 Equivalent data on zero-hours contracts are
           not available for Northern Ireland. While zero-hours contracts may provide flexibility,
           during the Covid-19 pandemic this often meant that care workers did not have a
           stable income and might not have qualified for Statutory Sick Pay (see Chapter 3:
           Protective measures).15

 1.12.     At the outset of the pandemic, staff vacancies also put the sector in a precarious
           position. In England, in March 2020, there were about 120,000 vacancies in the
           sector, amounting to 8% of the workforce.16 In Northern Ireland, as at 31 March 2020,
           Health and Social Care trusts were recruiting to fill vacancies of 7.8% in the social
           care workforce (including domiciliary care). This had risen to 12.2% by 31 December
           2021.17 In Scotland, 48% of care homes for adults reported vacancies in 2020, rising
           to 63% in 2021. In domiciliary care, 59% of services reported vacancies in 2020
           and 71% in 2021.18 Social Care Wales reported 5.3% of jobs in adult residential care
           as vacant on 31 March 2021.19 Albert Heaney (Deputy Director General Health and
           Social Services Group in the Welsh Government from March 2020 to June 2021 and
           Chief Social Care Officer for Wales from June 2021) explained that the adult social
           care sector in Wales was in a fragile and vulnerable state going into the pandemic,
           with a high turnover of staff and a very low-paid workforce.20 Jeane Freeman
           (Cabinet Secretary for Health and Sport in the Scottish Government from June 2018
           to May 2021) described a similar longstanding position in Scotland, noting significant
           difficulties in staff recruitment and retention in the care sector there.21

1.13.     Staff shortages increased throughout the pandemic as care workers became unwell
          or were required to self-isolate, as care workers told the Inquiry’s listening exercise,
          Every Story Matters.22


               “So not only were you having all the service users becoming poorly, you
                 were having all the staff becoming poorly as well … the shortage of staff
                 was unbelievable.”23

                Care home worker, England, Every Story Matters


1.14.     Despite valiant efforts by staff who continued to work, shortages necessarily affected
          the quality of care that could be delivered.24 Similar issues arose in domiciliary care.


               “I think one particular shift there was something like 20% of the workforce
                 working, because either they had Covid, or they were off with the stress.”25

                Domiciliary care worker, England, Every Story Matters


1.15.     The Inquiry heard of the stress caused by high levels of staff turnover during the
          pandemic.


               “The staff turnover was horrendous, we had new carers coming in every day,
                 [which was] difficult for infection control and for staff, to meet new people
                 every day meant we didn’t really have solidarity with one another. It felt like
                 we were disposable.”26

                Registered manager, England, Every Story Matters


1.16.     Prior to the pandemic, workforce vacancies and absences meant that care homes
          often relied on agency workers to ensure that there were safe staffing levels.27
          Agency staff would therefore often work across multiple settings. In February 2020,
          staff were identified as a possible route of transmission of the virus into care
          settings.28 (Staff movement is considered further in Chapter 3: Protective measures.)

=== PAGE 27 ===
     Module 6: Care sector




     Responsibility for the adult social care sector
     1.17.    Responsibility for setting the overarching policy and legal framework for adult social
              care across the UK lies with the UK government (for England) and each devolved
              administration:

              • In England, this responsibility rests with the Department of Health and Social
                  Care; in Northern Ireland, it is the Department of Health (Northern Ireland).29
              • In Wales, the Health and Social Services Group supports “Welsh Ministers take
                  over perform their policy oversight and expectations” of the care system.30
              • For the Scottish Government, social care was in the remit of the Community
                  Health and Care Directorate until June 2020, when the Directorate for Mental
                  Health and Social Care was established. Since December 2021, the Social Care
                  and National Care Service Development Directorate has led on social care
                  policy.31

     1.18.    The delivery of adult social care – for example, organising care assessments and
              assisting with securing appropriate placements – sits at a level below the four
              governments of the UK. In England and Wales, local authorities must plan and secure
              adult social care. This includes assessing whether an adult has needs for care and
              support. The local authority may then have a duty to meet identified needs,
              depending on the person’s circumstances.32 In Scotland, local authorities and health
              boards work together to plan and commission adult community health and social
              care services. In 31 of 32 local authority areas, this responsibility is delegated to an
              integration joint board, which uses a single, pooled budget to commission services
              from territorial health boards and local authorities, operating jointly as health and
              social care partnerships.33

     1.19.    This division of responsibilities – between the UK government, Welsh Government
              and Scottish Government and local government – adds a layer of complexity to the
              systems in England, Wales and Scotland. The Welsh Local Government Association
              (which represents all 22 local authorities in Wales) explained that managing the
              conflicting demands between local authorities (which have to address the needs
              and specific features of their areas) and national government (which is responsible
              for wider policy) remains a challenge for the sector.34

=== PAGE 28 ===
                                                             Chapter 1: Overview of the adult social care sector




1.20.     In Northern Ireland, delivery of health and social care is structurally integrated.
          The five Health and Social Care trusts (which cover geographical areas and deliver
          front-line services) are responsible for the provision of both health and social care
          services, including commissioning care for individuals.35

1.21.     Care is commissioned by local authorities and Health and Social Care trusts,
          including from care home and domiciliary care providers. Only a very small
          percentage of care homes are run by local authorities or Health and Social Care
          trusts themselves. In 2020, approximately 84% of care homes in Wales were in
          the private/independent sector.36 In 2023, more than 93% of care home beds in
          Northern Ireland were provided by the independent sector (services provided by
          private organisations).37 In 2024, about 95% of care home beds in England were
          provided by the independent sector (private and voluntary).38 In Scotland in 2025,
          84% of residents in care homes for older people were in homes run by the private
          sector.39 While data collection periods vary between nations, the majority of
          domiciliary care is similarly not provided by local authorities or Health and Social
          Care trusts themselves.40 This means that governments have far less oversight and
          control over the adult social care sector than over healthcare systems.

1.22.     The adult social care system in England is “large, fragmented and more distant from
          Government than services like the NHS”.41 The Covid-19 pandemic shone a light on
          the complex lines of accountability in the sector in England. While accountability
          ultimately rests with the Secretary of State for Health and Social Care, funding is
          provided by the Ministry of Housing, Communities and Local Government to local
          authorities.42 Local authorities commission and organise delivery of care from many
          thousands of different providers and those providers are regulated by the Care
          Quality Commission.43 This complicated arrangement is referred to in a report from
          the National Audit Office in March 2021.44

=== PAGE 29 ===
 Module 6: Care sector




 1.23.     Matt Hancock (Secretary of State for Health and Social Care from July 2018 to June
           2021) said that there was a “hodgepodge of accountability” in England for adult
           social care services, which meant that the levers that the UK government had were
           “weak”.45 Professor Sir Michael McBride (Chief Medical Officer for Northern Ireland
           from September 2006) agreed that “at times we didn’t have all the levers that we
           needed to be able to pull”.46

 1.24.     Ms Freeman told the Inquiry that, in contrast to the health service, there was no
           power for ministers to direct what should happen in adult social care.47 Vaughan
           Gething (Minister for Health and Social Services in the Welsh Government from May
           2016 to May 2021) explained that the adult social care sector is “in a difficult position
           compared to the NHS where you have one stream of accountability going through
           the service”.48

 1.25.     Greater oversight of the adult social care sector has been introduced in recent
           years. In England, the Health and Care Act 2022 introduced new powers to allow
           the Secretary of State to intervene where they are satisfied that local authorities
           have failed, or are failing, to discharge their functions.49 It also introduced a legal
           duty for the Care Quality Commission to assess local authorities’ delivery of their
           adult social care duties under Part 1 of the Care Act 2014 (which outlines the duties
           and role of local authorities to ensure that care and support are provided to people
           who need them).50

 1.26.     In April 2026, the Care Quality Commission published the Assessment Framework
           for Local Authority Assurance, outlining how local authorities will be assessed under
           four separate themes – working with people, support, safety and leadership.51 The
           commission obtains feedback, including from the relevant local authority, providers,
           unpaid carers, and voluntary and community groups. It also speaks directly to a small
           number of users of care and considers the responses received to its ‘Give feedback
           on care’ service.52 By early June 2026, the vast majority of assessment reports had
           been published.53 While this assessment process is still in its infancy and so cannot
           be fully evaluated, the Inquiry considers that this is a step in the right direction.

Pandemic planning for adult social care
1.27.     Many people who use adult social care services are at heightened risk of severe
          illness from respiratory viruses and may require hospitalisation as a result.54 Older
          adults with social care needs experience a larger number of emergency hospital
          admissions than those with no care needs.55 This brings into focus the close inter-
          relationship between the adult social care sector and healthcare.56 As the Welsh
          Government acknowledged, social care plays a “critical role in helping people to live
          well and stay well at home, so that they don’t need to be admitted to hospital either
          at all, or as often”.57

1.28.     When people are supported to receive the best possible health outcomes, pressures
          on healthcare settings are reduced. NHS England has recognised that, too often,
          people living with dementia have been admitted to hospital as a result of a lack of
          appropriate support in residential care and the community.58 Its Long Term Plan,
          published in 2019, observed that people resident in care homes:

              “account for 185,000 emergency admissions each year and 1.46 million
               emergency bed days, with 35–40% of emergency admissions potentially
               avoidable. Evidence suggests that many people living in care homes are not
               having their needs assessed and addressed as well as they could be, often
               resulting in unnecessary, unplanned and avoidable admissions to hospital
               and sub-optimal medication regimes.”59

1.29.     The Welsh Government has reflected its ambition for joined-up health and care
          services through A Healthier Wales: Our Plan for Health and Social Care.60 In July
          2022, the Health and Care Act 2022 created statutory integrated care partnerships
          in England. These brought together the NHS, local authorities and other partners in
          social care, as well as the voluntary, community and social enterprise sectors in each
          of 42 local areas jointly to plan and deliver health and care services. Integrated care
          partnerships were responsible for developing integrated care strategies for their
          local population.61 The Health Bill, which is currently progressing through Parliament,
          proposes the removal of the requirement for integrated care partnerships.62 It is too
          early to predict the impact this could have. However, it risks care providers having

=== PAGE 31 ===
 Module 6: Care sector




           less influence in planning for the delivery of care. Also, frequent changes to
           structures and legislation affecting the care sector, even if well intended, are likely
           to create confusion for those involved in the sector and undermine effective
           planning.

 1.30.     The adult social care sector should be viewed as part of the UK’s critical
           infrastructure, essential to ensuring that the health and social care needs of a
           vulnerable part of the population continue to be met. As examined below, planning
           and pandemic exercises prior to the Covid-19 pandemic did not lead to adequate
           planning for the impact that a pandemic would have on people using and providing
           adult social care and how identified risks could be mitigated.


 Government planning
 1.31.     The UK Influenza Pandemic Preparedness Strategy 2011 (prepared by the UK
           government and adopted by the devolved administrations) foresaw that there might
           be particular challenges in maintaining social care services in all but a mild influenza
           pandemic.63 However, this acknowledgement failed to translate into meaningful
           plans at a nation-specific level for how best to protect the care sector from the
           difficulties it would face.

 1.32.     The Department of Health and Social Care’s 2012 Health and Social Care Influenza
           Pandemic Preparedness and Response (intended to be read in conjunction with the
           UK Influenza Pandemic Preparedness Strategy 2011) focused on the operational
           aspects of pandemic response in both the health and social care sectors.
           The longest social care section ran to just four pages.64 This section was largely
           mirrored in guidance in Northern Ireland and Wales, in 2013 and 2014 respectively.65
           In Scotland, Influenza Pandemic Preparedness: Guidance for Health and Social Care
           Services in Scotland was still in draft form in 2019, shortly before the pandemic.
           The section on preparedness and response in adult social care was very short.66

 1.33.     The 2016 National Risk Assessment, conducted by the UK government to assist
           contingency planning for domestic emergencies, identified that social care was
           already under significant pressure, especially in respect of the workforce, provider
           capacity, quality and patient flow. It also noted that an emergency, including an
           influenza pandemic, would have an additional impact on these existing risk areas.67

=== PAGE 32 ===
                                                         Chapter 1: Overview of the adult social care sector




1.34.    Despite this understanding that a pandemic could have a severe impact on adult
         social care, prior to the arrival of Covid-19, few pandemic exercises had considered,
         practically, how the sector would respond and cope during a pandemic.


Pandemic exercises
1.35.    As outlined in the Inquiry’s Module 1 Report, a number of key exercises took
         place across the UK between 2003 and 2018 to stress-test the UK’s pandemic
         preparedness systems.68 Small-scale exercises took place in 2003 and 2004 to test
         the preparedness and response to an outbreak of severe acute respiratory syndrome
         (SARS). Some of those exercises mentioned the social care sector briefly, while
         others did not mention it at all.69

Social care lessons from exercises

1.36.    In Wales, following a pandemic influenza exercise held in April 2009 and the
         emergence of the 2009 to 2010 H1N1 influenza pandemic (‘swine flu’), it was noted
         in November 2009 that further work was required to enhance the engagement with,
         and preparedness in, the “independent care sector”.70 A further pandemic exercise
         took place in 2014.71 Dr Andrew Goodall (Director General of Health and Social
         Services in the Welsh Government and Chief Executive of NHS Wales from June
         2014 to November 2021) agreed that, at the time, capacity in the adult social care
         sector to cope with a pandemic was the most significant area of concern because
         it directly went to matters of life and death in care homes.72

1.37.    In Scotland, in April 2016, Exercise Silver Swan assessed preparedness and
         response to a pandemic influenza outbreak over a prolonged period. Concerns
         emerged about the robustness of plans in respect of backup capacity for the
         provision of social care. The exercise report noted that partners in resilience
         partnership areas needed to ensure that they had a comprehensive, multi-agency

               planning framework in place for health and social care to respond to influenza
               pandemics of varying severities.73

     1.38.     In October 2016, Exercise Cygnus – a major, three-day, cross-government exercise
               across the UK – took place to test a pandemic-scale response. It identified the
               following issues in respect of social care:

               • In general, further planning was required to “operationalise current thoughts
                   about how the social care system might cope during a pandemic”.74
               • The Department for Communities and Local Government, the Department of
                   Health and Social Care, and directors of adult social services should, together
                   with colleagues in the devolved administrations, develop a “methodology for
                   assessing social care capacity and surge capacity during a pandemic”
                   (Lesson Identified 18).75
               • There should be an examination (it was not specified by whom) of the “possibility
                   of expanding social care real-estate and staffing capacity in the event of a worst
                   case scenario pandemic” (Lesson Identified 19).76

     1.39.     In March 2017, a Pandemic Flu Readiness Board (co-chaired by officials from the
               Cabinet Office and the Department of Health and Social Care) was established to
               oversee the delivery of a cross-government and UK-wide programme to address the
               recommendations from Exercise Cygnus.77 The programme was divided into five
               workstreams, with the Department of Health and Social Care taking the lead on adult
               social care. The identified work on adult social care capacity remained incomplete by
               the onset of the Covid-19 pandemic.78

     1.40.     In late 2017, the Department of Health and Social Care commissioned the
               Association of Directors of Adult Social Services (a membership organisation for local
               authority adult social care leaders in England) to produce advice and guidance on

     organisations (the emergency services, local authorities and NHS bodies) at the core of the response to most emergencies, which are
     subject to the full set of civil protection duties. Category 2 responders are organisations (the Health and Safety Executive, transport
     and utility companies) that are heavily involved in incidents that affect their particular sectors but are less likely to be involved at the
     heart of planning work. They have a lesser set of duties: cooperating and sharing relevant information with other Category 1 and 2
     responders. See the Civil Contingencies Act 2004, Schedule 1 (https://www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/2004/36/contents).

         planning for an influenza pandemic. The Association of Directors of Adult Social
         Services produced reports which were shared with the Department of Health and
         Social Care as well as directors of adult social services in 2018.79 One report – which
         focused on the critical information and data needed for directors of adult social
         services and their partners to plan for and make timely decisions about the
         reprioritisation of services in response to an influenza pandemic – recognised the
         importance of forward planning for tracking bed capacity in care homes, capacity for
         care to be delivered at home and staff sickness levels.80 A Guide for Pandemic Flu
         Planning for Directors of Adult Social Services was produced and circulated by the
         Association of Directors of Adult Social Services among its members. However, the
         association was unclear as to what use the Department of Health and Social Care
         made of the reports it had produced prior to the pandemic.81

1.41.    While the Department of Health and Social Care said that the recommendations
         from the Association of Directors of Adult Social Services helped to inform the
         pandemic response, the department also confirmed that implementation of
         those recommendations was paused before the arrival of Covid-19 due to “other
         contingencies”.82 As noted in the Inquiry’s Module 1 Report, a number of workstreams
         for pandemic preparedness were paused to focus resources on the UK government’s
         contingency planning for a ‘no deal’ exit from the European Union (known as
         Operation Yellowhammer).83 Planning for the impact of a pandemic on the adult
         social care sector, and any steps that could be taken to mitigate this, had stalled.

1.42.    The Northern Ireland Pandemic Flu Oversight Group, Wales’s Pandemic Flu
         Preparedness Group and Scotland’s Pandemic Flu Preparedness Board (which
         were established following Exercise Cygnus) had, likewise, not completed their
         work on the adult social care sector prior to the pandemic.84 Identified work on
         social care capacity had not been completed in Scotland.85 In Northern Ireland,
         it was determined that further work was required on the Health and Social Care
         Influenza Pandemic Surge Guidance, but this remained incomplete at the onset of
         the pandemic.86 Mr Gething agreed that the Welsh Government could have made
         more progress on issues related to private care home capacity and contingency
         plans.87 Having identified concerns about adult social care capacity, all four
         governments failed adequately to plan for how those identified concerns would
         be managed in a pandemic.

=== PAGE 35 ===
 Module 6: Care sector




 Lack of sector representation

 1.43.     Pandemic exercises also suffered from a lack of adult social care sector
           representation. Key stakeholders, including the Royal College of Nursing, private
           care home providers and the Association of Directors of Adult Social Services, did
           not take part in Exercise Cygnus.88 Likewise, only 8 out of the 42 local resilience
           forums in England and Wales (which coordinate the response to emergencies by
           bringing together representatives from local public services, including the NHS, local
           authorities, emergency services and others) took part.89 The Local Government
           Association (a national membership body for local authorities in England and Wales)
           was not sighted on the conclusions of Exercise Cygnus until it was released in 2020
           as a result of legal proceedings brought by another body.90 The Welsh Local
           Government Association also had no records of the Welsh Government involving
           local authorities in Wales in any follow-up action following the exercise.91

 1.44.     Professor Harries said that few people in the Department of Health and Social Care
           had direct or practical experience of having worked in or with care homes or on the
           commissioning of care home services, or an understanding of the local connections
           that would underpin the emergency response in the adult social care sector.92
           This made the involvement of representatives of the sector all the more important.

 1.45.     UK government ministers were aware that they lacked direct levers over the adult
           social care sector and that they were not responsible for delivery of adult social
           care services in the same way as healthcare services. Without adequate involvement
           of the sector in Exercise Cygnus, key voices with the most comprehensive
           understanding of adult social care were missing. Without sight of the conclusions
           of Exercise Cygnus in England, or adequate local authority involvement in follow-up
           action following the exercise in Wales, the UK government and Welsh Government
           afforded no opportunity for those who delivered adult social care to act upon its
           recommendations.

 1.46.     Even as late as February 2020, there was no adult social care representative in
           Exercise Nimbus, a UK-wide ministerial desktop exercise commissioned by the
           Cabinet Office.93 This exercise imagined a fictional meeting of COBR (the UK
           government’s crisis management centre for responding to whole-system civil
           emergencies) taking place in April 2020 in order to test decision-making processes
           and strategies.94 However, despite COBR having been informed at the end of

          January 2020 that “the elderly and those with existing health conditions will be
          disproportionately affected”, this did not lead to an in-depth discussion of the adult
          social care sector during Exercise Nimbus and there remained little focus on the
          sector.95 Mr Hancock, who chaired the fictional meeting, said that this was a
          ministerial exercise, with Mr Hancock and Helen Whately MP (Minister of State for
          Social Care at the Department of Health and Social Care from February 2020 to
          September 2021) representing social care. Although Sir Simon Stevens (later Lord
          Stevens of Birmingham), Chief Executive of NHS England from April 2014 to July

          an equivalent representative of the social care sector. Mr Hancock told the Inquiry
          that this was one of the problems.96

1.47.     Planning at a government level for the impact that a pandemic may have on the
          adult social care sector following Exercise Cygnus commenced in each nation but
          remained incomplete. This meant that the specific issues identified in respect of
          adult social care quickly became matters that had to be addressed after the arrival
          of Covid-19 in the UK. By this point, it was too late.

1.48.     Had there been greater involvement of the sector, this might have ensured a greater
          focus on the sector in pandemic planning. The Inquiry welcomes the greater
          involvement of local resilience forums (and local resilience partnerships in Scotland)
          in Exercise Pegasus, a UK-wide pandemic preparedness exercise that commenced
          in 2025.97 However, it is too early to tell whether the UK government and devolved
          administrations have properly learned one of the key lessons of the pandemic –
          namely, the need for proper planning and for the full engagement in that planning
          of those who will be significantly involved in or affected by any response.


Collection of data in the adult social care sector
1.49.     Despite Exercise Cygnus having recommended the development of a proper method
          for assessing social care capacity, the Department of Health and Social Care did not
          have an England-wide source of operational data about people in receipt of care, the
          workforce or current spare capacity, prior to the Covid-19 pandemic.98

1.50.     The NHS England Capacity Tracker, introduced in 2017, was a tool used to give
          hospitals visibility of the number of vacancies in care homes in England. However,
          completion of the tracker was voluntary and only about half of regulated care homes

              in England used it to report vacancies.99 Similarly, prior to the pandemic, there was
              no national overview of available bed capacity in care homes in Scotland and, in
              Northern Ireland, the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority did not collect
              information on bed usage and capacity in care homes.100 Although local authorities in
              Wales held information on bed capacity among care providers, Welsh ministers did
              not have access to this information routinely at a nationwide level.101

     1.51.    This meant that systems to collect crucial data on bed capacity in care homes and
              staffing levels across the wider sector required development once Covid-19 had
              already arrived in the UK. These data were required to facilitate a cross-government
              response in each nation. Such data would have informed key decisions such as
              expedited hospital discharge and the impact this would have on the sector (see
              Chapter 2: Care home admissions, testing and isolation). Each government
              had to develop regular, streamlined methods of collecting these data in the middle
              of the crisis.

     Development of capacity data during the pandemic

     1.52.    In March 2020, in England, the NHS England Capacity Tracker was identified as
              the most immediate, suitable tool for Covid-19 data collection from care home
              providers.102 It was updated to record:

              • whether a facility was open, closed or partially closed to admissions;
              • the overall status of a facility, with a green, amber and red status for
                  identified risks;
              • the number of residents with confirmed or suspected Covid-19;
              • staffing numbers by type, including those in full-time posts, vacancies, absences
                  and additional staffing required;
              • workforce information and status (ie whether staffing ratios were being met),
                  with a green, amber and red status for identified risks; and
              • personal protective equipment (PPE) status, with a green, amber and red status
                  to identify how long the current level of equipment was expected to last.103

              By 2 April 2020, the Capacity Tracker was described as the “single mechanism
              across the country to report bed vacancies and help manage demand”.104

1.53.    Having identified a gap in respect of data in domiciliary care, an online survey for
         domiciliary care providers in England was rolled out by the Care Quality Commission,
         with support from the Department of Health and Social Care, in mid-April 2020.105
         The survey asked providers questions about:

         • the number of people using the service and the number with confirmed or
             suspected Covid-19;
         • staffing numbers, including those unable to work as a result of Covid-19;
         • current stocks of PPE; and
         • whether they had capacity to provide additional care hours and, if so, how
             many.106

         From 30 November 2020, this information was incorporated into a new section of the
         NHS England Capacity Tracker.107

1.54.    On 26 March 2020, the Welsh Government wrote to care home providers alerting
         them to the immediate availability of a new online capacity tool to track vacancies
         in adult care homes.108 From 4 May 2020, local authorities were asked to complete
         weekly collections of the following social care workforce data:

         • the number of full-time equivalents employed in social services;
         • staff absences due to confirmed or suspected Covid-19 or other illness and the
             numbers of staff who were shielding;
         • testing data (numbers of positive tests, negative tests and those awaiting results);
         • capacity, including number of students, volunteers and new employees; and
         • a green, amber and red rating indicating whether there were sufficient staff to
             cover essential roles and services.109

         Local authorities were also asked to collect and provide information on their ability
         to ensure that adult social care needs were being met.110

1.55.    On 30 March 2020, the Department of Health (Northern Ireland) identified a need to
         gather specific monitoring information on care homes.111 As explained by Professor
         Sean Holland (Chief Social Work Officer in the Department of Health (Northern
         Ireland) from July 2010 to October 2022), “it became really important that we knew,
         day-to-day, how the system was doing”.112 On 17 April 2020, Professor Holland

              confirmed that work had been undertaken to produce a reporting template.113
              From 22 April 2020, providers were required to make a single daily return to the
              Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority.114 In May 2020, guidance outlined
              that providers were asked to provide information on:

              • numbers of occupied and vacant beds (both usable and unusable);
              • outbreaks, including Covid-19 cases, and the number of symptomatic residents,
                  residents tested (with a breakdown of positive tests), residents in self-isolation,
                  total residents affected to date, residents hospitalised and resident deaths; and
              • information on staff, including new positive cases, current symptomatic cases,
                  staff who have tested positive, staff in self-isolation, and total staff tested and
                  affected to date.115

              Domiciliary care agencies were not required to provide information relating to
              staffing and capacity to the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority.116

     1.56.    In Scotland, providers are required to maintain certain records and notify the Care
              Inspectorate of death, illness or other events.117 Between 3 April and 22 June 2020,
              the Care Inspectorate added a notification requirement for registered services to
              confirm if they needed urgent staffing support. From 21 April 2020, services were
              asked to provide a weekly notification of staff absences due to Covid-19 and, from
              August 2021, this was expanded to include information on all staff vacancies. From
              17 April 2020, services were also asked to record every suspected or confirmed case
              of Covid-19 in a specific notification.118 From 24 August 2020, all care homes were
              expected to use a new digital Safety Huddle Tool.119 The information captured
              included numbers of staff and residents tested (including numbers positive for
              Covid-19), care home occupancy, the number of closed care homes and additional
              information on staffing.120 From December 2020, the Care Inspectorate provided
              some specific reporting for the Scottish Government which included the number
              of Covid-19 cases and staff absences in domiciliary care.121

     1.57.    Ascertaining whether a care home can safely care for its current residents, safely
              accept new residents and safely isolate residents (for infection prevention and
              control purposes) requires data on the number of beds (both occupied and available)
              and the staffing levels. Having access to these data at a sector-wide level in each
              nation during a pandemic is vital, so that pressures in the adult social care system
              can be understood and managed. In times of increased pressure on the healthcare

         system, governments may wish to see the effective and timely discharge of patients,
         including to the social care sector. Data on staffing capacity in domiciliary care will
         provide an indication of whether people can be safely cared for at home.
         Understanding staffing levels also supports the ability to balance compliance with
         self-isolation policies for staff with the maintenance of safe staffing levels for
         effective care.

1.58.    Methods to track capacity in the adult social care sector (including methods to
         determine how surge capacity would be assessed in a pandemic) should have been
         properly established and fully utilised following Exercise Cygnus by the Department
         of Health and Social Care, the Department of Health (Northern Ireland) and the
         Scottish Government and Welsh Government. This work should have been
         completed prior to 2020. Decision-makers should have had immediate access to this
         information at the point at which they started making decisions that would impact the
         sector. In the future, there must be established methods to collect these data prior to
         a pandemic, so that fully informed decisions can be made from the outset. The more
         comprehensive the information that is collected, the better informed decisions will
         be. Plans should identify how the provision of data by care homes, domiciliary care
         providers or local partners can be streamlined to reduce administrative burdens on
         data providers.

1.59.    Given the importance of these data in both pandemic and non-pandemic times,
         data collection in the adult social care sector is a pressing priority.

Post-pandemic data collection

1.60.    The Inquiry welcomes the steps already taken in England, Scotland, Wales and
         Northern Ireland to improve the collection of data. However, collection of these
         data must be fully integrated into pandemic plans.

1.61.    In Scotland, work is ongoing to define a “care home minimum dataset”.122 In Wales,
         data collection continues on a monthly basis.123 In March 2023, the Northern Ireland
         Social Care Council (the regulatory body for the social care workforce in Northern
         Ireland) established the Social Care Collaborative Forum. The Inquiry understands
         that work is being taken forward there to progress a “regional digital solution for bed
         capacity” which will “improve communication regarding location of available beds”
         and provide an improved approach to data-gathering.124 Since July 2022, in England,
         all providers regulated by the Care Quality Commission have had a statutory duty to
         provide mandatory information to the Capacity Tracker. This information includes:
         “(i) care home bed vacancies, (ii) workforce resourcing and absences, (iii) vaccination
         statuses of staff and residents, and (iv) visiting options being supported.”125

     1.62.    The Inquiry also notes the publication of the UK government’s Pandemic
              Preparedness Strategy in March 2026. The UK government recognises the need to
              enhance “communication and data flows between the adult social care, public health
              and NHS systems” in England, which includes “sharing of available beds and service
              capacity”.126 However, plans must be translated into action.


     Provider and local-level plans
     1.63.    The Inquiry agrees with Ms Whately, who observed:

                   “[ Y]ou need a plan within a care provider … You need a plan at a local authority
                     level. You need a plan at the government level. And you probably need … routine
                     oversight of that process.”127

     1.64.    Local-level plans sit between plans held by care home providers and domiciliary care
              providers and agencies on the one hand and those held by the UK government and
              devolved administrations on the other. The Inquiry heard about two main types of
              plans at the provider and local levels:

              • business continuity plans, which address a wide range of potential incidents
                  (eg cyber attacks) and outline how a body or organisation will maintain essential
                  functions during an emergency;128 and
              • pandemic plans, which specifically focus on preparing for, responding to and
                  mitigating the impacts of a pandemic.129

              It was not possible to determine whether the terms ‘contingency plan’ and ‘business
              continuity plan’ were used interchangeably, or if they were plans that had different
              requirements.

     Provider-level plans

     1.65.    There was a confusing picture about precisely which plans providers were meant
              to have and what the plans were meant to address. The 2012 Health and Social
              Care Influenza Pandemic Preparedness and Response document produced by the
              Department of Health and Social Care and NHS England provided a high-level list
              of what should be included in “care homes plans” but did not specify who had
              responsibility for producing or assuring them.130 This was mirrored in guidance

          included the same list of requirements, but provided more detail, noting that care
          homes were expected to have appropriate pandemic influenza plans in place and
          a contingency plan for dealing with an influenza pandemic.132

1.66.     Some care providers either did not have, or had inadequate, business continuity
          or pandemic plans. For example, in Scotland, despite it being a condition of their
          registration with the Care Inspectorate, prior to the pandemic, not all care homes
          had business continuity plans in place.133 In England, prior to the pandemic, the Care
          Quality Commission’s guidance stated that, in order to comply with the requirement
          to provide care safely, providers must be able to manage major incidents and
          emergencies and have plans with other providers for such incidents and events.134
          However, the Care Quality Commission’s current guidance does not specifically
          require care providers to have pandemic plans; evidence suggests that this was also
          the case prior to 2020.135 Before Covid-19, the Department of Health and Social Care
          noted that there were few resilience experts in the Care Quality Commission, and so
          the degree to which it could scrutinise providers’ business continuity arrangements
          might be limited.136

1.67.     A Rapid Review for Care Homes in Relation to Covid-19 in Wales (undertaken
          from July to September 2020) identified that there should be “continuity plans”
          for individual care homes.137 Prior to publication of the review, seminars were held
          for local authorities and health boards to discuss the lessons that had been learned
          during the review and to compile “action plans” to build upon its
          recommendations.138 The recommendation that every care home should be assisted
          to have a business continuity plan was well received and reflected in the action
          plans.139 The fact that such a recommendation was necessary suggests that there
          was a lack of adequate provider-level plans prior to the pandemic, despite a
          pandemic exercise in 2014 stating that care homes were required to have “robust
          contingency plans”.140
