     Local-level plans

     1.68.     The picture of what adult social care plans should have existed above the provider
               level but below the level of the four governments of the UK, and what those plans
               should include, was also confusing. The Civil Contingencies Act 2004 placed local
               authorities in England, Scotland and Wales under a legal duty to maintain plans to
               enable an effective response to emergencies.141 However, in England, it was unclear
               whether local authorities should have their own influenza plans (which would
               consider how the services they provided, funded and oversaw would be affected
               during an influenza pandemic) or if they were only expected to feed into local
               resilience forum plans. The Department of Health and Social Care’s 2012 Health
               and Social Care Influenza Pandemic Preparedness and Response document merely
               said that social care might face challenges at the height of a pandemic, including as
               demand for hospital care increased, because discharged patients might require a
               greater level of care than normal. Therefore:

                    “as part of business continuity plans, local authorities should ensure they have
                      in place arrangements for responding to increased demand for assessments
                      and support alongside reduced capacity to deal with such circumstances”.142

               This was largely mirrored in similar guidance published in Northern Ireland in 2013
               and Wales in 2014. Both documents said that “arrangements should be in place for
               responding to increased demand”, without a reference to local authorities.143

     1.69.     In 2013, the Cabinet Office published Preparing for Pandemic Influenza: Guidance for
               Local Planners, which was aimed at “Local Resilience Fora”.144 It explained that the:

                    “development, maintenance and testing of local level multi-agency plans plays a
                     crucial role in ensuring that the objectives set out in the [UK Influenza Pandemic
                     Preparedness Strategy 2011] can be met in the event of a pandemic”.145

               This document included a very brief section on adult social care (and a very brief
               section relating to vulnerable people), with minimal practical guidance for what
               should be included in plans for adult social care.146

     1.70.     In December 2019, the Cabinet Office published the National Resilience Standards
               for local resilience forums, which, for the first time, related specifically to pandemic
               influenza. These standards stated that local resilience forums should have pandemic

1.71.    On 11 February 2020, UK government officials undertook a stocktake of pandemic
         preparedness in adult social care in England.149 Sir Christopher Wormald (later Lord
         Wormald), Permanent Secretary to the Department of Health and Social Care from
         May 2016 to December 2024, said that this arose from his “concern that there had
         been less focus in discussion on adult social care than on the NHS”.150 Mr Hancock’s
         view that the primary planning responsibility for the response to Covid-19 in adult
         social care rested with local authorities was noted.151

1.72.    It was the Department of Health and Social Care’s understanding that, further to
         a request in 2018, local authorities should have a “flu plan”.152 Ms Whately said that
         she had discovered this expectation in late February or early March 2020, when
         she asked for assurance about the preparedness of the sector. She requested
         copies of local authority pandemic response plans. On or about 3 March 2020, the
         Department of Health and Social Care provided her with two examples of influenza
         plans. These were local resilience forum plans that had been produced by two local
         authorities, jointly with local NHS teams.153 Ms Whately found these plans to be
         inadequate and raised her concerns with Mr Hancock.154

1.73.    Ms Whately agreed that a review should take place to identify any good plans
         from local authorities that could be used as templates for other local authorities.155
         Given that the plans provided to Ms Whately were influenza plans, it is reasonable
         to assume that she was seeking additional influenza plans, rather than any business
         continuity plans that may have existed. On 5 March 2020, Ms Whately was told by
         Professor Harries that there were “hugely detailed plans sitting at local levels”.156
         She was also told by Ros Roughton (Director General for Adult Social Care in the
         Department of Health and Social Care from April to July 2020) that there were
         more detailed, Covid-19-specific plans and work was underway with the Ministry
         of Housing, Communities and Local Government to review and assure those plans.157

1.74.    It is not clear if this was a reference to individual local authority plans or local
         resilience forum plans into which local authorities had fed. In any event, no further
         plans were shared with Ms Whately. As Ms Whately suggested, both on 5 March

               2020 and to the Inquiry, the most obvious conclusion was that the plans did

     1.75.     In early March 2020, Covid-19 was spreading fast and the care sector was looking
               to the Department of Health and Social Care for help and guidance. Ms Whately and
               Mr Hancock therefore agreed that the adult social care response to Covid-19 should
               be led by the UK government.159 As outlined in a report by the Nuffield Trust (an
               independent health and social care think tank) in May 2023, it was not sufficiently
               clear who, or which organisation, was responsible for different aspects of the
               response for social care in England during February to May 2020. The Nuffield Trust
               noted the importance of ensuring that clarity about “responsibilities and leadership
               for all parts of the system is established both in ‘normal’ times but particularly in
               preparation for a future crisis”.160

     1.76.     In Wales, in advance of a civil contingencies preparedness discussion on 4 March
               2020, it was noted that directors of social services were reviewing contingency plans
               and raising awareness with commissioned social care providers.161 The Inquiry has
               seen no other evidence about the content of these plans or whether any concerns
               were identified. Mr Gething relied on his understanding that plans were being
               reviewed – he explained that it would have been unreasonable for him to have
               asked to review the plans of all health boards and local authorities.162

     1.77.     In Scotland, Exercise Silver Swan in 2016 had recommended that regional resilience
               partnerships ensure that multi-agency pandemic influenza plans were produced that
               included health and social care partnerships.163 Ms Freeman said that, at the start
               of the pandemic, she did not have oversight of how many health and social care
               partnerships had been integrated into local pandemic plans. She agreed that, to
               ensure the existence and adequacy of those plans and to inform the response
               of the sector, the relevant minister should have oversight of plans.164

     1.78.     There was, therefore, general confusion at the local and provider levels in England,
               Scotland and Wales about which plans should have existed prior to the pandemic
               and what any plans should address. In England, there had been no proper
               government oversight or evaluation of plans that had been produced prior to the
               pandemic by or with local authorities. Plans were not in place that had considered
               how the response might be managed at both the UK government and local
               government levels. Due to a lack of oversight from the Scottish Government,
               it is unclear if health and social care partnerships had been fully integrated into

         pandemic plans in Scotland. A similar lack of oversight by the Welsh Government
         meant that it was unclear which, if any, plans produced by or with local authorities
         or health boards had been reviewed or assured in Wales.

Plans in Northern Ireland

1.79.    In Northern Ireland, Professor McBride said that he reviewed the Covid-19 surge
         plans of the Health and Social Care trusts (which included surge planning for adult
         social care) and concluded that the regional coordination needed to be “much
         more robust”.165 He commissioned further work to quality assure the plans in late
         March 2020.166

1.80.    A review of those plans, undertaken in July 2020, identified that there had initially
         been no formal assurance process. The review found that, while extensive work had
         been completed by the trusts, variations were observed. Gaps were identified – for
         example, arrangements for the clinical support (such as nursing support) that would
         be provided to the sector were not always clear.167 More detailed pandemic planning
         could have identified the need for formal quality assurance processes and identified
         the significance of matters such as clinical support for care homes in a pandemic.


Improving planning for the adult social care sector
1.81.    Entering the Covid-19 pandemic, it was well understood that the adult social care
         sector would be severely tested. The heightened vulnerability to respiratory illnesses
         of many people who draw on social care, the fragmentation of services across
         different sectors, and the high turnover of staff all contributed to the sector’s lack
         of resilience before Covid-19 arrived. However, pandemic planning had not given
         adequate consideration to how these risks could be mitigated given the likely impact
         that a pandemic would have on the sector. Concerns identified – for example,
         through testing exercises – were not addressed.

1.82.    As a result, it took time for the UK government and devolved administrations to
         establish adequate systems fully and routinely to collect key data about bed capacity
         in care homes and staffing capacity in the wider sector. These data should have
         been available before decisions had to be taken. Better data might have led to more
         informed decisions early in the pandemic response, such as those on expedited
         hospital discharge (see Chapter 2: Care home admissions, testing and isolation).
         This would likely also have given decision-makers a fuller understanding of the
         impact of early decisions.

1.83.    Better pandemic planning is needed. There should be clarity on exactly what plans
         are required by providers and on the plans required at the level between providers

              and the governments in each nation, including identifying who is responsible for
              producing and assuring them. Such plans are necessary to understand and address
              how different organisations and bodies will work together to mitigate the impact of
              a pandemic. The UK government’s March 2026 Pandemic Preparedness Strategy
              commits to the development of an adult social care pandemic action plan in
              England by 2027 to respond to future threats. This aims to ensure the necessary
              clarity of local and England-wide roles and responsibilities. It should also consider
              how changes to structures set out in the Health Bill may impact on responsibilities
              for pandemic planning in the care sector.168 The Inquiry considers that further work
              is required to ensure that pandemic planning is sufficiently detailed to be of
              practical utility.

     1.84.    The Inquiry recommends that the UK government and devolved administrations work
              with those responsible for the delivery of adult social care (including local authorities,
              health and social care partnerships, and Health and Social Care trusts and providers)
              to ensure that there are adequate plans in place for the sector to shape a pandemic
              response and for collecting the data needed to inform key decisions from the outset
              of a pandemic.

=== PAGE 48 ===
                                    Chapter 1: Overview of the adult social care sector




Recommendation 1: Improved pandemic planning for the adult social
care sector
The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and
Northern Ireland Executive should each establish clear arrangements for
pandemic planning in adult social care. Those arrangements should facilitate
the production of pandemic plans for adult social care at the government,
local and provider levels.
Plans at a government level should identify:

• the roles and responsibilities of each government in relation to adult social care
  during a pandemic;

• the requirements for local-level plans, including the roles and responsibilities
  of local authorities, Health and Social Care trusts, and health and social care
  partnerships in producing those plans;

• the requirements for provider-level plans;
• how and by whom the adequacy of local and provider-level plans will be
  assured;

• how bed capacity and staffing levels will be tracked in care homes and
  domiciliary care; and

• how the adult social care sector will be represented in the pandemic planning
  of the four governments.
Government pandemic plans for adult social care should be regularly reviewed
and tested as part of the pandemic response exercises recommended in the
Inquiry’s Module 1 Report (Recommendation 6).

Chapter 2: Care home

=== PAGE 50 ===
                                                   Chapter 2: Care home admissions, testing and isolation




Introduction
2.1.     As Covid-19 spread, more people became infected, including many who would go
         on to require hospital treatment. By March 2020, the UK government and devolved
         administrations considered that emergency measures were necessary to increase
         hospital capacity across the UK. One of those measures (early hospital discharge)
         had been set out in the Coronavirus: Action Plan, published on 3 March 2020
         on behalf of the four nations of the UK.1 By 12 March 2020, it was estimated that,
         before the peak of the virus and even with the implementation of measures such as
         self-isolation, demand for healthcare beds would outstrip supply by 328,000 beds in
         England, 37,000 beds in Scotland and 24,000 beds in Wales.2 A policy of expediting
         the discharge of medically fit hospital patients was introduced in each nation by
         mid-March.

2.2.     However, a lack of testing capacity at this stage of the pandemic meant that, over
         the following weeks, many patients were discharged to care homes and to their own
         homes, without knowing whether they had Covid-19. This led to widespread criticism
         and a concern that expedited discharges contributed to outbreaks of Covid-19 in
         care homes.

2.3.     This chapter examines the basis for expedited hospital discharge policies in March
         2020 and the guidance published in each of the four nations of the UK about the
         isolation of people admitted to care homes following hospital discharge. It provides
         an overview of each nation’s approach to the testing of people discharged from
         hospital on admission to care homes from mid-April 2020, and considers the impact
         of expedited discharge on care homes. It also considers the wider introduction of
         symptomatic testing (testing for those with symptoms) and asymptomatic testing
         (testing for those without symptoms) for those using and working in adult social care.


Entry of Covid-19 to care homes
2.4.     It was obvious from the outset of the pandemic that admitting a person infected with
         Covid-19 to a care home could introduce the virus to the home and lead to onward
         transmission. Residents living in close proximity to each other and with underlying
         health conditions are particularly vulnerable to infectious diseases. Once the virus
         entered a care home, it was highly likely to spread, with possibly fatal consequences.

2.5.     At an adult social care meeting on 11 February 2020 between UK government
         officials, the three likely ways the virus could enter a care home were identified as
         infected people moving into homes, staff and visitors. It was agreed that these routes

              Regulation and safeguarding.

Asymptomatic and pre-symptomatic transmission
2.7.     By 24 January 2020, SAGE had advised the UK government and devolved
         administrations that it was likely that Covid-19 could be spread by people who
         were pre-symptomatic (ie infected but not initially showing any symptoms).5

2.8.     The four governments were also aware that infected people may be asymptomatic
         (ie infected but never developing any symptoms). At its meeting on 28 January 2020,
         SAGE noted that, while there was limited evidence of asymptomatic transmission,
         early indications implied that some was occurring.6 That day, Professor (later Sir)
         Christopher Whitty (Chief Medical Officer for England from October 2019) discussed
         asymptomatic transmission with the other Chief Medical Officers of the UK, stating
         that they should “assume it may be happening”.7

2.9.     On 28 January 2020, SAGE advised that it would not be useful to test asymptomatic
         individuals, as a negative test result could not be interpreted with certainty – this
         accorded with advice from Health Protection Scotland.8 Public Health Wales advised
         that there was “no value” in testing those without symptoms.9 Both Professor Whitty
         and Professor Sir Patrick Vallance (later Lord Vallance of Balham), Government Chief
         Scientific Adviser from April 2018 to March 2023, agreed in March 2020 that while
         testing could identify some asymptomatic cases, it would miss a large number of
         cases and so was not recommended as a way of confirming definitively that a person
         was not infected.10

2.10.    Matt Hancock (Secretary of State for Health and Social Care from July 2018
         to June 2021) said that the “initial consensus” that Covid-19 could not transmit
         asymptomatically underpinned initial advice given by the Department of Health
         and Social Care on the management of Covid-19 in care homes.11 He also relied on
         scientific advice not to test those who did not have symptoms.12

2.11.    While scientific advice was not certain that asymptomatic transmission was
         occurring, as noted in the Inquiry’s combined Report relating to Modules 2, 2A, 2B
         and 2C, there were clear signs by the end of January 2020 to suggest that it was.13
         It was also known from an early stage that the “elderly and those with existing health
         conditions” were at particular risk from Covid-19.14 The Inquiry acknowledges the
         constraints on testing capacity at this stage of the pandemic (considered further
         below). However, the discharge policy involved discharging many potentially

          vulnerable patients to care homes, where they would be surrounded by other very
          vulnerable people. The most sensible way for the UK government and devolved
          administrations to proceed would have been to adopt a default assumption that not
          every infected person would necessarily show symptoms and that asymptomatic
          transmission was occurring and was a potentially significant factor in how the virus
          was spreading. As the number of confirmed cases grew across the UK in February
          and early March 2020 (which in itself was an underestimate of the true number of
          cases), there was a real risk that individuals infected with Covid-19 were entering
          care homes but presenting without any symptoms. Testing might have had some
          value, even if limited, as Professors Whitty and Vallance believed.15


 Hospital discharges
 2.12.    Timely hospital discharge has two primary objectives: to ensure that there is
          capacity in the healthcare system to admit and treat people who require acute care;
          and to remove vulnerable people from hospital settings that pose risks to their
          health and wellbeing, including a risk of hospital-acquired infections.16 Managing
          hospital capacity by discharging medically fit patients is not a new concept and
          is common in winter and influenza seasons.17 The provisions contained in the
          draft Pandemic Influenza Bill in 2019, prepared following Exercise Cygnus in 2016
          (a cross-government exercise across the UK to test a pandemic-scale response),
          acknowledged the importance that timely hospital discharge might play in a
          future pandemic.18


 The introduction of discharge policies in March 2020
 2.13.    Decisions were taken to introduce expedited hospital discharge policies during the
          first three weeks of March 2020.19

 Testing capacity in March 2020

 2.14.    Testing is a crucial element of the public health response to any pandemic.
          In January 2020, Public Health England developed an accurate diagnostic test

         for Covid-19.20 It served a number of purposes in the adult social care sector,
         including confirming infection, managing outbreaks, guiding isolation requirements,
         preventing asymptomatic spread and providing surveillance.

2.15.    However, as is being considered by the Inquiry in Module 7: Test, trace and isolate,
         it took time for testing capacity to be scaled up during the pandemic to allow for its
         deployment across the sector. In the first weeks of March 2020, testing capacity for
         Covid-19 was limited. On 11 March 2020, Professor Whitty advised COBR that there
         were an estimated 5,000 to 10,000 cases of Covid-19 in the UK.21 At that stage,
         testing capacity in England was about 3,000 tests per day.22 The devolved nations
         also had very limited testing capacity in March 2020.23

2.16.    The limited testing capacity meant that use of tests had to be prioritised. The
         Department of Health and Social Care, Public Health England and NHS England
         agreed on 11 March 2020 to prioritise tests for clinical need. This meant that testing
         was focused on case detection in symptomatic patients requiring critical care
         or hospital admission.24 Testing was similarly prioritised in Scotland, Wales and
         Northern Ireland.25 On 12 March 2020, the UK government announced that testing
         of symptomatic people in the wider community would be paused in order to prioritise
         tests for use in hospitals. Community testing was paused at a similar time in the
         devolved nations.26 Testing of care home residents across the UK was therefore
         limited to small numbers to confirm the existence of an outbreak.27

2.17.    As explained by Professor Whitty, it was obvious from mid-March 2020 that it would
         have been ideal to test patients moving from hospitals and other settings to care
         homes for Covid-19, had testing capacity and turnaround times for testing allowed
         this.28 The view of NHS England on 12 March 2020 was that the protection of care
         home residents, who were highly vulnerable, should be a priority. Their view was that
         discharging patients who presented with possible Covid-19 symptoms to care homes
         without a negative test should be avoided.29 However, in light of limited testing
         capacity, the Department of Health and Social Care considered that this was not
         viable.30 A few days later, on 17 March 2020, it was noted at an internal NHS England
         meeting that there should be further consideration of the impact on care homes,
         including “testing practices at discharge to support safe care home admissions”.31

               However, the Department of Health and Social Care and Public Health England
               considered that testing capacity remained insufficient.32


     A difficult choice
     2.18.     Decision-makers were therefore faced with an unenviable choice. They could keep
               patients in hospital pending tests becoming available and run the risk of running out
               of hospital beds for Covid-19 patients and increasing the risk to vulnerable patients
               fit for discharge. Alternatively, to free up capacity for Covid-19 patients, they could
               discharge patients who were potentially Covid-19-positive and run the risk of
               Covid-19 being introduced to vulnerable residents in care homes.

     2.19.     Choosing the latter course of action created the perception that hospital capacity
               was of greater importance to the government than residential and domiciliary care
               capacity.33 Mr Hancock explained that it “was a question of what is the least worst
               solution to this terrible problem”, and that if a better option had been available, he
               would have strongly supported it.34

     2.20. As a result, many thousands of people across the UK were discharged to care homes
           in the early months of the Covid-19 pandemic (see Table 2).

2.21.     The decision to introduce expedited hospital discharge was discussed and agreed
          by Boris Johnson (Prime Minister from July 2019 to September 2022), Mr Hancock
          and Sir Simon Stevens (later Lord Stevens of Birmingham), Chief Executive of NHS
          England from April 2014 to July 2021, and was discussed both at COBR and in the
          UK Cabinet.35 Mr Hancock explained that the policy was:

              “formally a government decision. It was signed off by the Prime Minister.
               It was really driven by Simon Stevens … but it was widely discussed, both
               in the department, with the NHS, and with the centre.”36

          While he said it was the UK government’s decision, Mr Hancock said he took
          responsibility for it and defended it at the time.37

2.22. Decisions associated with a focus on hospital discharge were also taken at a
      government level in Scotland and Wales.38 In Northern Ireland, the Department
      of Health (Northern Ireland) was at the centre of the decisions.39

2.23. Jeane Freeman (Cabinet Secretary for Health and Sport in the Scottish Government
      from June 2018 to May 2021) told the Inquiry that, had there been sufficient testing
      capacity, she would have introduced testing on admission to care homes earlier.40
      Robin Swann, Minister of Health for Northern Ireland from January 2020 to October
      2022, acknowledged that the challenge at the time of expedited hospital discharge
      was the lack of available testing.41

2.24. Albert Heaney (Deputy Director General Health and Social Services Group in the
      Welsh Government from March 2020 to June 2021) agreed that it would have been
      preferable to test all patients on discharge when guidance was published in April
      2020, but that this was not possible due to testing capacity.42 He explained that this
      was his view in hindsight and decisions at the time were based on scientific and
      medical advice and not limited capacity.43 Vaughan Gething (Minister for Health
      and Social Services in the Welsh Government from May 2016 to May 2021) said
      that, in hindsight, testing on discharge from hospital could have eliminated risk
      and maintained confidence within the residential care sector.44 However, Mr Gething
      said that, prior to publication of advice for care homes in Wales in April 2020, he
      did not receive advice that “testing of all patients upon discharge will be preferred,

              scientific advisers in the other nations, it is clear that testing capacity must also have
              influenced the advice and associated decision not to test all people discharged from
              hospital in Wales in March 2020.

     2.25. As explained by the UK’s Chief Medical Officers and the Government Chief Scientific
           Adviser in their Technical Report on the COVID-19 Pandemic in the UK, published on
           1 December 2022, discharge from hospitals to care homes “connects 2 high-contact
           environments, and it was and should remain a high priority for preventive actions in
           similar pandemics”.46

     2.26. In the absence of testing people discharged from hospital upon admission to care
           homes, with evidence of asymptomatic and pre-symptomatic transmission, limited
           availability of personal protective equipment (PPE) for adult social care settings (see
           Chapter 3: Protective measures) and no vaccine for Covid-19, it was incumbent upon
           the UK government and devolved administrations to ensure that precautions were
           in place when people were admitted to care homes from hospital. Isolation was
           probably the only truly effective precaution available at that time.


     Isolation of residents following hospital discharge
     2.27.    The isolation of care home residents – whether infectious or potentially infectious
              – has long been recognised as a routine infection prevention and control measure
              in the management of respiratory and gastrointestinal infections such as influenza
              and norovirus.47 Isolation reduces exposure to other residents and staff who are not
              providing direct care, and thus aims to reduce the risks of onward transmission.

     2.28. Guidance was issued in each of the four nations in March and early April 2020 that
           outlined the approach to be taken to people admitted to care homes. Care homes
           in each nation were advised when and for how long care home residents should be
           isolated on admission (see Table 3).

=== PAGE 60 ===
                                                   Chapter 2: Care home admissions, testing and isolation




2.29. Helen Whately MP (Minister of State for Social Care at the Department of Health
      and Social Care from February 2020 to September 2021) told the Inquiry that she
      was not involved in the development of the COVID-19 Hospital Discharge Service
      Requirements guidance issued on 19 March 2020 by the UK government and
      NHS England.48 In light of the fact that this guidance specifically referred to social
      care, she believed they should have run it past the minister with responsibility.49
      The Inquiry agrees. The Department of Health and Social Care should have ensured
      that the Minister for Social Care had greater involvement in the development of the
      guidance regarding expedited discharge from hospitals, which included discharges
      to the adult social care sector. This might have ensured a better understanding of
      the potential impact that the policy and associated guidance would have on the
      adult social care sector.


Isolation guidance following discharge from hospital
2.30. Professor Harries explained that, in her view, the inability to test hospital patients
      discharged to care homes would not reasonably have played a significant part in
      managing transmission rates in care homes in the spring of 2020 because “the
      strongest mitigation was a robust isolation period regardless of whether the patient/
      resident had tested negative or positive at the point of discharge”.50

2.31.    Yet, despite its importance as a mitigation measure, when the expedited discharge
         policies were rolled out in March 2020, isolation of all those discharged untested
         from hospital was not advised. Scotland was the first nation to advise isolation of
         all people discharged from hospital and admitted to care homes, including those
         who had not been tested (see Table 3). Following a meeting on 18 March 2020
         with Dr Donald Macaskill (Chief Executive of Scottish Care, a membership body
         representing independent social care providers in Scotland) and advice from
         Professor Graham Ellis (Deputy Chief Medical Officer for Scotland from September
         2021), Ms Freeman agreed that all new admissions to care homes should be isolated.
         This was reflected in Scottish Government guidance on 26 March 2020.51

 2.32. Isolation of people admitted from hospital was not advised until 15 April 2020 in
       England, not communicated until 22 April in Wales and not advised in Northern
       Ireland until 26 April.52

 2.33. When commenting on why isolation for all people admitted to care homes was not
       introduced sooner, Public Health England said that when the March 2020 hospital
       discharge policy was introduced, asymptomatic transmission was not considered to
       pose a sufficiently high risk to justify that kind of quarantine and that it was important
       to weigh up the benefits and harms of isolation, which came with risks for vulnerable
       people.53 While Public Health England was not involved in the drafting of the hospital
       discharge guidance, it was commissioned by the Department of Health and Social
       Care to work with NHS England to produce admissions guidance for care homes on
       23 March 2020.54 Professor Fu-Meng Khaw, National Director of Health Protection
       and Screening Services and Executive Medical Director of Public Health Wales
       from June 2021, said advice on isolation of patients following discharge evolved
       as evidence of asymptomatic transmission “became clear”.55

 2.34. In Northern Ireland, Heather Reid (Interim Director of Nursing, Midwifery and Allied
       Health Professionals at the Public Health Agency (Northern Ireland) from May
       2023 to November 2025) said that evidence on asymptomatic transmission did
       not become available until the end of the first week of April 2020.56 Ms Reid also
       explained that, generally speaking, the Public Health Agency (Northern Ireland) did
       not have the capacity, expertise or access to the most up-to-date information to
       attempt to produce technical guidance from scratch. Technical guidance from other
       nations was amended to reflect the structures and terminology used in Northern
       Ireland.57 As discussed in the Inquiry’s combined Module 2ABC Report, the capacity
       of the Public Health Agency (Northern Ireland) had been steadily eroded in the years
       leading up to the pandemic and a review published in July 2020 found that there
       was not “sufficient resource in the agency to do all of the things which need to be
       done at this time”.58

 2.35. Mr Hancock told the Inquiry that the best thing as at 17 March 2020 (the date when
       NHS England communicated the need for expedited discharge) would have been to
       treat people discharged from hospital as Covid-19-positive and isolate them. With
       hindsight, he accepted that this should have been directed by the UK government.59

=== PAGE 62 ===
                                              Chapter 2: Care home admissions, testing and isolation




        Mr Gething agreed that, in the event of limited testing capacity, isolation should be
        considered as an additional measure.60

2.36. The early introduction of isolation in Scotland is likely to have reduced the risks of
      people admitted to care homes who were potentially Covid-19-positive infecting
      others.

2.37.   By mid-March 2020, there was sufficient scientific evidence of the potential of both
        pre-symptomatic and asymptomatic transmission to justify a precautionary approach
        to the admission to care homes of residents who had been discharged from hospital.
        The entry and spread of Covid-19 posed significant risks to vulnerable residents and
        the staff who were caring for them. The Inquiry acknowledges that these risks had
        to be balanced against the risks that can result from social isolation and loneliness,
        which are established risk factors in mortality.61 It is also accepted that it is more
        difficult to isolate residents with dementia.62 However, in March 2020, the risks
        posed by isolation were outweighed by the need to prevent a highly transmissible
        virus being introduced and rapidly spreading through care homes to residents who
        were at significant risk of serious illness and death from the virus itself.

2.38. The Department of Health and Social Care, the Welsh Government, the Department
      of Health (Northern Ireland), Public Health England, Public Health Wales and the
      Public Health Agency (Northern Ireland) should have advised isolation of all people
      admitted from hospitals to care homes in March 2020. This would have mitigated the
      risks posed by pre-symptomatic and asymptomatic transmission.


The ability of care homes to isolate residents
2.39. Once isolation was advised in each nation in March 2020, some care homes
      were unable to follow the advice and implement effective isolation of residents.
      The precise numbers of care homes that could not isolate were not known. When
      making the decision to discharge vulnerable patients to care homes, this was key
      information to which the UK government and devolved administrations should have
      had access.

2.40. A number of witnesses from the UK government, devolved administrations, public
      health agencies and bodies, and the Care Quality Commission acknowledged that
      not all care homes had adequate isolation facilities and smaller homes in particular
      would struggle to implement isolation effectively.63 Across the UK, governments
      and public health agencies and bodies did not have precise information about the

 2.41.    In Northern Ireland, some weeks after expedited hospital discharges began, the
          Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority undertook an assessment of the
          ability of care homes to isolate people who had been discharged from hospital in
          Northern Ireland. The findings of 24 April 2020 recognised the challenge of isolating
          and shielding residents in care homes for people with dementia, learning disabilities
          and mental health care needs. A small number of care homes in Northern Ireland still
          used double occupancy rooms.66

 2.42. In response to surveys conducted after the pandemic, the majority of local
       authorities in England and Wales, and half of local authorities in Scotland, considered
       that care homes in their areas were only able to isolate residents returning from
       hospital to a moderate or small extent.67

 2.43. Mr Gething recommended that, in the future, there should be more investment to
       ensure the consistency of isolation facilities in care homes. This would be relevant
       during influenza seasons and outbreaks of other infectious conditions, even in non-
       pandemic times.68 Mr Hancock agreed that every care home should have isolation
       facilities, and that this should be a requirement for the provision of care.69

 2.44. The lack of data about the number of care homes that could safely isolate residents
       echoes the Inquiry’s finding in Chapter 1: Overview of the adult social care sector
       that the UK government and devolved administrations had limited oversight of,
       or knowledge about, the adult social care sector. For care homes that could not
       facilitate isolation, it was incumbent upon governments to have alternative plans.
       During the Covid-19 pandemic, these plans had to be developed mid-crisis.


 Use of other facilities to isolate patients on discharge
 2.45. Where isolation in a person’s intended care home is not possible, other facilities –
       such as step-down facilities (which provide intermediate care after acute hospital
       treatment and before returning home or to a usual care home setting) – offer an
       alternative way to isolate people being discharged from hospital.

 2.46. Professor Harries outlined a number of risks associated with discharging patients
       to alternative settings for isolation. This included the potential to build a higher-risk

         environment, unless there is “absolutely perfect” infection prevention and control.70
         For older and frail people, mortality statistics rise each time they are moved.
         Professor Harries noted that multiple periods of isolation in different settings could
         also lead to deconditioning (a decline in physical and mental functioning). However,
         she reflected that not all of these risks are overwhelming and that there are balanced
         arguments and risks that all need to be considered.71 The Inquiry considers that,
         in a pandemic with a highly infectious virus that poses significant risks to care
         home residents, prior to the development of a vaccine, the benefits of isolating
         symptomatic or positive residents in settings away from other residents for a
         relatively short period are, generally speaking, likely to outweigh the risks.

2.47.    By 12 March 2020, NHS England was considering the possibility of transferring
         people suspected of having Covid-19 into alternative facilities for isolation. However,
         following discussion with the Department of Health and Social Care and care sector
         representatives, this was not considered to be feasible because adequate facilities,
         with qualified staff, did not exist.72

2.48. On 13 April 2020, Ms Whately raised concerns with Mr Hancock that care homes
      should not be forced to accept admissions if there was a risk of Covid-19 infection.
      Some local authorities were already using other venues such as hotels for people
      who could not be transferred directly to their care home.73 From 15 April 2020,
      guidance for care homes in England stated that people who were symptomatic and
      had tested positive for Covid-19 would complete isolation in alternative, appropriate
      accommodation secured by local authorities if local care homes could not safely
      support isolation. The UK government had already announced on 11 March 2020 that
      £1.3 billion would be made available to support enhanced hospital discharges and
      guidance confirmed that this could be drawn upon to support alternative isolation
      arrangements.74 This policy remained in place for a number of months, but it took
      time for local authorities to establish a substantial estate of external facilities for
      isolation.75

2.49. In Wales, as outlined to the care sector on 22 April 2020 and formalised in guidance
      on 29 April 2020, people discharged from hospital who had tested positive for
      Covid-19, were still symptomatic or had not completed isolation would be transferred
      to step-down care in local settings identified by health boards and local authorities.
      They would be tested again to ensure a negative result before admission to a care
      home.76 From 25 April 2020, in Northern Ireland, the relevant trust was asked to

 2.50. The Scottish Government did not introduce a policy requiring the use of step-down
       facilities for isolation. Ms Freeman explained that this was because of difficulties with
       the availability of facilities, the impact that moving a person more than once could
       have and staffing concerns.78 There were arrangements in some parts of Scotland
       to accommodate Covid-19-positive residents during their isolation period in settings
       other than their usual care home.79 Such facilities were therefore of utility in some
       areas in Scotland.

 2.51.    Following discussions with Mr Johnson in September 2020, work commenced on
          the development of a “designated settings” policy in England.80 The first settings
          became available for use in November 2020 and guidance on discharges to
          designated settings was published on 16 December 2020.81 The guidance outlined
          that everyone who tested positive on discharge should first be transferred to
          a designated setting for isolation before then moving to a residential setting.82
          Ms Whately said this was an evolution of existing guidance issued in April 2020.83
          Designated settings were identified by local authorities and had to be approved for
          use by the Care Quality Commission. Designated settings were required to have the
          policies, procedures, equipment, staffing and training in place to maintain infection
          control and support the care needs of residents.84

 2.52. From 15 December 2020 in Wales, informed by scientific advice, the decision was
       taken that people with a “low positive” result (which indicated that they were “no
       longer infectious”) could be discharged back to their home or social care setting
       – provided 14 days had elapsed since the last positive test or onset of symptoms,
       they had been free from fever for three days and their other symptoms had shown
       improvement.85

=== PAGE 66 ===
                                                    Chapter 2: Care home admissions, testing and isolation




2.53. Across the UK, there were no contingency plans in place in March 2020 for care
      homes that could not safely accommodate isolation of residents. In those critical
      weeks in March and April 2020, when care homes were asked to accept people
      admitted from hospital, including those who had tested positive or may have
      been exposed to Covid-19, step-down facilities and designated settings were not
      introduced as an option for care homes that could not safely implement isolation
      policies.

2.54. The report produced in 2017 following Exercise Cygnus had recommended that
      there should be an examination (it was not specified by whom) of the “possibility of
      expanding social care real-estate and staffing capacity in the event of a worst case
      scenario pandemic”.86 This included the possibility of using other establishments to
      expand bed capacity in adult social care. Work on that recommendation remained
      incomplete at the outset of the pandemic.87 Had it been completed, this might have
      allowed for the rapid identification of additional capacity for alternative isolation
      settings in March 2020 and helped ensure the safe isolation of all people admitted
      to care homes – either in care homes that could accommodate them or in alternative
      settings.


Isolation facilities in a future pandemic
2.55. The Department of Health and Social Care viewed the designated settings policy
      as a success and one which would be considered in a future pandemic, subject
      to clinical advice.88 A number of witnesses – including Ms Whately, Mr Gething,
      Dr Maria Rossi (Interim Head of Division for Environmental and Emergency Response
      in the Clinical and Protecting Health Directorate at Public Health Scotland from
      March 2020) and Mr Heaney – also agreed that the use of alternative settings for
      isolation, such as step-down care, should be considered in pandemic planning.89

2.56. The Inquiry notes the UK government’s commitment in its March 2026 Pandemic
      Preparedness Strategy to developing an actionable plan for England by March 2027,
      to ensure safe and timely hospital discharges in a pandemic in order to minimise
      the risk of onward infection. This will include developing plans to expand capacity
      to discharge patients safely from hospital, including consideration of the use of
      designated facilities and step-down care.90

2.57.    Across the UK, there is a need to identify settings for isolation. These are
         likely to become particularly important where infection rates and high levels

 Module 6: Care sector




        of transmissibility may overwhelm the ability of some care homes to isolate residents
        safely. It may not be possible to have standing capacity for such settings in non-
        pandemic times. The Inquiry therefore recommends that pandemic planning should:

        • include provision for how and by whom settings for isolation will be identified
          and communicated to hospitals and the adult social care sector in each nation;
        • consider how those settings could be used quickly if necessary in a future
          pandemic; and
        • outline what steps will be taken to ensure the safe operation of those settings,
          to minimise the risk of residents and staff being infected and to ensure that
          standards for the quality and safety of care are maintained.

 2.58. Planning for the delivery of settings for isolation requires the close involvement
       of those who are responsible for the delivery of adult social care in each nation:
       local authorities in England and Wales, health and social care partnerships in
       Scotland and Health and Social Care trusts in Northern Ireland. It should also
       involve representatives of care home providers, who can contribute their wealth
       of knowledge about how isolation operates in practice. There also needs to be an
       understanding of which care homes can safely accommodate effective isolation
       policies, to inform how many settings to facilitate isolation may be required and at
       what point they may be required. The UK government and devolved administrations
       should therefore have access to information about which care homes have adequate
       isolation facilities to accommodate isolating residents. This information should also
       be accessible to healthcare providers, care home managers, local authorities, health
       and social care partnerships and Health and Social Care trusts, and should be used
       to support the discharge process.

 2.59. Planning should consider how settings for isolation (which may include designated
       settings and step-down facilities) can operate as safely as possible. There must be
       sufficient staff to ensure their safe operation, adequate supplies of PPE and rigorous
       infection prevention and control measures (see Chapter 3: Protective measures).
       Discharges should be informed by clinical assessments to weigh up the risks posed
       to people.

 2.60. The Inquiry considers that development of the UK government’s commitment to an
       action plan by March 2027 will partly meet Recommendation 2 below. However, the
       UK government should set out how it intends to collect data on the availability of
       isolation facilities in care homes to inform its planning, and ensure that guidance is
       produced to ensure that isolation settings operate safely.

=== PAGE 68 ===
                                       Chapter 2: Care home admissions, testing and isolation




        Recommendation 2: Enable the safe and timely isolation of care
        home residents, including following discharge from hospital
        The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
        Ireland Executive should ensure that pandemic plans for adult social care contain
        practical steps rapidly to scale up isolation facilities for care home residents in a
        future pandemic.
        As a minimum, plans should:

        • establish mechanisms to record systematically the availability of isolation
          facilities in care homes in each nation and keep these up to date;

        • identify settings that can be used in a future pandemic to isolate and meet the
          needs of residents, including those who need to be discharged from hospital
          into a care home; and

        • establish guidance for how these settings can be run to ensure high-quality,
          safe care and effective infection prevention and control.



Pressure to accept admissions and potential
false assurances
2.61.   Across the UK, there were concerns that care providers felt pressure to accept new
        admissions, including of people with confirmed or suspected Covid-19. On 16 March
        2020, Ros Roughton (Director General for Adult Social Care in the Department
        of Health and Social Care from April to July 2020) asked Professor Harries what
        the approach should be to discharging symptomatic patients to care homes.
        Ms Roughton said she assumed that discharges would have to be allowed with strict
        infection control, but this was a significant ethical issue for care home providers.91
        In response, Professor Harries stated:

           “[ While] the prospect is perhaps what none of us would wish to plan for I believe
             the reality will be that we will need to discharge Covid-19 positive patients into
             residential care settings.”92

2.62. A draft version of the 2 April 2020 admissions guidance in England (discussed
      below) included advice from Public Health England that people who had tested
      positive, but had not yet completed seven days of isolation, should not be transferred
      to care homes that had no cases of Covid-19. The same was said for patients who
      had been exposed to Covid-19 but had not yet completed 14 days of isolation,
      although they could be accepted into a care home if it could implement “strict


91 INQ000325243_0001-0002
92 INQ000325243_0001


                                                                                                  55

=== PAGE 69 ===
     Module 6: Care sector




              isolation”.93 However, NHS England raised concerns regarding the draft guidance,
              including that it would “frighten the care home sector and create blocks in the
              system”.94 It was noted that a UK government priority was to free up hospital beds.
              The draft guidance was therefore initially amended to say that people with confirmed
              Covid-19 who had not yet completed seven days of isolation should “return to their
              care home with strict isolation procedures”.95

     2.63. On 28 March 2020, having received the updated draft guidance, Ms Whately
           queried whether Covid-19-positive or symptomatic patients should be discharged to
           care homes that did not have cases of Covid-19, expressing concerns that patients
           would take Covid-19 into care homes. The Department of Health and Social Care
           responded, stating that care homes may need to accept such cases as a result
           of capacity concerns but it would expect them to undertake a risk assessment to
           ensure the availability of appropriate isolation facilities.96 However, Ms Whately
           said that, in practice, this did not appear to happen.97 The final published guidance
           did not advise that Covid-19-positive patients or those who had been exposed to
           Covid-19 had to complete isolation before admission to care homes that had no
           cases of Covid-19.98

     2.64. On 22 March 2020, in response to concerns that a care home in Wales was
           being asked to accept patients from a hospital with Covid-19 patients, Mr Heaney
           confirmed that discharges needed to continue, that there was “no need for
           unnecessary testing” and that “when discharging people who have a positive
           coronavirus diagnosis then personal protective equipment should be used”.99
           This advice was clearly predicated on the care home having sufficient PPE, which,
           as examined in Chapter 3: Protective measures, was not always the case.

     2.65. Dr Macaskill said that care homes in Scotland felt undeniable pressure to help NHS
           Scotland create capacity and referred to the “constant barrage in the popular media
           … about how important it was that the social care sector came up to the plate”.100
           He also referred to the pressure resulting from the National Care Home Contract in
           Scotland (which covered 70% of those in care homes in Scotland). The contract said
           that a registered care home “should not ordinarily refuse admission unless there
           were very clear clinical reasons not to do so”.101

     2.66. The Inquiry’s listening exercise, Every Story Matters, was told by one care home
           manager in England that they had to accept patients with Covid-19.

               “I used to get a phone call in the middle of the night to say, ‘There’s an

2.67.    Particular phrases in the guidance for, and correspondence with, the adult social
         care sector in early 2020 were plainly intended to actively encourage care homes to
         accept admissions to ease pressure on hospital settings. The Scottish Government
         clinical guidance issued on 26 March 2020 said it was “imperative” for care homes
         to continue to take admissions where clinically safe to do so.103 The 2 April 2020 care
         homes admissions guidance issued by the Department of Health and Social Care,
         with input from Public Health England and the Care Quality Commission, stated that
         care homes “need … to continue to make their full capacity available to support the
         national effort”, which would “help to save thousands of lives”.104 In Wales, guidance
         issued by Public Health Wales on 8 April 2020 stated that the care sector played “a
         vital role” in accepting patients discharged from hospital.105

2.68. In Northern Ireland, a letter from Richard Pengelly (Permanent Secretary to the
      Department of Health (Northern Ireland) from July 2014 to April 2022) to arm’s length
      bodies on 26 March 2020 said “it will be more important than ever” for trusts to
      have effective discharge arrangements in place.106 While this was not sent directly to
      providers, the language in this letter may have contributed to pressure being placed
      on care homes to accept admissions. During its inquiry into the impact of Covid-19
      in care homes, the Committee for Health in Northern Ireland heard of a “palpable
      sense of pressure to admit patients who may have been COVID-positive”.107

                    “[E]verywhere was so under pressure and we were being told, ‘Well, they

     2.69. Professor Harries said that, in her experience, the UK government never intended
           to put pressure on care homes to accept people discharged from hospital.109
           Ms Freeman thought that care homes understood that they could refuse new
           admissions, and there were instances where they had done so.110 Professor
           Khaw said that care homes were not encouraged to accept Covid-19-positive
           patients.111 Mr Swann felt that the guidance was clear that care homes could say
           no to admissions and he would have wanted to ensure that care homes did not
           feel pressured to accept admissions.112 The Care Quality Commission said it was
           “clear with providers” that admissions could be refused if certain criteria could
           not be met and that it supported care homes in those circumstances.113

     2.70. However, Ms Whately had queried the use of the word “need” in the 2 April 2020
           admissions guidance, which she said suggested a lack of clarity about how directive
           the process was.114 The discharge policy concerned her because care homes were
           put in a difficult position in which they felt that they were required to admit people
           discharged from hospital despite being very worried.115 Ms Whately accepted that
           the guidance did not clearly express that care homes did not have to take new
           admissions. She explained that she would make changes on draft guidance but
           that these would disappear. She tried to mitigate this by writing to local authorities
           to emphasise that discharged patients did not have to be accepted.116

     2.71.    In June 2020, care home admissions guidance published by the UK government said
              that no care home would be “forced” to take admissions if they could not cope with
              the impact of a resident’s Covid-19 illness for the duration of isolation.117 The addition
              of this text suggests that the UK government acknowledged the pressure felt by
              providers.

=== PAGE 72 ===
                                                    Chapter 2: Care home admissions, testing and isolation




Potential false assurances in England and Wales
2.72. The guidance issued to care providers in early April 2020 in England and Wales also
      did not properly reflect the risks posed by asymptomatic transmission. The 2 April
      2020 guidance issued by the Department of Health and Social Care (with input
      from Public Health England and the Care Quality Commission) and the 8 April 2020
      Public Health Wales guidance both said that some care home admissions “may have
      Covid-19, whether symptomatic or asymptomatic. All of these patients can be safely
      cared for in a care home if this guidance is followed.”118 Looking back, Professor
      Susan Hopkins (Deputy Director of the National Infection Service at Public Health
      England from 2018 to 2020) agreed that the wording in this guidance was potentially
      too reassuring to the sector.119 Care Inspectorate Wales expressed concerns about
      this wording and ultimately did not agree to its logo appearing on the guidance.120

2.73. The four governments of the UK plainly intended to encourage admissions of people
      discharged from hospital who had not been tested. In England and Wales, guidance
      went further and had the potential falsely to reassure care homes that people who
      were admitted and were Covid-19-positive, including those who might have been
      asymptomatic (and therefore might not have been tested), could be accepted
      because they could be safely cared for. Yet, in England, Wales and Northern Ireland,
      isolation of all new admissions had not been advised and, in any event, across the
      UK it was known that not all care homes would be able safely to isolate people
      admitted to care homes and residents. Care homes were encouraged to accept
      untested or Covid-19-positive people (and sometimes both), which led to risks of
      transmission that some care homes did not have the ability to mitigate sufficiently.

2.74.     The focus on capacity in healthcare services and the phrases used in respect of
          the care sector also likely contributed to the belief that the adult social care sector
          was the undervalued “Cinderella” service within healthcare systems (see Chapter 8:
          Recognition and protection).121

=== PAGE 73 ===
     Module 6: Care sector




     Further evidence of asymptomatic transmission
     2.75. In March and April 2020, two significant studies evidenced the potential significance
           of pre-symptomatic and asymptomatic transmission of Covid-19 in care homes.
           On 27 March 2020, the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention published
           an early release of a study conducted in care homes in Washington State, USA. It
           suggested that residents who were asymptomatic or pre-symptomatic with Covid-19
           had the potential to be substantially contagious to others.122 On 3 April 2020, it
           published a statement citing recent studies, which said:

                   “[A] significant portion of individuals with coronavirus lack symptoms
                     (‘asymptomatic’) and … even those who eventually develop symptoms
                     (‘pre-symptomatic’) can transmit the virus to others before showing symptoms.”123

     2.76. In April 2020, Public Health England undertook a study of six care homes in London
           which reported an outbreak of Covid-19 between 10 and 13 April. Phase 1 of the
           study (all phases being known collectively as the ‘Easter 6’ study) involved swabbing
           of residents and staff over the Easter weekend (the study was not commenced
           earlier as a result of limited testing capacity).124 Professor Hopkins explained
           that initial results first became available on 15 April 2020. She explained that the
           collective study provided the strongest evidence yet to show that it was likely the
           virus was being transmitted asymptomatically.125 Final results of Phase 1 of the study
           showed that, across the six care homes: 39.9% of residents were Covid-19-positive
           and only 27.1% of those were symptomatic at the time of testing; and 20.4% of staff
           were Covid-19-positive but 56.9% remained asymptomatic at the time of testing.126
           Professor Hopkins said that the collective study:

                   “underpinned the need for reprioritisation of testing to enable testing of both
                     symptomatic and asymptomatic residents and staff where COVID-19 outbreaks
                     were suspected, and the introduction of asymptomatic testing for ASC [adult
                     social care] staff and visitors”.127

     (2020), 69(13), 377–381, p378 (https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/69/wr/mm6913e1.htm?s_cid=mm6913e1_w; INQ000224063)

Introduction of further testing in adult social
care settings
2.77.     Throughout March and early April 2020, the number of confirmed and suspected
          Covid-19 patients in hospital increased substantially.128 The discharge of untested
          hospital patients to care homes therefore posed an increasing risk.


Testing of patients discharged from hospitals to care homes
2.78. By mid-April 2020, testing capacity had increased in each nation – to 38,766 tests
      per day by 15 April in England, 1,556 per day by 16 April in Northern Ireland, 1,300
      per day by 16 April in Wales, and 2,988 per day by 20 April in Scotland.129 As testing
      capacity increased, each government updated its discharge policies to introduce
      testing of all patients admitted to care homes from hospital.

2.79. Mr Hancock said that, following the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention’s
      statement on 3 April 2020 that asymptomatic transmission was likely to be occurring,
      he immediately contacted Professor Whitty and asked him to update his advice in
      light of the new information.130 Following a conversation with Professor Whitty on
      13 April 2020, Professor Vallance commissioned a rapid review of the effectiveness
      of testing people who were asymptomatic. The review concluded that asymptomatic
      testing was an important measure for controlling the virus and was being used
      elsewhere in the world. The following morning, Professor Vallance asked Professor
      Whitty to share this paper with Mr Hancock.131 On 14 April 2020, Professor Whitty
      recommended testing all people admitted to care homes; however, he explained
      that putting this recommendation into practice was dependent upon testing capacity,
      which was unrealistic in early April.132 On 15 April 2020, the Department of Health
      and Social Care published COVID-19: Our Action Plan for Adult Social Care, which
      confirmed a move to testing for all those discharged from hospitals to care homes.
      Isolation of those with a negative test result for 14 days was recommended.133
      Mr Hancock explained that, by 15 April 2020, testing capacity had been increased
      to allow introduction of the policy.134

2.80. On 15 April 2020, in Wales, ministers acknowledged that Care Forum Wales and Care
      Inspectorate Wales were concerned about discharges to care homes and testing on
      discharge. It was noted that there was planning in England to address these issues

Testing of admissions from the community
2.84. The updated discharge testing policies in each of the four nations also addressed
      the testing of residents from the community upon admission. On 12 April 2020,
      Ms Whately asked that COVID-19: Our Action Plan for Adult Social Care specifically
      address people admitted from the community who might have Covid-19, including
      the use of testing to confirm this.151 During internal discussions, officials in the
      Department of Health and Social Care agreed that people moving from their own
      home to care homes were at much lower risk of Covid-19 because they would
      have been isolating for the last three weeks. The UK government’s intention was
      to introduce testing of people admitted from the community when testing capacity
      allowed.152 The Action Plan did not introduce such testing, but indicated that it would
      follow at a later date. The Action Plan did note that providers “may wish” to isolate
      such admissions for 14 days.153 On 19 June 2020, updated admissions guidance
      published by the UK government said that new admissions from the community
      “should” be isolated.154 Testing people on admission from the community who were
      asymptomatic was not introduced in England until 14 August 2020.155

2.85. In contrast, testing of all people admitted from the community was introduced
      earlier, in late April 2020, by the devolved administrations.156 This should be given

     Wider testing of care home residents and social care staff

     Symptomatic testing

     2.86. In March and early April 2020, while there was some testing of symptomatic care
           staff across the UK, testing capacity was insufficient to test all symptomatic staff.157
           From mid-April, once testing capacity allowed, testing of symptomatic residents
           and staff working in adult care homes was expanded across the UK.158

     Asymptomatic testing

     2.87.     The Easter 6 study provided clear evidence of the benefits of asymptomatic testing
               in care homes. Testing could identify asymptomatic or pre-symptomatic infections of
               both care home residents and staff (who regularly move between settings) and lead
               to the implementation of infection prevention and control measures to control further
               transmission.

     2.88. On 28 April 2020, Public Health England produced a testing options paper which
           explained that the care sector was experiencing large numbers of cases and that
           the role of asymptomatic infections may be underestimated.159 This was shared
           with officials in Wales and Northern Ireland.160 That same day, the UK government
           announced that testing would be expanded to asymptomatic staff and residents in
           care homes.161 Testing whole care homes began in England in May 2020. On 11 May
           2020, an online portal was introduced for care homes to request testing (initially
           prioritised for care homes for people aged over 65 or with dementia).162 That day,
           the UK government published Our Plan to Rebuild: The UK Government’s COVID-19
           Recovery Strategy, which promised that every care home for people aged over 65

          would be offered testing for residents and staff by 6 June 2020.163 By 8 June 2020,
          test kits had been distributed to all care homes in England.164

2.89. In Wales, on 30 April 2020, draft ministerial advice for Mr Gething stated that
      Public Health Wales considered that serial testing (ie repeated testing of the same
      person over a period of time) of asymptomatic care workers would help prevent
      the introduction of Covid-19 into care homes. The draft advice therefore included
      an option to introduce serial testing of asymptomatic care workers in care homes
      free of Covid-19.165

2.90. The final version of the ministerial advice sent to Mr Gething and Mark Drakeford,
      First Minister of Wales from December 2018 to March 2024, on 1 May 2020 did not
      include the option for serial asymptomatic testing of care workers. However, it did
      make clear that Public Health Wales considered that testing of asymptomatic staff
      in care homes could help prevent the introduction of Covid-19 into care homes.166
      Both the draft and the final advice noted that testing all care home residents alone
      would require 25,000 extra tests and that further modelling for policy decisions
      would be required. Both also stated: “Expanding into asymptomatic individuals still
      lacks the evidence base to support this being the best use of testing capacity.”167

2.91.     The final advice recommended that the Welsh Government test all residents (both
          symptomatic and asymptomatic) in care homes with more than 50 beds and in those
          where an outbreak occurred.168 This recommendation was followed and announced
          the next day, and included staff.169 The advice reflected Dr Atherton’s understanding
          of how the UK government intended to prioritise testing in care homes.170

2.92. On 12 May 2020, SAGE concluded (with medium confidence, meaning that there
      was a reasonable degree of certainty with evidence to support the conclusion but
      some important gaps) that there was a strong scientific rationale to test all residents
      and staff working in care homes (including those without cases of Covid-19), given
      the strong evidence of asymptomatic transmission in those settings.171 On 13 May
      2020, testing in Wales was extended to care homes with an ongoing outbreak

 The Vivaldi 1 study
 2.93. Professor Hopkins approached Professor Laura Shallcross (Professor of Public Health
       and Translational Data Science at University College London from 2021) in early May
       2020 about the need for a study to provide information on the burden of Covid-19
       infections on care homes in England, to inform policy decisions. The Vivaldi 1 study
       ran from 26 May to 19 June 2020 and surveyed 5,126 care homes for those aged
       over 65 in England, and the survey responses were then linked to Covid-19 test
       results.174 On 3 July 2020, initial results identified that 80.9% of residents who tested
       positive for Covid-19 were asymptomatic.175

 2.94. On 3 July 2020, the UK government announced that routine testing would begin
       in care homes in England for those aged over 65 or with dementia.176 However, on
       31 July 2020, it was confirmed that the rollout would be delayed.177 Professor Vic
       Rayner (Chief Executive Officer of the National Care Forum from 2016) said that,
       in practice, it took until October 2020 for routine asymptomatic testing to be rolled
       out to all care homes for people under 65 in England.178

 2.95. On 28 July 2020, informed by the findings of the Vivaldi 1 study, Mr Swann
       announced the introduction of a regular programme of testing in care homes in
       Northern Ireland to begin from 3 August 2020.179 Routine asymptomatic testing of
       staff in care homes was further increased in Northern Ireland in November 2020.180

Domiciliary care
2.96. Regular asymptomatic testing of people working in domiciliary care was also
      introduced in each nation of the UK, but later than for people working in care homes.

2.97.    Testing capacity in May 2020 was considered to be insufficient to roll out regular,
         asymptomatic testing for domiciliary care workers in England.181 A study conducted
         by Public Health England in July 2020 found that the estimated prevalence of
         Covid-19 in domiciliary care workers was 0.1%. However, Public Health England
         acknowledged limitations to the study and said the results should be used with
         caution. Regular testing of all domiciliary care workers was not recommended
         at this time.182 On 9 November 2020, advice to Ms Whately noted rising rates of
         community transmission and therefore recommended a change in approach to
         testing in domiciliary care.183 Regular PCR testing of domiciliary care workers began
         on 23 November 2020. It was anticipated that lateral flow tests (a portable test with
         quick results) would create greater capacity in the near future.184 Dr Jane Townson
         (Chief Executive of the Homecare Association from May 2019) said that the delay in
         adopting regular asymptomatic testing left providers unable to identify and isolate
         cases early, which created significant risks for staff and users of care.185

2.98. On 9 July 2020, a paper that supported routine asymptomatic testing of domiciliary
      care workers was submitted to the Welsh Government’s Testing Cell. However, the
      Testing Cell did not consider the paper at that time. Mr Heaney said his officials and
      care provider representatives continued to “press” for the inclusion of domiciliary
      care workers in regular testing.186 He believed that it took much longer than it
      should have done to establish these workers as a priority group for testing.187
      On 4 December 2020, Mr Gething announced that regular lateral flow testing of
      health and social care workers, including asymptomatic domiciliary care workers,
      would commence on an incremental basis from 14 December 2020.188

2.99. As a result of increased testing capacity, on 25 November 2020, the Scottish
      Government confirmed that regular testing of the domiciliary care workforce would
      begin on 18 January 2021.189 The Scottish Government cited logistical issues prior to
      the commencement of this testing, including confirmation that people would be able
      to self-test using lateral flow tests.190 In the winter of 2020, when Covid-19 prevalence

         was high and when testing capacity allowed, the UK government, Scottish
         Government and Welsh Government introduced regular testing of domiciliary
         care workers, which included the use of lateral flow tests. In the event of a future
         pandemic that is known to pose risks to the adult social care sector, domiciliary
