 2.100. In November 2020, in Northern Ireland, it was concluded that it would not be feasible
        to provide the same level of testing to domiciliary care workers as to those in care
        homes and that wider testing of domiciliary care workers would need to await testing
        developments, including the availability of lateral flow tests.191 Regular asymptomatic
        testing for domiciliary care workers directly employed by Health and Social Care
        trusts was not rolled out until March 2021 and it was not confirmed until 17 August
        2021 that lateral flow testing was available specifically to asymptomatic workers
        employed by independent domiciliary care providers.192 Professor Sir Michael
        McBride (Chief Medical Officer for Northern Ireland from September 2006) said that,
        in April 2021, lateral flow tests were made available to the general public and access
        for domiciliary care workers in the independent sector was formally communicated
        in August 2021. He also cited logistical difficulties in identifying and registering
        domiciliary care workers for testing. He said that, if it had been possible to introduce
        widespread testing of domiciliary care workers earlier, the Department of Health
        (Northern Ireland) would have done so.193 However, these difficulties could have
        been foreseen and plans put in place to ensure that all domiciliary care workers
        were included in regular asymptomatic testing as soon as capacity allowed.

 2.101. It took time to roll out symptomatic and asymptomatic testing for residents and staff
        in adult social care in each of the four nations. Regular testing in the adult social care
        sector may prove crucial in a future pandemic and should be considered as soon as
        capacity allows. While testing was limited, other protective measures were essential
        (see Chapter 3: Protective measures).


 Impact of expedited discharge without testing on
 transmission in care homes
 2.102. A number of studies were undertaken across the UK which tried to assess whether –
        and, if so, to what extent – the admission of hospital patients to care homes resulted
        in rising infection rates in care home settings.

 2.103. Preliminary analysis from the Vivaldi 1 study indicated that new admissions to care
        homes and the number of residents discharged back to care homes from hospital –
        and the subset of these who tested positive for Covid-19 – might be associated with

         reliably answered for the following reasons:

         • Questions about hospital discharge were “poorly completed” and there was a risk
             of “recall bias” (people not accurately reporting details due to issues with memory

2.104. Professor Shallcross explained that the Vivaldi 1 study was not designed to address
       comprehensively the relative importance of each route of transmission of Covid-19
       into care homes, nor was it possible to create a study that could do so at that time.
       Therefore, Vivaldi 1 could not provide evidence on this.195

2.105. On 6 November 2020, a report was produced for the Department of Health
       (Northern Ireland) by Dr Niall Herity (Consultant Cardiologist at the Belfast Health
       and Social Care Trust). It concluded that outbreaks of Covid-19 in care homes
       corresponded more closely with hospital admission rates for Covid-19 (which
       provided an indication of transmission and infection rates in the wider community)
       than with the number of people who were discharged from hospitals to care
       homes.196 Professor McBride said the report was “a descriptive piece”, subject to
       limitations including the limited numbers of tests undertaken early in the pandemic.197
       He considered it highly likely that some care home outbreaks were caused, or
       exacerbated by, discharges from hospital. He thought it “unwise to attribute undue
       significance to one mode of entry into a care home without the evidence base
       for it”.198

2.106. On 28 October 2020, a report commissioned by Ms Freeman and produced
       by Public Health Scotland (which had succeeded Health Protection Scotland in
       April 2020) concluded that, when considered on its own, hospital discharge was
       associated with an increased risk of an outbreak. However, it said: “[A]fter accounting

     2.107. Following a letter received from Ed Humpherson, Director General for Regulation at
            the Office for Statistics Regulation from January 2014, a revised version of the report
            was republished on 21 April 2021. This clarified that a “small effect” from hospital
            discharges on care home outbreaks could not be ruled out.200 Both versions of the
            report acknowledged that testing in care homes in March and April 2020 was limited
            and, prior to testing all symptomatic residents, the “numbers of positive tests will be
            less than the true number of cases”.201 Even when all symptomatic residents were
            tested, the number of positive tests was still “likely to be less than the true number
            of cases”.202 The republished report also explained that lack of sequencing data
            available in Scotland for the first three months of the pandemic limited the ability to
            determine if an admission from hospital could have been responsible for starting an
            outbreak or introducing further lines of infection in a care home.203

            the SAGE Social Care Working Group, Public Health England published findings that
            suggested:

                   “[T]he impact of suspected hospital associated seeding on care home outbreaks
                     appears to be relatively small, potentially contributing to 1.6% of all care home
                     outbreaks.”204

               Professor Hopkins acknowledged that, as a result of limited testing in March and
               April 2020, “clearly this was an underestimate, but we did not know and we cannot
               know in retrospect what the real estimate was”.205 The findings also suggested that
               the mitigation measures introduced on 15 April 2020 (testing on discharge and
               isolating those awaiting results) “may have supported the decline seen in these
               types of outbreaks, contributing to an overall reduction in care home cases”.206

2.109. Public Health Wales produced two papers relevant to hospital discharge. The
       findings of one, published in February 2021, concluded that large care homes were
       at considerably greater risk of outbreaks and, after adjustments were made for care
       home size, “a discharge from hospital was not associated with a significant increase
       in risk”.207 However, it was not possible to determine if the introduction of Covid-19
       that led to an outbreak was from a discharged hospital patient.208 The findings of
       another paper, published in March 2022, suggested that the highest risk of the
       introduction of Covid-19 to care homes came from interaction with the community.
       However, the paper acknowledged that factors, including limited testing and rising
       community transmission, meant that it was not possible to conclusively estimate the
       effect of rapid hospital discharge on transmission.209

2.110. On 26 May 2022, SAGE published its Consensus Statement on the Association
       Between the Discharge of Patients from Hospitals and COVID in Care Homes.
       It concluded that hospital discharges without testing was:

               “highly likely to have caused some outbreaks or been one of the often multiple
                 introductions of infection to care homes which experienced an outbreak.
                 However, it is highly unlikely to have been the dominant driver of all care
                 home outbreaks in wave 1.”210

2.111.    SAGE acknowledged that there were important data limitations, including low and
          variable levels of testing during the first wave of the pandemic, and that no nation in
          the UK could easily and completely identify who was resident in care homes or who
          was discharged from hospital to care homes.211 It did, however, recommend:

               “In any future epidemic with high consequence in older and vulnerable people,
                greater consideration should be given to minimising potential exposure in care
                homes. This includes consideration of how to safely transfer existing or new
                residents to care homes from hospitals or from the community.”212

=== PAGE 85 ===
     Module 6: Care sector




     2.112. The limited testing of people discharged from hospital to care homes in the early
            months of the pandemic meant that the impact of untested hospital discharges
            on the introduction and spread of Covid-19 in care homes could not be fully and
            reliably assessed in studies undertaken in each nation. Without such data, no
            proper analysis of the likely impact could take place. However, while the impact
            cannot be confidently quantified, untested patients who were infected with Covid-19
            and discharged to care homes are highly likely to have introduced infections or
            contributed to further transmission of the virus in those settings. Therefore, additional
            precautions were required when the expedited discharge policies were introduced.


     Protection of people using adult social care
     2.113. The expedited discharge policies were fraught with risk. People were discharged into
            care homes without knowing their Covid-19 status and without the added precaution
            of advice to isolate all people admitted from hospitals in England, Wales and
            Northern Ireland until mid to late April 2020. Some care homes into which people
            were discharged could not implement isolation policies safely and no alternatives
            were in place to mitigate this. In those circumstances, the use of infection prevention
            and control measures and PPE become all the more important. Whether those
            measures were sufficient to protect care home residents is considered in the next
            chapter of this Report.

     2.114. In a future pandemic, hospital discharges to care homes must be done as safely
            as possible. Steps must be taken to prevent the pandemic disease from entering
            care homes and infecting a highly vulnerable population. There must be plans in
            place to ensure the safe isolation of residents who may pose a risk of infection
            and transmission. Planning must start now.




72

=== PAGE 86 ===
Chapter 3: Protective
measures

=== PAGE 87 ===
 Module 6: Care sector




 Introduction
 3.1.      Severely limited testing capacity and the absence of a vaccine during the early
           months of the Covid-19 pandemic meant that other protective measures were
           required to keep those using and working in adult social care safe.

 3.2.      It was well known before the pandemic that influenza and other respiratory illnesses
           are “a major cause of hospitalisation, morbidity, and death among the elderly”.1 Older
           adults make up a significant portion of those using adult social care (see Chapter 1:
           Overview of the adult social care sector). However, preventing transmission of a
           potentially deadly virus is far from straightforward. Not all viruses spread in the
           same way and viruses can spread rapidly in care homes due to prolonged close
           contact between residents, patients and care workers, “which in itself is a critical and
           necessary part of the resident’s daily care”.2 Prevention is also difficult because staff
           often work in multiple settings.3

 3.3.      This chapter examines some of the infection prevention and control (IPC) measures
           used during the Covid-19 pandemic in adult social care to try to stop or reduce
           the spread of disease. It focuses, in particular, on guidance about the use of IPC,
           the supply and use of personal protective equipment (PPE) and the steps taken to
           limit care workers inadvertently spreading Covid-19 in and between care settings.
           This includes the provision of funding to support those who were ill or otherwise
           self-isolating.


 Infection prevention and control in adult social care
 3.4.      Whatever the route of transmission of a virus, IPC measures are key protective
           measures, central to ensuring that care is provided to residents in ways that mitigate
           the risks of infection insofar as possible.4 In addition to isolation (see Chapter 2: Care
           home admissions, testing and isolation), measures include hand hygiene, respiratory
           hygiene (using a tissue to catch coughs and sneezes and disposing of the tissue
           safely) and regular cleaning of surfaces, equipment, clothing and bed linen. PPE is
           also a major element of IPC as it protects the wearer from the risk of infection (and
           can also help prevent the wearer from spreading infection to other people).5

 3.5.      Another important IPC measure is ventilation (ie the introduction of fresh air). Given
           that adult social care is delivered indoors and often at close range, ventilation can

 1    INQ000223342_0004
 2    INQ000587772_0022 para 3.4
 3    INQ000587702_0089 para 338
 4    Further details regarding IPC measures are set out in Module 3: The impact of the Covid-19 pandemic on the healthcare systems
 of the United Kingdom, UK Covid-19 Inquiry, March 2026, Chapter 1 (https://covid19.public-inquiry.uk/documents/module-3-full-report).
 Measures include both standard infection control precautions (basic IPC measures that should be used in all care settings to reduce
 the risk of infection from known and unknown risks) and transmission-based precautions (measures required when dealing with a
 specific pathogen, which vary depending on the relevant disease and route of transmission); INQ000474282_0020, 0043 paras
 1.35-1.36, 1.38, 4.8.

=== PAGE 88 ===
                                                                                    Chapter 3: Protective measures




         help to reduce the build-up of infectious particles.6 However, not all buildings are
         built or equipped in such a way that fresh or better-quality air can be introduced
         easily, and there is a tension between the positive effects of ventilation in reducing
         the amount of infection in a closed setting and the risk of exposing recipients of care,
         especially older people, to particularly hot or cold temperatures.7

3.6.     This was not an easy balance to strike during the pandemic. Covid-19 guidance
         for care homes in England and Northern Ireland in March 2020 and in Scotland in
         April 2020 advised that, as a general IPC measure, windows should be opened
         where it was appropriate to do so.8 Guidance from the Welsh Government in March
         2020 recommended opening windows for ventilation in a room where a person
         was isolating when unwell and it was believed they had been exposed to Covid-
         19.9 Visiting guidance issued in each nation in the winter of 2020 also included
         references to ventilation.10 The Inquiry has not seen references to the importance of
         ventilation in care home guidance issued by Public Health Wales prior to March 2021
         – it should have been included from a much earlier stage.

3.7.     Before the arrival of Covid-19, a range of IPC guidance was available throughout
         the UK to support adult social care settings in dealing with outbreaks of infectious
         diseases, including influenza and other respiratory diseases.11 However, Covid-19 was
         a new virus with different characteristics, knowledge of which took time to evolve.
         The care sector needed to know whether pre-existing guidance was applicable or
         whether new IPC guidance should apply.


Timeliness of guidance
3.8.     From January 2020, representatives of the adult social care sector were calling
         for specific Covid-19 guidance. Professor Vic Rayner (Chief Executive Officer of
         the National Care Forum from 2016, a membership organisation representing not-
         for-profit social care and support providers) received reports of growing concerns
         from care providers in January 2020 about what action they should be taking to
         prepare for Covid-19. She contacted the Department of Health and Social Care on
         24 January and was advised that the best guidance to share with members was
         guidance produced for healthcare professionals.12 The guidance from Public Health
         England for healthcare professionals at that time had been developed from previous
         guidance for dealing with Middle East respiratory syndrome (MERS) and treated
         Covid-19 as a high consequence infectious disease. This meant that suspected or
         confirmed Covid-19 cases needed to be treated in specialist facilities (including

           high-level isolation units) and healthcare workers had to comply with strict IPC
           requirements.13 Guidance for primary care advised that people with suspected cases
           should be isolated as soon as possible and should not be allowed to use communal
           bathrooms.14

 3.9.      On 29 January 2020, COBR (the UK government’s national crisis management
           centre for responding to whole-system civil emergencies) agreed that planning for
           a reasonable worst-case scenario should be informed by the additional information
           that the “elderly and those with existing health conditions will be disproportionately
           affected”.15 All four UK health ministers attended.16 On 4 February 2020, the Scientific
           Advisory Group for Emergencies (SAGE) noted: “WN-CoV [Wuhan novel coronavirus]
           appears to mirror a flu mortality curve, with most deaths among the over-60s.”17

 3.10.     UK government officials undertook a stocktake of pandemic preparedness in adult
           social care in England on 11 February 2020. They recognised that there was a need
           to raise awareness of the risks that Covid-19 could pose to the sector to promote
           prevention. It was agreed that the Deputy Chief Medical Officer would draft clinical
           advice for care homes with a Covid-19 case as soon as possible.18

 3.11.     Specific Covid-19 guidance for community care and residential settings was
           published on 25 February 2020 by Public Health England and on 27 February by
           the Department of Health (Northern Ireland) (which closely reflected the Public
           Health England guidance with adaptations to make it relevant to Northern Ireland).19

 3.12.     In Scotland and Wales, no Covid-19-specific guidance for care homes was published
           in February 2020. In Wales, initially, advice was provided to individual care homes by
           a specific team in Public Health Wales (based on the 2016 Infection Prevention and
           Control in Care Homes – All Wales Guidance). This contained guidance on standard
           IPC and isolation of symptomatic residents.20 However, Helen Hough (a former
           care home owner and member of Covid-19 Bereaved Families for Justice Cymru)

         explained that, although in February 2020 “we were all talking about Covid”,21 the
         sector received little to no guidance or communications from the Welsh Government.
         She said that in “early 2020” her care home in Wales was not told how to manage
         an outbreak and so they relied on her existing nursing training and knowledge to
         implement IPC measures as best they could.22

3.13.    On 26 February 2020, Mary Wimbury (Chief Executive of Care Forum Wales)
         emailed Dr Frank Atherton (later Sir Frank Atherton, Chief Medical Officer for Wales
         from August 2016 to 2025) raising concerns from care providers that they had not
         received any specific up-to-date advice on how to deal with an outbreak of Covid-
         19.23 Ms Wimbury emailed again on 2 March 2020 noting that, in the absence of
         other information, it was assumed that advice would be as per the Public Health
         England guidance for care homes.24 It was not until 9 March 2020 that the Welsh
         Government published guidance for community care and residential settings.25

3.14.    Concerned by the absence of practical advice for the sector in February 2020,
         Scottish Care (a membership body representing independent social care providers
         in Scotland) published its own guidance on 24 February.26 Jeane Freeman (Cabinet
         Secretary for Health and Sport in the Scottish Government from June 2018 to May
         2021) and Nicola Sturgeon (First Minister of Scotland from November 2014 to March
         2023) were advised on 11 March 2020 that urgent advice tailored to the sector was
         required.27 Health Protection Scotland’s guidance for community care and residential
         settings was not published until 12 March.28

3.15.    It was known by the end of January 2020 that Covid-19 would have a
         disproportionate impact on older adults and those with pre-existing conditions,
         who are found in high concentrations in care homes. Early guidance on handling
         a confirmed or suspected Covid-19 case was therefore crucial to ensure that those
         working in adult social care knew what IPC measures to implement to protect both
         themselves and residents.

3.16.    The existing Covid-19 healthcare guidance, first published in January 2020, was
         of minimal practical benefit to the care sector because self-isolation was generally
         problematic in care homes and, in some care homes, residents might share
         facilities such as bathrooms. As Professor Rayner had informed the Department
         of Health and Social Care in February 2020, guidance did not sufficiently address
         the particular concerns of the adult social care sector. She described the need for

 Module 6: Care sector




          guidance tailored to adult social care as imperative.29 The differences between
          delivery of care in healthcare and social care settings should be considered during
          pandemic planning, and guidance tailored for social care settings should be available
          as quickly as possible, rather than the sector having to rely on healthcare guidance.

 3.17.    Specific guidance for adult social care came too late across the UK. It should have
          been published in early February 2020. Unsurprisingly, the sector was seeking
          reassurance in uncertain times. Guidance could have briefly explained what was then
          known about the virus and that scientific understanding was evolving. It could have
          reminded care homes of any existing guidance where appropriate. This would have
          helped allay concerns of providers and provided reassurance that they were acting
          on the best advice and information available at that time.


 Flaws in care sector guidance
 3.18.    Once published in late February and early March 2020, Covid-19 guidance produced
          for the care sector suffered from a number of flaws, as analysed below.


 Insufficiently tailored guidance
 3.19.    The close contact nature of social care requires care workers to help care users
          with daily activities such as washing, dressing and eating.30 It should therefore have
          been obvious that initial IPC guidance for adult social care was insufficiently tailored
          to the practical realities of how adult social care is delivered when it recommended
          in England, Northern Ireland and Wales:

               “[T]he best way to reduce any risk of infection for anyone is good hygiene
                 and avoiding direct or close contact (within 2 metres) with any potentially
                 infected person.”31

 3.20. In England, on 19 February 2020, a draft of Covid-19 care home guidance was
       shared with a number of bodies involved in the delivery of adult social care, including
       the National Care Forum, the Care Quality Commission and the Local Government
       Association.32 In its comments on the draft guidance on 20 February 2020, the Local
       Government Association explained that this advice would “not work for personal
       care”.33 Claire Sutton (Head of Independent Health and Social Care at the Royal
       College of Nursing from August 2022) described it as impossible to implement

                                                                                   Chapter 3: Protective measures




         because one could not provide “direct care and support, to individuals if you can’t
         get within 2 metres of them”.34

3.21.    On 8 March 2020, Ros Roughton (Director General for Adult Social Care in the
         Department of Health and Social Care from April to July 2020) acknowledged that
         avoiding close contact was “[c]learly … not possible for many of our care workers”.35
         Professor Dame Jenny Harries (Deputy Chief Medical Officer for England from July
         2019 to March 2021) said that the two-metre guidance reflected an “inherent tension”
         between “advising steps which were thought to be protective” and “accepting that
         for many close personal care simply could not be withdrawn”.36

3.22. Updated guidance for care homes issued in England (13 March 2020) and in
      Northern Ireland (17 March 2020) acknowledged that much of the care delivered
      in care homes requires close personal contact.37

3.23. In times of crisis, there will be little time for consultation because guidance
      must be issued quickly. This underlines the importance of consulting the sector in
      pre-pandemic discussions about guidance that might be needed and the practical
      considerations to be taken into account. As Professor Susan Hopkins (Deputy
      Director of the National Infection Service at Public Health England from 2018 to
      2020) stated, it is important to have principles for guidance laid out in advance of
      an emergency, “so that we are able to bring in stakeholders’ views, the lived view”
      to help people to understand the reasons behind the guidance.38

3.24. The UK government’s Pandemic Preparedness Strategy published in March 2026
      recognised that guidance provided in England during the pandemic was not
      always appropriate for the sector. When tested during Exercise Pegasus (a UK-
      wide pandemic preparedness exercise that commenced in 2025), the clarity of IPC
      guidance for adult social care remained an issue. The Inquiry welcomes the practical
      steps being put in place by the UK government to enhance the effectiveness of IPC
      guidance.39 The devolved administrations are encouraged to do the same.


Inadequate communication of risk in England and Northern Ireland
3.25. In mid-February 2020, there was a growing concern that sustained transmission
      of Covid-19 was already present in the UK. Sustained transmission was described
      to COBR as the spread of infection across communities, not just within households.40
      On 11 February 2020, the Scientific Pandemic Influenza Group on Modelling,

=== PAGE 93 ===
 Module 6: Care sector




           Operational sub-group (SPI-M-O, a sub-group of SAGE) advised SAGE of “a realistic
           probability that there is already sustained transmission in the UK, or that it will …
           become established in the coming weeks”.41

 3.26. On 17 February 2020, there were differing views in SPI-M-O about the likelihood
       of sustained transmission in the UK, with some believing that it would soon
       become established and others believing that it was already present.42 If sustained
       transmission was occurring by mid-February 2020, infected people, such as staff and
       visitors, may have already been entering care homes on a regular basis.43 However,
       the IPC guidance issued by Public Health England and the Department of Health
       (Northern Ireland) at the end of February 2020 failed to warn of the risk of sustained
       transmission and, in fact, went further, stating that it was “very unlikely that anyone
       receiving care in a care home or the community will become infected”.44

 3.27.     The guidance expressly stated that it was “intended for the current position in
           the UK where there is currently no transmission of COVID-19 in the community”.45
           The inclusion of this wording did not reflect the scientific advice that there may have
           already been sustained transmission in the UK or that it would become established
           in the very near future. This guidance therefore failed to communicate the risk that
           care workers in care homes might shortly have to deal with both staff and residents
           exhibiting symptoms of Covid-19.

 3.28. Matt Hancock (Secretary of State for Health and Social Care from July 2018 to June
       2021) said that, at that time, he would have changed the wording from “very unlikely”
       that those using adult social care “will become infected” to the present rather than
       future tense (ie it is very unlikely that those using adult social care will ‘be’ infected).46
       He said that he could not understand the choice of words when “it was so clear that
       we were running into a major problem”.47 With hindsight, Professor Hopkins agreed
       that the guidance should have highlighted the possibility of future transmission.48

 3.29. In a future pandemic, guidance should accurately communicate risks and should be
       updated in a timely manner in the event of significant new information or evidence.

                                                               Chapter 3: Protective measures




Failure to advise preparatory measures
3.30. Guidance provided to healthcare settings in January 2020 recommended that staff
      who might be involved in the investigation or management and care of possible
      cases should familiarise themselves with IPC measures and should be trained in
      donning and doffing (putting on and taking off) PPE.49 By contrast, advice to care
      providers in England and Northern Ireland in February 2020 stated that there was
      “no need to do anything differently in any care setting at present”.50 As explained
      above, this advice was predicated on there being “no transmission of COVID-19 in
      the community”, despite the known risks that sustained transmission might have
      already been occurring or would occur in the very near future.51

3.31.    In Wales, guidance published on 9 March 2020 stated that no different steps were
         required but said that staff should be reminded about good practice on routine
         infection control.52 In Scotland, guidance published on 12 March 2020 stated that,
         when dealing with a probable or confirmed Covid-19 case, staff must comply with
         all infection control procedures set out in the guidance and the National Infection
         Prevention and Control Manual adopted in Scotland. It also included guidance on
         the donning and doffing of PPE.53

3.32. By mid-February 2020, there was a growing concern that a potentially fatal virus was
      already, or would very shortly become, established in the community. Guidance in
      each nation should have been published in February 2020 that advised care staff
      to familiarise themselves with IPC policies and the correct way to don and doff PPE.
      This was particularly significant because these were matters with which staff working
      in adult social care were less familiar.


Training in infection prevention and control measures
Skills and training in adult social care prior to the pandemic
3.33. The Inquiry heard that, prior to the pandemic, workers in the adult social care sector
      did not always have training on, exposure to and familiarity with the use of IPC
      measures.54 Professor Harries told the Inquiry that, while adult social care settings in
      England were expected routinely to manage infections, IPC skills and capacity were
      probably weaker than in the NHS. There were recognised systemic weaknesses,
      including the fact that the majority of care in care homes is provided by non-clinical
      staff.55

=== PAGE 95 ===
 Module 6: Care sector




 3.34. Dr Gee Yen Shin, Professor Dinah Gould and Dr Ben Warne (expert witnesses on
       IPC) explained that training on PPE was especially important for staff safety as PPE
       needed to be removed in a specific order to minimise the risk to the staff member.56
       The 2022 Technical Report on the COVID-19 Pandemic in the UK, produced by the
       UK’s Chief Medical Officers and Chief Scientific Advisers, noted that, while PPE had
       been used in the adult social care sector prior to the pandemic, its universal use
       during the pandemic “required updates in knowledge, skills and practices in care
       homes in line with practices previously standard only in specific acute healthcare
       settings”.57

 3.35. If care workers did not know how to wear and use PPE properly, its effectiveness
       would be undermined.


 Training during the Covid-19 pandemic
 3.36. During the pandemic, the UK government and devolved administrations
       acknowledged that more (and better-quality) IPC training was necessary in adult
       social care. The Inquiry notes that, prior to the pandemic, the UK government and
       devolved administrations were not directly responsible for overseeing or organising
       the delivery of training in adult social care.58 However, during the pandemic, the UK
       government and devolved administrations recognised that additional training was
       required and therefore took steps to ensure that the care sector was provided with
       training resources and support. That additional training on IPC and PPE measures
       was not widely and consistently delivered until after the first UK lockdown. By then,
       Covid-19 was already spreading quickly and care workers had been left unprepared.

 3.37.    In Scotland, an enhanced system of oversight by a multidisciplinary team in each
          NHS health board region was introduced in mid-April 2020. Local teams were able
          to offer care home providers clinical support on matters including IPC and the
          appropriate use of PPE.59 Ms Freeman said that, prior to the pandemic, she had
          presumed that staff in Scotland were consistently trained on the 2012 National
          Infection Prevention and Control Manual and that it was being applied. However,
          from “early in the pandemic response”, she said that it became clear that this was not
          consistently the case and, had she known that, the Scottish Government could have
          introduced “wraparound support” for the sector at an earlier point.60

 3.38. On 29 April 2020, NHS England agreed to deliver training and education on IPC,
       including PPE, to care homes in England in May 2020. By 29 May 2020, 99.96% of

         care homes either had received or declined training or had assured IPC and PPE
         procedures in place.61

3.39. Professor Sir Michael McBride (Chief Medical Officer for Northern Ireland from
      September 2006) stressed the importance of “skilling up” health and social care
      staff in Northern Ireland.62 He acknowledged that there should have been more
      enhanced IPC training for social and healthcare workers in Northern Ireland before
      the pandemic.63 Free IPC training was offered to care homes and domiciliary care
      providers from April 2020 by the Health and Social Care Clinical Education Centre.
      The Department of Health (Northern Ireland) also sent IPC nurses to provide training
      and the Chief Nursing Officer developed training videos.64 Health and Social Care
      trusts supported local care providers by, for example, providing PPE training and
      sharing their IPC resources.65

3.40. In Wales, some health boards that had not ordinarily done so offered IPC and PPE
      training and advice to care homes.66 The Welsh Government agreed funding to
      deliver an IPC training programme that was established in early 2021.67

3.41.    These developments were welcome, but they came too late. Had the UK government
         and devolved administrations recognised the importance of adequate IPC training
         in the adult social care sector from late January 2020 and acted with sufficient
         urgency to ensure that additional training was provided, greater protection could
         have been provided for both staff and users of adult social care. As Professor Harries
         noted, a greater focus on IPC in care homes could have provided better pandemic
         preparedness and prevented unnecessary infection.68

3.42. The UK government’s March 2026 Pandemic Preparedness Strategy commits to
      enhancing guidance and training on IPC in adult social care.69 There is a clear need
      for adult social care staff to receive regular training in IPC measures and the donning
      and doffing of standard PPE in advance of the next pandemic, to ensure that new
      staff are trained and that existing staff keep their skills up to date. The introduction
      of a workforce register for adult social care workers in England to assist with the
      delivery of training in the sector, including during a pandemic, is considered in
      Chapter 8: Recognition and protection.

=== PAGE 97 ===
     Module 6: Care sector




     Provision of personal protective equipment
     3.43. As outlined in Chapter 2: Care home admissions, testing and isolation, despite
           isolation being a key IPC measure, some care homes were unable to isolate
           residents safely. Where isolation could not be complied with safely, PPE became
           all the more important as a way to protect staff and users of care from becoming
           infected.

     3.44. Most nursing and personal care involved close or hands-on contact, often repeatedly
           throughout the day.70 Care is frequently delivered by multiple care givers, including
           by some who work in more than one setting.71 Some people receiving care could not
           tolerate wearing face coverings or could not wear them consistently.72 These factors
           increased the risk of transmission of Covid-19. PPE worn by staff was therefore often
           the sole barrier preventing transmission during close contact and as a result was
           critical in adult social care during the pandemic.


     Personal protective equipment guidance in March and early
     April 2020
     3.45. On 13 March 2020, the UK’s public health bodies and the Department of Health
           and Social Care published guidance for healthcare settings. This guidance stated
           that transmission of Covid-19 was “mainly through respiratory droplets generated
           by coughing and sneezing, and through contact with contaminated surfaces”.73
           All healthcare workers dealing with confirmed or suspected cases were advised to
           wear standard fluid-resistant surgical masks for close contact and, more generally,
           PPE was advised where a task or procedure carried a risk of exposure to Covid-19.74

     3.46. At about this time, guidance on PPE was issued in each nation for dealing with
           confirmed, suspected and symptomatic Covid-19 cases in care homes and
           domiciliary care (see Table 4). This guidance coincided with decisions being taken to
           expedite the discharge of hospital patients, including to care homes (see Chapter 2:
           Care home admissions, testing and isolation).

 3.47.       UK-wide IPC guidance was updated on 2 April 2020 to reflect the fact that Covid-19
             was confirmed as widespread in the community. Health and social care workers were
             also advised to wear gloves and aprons and, upon a risk assessment, fluid-resistant
             surgical face coverings and eye protection, when providing direct personal care
             (within two metres) to people who had confirmed or suspected Covid-19.75


 The pandemic stockpile and personal protective equipment in the
 adult social care sector prior to Covid-19
 3.48. Adult social care is predominantly delivered by providers that are independent of
       local authorities and Health and Social Care trusts. As a result, prior to and at the
       outset of the pandemic, most care providers in the UK were expected to secure
       and provide their own PPE.76 Helen Whately MP (Minister of State for Social Care at
       the Department of Health and Social Care from February 2020 to September 2021)
       reflected that there did not appear to be a plan to make sure care providers would
       be able to supply their staff with PPE in the event of a pandemic.77

 3.49. Some providers had small amounts of PPE stocked prior to the pandemic, while
       others ordered PPE as and when it was needed, meaning that they kept very little
       stock. Many providers lacked the physical space to store spare PPE. This was
       especially true for many domiciliary care providers.78 Not all providers had
       relationships with PPE wholesalers.79 Experience of using different items of PPE
       prior to Covid-19 varied in the sector.80

=== PAGE 100 ===
                                                                                      Chapter 3: Protective measures




3.50. Following the 2009 to 2010 H1N1 influenza pandemic (‘swine flu’), the UK
      government established the UK’s pandemic stockpile. The stockpile included PPE
      such as masks, gowns and gloves, which was allocated and stored in each of the
      four nations according to population size. The stock was intended only for use in
      hospitals, for dealing with symptomatic cases, and was designed to last for 15 weeks,
      the expected duration of a single wave of an influenza pandemic.81 As outlined in
      the Inquiry’s Module 5 Report, which examines the procurement of PPE in detail,
      the stockpile had been prepared for a different pandemic from the one that arrived.
      It also contained significant quantities of expired stock, and the target volumes
      of stock were not being met. The combination of all these factors meant that the
      stockpile was insufficient to meet the demands of the Covid-19 pandemic generally,
      let alone the shortages of PPE supplies in adult social care.82


Supply of personal protective equipment to adult social care
settings during the pandemic
3.51.     PPE could only protect if it was available in sufficient quantity and was of adequate
          quality. During the early months of the pandemic, global demand for PPE surged
          and many adult social care organisations struggled to acquire the necessary PPE to
          comply with guidance.83 Professor Rayner characterised access to PPE during this
          period as particularly chaotic.84 Albert Heaney (Deputy Director General Health and
          Social Services Group in the Welsh Government from March 2020 to June 2021)
          described providing PPE in Wales as one of the most significant challenges of the
          entire pandemic.85

3.52. The UK government and devolved administrations were aware of PPE supply issues
      in adult social care in March 2020. As explained by Vaughan Gething (Minister for
      Health and Social Services in the Welsh Government from May 2016 to May 2021),
      at that time “global supply chains were collapsing”.86

3.53. To address the difficulties, the UK government and devolved administrations
      each took steps to procure PPE and distribute it to the sector. The procurement
      and distribution of healthcare equipment are devolved (or, in Northern Ireland,
      ‘transferred’) matters and are generally carried out by the central procurement
      bodies in each of the four nations of the UK: Supply Chain Coordination Ltd (in
      England), NHS National Services Scotland (in Scotland), NHS Wales Shared Services

     England

     3.54. The Department of Health and Social Care took steps to release stock from the
           pandemic stockpile to the adult social care sector in March 2020. Following
           a change in guidance about face masks in adult social care on 13 March, the
           department distributed 300 face masks free of charge to each of more than 25,000
           care homes between about 18 and 24 March 2020.88 Mr Hancock said that this was
           “all the resources that were available at the time, given the clinical prioritisation of
           PPE into hospitals”.89 While the Inquiry recognises that stocks were exceptionally
           limited at this time, the National Care Association noted that, in a residential care
           home with 40 beds, 300 masks “provided just 36 hours of protection”, which it said
           highlighted the severe shortages faced in meeting ongoing safety needs during
           the pandemic.90 Following the delivery, care providers were advised to continue to
           source other PPE from their usual suppliers.91

     3.55. On 21 March 2020, the National Supply Disruption Response hotline (intended for
           emergency provision of PPE), first established on 16 March 2020, was expanded to
           a 24/7 service.92 The demand for PPE through the hotline was such that, in March
           2020, Ms Whately described it as being overwhelmed with calls.93 The Department
           of Health and Social Care said that more than half a million items of PPE were
           dispatched within the first week of the hotline being set up and that the largest
           number of requests received between March and October 2020 came from the
           adult social care sector.94 The National Care Forum reported, however, that many
           providers experienced difficulty accessing supplies through the hotline. Some
           reported being referred to “the cycle of doom”, where they were advised by the
           hotline to contact their usual suppliers, only then to be referred back to the hotline.95

     3.56. A Homecare Association survey revealed that, by early April 2020, 80% of
           domiciliary care providers lacked sufficient PPE.96 Dr Jane Townson (Chief Executive
           of the Homecare Association from May 2019) said that a lack of PPE meant some
           care workers were “forced … to deliver care without appropriate protection”.97
           Vulnerable people who were shielding in their homes sometimes chose not to

3.57.    In April 2020, the Department of Health and Social Care launched a pilot of an
         online portal (known as the ‘PPE portal’). This new central distribution portal enabled
         registered community healthcare, care home and domiciliary care providers to order
         PPE supplies online. Phased registration began with smaller domiciliary and care
         home providers (all had been invited to register by 5 June 2020); registration of all
         providers in England was complete by September 2020. The portal continued to be
         developed to ensure that it met the needs of its users. For example, in September
         2020, more types of PPE were made available and order limits were adjusted.
         It remained in use until 2024.99

3.58. On 15 May 2020, the Department of Health and Social Care launched the Infection
      Control Fund of £600 million. Guidance in June 2020 confirmed that 25% of the
      funding could be used on wider IPC measures, including the purchase of additional
      PPE by providers or local authorities.100 On 20 July 2020, Mr Hancock agreed to
      provide all social care services in England with free PPE. Prior to this date, the
      department had focused largely on emergency provision of PPE.101 This was a huge
      undertaking. A review in November 2021 by Sir David Pearson (Chair of the Social
      Care Sector COVID-19 Support Taskforce) outlined that, by 9 May 2021, about
      2.2 billion items of free PPE had been provided through the portal to adult social
      care providers, that stakeholders were unanimous in praising the improvements,
      and that barely any providers were reporting shortages.102 The free PPE scheme
      was extended on several occasions and ended when stocks were depleted in
      March 2024.103

3.59. In Northern Ireland, the remit of the Business Services Organisation’s Procurement
      and Logistics Service was extended to procuring PPE for the entire social care
      sector, not just for those social care services that were provided directly by Health
      and Social Care trusts.104 Interim guidance issued by the Department of Health
      (Northern Ireland) on 12 March 2020 asked trusts to support independent adult

 3.60. Professor Sean Holland (Chief Social Work Officer in the Department of Health
       (Northern Ireland) from July 2010 to October 2022) acknowledged that there were
       PPE shortages across the health and care sector, including in independent care
       homes, “well into April 2020”.107 A care home manager in Northern Ireland reported
       that it was not until May 2020 that they had full PPE for a week.108 Robin Swann
       (Minister of Health for Northern Ireland from January 2020 to October 2022 and
       from February to May 2024) said he deeply regretted the fact that the Northern
       Ireland care sector did not have adequate supplies of PPE in the initial weeks of the
       pandemic. Mr Swann recommended that each jurisdiction should have PPE plans
       that clearly set out “the support the sector will receive, how they will receive it and
       a clear chain of responsibility and command”.109

 Scotland

 3.61.     Following discussions on 13 March 2020, the Scottish Government confirmed that
           national pandemic stocks should be made available to social care organisations as
           a stock of last resort. From the week commencing 17 March 2020, PPE was provided
           to the sector from pandemic stocks free of charge for both top-up and emergency
           provision if usual supply routes were unsuccessful. PPE was distributed through local
           PPE hubs.110 A triage helpline was introduced on 19 March 2020 to help manage the
           system.111 However, a GMB Scotland trade union survey of its private care members
           on 30 March 2020 found that 76% had not been provided with appropriate PPE.112
           Scottish Care reported on 13 April 2020 that the hub model worked better in some
           areas than others and that some providers were having difficulties accessing PPE.113

 3.62. On 17 April 2020, Ms Freeman announced that all care homes would receive a top-
       up of at least a week’s supply of aprons, masks and gloves, which were delivered
       by 23 April.114 On 25 April, the Scottish Government announced that, from 27 April,
       where normal supply routes were not successful, local hubs would distribute PPE to
       the entire social care sector.115 Caroline Lamb (Director General for Health and Social
       Care in the Scottish Government and Chief Executive of NHS Scotland from January

Wales

3.63. Mr Gething confirmed on 19 March 2020 that NHS Wales Shared Services
      Partnership’s remit was being expanded to include procuring PPE and supplying it
      to social care settings in Wales. While arrangements were scaled up, providers could
      seek urgent assistance from local health boards if they had a confirmed case of
      Covid-19.117 From March 2020, PPE provided to the adult social care sector was free
      of charge.118

3.64. On 25 March 2020, the Welsh Government confirmed that it had released supplies
      from the stockpile to local health boards for distribution to care providers. From
      2 April 2020, Welsh ministers had oversight of the supply of PPE to adult social care
      through PPE ministerial meetings.119 Issues with supply continued, with Mr Gething
      confirming on 21 April that Wales had enough items of stock to last only for a few
      days.120 Mr Gething said that, by 7 May 2020, about two-thirds of social care PPE
      needs were being met via NHS Wales Shared Services Partnership (with the other
      third obtaining PPE by other routes).121 The Welsh Local Government Association
      reported that operational arrangements were relatively stable by June 2020.122

3.65. In Northern Ireland, Scotland and Wales, supply and distribution issues appeared
      to have been largely resolved by about May 2020. Resolution of these issues took
      longer in England, but, once established and operating successfully, the PPE portal
      significantly improved access to PPE in the care sector.

Front-line experiences of personal protective equipment supply

3.66. In responses to surveys conducted after the pandemic, 87% of English local
      authorities, 55% of Welsh local authorities and 47% of Scottish local authorities
      reported that it had been “very difficult” or “fairly difficult” for care providers to
      access PPE in the first six months of the pandemic.123

3.67.     Despite the efforts in each nation to quickly expand the provision of PPE to the adult
          social care sector, inadequate supplies of PPE, particularly in the first wave of the
          pandemic, left care workers insufficiently protected. Ms Whately stated that care

 3.68. Without adequate access to PPE, adult social care staff resorted to making PPE
       themselves or trying to repurpose it from other items. Some care workers reported
       using “festival waterproof ponchos”, painters’ overalls, and gloves purchased from
       Amazon, DIY shops and other retailers.125 Others fashioned home-made PPE from
       fabric, bin bags, bed linen and other household items.126 Some used laminated plastic
       to make face masks and elastic from leggings to tie them around their heads.127
       Being unable to access PPE left some care services sourcing PPE from veterinary
       or construction industry suppliers or calling around asking for PPE from local
       businesses and neighbours.128 Some social care workers were asked by employers
       to reuse single-use masks or not to use them at all. The Inquiry’s listening exercise,
       Every Story Matters, heard the experience of people working in care homes.


               “I could see NHS staff in masks and full protective wear, we had paper masks
                 which we were told to only wear when you are providing personal care to the
                 most vulnerable as the care home did not have enough stock.”129

 3.69. PPE was also crucial to ensuring that people using care felt reassured and were
       protected during the pandemic.

       PPE at a time of global shortages. However, had the UK government and devolved
       administrations properly taken into account the adult social care sector in their
       pre-pandemic stockpile planning and considered how PPE would be supplied to

3.71.     The next pandemic could be both longer and more deadly. The care sector may
          require emergency provisions of PPE as it did during the response to the Covid-19
          pandemic. In its Module 5 Report, the Inquiry therefore recommended that the UK
          government and devolved administrations:

          • maintain a minimum three-month supply of PPE for the entire health and social
             care sectors; and
          • create a strategy for domestic manufacturing and international procurement
             of healthcare equipment.132

Concerns that personal protective equipment was requisitioned for
healthcare settings

3.72. Problems accessing PPE in adult social care were particularly acute during the first
      wave of the pandemic, when there were concerns that healthcare settings were
      taking precedence for supplies of PPE.

3.73. On 19 March 2020, in England, the organisations and groups that attended the first
      adult social care workforce meeting (which included attendees from the Homecare
      Association, Care England, the Association of Directors of Adult Social Services, the
      Local Government Association, Skills for Care, Social Work England and the Care
      Quality Commission) were told that the NHS and social care organisations would
      have equal priority as distribution of PPE was stepped up.133 However, front-line care
      providers and workers reported instances of suppliers redirecting PPE intended for
      the care sector to the NHS or refusing to supply organisations outside the NHS.134 On
      25 March 2020, in Wales, Mr Gething confirmed a need to ensure that access to PPE
      stock was reserved for the use of front-line healthcare staff.135

3.74.     On 31 March 2020, at a meeting of the PPE oversight board chaired by Professor
          Harries, a prioritisation framework was discussed that set out priority groups for PPE
          “based on risk of contact with those who had COVID-19, the closeness of contact if
          present and whether the workforce was defined as critical”.136 Prioritisation of PPE
          based on this framework was subsequently agreed by all four nations on 2 April
          2020. The highest category of need was acute healthcare settings and settings

 3.75. On 31 March 2020, Ms Whately’s private office received a list of collated concerns
       from PPE suppliers, who explained that they had prioritised the NHS, which had
       left “social care providers with nothing”.138 One supplier reported that its PPE had
       been “[r]equisitioned for the NHS or requisitioned for the national supply stock”.139
       Another said that it would only deal with NHS hospitals given the high demand.
       One reported having been told that certain products had to be ring-fenced for the
       NHS as sanctioned by the UK government.140

 3.76. On 3 April 2020, Mr Gething heard concerns from council leaders that the NHS
       in Wales was being prioritised for PPE over social care settings. The Deputy
       Chief Medical Officer (Wales) explained that PPE guidance was based on a risk
       assessment, and that the risk to those working in adult social care settings “is likely
       to be less than in hospital settings since residents are self-isolating and visitors are
       banned”.141 Mr Gething was informed that steps had been taken to provide PPE to
       care homes with suspected cases.142 Public Health Wales advised that PPE guidance
       for the care sector in Wales was consistent with other parts of the UK.143 Mr Gething
       said that he was sensitive to concerns about prioritisation of PPE and information
       was published about supplies of PPE being provided to the adult social care sector
       to provide reassurance.144

 3.77.     In response to surveys conducted after the pandemic, 44% of local authorities
           in England, 27% of Welsh local authorities and 38% of Scottish local authorities
           reported that orders of PPE were diverted to the NHS “very often” or “fairly often”
           in the first six months of the pandemic.145

 3.78. In a survey conducted by the National Association of Care and Support Workers,
       one member reported feeling like the prioritisation of PPE for hospitals was because
       “care home residents weren’t worth saving”.146 The manager of a care home in
       Northern Ireland said that PPE only started to be received from the relevant Health
       and Social Care trust in late April 2020. They said it was stressed to trusts repeatedly
       that the community and care homes were extremely vulnerable and needed PPE first
       to prevent spread; however, “the acute settings of hospitals all seemed to get first

          before the care homes”.147 Dr Townson accused the UK government of redirecting
          the supply of PPE for domiciliary care and putting care workers disproportionately
          at risk compared with health sector colleagues.148

3.79.     Mr Hancock explained that there was clinical prioritisation, which was based on
          advice, but he did not agree that supplies of PPE to the NHS were being prioritised
          “at the expense” of the care sector.149 While Mr Hancock was aware that people “felt
          there was a prioritisation, and that the NHS hoarded PPE”, he said that the evidence
          he saw pointed to a situation of desperately short supply in which “everyone was
          trying to get their hands on PPE, and if they got their hands on PPE then they would
          tend to hold on to it”.150 Ms Whately was told by officials in the Department of Health
          and Social Care that there was no “national instruction” that the NHS should be
          prioritised over social care.151 Ms Lamb said that the Scottish Government focused
          very much on what would be the requirement in healthcare settings prior to the
          pandemic.152

3.80. It is clear that, when PPE supplies were limited in March and April 2020, the UK
      government and devolved administrations agreed that acute healthcare settings
      were the priority for receipt. Had there been better planning for the provision of
      PPE to all settings, this may not have been necessary. Once more stock became
      available, governments tried to ensure that other healthcare settings and adult social
      care were given equal priority. However, given the sheer volume of reports of PPE
      being diverted to healthcare systems, the belief persisted that the UK government
      and devolved administrations failed to prioritise the important role of PPE in the care
      sector to protect users and staff.

Quality and suitability of personal protective equipment

3.81.     During the pandemic, problems with poor-quality PPE were common in the
          adult social care sector – the PPE available was described to Every Story Matters
          as “awful” and “out of date” – and left care workers worrying about the risk of
          infection.153 UNISON (a public services union that represents members in the social
          care sector) described problems with quality and access to PPE as “multiple and
          coming from all over the country”.154 As explored in the Inquiry’s Module 3 and
          Module 5 Reports, these problems were not unique to adult social care.155

 Module 6: Care sector




 3.82. Care UK said that it had serious concerns regarding the quality of PPE provided by
       the UK government. For example, face masks that were initially provided had ties
       that detached from the mask “on many occasions”.156 Care UK said that it had much
       more confidence in the quality of equipment provided by the end of the summer
       of 2020.157

 3.83. In response to surveys conducted after the pandemic, 41% of English local
       authorities, 45% of Welsh local authorities and 17% of Scottish local authorities
       reported that their council or local care providers had experienced orders of poor-
       quality PPE “very often” or “fairly often” in the first six months of the pandemic.158

 3.84. The manager of a care home in England said that they:

               “never knew what the quality was going to be like until it arrived. I remember
                 batches of aprons that didn’t have a hole for the head, there were aprons
                 without ties and gloves that broke as soon as you put your hand in. Some aprons
                 were cut on a slant so when you opened them up there was only half an apron …
                 Some PPE … had to be put in the bin straight away as they were completely
                 unusable and unsafe.”159

 3.85. The Inquiry’s Module 5 Report considered in more detail the need to ensure the
       quality of PPE in the pandemic stockpile and recommended that the UK government
       and devolved administrations make changes to emergency healthcare equipment
       regulations (which include PPE) to clarify the inspection process and to quality-
       assure the expanded pandemic stockpile.160


 Limiting the risk of Covid-19 transmission by staff
 3.86. As set out in Chapter 2: Care home admissions, testing and isolation, care workers
       entering care settings were, inadvertently, one of the routes by which Covid-19
       could be spread to people receiving and working in adult social care. Staff had been
       recognised as a potential route of transmission by the Department of Health and
       Social Care on 11 February 2020.161 The initial results of the Vivaldi 1 study (which
       considered Covid-19 infection in care homes), published on 3 July 2020, did not
       provide evidence on the relative importance of all routes of transmission in care
       homes but did point to the important role of staff in transmission of infection.162

=== PAGE 110 ===
                                                                                      Chapter 3: Protective measures




3.87.     Care workers move in and between residents’ rooms and communal areas in care
          homes and can work across multiple care homes, settings and agencies. The use
          of agency staff to cover sickness absences was not uncommon. Larger providers
          moved care workers between care homes located in different regions and across
          borders to help with staff shortages.163 Some social care staff had part-time jobs in
          other industries.164 Domiciliary care workers frequently travelled to several different
          homes during a working day.165 The risks of cross-infection were therefore inherent
          in the system and so steps were taken by care providers, the UK government and
          the devolved administrations to try to reduce those risks.


Cohorting
3.88. Restricting staff movement in care settings was part of the IPC toolkit for controlling
      the transmission of infectious respiratory diseases.166 During the pandemic, limiting
      movement in care settings was primarily done by grouping or ‘cohorting’ staff to
      restrict workers moving between symptomatic and non-symptomatic residents.167

3.89. Ms Hough described splitting the home she managed in Wales into three sections,
      including one that was designated for symptomatic residents. Staff were divided
      into groups and allocated to work in a particular section. She believed this system
      helped reduce infection. However, it was difficult to maintain when staff members
      had to cover absences, sometimes for another team.168 The Inquiry also heard of
      other initiatives to restrict staff movement in care homes. One care home in England
      described dividing its two units – one a nursing unit and one a dementia unit – into
      “bubbles” so that staff would remain in one “bubble” and not walk into other areas of
      the home. The home also allocated specific lavatories and changing areas to staff.169
      However, variations in the size and layout of care homes meant it was not possible
      for all care homes to implement cohorting.170

3.90. In domiciliary care, some providers arranged their workforce into teams, with one
      caring for people with Covid-19 and the other caring for people without Covid-19.
      However, this was difficult to implement when testing was limited and a person’s
      Covid-19 status was unknown. The nature of domiciliary care also meant that many
      care workers had to deliver care in multiple people’s houses during a shift.171

=== PAGE 111 ===
     Module 6: Care sector




     3.91.    While cohorting was a useful IPC measure for providers that were able to implement
              it, those that were unable to do so needed to rely on other IPC measures to try to
              protect staff and residents.


     Limiting staff movement between care settings
     3.92. Restricting staff movement gave rise to significant concerns about how the sector
           would be able to maintain safe levels of care. Before the Covid-19 pandemic, many
           care providers already had difficulties finding sufficient numbers of staff to provide
           care to people in care homes and at home.172 During the pandemic, large numbers
           of the workforce were absent through self-isolation or ill-health, making workforce
           shortages all the greater.173

     3.93. The tension between limiting staff movement and the need to provide safe levels
           of care was acknowledged by a number of witnesses. Professor Harries said that
           the problem was very difficult to solve. She explained that to isolate care workers
           completely could mean that “a care home might fall over”.174 She said the precarious
           state of the social care workforce was a problem throughout the pandemic.175
           Professor McBride described balancing safe staffing levels while dealing with
           high levels of staff absence as challenging.176 When asked how best to balance
           the provision of care with the need to restrict staff movement, Professor McBride
           said that planning for interventions needed to start now. He acknowledged that
           underlying issues around remuneration and recognition needed to be addressed
           and said that there needed to be a mechanism to track staff movements readily.177

     3.94. One member of the National Association of Care and Support Workers said that
           there was “no choice but to move around because everywhere was so under
           staffed”.178 Another said that short staffing and self-isolation policies meant it was
           “essential for care workers to work in different places” to ensure that care could be
           provided.179 Having the correct PPE was identified as one factor that would have
           reduced the risk of spreading the virus via staff movement.180 Dr Townson described
           how, in domiciliary care, at times more than a quarter of an organisation’s staff
           were isolating. She said that, in light of existing workforce shortages, this became
           a serious issue, with many providers finding it difficult to cover shifts.181

                                                                                   Chapter 3: Protective measures




Guidance on limiting staff movement

3.95. Notwithstanding these inherent difficulties, guidance was issued across the UK
      seeking to limit the risk of staff spreading the virus. As Ms Lamb told the Inquiry,
      stopping staff movement in and between care settings was critical to minimise the
      risk of infection from Covid-19.182 Scotland was the first nation to issue guidance on
      limiting staff movement in adult social care when, on 12 March 2020, guidance from
      Health Protection Scotland stated that the use of bank or agency staff in care homes
      and in domiciliary care “should be avoided wherever possible”.183

3.96. In Northern Ireland, guidance dated 26 April 2020 stated:

              “As far as possible homes should seek to limit turnover in staff they use and seek
                to limit the number of staff moving between different homes. This might include
                block booking agency staff to ensure consistency.”184

3.97.    In England, Department of Health and Social Care guidance – Coronavirus (Covid-19):
         Care Home Support Package, dated 15 May 2020 – stated that “all possible steps”
         should be taken by providers to “minimise staff movement between care homes,
         to stop infection spreading between locations”.185

3.98. In May 2020, the Welsh Government received scientific advice that staff movement
      between care homes should be prevented if at all possible, acknowledging that this
      could put significant economic strain on the sector.186 Mr Heaney explained that the
      Welsh Government was not in a position to prevent the movement of care workers
      at that time because this would have led to insufficient levels of staffing to deliver
      care.187 It was not until 17 August 2020 that the Welsh Government advised that staff
      movement in social care settings should be restricted. In a letter to local authorities,
      directors of social services and managers of adult care homes, Mr Heaney
      acknowledged the steps already taken by social care settings to minimise movement
      of the workforce and noted that “up to 90% of workers from agencies are allocated
      to a single setting”.188 The letter stated that, in addition to cohorting staff, providers
      should ensure that staff worked in only one care home.189

3.99. However, in about late 2020 or early 2021, workforce shortages due to shielding,
      sickness and childcare responsibilities resulted in Social Care Wales publishing
      advice supporting mutual aid to enable staff movement between neighbouring

               local authorities. Mr Heaney said that the situation was extremely challenging

      3.100. Mr Hancock acknowledged that limiting staff movement in domiciliary care would
             have required those workers to “only work with one person or a smaller number
             of people, and that would probably have been impractical”.191 The Scottish
             Government’s Adult Social Care Winter Preparedness Plan 2020–21 continued to
             encourage a reduction in staff movement in domiciliary care. It also required agency
             staff for care homes with cases of Covid-19 to self-isolate for 14 days before moving
             to another setting.192

      3.101. While the UK government and devolved administrations attempted to reduce
             transmission risk in adult social care by discouraging staff movement between care
             settings, this proved to be very difficult because of staffing pressures and the need
             to ensure that care could be delivered. Given that care workers will almost inevitably
             be a means by which a pandemic disease can enter care settings, a future pandemic
             may again necessitate efforts to limit staff movement. This reinforces the need
             to plan for restrictions on staff movement and to encourage the recruitment and
             retention of adult social care staff (see Chapter 8: Recognition and protection).

      Attempted legislation to restrict staff movement

      3.102. Mr Hancock said that in May 2020 it was not possible to introduce legislation to
             restrict staff movement. He said that not only did it take time to introduce legislation
             but officials had advised him that there was a risk that some providers would be
             forced to operate with unsafe staffing levels.193 However, following agreement at
             a Covid-19 Operations Committee meeting in September 2020, the Department
             of Health and Social Care proposed that The Health and Social Care Act 2008
             (Regulated Activities) Regulations 2014 be amended to require care home providers
             not to use staff (whether agency, temporary or otherwise) who attended more
             than one health or social care setting. The proposal included an exception: where
             a provider had insufficient staff to provide safe care, people who also worked in
             another health or social care setting could be employed “for as long as is reasonably
             necessary to make alternative arrangements”.194

      3.103. Between 13 and 23 November 2020, the Department of Health and Social Care ran
             a consultation on these proposals. More than half the respondents (55%) believed
             that the proposals had the potential to reduce staff movement. However, 86% of
             respondents believed that the proposals would be difficult to implement and 68%
             believed that exceptions would need to be used at least weekly. Respondents also
             had concerns that the legislation would cause financial hardship and affect staff

         wellbeing, and could lead to a risk of burn-out.195 The Nuffield Trust’s response said
         that care homes had already “been brought to their knees during the first wave of
         the pandemic” and in the absence of timely support had struggled to continue to
         deliver services.196 It also believed that domiciliary care workers should be exempt to

3.104. By December 2020, a submission to Ms Whately noted that approximately one-third
       of care homes were experiencing outbreaks of Covid-19. The Department of Health
       and Social Care maintained that proposed legislation continued to be an important
       mechanism in helping to reduce the risk of Covid-19 outbreaks and infections.
       It cited research by the Cabinet Office into a small sample of homes that had not
       experienced outbreaks, which identified no staff movement as a good practice
       measure.199

3.105. Mr Hancock said that, by about late December 2020, the Treasury was reluctant to
       fund a scheme to support staff affected by the proposed regulation. On 7 January
       2021, Mr Hancock said he accepted that a full ban would not be possible “because of
       opposition from key system partners”, particularly in light of both the vaccine rollout
       and the decision to bring in a further lockdown.200 Guidance against staff movement
       between care homes was, however, maintained.201

3.106. Ms Freeman said she was not sure that mandating the restriction of staff movement
       in Scotland would be feasible, given that care was provided by both the public
       and private sectors.202 Ms Lamb said that introducing guidance was quicker and
       she thought legislation would be complex.203 Professor McBride likewise thought
       that introducing legislation in Northern Ireland might not be quick and that it could
       disproportionately impact low-paid workers and women. He said that, while it may
       be “very tempting to reach for the lever which says, ‘[m]ake something mandatory
       and it will happen’”, in his experience that was not always the case.204

3.107. As the consultation responses made clear, introducing legislation during the
       pandemic to restrict staff movement would have posed significant practical
       difficulties for the adult social care sector. The UK government and devolved
       administrations did, however, put in place funding to mitigate financial difficulties
       caused to care workers and providers.
