=== PAGE 115 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




  Funding to support limiting staff movement

  3.108. It was widely acknowledged that limiting staff movement could materially affect
         the financial circumstances and earnings of both care workers and care providers.
         People who worked in multiple settings could lose a significant source of income,
         and restricting staff to one care setting also required providers to ensure that there
         was sufficient workforce capacity, which often meant a need to employ additional
         staff.205

  3.109. In England and Scotland, specific funds were made available to try to cover the costs
         of limiting staff to one care home. There were three funding streams in England:

          • The Infection Control Fund of £600 million was launched by the Department
             of Health and Social Care on 15 May 2020.206 It was paid to local authorities in
             two instalments (May and July 2020) to distribute to care providers to meet the
             additional costs of restricting staff to work in only one care home and to pay the
             wages of those self-isolating.207 The Infection Control Fund was extended to
             March 2021, with £546 million paid in two instalments in October and December
             2020.208
          • The Workforce Capacity Fund was announced on 16 January 2021. This allocated
             £120 million of ring-fenced funding to local authorities and could be spent directly
             by local authorities or passed on to care providers with the aim of increasing the
             size of the workforce in care homes and domiciliary care. It had to be spent by
             31 March 2021.209
          • From 29 March 2021, the Infection Control Fund and the Rapid Testing Fund
             (introduced on 23 December 2020 to support increased lateral flow testing
             in care homes) were consolidated into the Infection Control and Testing Fund.
             This fund of £341 million required local authorities to pass on a proportion
             of their allocations to every care provider in their local area. The restriction
             of staff movement remained one intended aim of the funding.210

  3.110. There were mixed reports about whether these funding regimes fully achieved
         their intended aims. To be eligible for funding from the Infection Control Fund, care
         homes had to complete the Capacity Tracker (an online tool to collect data on care
         home capacity and other relevant information).211 In July 2020, Capacity Tracker
         data suggested that 90% of care homes had taken action to restrict staff movement
         between settings, compared with 78% at the end of May 2020.212 However, technical
         issues prevented some providers from completing the Capacity Tracker, which put
         their funding at risk. Some providers complained that they had not received funding
  205 INQ000059167_0002, 0004 paras 2, 8-9; INQ000553295_0001-0002; INQ000543049_0040-0041 para 215

         from the Infection Control Fund via their local authority, and some local authorities
         reported delays in being able to distribute funding to providers.213

3.111.   The Workforce Capacity Fund led to the recruitment of just under 40,000 staff.214
         This was remarkable in the context of a pandemic. In response to a survey
         conducted between 13 July and 10 August 2021, 64% of 59 local authorities that
         participated either agreed or strongly agreed that the fund helped to reduce
         staff movement between care homes and health and care settings.215 A review
         in November 2021 by Sir David Pearson found that stakeholders welcomed the
         fund but felt that it was introduced late. They believed that, if the funding had been
         introduced a few months earlier, it would have lowered the chance of a small number
         of staff who tested positive for Covid-19 continuing to work in care homes.216

3.112. The Homecare Association described the Infection Control and Testing Fund as
       unpredictable and time-consuming to administer, but also said that it was invaluable
       in terms of maintaining workforce capacity and morale and, without this funding,
       many services would not have remained viable.217

3.113. The Scottish Government’s Adult Social Care Winter Preparedness Plan 2020–21,
       published on 2 November 2020, stated that “providers will now be required to
       demonstrate they have done everything they can to limit staff movement”.218 This
       required robust recording of unavoidable staff movement. The plan committed up to
       £50 million to help care providers implement these requirements and acknowledged
       that it “will be important that every effort is made to ensure any measures do not
       negatively impact on individual members of this vital workforce”.219

3.114. The Inquiry has seen no evidence that funding in Northern Ireland and Wales was
       ring-fenced specifically to help restrict the movement of staff between care settings.
       However, substantial funding was provided throughout the course of the pandemic
       to the adult social care sector in both nations, including for IPC measures, which
       could include restricting staff movement.220

3.115. The funding to restrict staff movement in England was welcomed by the sector and
       there is evidence that, overall, it had the intended effect of reducing staff movement
       between care settings. There were, however, some issues with access to and
       distribution of the funding. Planning across the UK for a future pandemic where

      Funding to support sick or self-isolating care workers
      3.116. As noted in the Inquiry’s combined Report relating to Modules 2, 2A, 2B and 2C,
             many people in low-paid professions had no option but to continue working during
             the pandemic. This was especially true in the adult social care sector, where low
             levels of pay meant care workers faced the increased risk of infection from Covid-19
             and increased financial hardship when self-isolating.221

      3.117. The UK government and devolved administrations therefore took steps to limit the
             risk of Covid-19 being spread by individuals who felt they had to continue to work
             when they should have been isolating (whether positive, symptomatic or a close
             contact). This included changes made in the March 2020 budget which meant that
             employees self-isolating due to Covid-19 were entitled to Statutory Sick Pay from the
             first day of their absence from work (as opposed to day four).222 The changes came
             into force in all four nations later that month.223 However, some people, for example
             those on zero-hours contracts (see Chapter 1: Overview of the adult social care
             sector), might not have qualified for Statutory Sick Pay.224 Since the pandemic, the
             Employment Rights Act 2025 has removed the lower earnings limit for Statutory Sick
             Pay and the waiting period to qualify (with effect from April 2026) in the UK.225

      3.118. Each government also introduced grants for people required to self-isolate.
             The Discretionary Support self-isolation grant was available in Northern Ireland
             from 25 March 2020. The UK government introduced a £500 Test and Trace Support
             Payment on 28 September 2020 and similar schemes were introduced in Scotland
             in October 2020 and Wales in November 2020.226 These schemes are considered
             further in Module 7: Test, trace and isolate.

=== PAGE 118 ===
                                                                                    Chapter 3: Protective measures




3.119. Specific funding was also provided for the adult social care sector to limit both the
       financial loss to the care worker and the risk of onward transmission of Covid-19.


Top-up to Statutory Sick Pay
3.120. Care sector members of UNISON described Statutory Sick Pay as “low level”,
       which placed care workers under pressure to work when they were meant to be
       isolating.227 Some people continued to work when symptomatic, feeling unwell or
       having been exposed to Covid-19, while others were reluctant to test in case they
       received a positive result.228 Professor Rayner said that one of the biggest issues
       for National Care Forum members was insufficient funding to ensure that staff were
       consistently paid higher than Statutory Sick Pay “to guarantee staff isolation”.229
       The National Care Forum and trade unions were concerned that guidance was
       introduced “without the funding to make it possible for people to isolate”, which
       Professor Rayner said was a repeated problem throughout the pandemic.230

3.121. During the pandemic, funding was therefore made available to care providers to
       enable them to pay staff a higher wage (ie a wage above the minimum sick pay
       required by law). Sometimes referred to as Enhanced Statutory Sick Pay, funding
       was made available in England, Scotland and Northern Ireland in May and June 2020
       as follows:

         • Part of the Infection Control Fund introduced in England on 15 May 2020,
             and extended on a number of occasions, was intended to ensure that social care
             staff who were self-isolating received their normal wages.231 This supplemented
             £1.6 billion of initial funding that had been made available to local authorities in
             March 2020, in part to ensure that adult social care staff who were self-isolating
             did not lose out financially.232 However, that money had not been ring-fenced
             specifically for adult social care.233 The Vivaldi 1 study found that care homes
             that did not pay full sick pay were more likely to have infections in both residents
             and staff.234

      Front-line experience
      3.123. Surveys conducted by the National Association of Care and Support Workers
             highlighted some of the barriers faced by workers in accessing Enhanced Statutory
             Sick Pay. For example, to be eligible for funding, a polymerase chain reaction (PCR)
             test result had to be submitted and, as is being considered in Module 7, they were
             not always available to order.238

      3.124. Not all staff received full wages when sick or self-isolating.239 The Department of
             Health and Social Care noted that the proportion of providers that reported paying
             staff full wages in England during isolation rose from 37% in May 2020 to 63% in July
             2020.240 However, in August 2020, UNISON reported results of a recent survey it
             had undertaken in which this figure was reported as only 25% of employers (it is not
             known how many people took part in this survey).241 According to the Department
             of Health and Social Care, as at April 2021, data obtained from the Capacity Tracker
             showed that the proportion of care homes paying full wages for staff who were
             isolating was 83.6%.242

      3.125. The Inquiry heard about the stress and anxiety that care workers felt when receiving
             only basic sick pay. One care worker said that they had to borrow money “to make
             it through Christmas” as a result of receiving basic Statutory Sick Pay when self-

3.126. While the Infection Control Fund helped some domiciliary care providers maintain

       the workforce experienced “increased financial hardship” during periods of illness
       or self-isolation and 18 said the same issue was experienced by domiciliary care

Enhanced Statutory Sick Pay in Wales
3.128. It was not until 1 November 2020 that the Covid-19 Statutory Sick Pay Enhancement
       Scheme was introduced in Wales. The scheme provided funding for care homes
       and domiciliary care providers to ensure that care workers received full pay if they
       were self-isolating, had symptoms of Covid-19 or tested positive.248 The scheme
       was extended to August 2022.249 When asked why the Enhanced Statutory Sick
       Pay Scheme was introduced later in Wales than in other parts of the UK, Mr Gething
       said that there were huge pressures on Welsh Government budgets at this time.250
       Mr Gething explained that the Barnett formula meant there was no financial certainty
       to be able to increase sick pay funding until later in the pandemic. He said that he
       would have:

              “liked us to have been in a position to have confirmed the position on sick pay
                earlier, but there was a level of real caution and concern that our budgets would
                be changed later in the year and we would find ourselves not able to meet the
                commitments that we wanted to make”.251

=== PAGE 121 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




               When asked to consider Scotland and Northern Ireland, which were subject to similar
               financial pressures yet introduced schemes earlier, Mr Gething repeated that there
               was a level of caution and he “would definitely have wanted that scheme to have
               come in earlier than it did”.252

      3.129. Given the importance of ensuring that care workers who needed to self-isolate
             were not financially disadvantaged and remained away from work, the provision
             of Enhanced Statutory Sick Pay in Wales came too late.


      Future funding for adult social care in a pandemic
      3.130. Care workers in each nation would have benefited from an earlier introduction
             of funding to support them when they were required to self-isolate as a result of
             Covid-19. Ensuring that care workers received adequate funding to self-isolate was
             particularly important to control infection rates. In July 2020, evidence suggested
             that care homes “that paid sick leave immediately when someone had to self-isolate,
             had Covid case levels around 13% lower tha[n] care homes that did not”.253

      3.131. In the event that a future pandemic requires care workers to self-isolate, the UK
             government and devolved administrations will need to implement a package of
             financial support that enables high-risk, low-income care workers to self-isolate.
             As set out in the Inquiry’s combined Module 2ABC Report, governments must plan
             for adequate financial provision to ensure that people have the economic ability to
             comply with self-isolation, and there must be mechanisms in place for payments.254
             Those mechanisms should support the introduction of rapid changes to financial
             support and ensure that any additional funds reach care workers. The Inquiry
             therefore recommends that planning for this financial support begins now so
             that there is an emergency funding mechanism in place ready to stand up at the
             beginning of a pandemic.

      3.132. The UK government and devolved administrations should also have plans to
             introduce funding to limit the movement of staff in the event that they are identified
             as a route of transmission in a future pandemic. This may include, for example,
             funding for providers to hire additional staff and funding to restrict staff to working
             in one setting. Financial support schemes should be designed in partnership with
             representatives from the adult social care sector, who can bring experience of how
             the delivery of care and staffing arrangements operate in practice.

=== PAGE 122 ===
                                                        Chapter 3: Protective measures




Recommendation 3: Financial support to adult social care staff to
self-isolate or to limit working across multiple sites
The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
Ireland Executive should put in place financial support schemes that should be
made available and that can be implemented at the start of a future pandemic:

• to protect adult social care staff against financial loss as a result of compliance
  with mandatory self-isolation rules; and

• to ensure that adult social care providers and staff can comply with rules or
  guidance restricting the number of sites at which adult social care staff may
  work during a pandemic.
The following principles should apply:

• There should be clear and straightforward guidance about eligibility criteria,
  application processes and payment arrangements for the support schemes.

• Information about the introduction of support schemes and how adult social
  care staff and providers can receive support should be communicated clearly
  and made available in accessible formats.

• Mechanisms should be in place to monitor the effectiveness of the
  support schemes.
These financial support schemes should be designed in partnership with
representatives from across the adult social care sector.

=== PAGE 123 ===
Chapter 4: Visiting
restrictions in care homes

=== PAGE 124 ===
                                                                 Chapter 4: Visiting restrictions in care homes




Introduction
4.1.     Care homes are where many people “live out their lives, with as much independence
         as their own unique situation permits and as much dignity, compassion and care
         as society can provide”.1 It is therefore unsurprising that the decision in March 2020
         to impose visiting restrictions in care homes across the UK was one of the most
         emotive and controversial aspects of the Covid-19 pandemic response.2

4.2.     In the absence of testing or a vaccine in the early months of the Covid-19 pandemic,
         restrictions on visits were considered an essential measure to mitigate the risk
         of infectious people inadvertently transmitting the virus to residents and those
         providing care.3 Decisions to restrict visits and access were taken at national and
         local government level and by individual care homes.

4.3.     This approach was not new. Visiting restrictions have previously been used to
         manage outbreaks of infectious diseases such as norovirus and seasonal influenza.4
         However, such restrictions were typically of short duration, as outbreaks rarely
         exceeded two weeks.5 The prolonged visiting restrictions during the Covid-19
         pandemic were therefore unprecedented.6 The need to protect highly vulnerable
         residents from infection had to be balanced against ensuring dignity and quality of
         life for residents and the availability of the support of their loved ones when they
         needed it most.7 The longer the restrictions persisted, the harder it became to
         achieve the right balance.

4.4.     This chapter examines the decisions taken to restrict visiting from loved ones and
         in-person healthcare in care homes and whether the right balance was struck. It also
         considers the impact on residents.


The importance of visiting
4.5.     The importance of visiting cannot be overstated. Family and loved ones fulfil a
         central role in the life and everyday care of residents.8 They may spend hours
         encouraging residents to eat and drink and providing necessary help – for example,

content/uploads/2020/06/Care-home-visiting-guidance-final-20.06.25.pdf; INQ000147431)

=== PAGE 125 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




           in ‘toileting’, washing, brushing hair and teeth, shaving and dressing.9 While spending
           time in a care home, they may also be able to spot signs of abuse, neglect or other
           safeguarding issues.10

  4.6.     Visits are also important for residents’ mental wellbeing through stimulation, the
           connection to the outside world and the maintenance of relationships.11 Visits from
           loved ones are often integral to a person’s sense of identity.12 Julia Jones, co-founder
           of John’s Campaign (a group that advocates and campaigns for the rights of people
           with dementia across different care settings), stated: “[V]isits from those who matter
           most are an integral part of an individual’s sense of identity and self-worth.”13

  4.7.     For people with complex cognitive conditions, such as dementia and learning
           disabilities, or communication difficulties, visits are critically important. Sustained
           interaction and connection with familiar faces helps to preserve cognitive function,
           reduce isolation and agitation, and improves emotional wellbeing beyond standard
           care.14 Professor Sube Banerjee, expert witness on the care and treatment of people
           with dementia and their families, described this supportive function as “very special”,
           noting that its absence causes deterioration, potential depression, loss of skills and
           more rapid disease progression.15

  4.8.     The continued, regular presence of loved ones is therefore not merely a comfort to
           residents, but a crucial element of care that actively sustains physical and mental
           wellbeing and is likely to slow the pace of cognitive and physical decline.


  Restrictions on visiting
  4.9.     Scientific and public health advice in the early months of the pandemic focused
           upon the risks of Covid-19 infection. Visitors represented a potential route of
           infection into care homes, whose residents faced a very high risk of death or
           serious illness from Covid-19.16 Care homes were identified as a “substantially higher
           risk setting for COVID-19” due to the vulnerable nature of the population and the
           indoor environments in which the infection could spread.17 The risk of transmission
           of Covid-19 through a visit was not confined to the resident being visited but also
           affected other residents.18

=== PAGE 126 ===
                                                                    Chapter 4: Visiting restrictions in care homes




4.10.     In the absence of adequate diagnostic testing, tracing capacity, a vaccine or
          treatment, and with limited supplies of personal protective equipment (PPE), visiting
          restrictions were one of a range of measures across all four nations of the UK
          “concentrated on trying to prevent ingress and minimise transmission”.19

4.11.     Policy decisions about visiting restrictions were taken by the UK government and
          devolved administrations, informed by scientific advice, and then published in
          guidance.20

4.12.     Visiting guidance was introduced and evolved at different times across the UK.
          Initial Public Health England guidance dated 25 February 2020 advised that care
          home managers and infection prevention and control specialists undertake risk
          assessments of visits.21 By 23 March 2020, when the mandatory UK-wide lockdown
          was introduced, each government had advised restrictions on visiting in care homes
          to facilitate essential visits only.22 Scottish guidance issued on 26 March 2020
          extended the guidance to permit visits to residents with dementia experiencing
          distress.23

4.13.     Many care homes had already pre-emptively decided to restrict visiting by
          this point. This was likely due to the growing public awareness of the risks
          posed by the pandemic, in particular the risks to people aged over 70, or with
          underlying conditions, and the broader government guidance on social distancing
          and shielding.24

=== PAGE 127 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      4.14.    By the end of April 2020, guidance recommending strict restrictions was in place
               across the UK. In practice, this amounted to care homes being ‘locked down’; visits
               only took place in exceptional circumstances. In England, Wales and Northern
               Ireland, this was effectively limited to end-of-life visits.25

      4.15.    Professor Ian Hall (Academic Co-Chair of the Care Home Working Sub-Group,
               which became the Social Care Working Group of the Scientific Advisory Group for
               Emergencies (SAGE)) explained that scientific advice and models in April and May
               2020 concentrated on infection risks and not psychological impacts of isolation
               from loved ones, and that the benefits of social visits were not easily scientifically
               quantifiable.26

      4.16.    While the focus on infection risks was understandable (given the vulnerability of
               the residents in care homes to a respiratory virus), Heléna Herklots, Older People’s
               Commissioner for Wales from August 2018 to August 2024, considered that the
               decision to restrict visiting “did not take account of the harm to health and wellbeing
               from older people being isolated”.27

      4.17.    Given the acute nature of the threat posed, with death likely for many infected
               residents, protection of life was paramount. Care home providers and policy-
               makers therefore had little choice but to be cautious and to impose the restrictions,
               notwithstanding the hugely damaging impact on residents, their loved ones and staff.
               The Inquiry accepts that the initial decisions taken to restrict visits were rational and
               broadly appropriate in principle. Indeed, some loved ones of care home residents
               told the Inquiry’s listening exercise, Every Story Matters, that they recognised that
               the restrictions were necessary.


                     “None of us knew what to do, so they were very strict and had they not been,
                       we would have lost more lives.”28

                     Loved one of a care home resident, Scotland, Every Story Matters


      4.18.    However, whether restrictions on visiting remained appropriate, as knowledge of the
               virus and the impact of continued restrictions on residents developed, is considered
               further below.

=== PAGE 128 ===
                                                                Chapter 4: Visiting restrictions in care homes




A greater understanding of risk and harms
4.19.    In the summer of 2020, testing capacity expanded and a greater understanding of
         the impact of the visiting restrictions developed. At this point, the UK was emerging
         from the first lockdown, with rates of infection decreasing and a loosening of societal
         restrictions.29 Visiting restrictions were reviewed and amended in an attempt to
         enable more visiting, resulting in:

         • a move to a more localised approach, with a focus on infection rates in the
             relevant area and risk assessments in individual care homes;30
         • the identification of individual named, designated or essential visitors; and                   31


         • the consideration and facilitation of other forms of visits, including the use
             of technology or physical barriers for in-person visits.32


The move to a localised approach
4.20. The initial restrictions had proceeded on the basis that “all footfall” in care homes
      “brought risk with it”.33 From June 2020, growing concerns about the impact of
      continued and prolonged isolation on residents and a better understanding of the
      risk posed by visitors prompted a shift towards more localised, individual, risk-based
      decision-making and staged reintroduction of visiting.34

4.21.    In Wales, from 25 June 2020, care home providers were encouraged to facilitate
         outdoor visiting, with an emphasis on individual needs and the minimisation of
         harms.35 Scottish guidance of the same date recommended that directors of
         public health assess the appropriateness of visiting by considering the wider risk
         environment and that each care home should conduct an individual risk assessment
         against an overarching framework.36 Guidance in Northern Ireland published on
         30 June 2020 placed responsibility on managers to permit visiting based on factors
         such as the care home’s ability to maintain social distance, safety and regional
         hospital capacity. During an outbreak, visiting was only permitted in exceptional
         circumstances.37

=== PAGE 129 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




  4.22. In England, changes to visiting restrictions were introduced by the end of July 2020.
        On 3 July 2020, the Department of Health and Social Care published the high-level
        findings of the Vivaldi 1 study, which considered Covid-19 infection in care homes.38
        The findings indicated that staff were a point of entry for Covid-19 and played an
        important role in transmission of infection.39 According to Professor Hall, it could be
        inferred from this that allowing family visiting may not lead to a substantial increase
        in risk.40

  4.23. This was reflected in ministerial and scientific advice. On 8 July 2020, a departmental
        submission to Helen Whately MP (Minister of State for Social Care at the Department
        of Health and Social Care from February 2020 to September 2021) noted anecdotal
        evidence of residents “fading away”.41 The submission also noted some evidence of
        the negative impact of restrictions and the psychological benefits of visits.42 Scientific
        advice considered that, as the prevalence of Covid-19 fell in the community, the
        benefits of visits would outweigh the risks.43 This led to guidance issued on 22 July
        2020 in England, which set out principles for a localised approach and the need for
        a dynamic risk assessment by directors of public health. The guidance also included
        principles for care homes to apply in developing policies for visits, including limiting
        visits to a single visitor.44

  4.24. By mid-September 2020, there was no “strong evidence” of visitors posing a
        particularly high risk of transmission of Covid-19, and evidence did not suggest such
        visits were a dominant cause of outbreaks in care homes.45 Modelling estimated
        that allowing visiting would have a “marginal impact” (a median increase of one
        additional case in an outbreak of about 30 cases in a medium-sized care home).46
        Research indicated that the risk presented by loneliness and isolation to residents
        was “likely an order of magnitude higher” than that posed by the risk of infection.47
        Accordingly, UK government advisers supported limited visits to be guided by
        directors of public health and also by risk assessments.48

=== PAGE 130 ===
                                                                  Chapter 4: Visiting restrictions in care homes




4.25. During the autumn and winter of 2020, guidance across the UK began to place
      greater emphasis on the promotion of visits and the avoidance of an ‘across the
      board’ prohibition or restrictions on visiting that applied without exception or
      consideration of individual circumstances – often referred to as ‘blanket bans’.49
      For example, in Northern Ireland, guidance outlined a variety of possible visiting
      arrangements dependent upon local infection rates.50

4.26. In England, a significant update was made on 1 December 2020, when guidance
      advised that the starting point should be one of supporting and enabling visits where
      safe to do so, with testing prior to entry.51 Guidance issued in Wales on 23 December
      2020 similarly encouraged the facilitation of visits wherever possible.52

4.27.    Nonetheless, specific concerns were raised in relation to the use of blanket bans.
         In a November 2020 survey conducted by John’s Campaign, 41% of respondents
         reported complete bans on visitors in care homes.53 On 16 December 2020, the Care
         Quality Commission advised that blanket bans would be treated as a risk indicator
         of a closed culture, investigated and inspected if necessary.54 It concluded after
         investigation that none of the 27 concerns raised with it were “genuine” blanket
         bans, noting that the majority were the product of miscommunication or a lack of
         understanding about restrictions in the event of an outbreak.55

4.28. Whether or not there were many blanket bans in existence, towards the end of 2020
      visiting still remained restricted in many care homes. This could be attributed to:

         • the underlying emphasis in much of the guidance that visiting was “the exception
             not the rule … with alternatives sought wherever possible”;56
         • local or institutional circumstances, such as the ability of care homes to facilitate
             visiting, infection prevention and control measures and the prevalence of
             outbreaks;57

  4.29. As the Covid-19 pandemic continued into 2021, each nation in the UK sought to
        introduce ‘overarching frameworks’ to ensure a return to visiting arrangements
        equivalent to those prior to the pandemic. This was against the backdrop of the
        rollout of the vaccine, with prioritisation of residents in a care home for older adults
        and their carers, and a greater understanding of managing risk. There was also
        increased availability of both testing and PPE.61

  4.30. Each of the four governments considered the localised approach with delegated
        decision-making to be the appropriate model, with local public health and care
        providers best placed to assess risk and residents’ needs.62 Given the diverse nature
        of the sector and the variations in the care home estate, the localised approach was
        understandable. A system that allows for variation, depending on local infection
        rates or individual risk in specific care homes, is significantly more balanced and
        proportionate than blanket bans or restrictions imposed at a national level. However,

          the localised approach led to variation in guidance and visiting policies across the

Interpretation of guidance

4.31.     While a degree of variation in visiting restrictions was inevitable, there were
          significant concerns that the guidance was being inconsistently interpreted and
          applied.

4.32. Some care homes continued to restrict visiting as a result of overly restrictive
      interpretations of guidance and concerns about the impact of further transmission of
      Covid-19 into care homes on the welfare of residents.64 Others considered that the
      measures introduced to facilitate visiting, as discussed below, were costly, unrealistic
      or required significant amounts of resource and time.65 Care Home Relatives
      Scotland told the Inquiry that restrictions became “a complete postcode lottery
      of care … Some care home providers based in England would apply the English
      guidance within their Scottish homes.”66

4.33. The variations in restrictions – justified or not – caused much distress, particularly
      for end-of-life visits.67 Some care homes interpreted ‘end of life’ very narrowly to
      mean within hours or less of impending death, and some providers refused visits
      – depriving residents of the opportunity of being with loved ones and saying final
      goodbyes.68 The impact upon residents and their loved ones was devastating.
      End-of-life visits should be facilitated in all but the most truly exceptional of
      circumstances.


               “[The grief] breaks our hearts as we feel he was deserted at the very time he
                 needed us.”69

                Bereaved family member of a care home resident, Wales, Every Story
                Matters

=== PAGE 133 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      4.34. Conflict between national, local and individual care home guidance and policies also
            produced and exacerbated inconsistencies and frustrations and undermined the
            ability of care home managers to take localised individual decisions. Nadra Ahmed,
            Executive Co-Chair of the National Care Association from 2001, explained:

                  “The national Public Health England (PHE) guidelines often conflicted with local
                   health authority interpretations, leaving care providers confused about which
                   advice to follow. Some local authorities imposed stricter rules than national
                   guidance, while others were more lenient. This inconsistency caused difficulties
                   in implementing a uniform approach to visitation, as care homes had to navigate
                   conflicting advice on what precautions (such as PPE use, handwashing, or
                   ventilation) should be prioritised.”70

      4.35. The multitude of guidance and policies that was applicable at any one time also
            resulted in uncertainty and confusion among recipients of care, visitors, providers,
            local government and public health bodies.71 Announcements of changes in visiting
            guidance, with subsequent delays in issuing the underlying guidance, resulted
            in a mismatch between public understanding of the situation and the action
            that care providers were instructed to take.72 This caused distress and created
            additional pressures on providers.73 The frequent updating of guidance also made
            implementation difficult.74

      4.36. It is inevitable that guidance will be amended as circumstances change and
            knowledge develops, and that the localised approach and the use of discretion
            may well result in different approaches being taken to visiting. There is, therefore,
            a need for decision-makers and care home providers to have the benefit of clear
            guidance and principles to ensure that discretion is exercised fairly and any variation
            is justifiable.

      4.37.   In a future pandemic or health emergency, the UK government and devolved
              administrations must provide guidance and principles relating to visiting care
              home residents that is as clear and consistent as possible and publicly available.
              There must also be a concerted effort to ensure better communication between
              government and local and individual providers in order to avoid the uncertainty and
              confusion experienced during the Covid-19 pandemic.

Alternative forms of visiting
4.38. By April 2020, guidance across the UK sought to encourage alternatives to in-person
      visiting.75 Some providers were resourceful and innovative in developing alternative
      forms of facilitating visiting.76 These alternatives were not a replacement for in-
      person visits but were preferable to no visits. However, many took time to develop
      and they were not suitable for all.

4.39. Visits conducted through a window presented challenges in terms of the ability of
      residents to hear or understand their loved ones.77 Guidance encouraged the use
      of Perspex screens or physical barriers to enable visitors and residents to be in the
      same room, but this was dependent on the layout of the care home.78 Where the
      use of screens either in the home or in a bespoke pod was possible, the evidence
      of impact was mixed. Described to Every Story Matters as a “blessing” for some
      residents and visitors, for others it caused distress, confusion and created the
      “impression of prison visiting”.79 Garden and outdoor visits, while cost-effective,
      were dependent on the weather, staff intensive and not always appropriate, with the
      requirement to be socially distanced sometimes leading to distress.80

4.40. Each of these measures presented challenges both for residents and for care
      providers. They could not cater appropriately for residents who were not well
      enough to leave their beds, had limited mobility, were susceptible to cold or had
      dementia, learning disabilities or cognitive decline.81 The ability to implement
      measures depended on facilities and the ability of care providers – who were under
      significant financial pressure – to pay for them.82

4.41.    Greater use of technology to enable remote visits with loved ones was promoted,
         with schemes and initiatives introduced to ensure that care homes were able to
         facilitate digital contact.83 Again, this was not suitable for all. Some residents and
         visitors did not have access to the required technology or had cognitive and sensory
         impairments, including poor eyesight and hearing. For them, the effective use of
         technology was sometimes difficult or impossible.84 Establishing consistent and

           sufficient internet connection was also challenging in some care homes, due to their
           architecture, age or size of the estate.85


                “[H]ow does a 90-year-old know how to use an iPad and talk to you on a

  4.42. Not all care homes could facilitate remote visits or do so while maintaining privacy.87
        Remote alternatives placed a significant administrative burden on staff.88 Many
        residents required staff support, which prompted concerns about contact being
        supervised.89

  4.43. The experience of remote calls led to some residents becoming distressed. However,
        some relatives found video calls easier to handle emotionally than window visits or
        in-person visits without touch.90 Others reported that the experience of video calls
        or watching their loved one decline via a screen was “deeply distressing”.91

  4.44. The use of technology was useful in supporting social contact and in mitigating
        some of the harms of isolation.92 It may be the only tool available in future
        pandemics. However, it undoubtedly had significant limitations.93 As with other
        alternatives, remote contact is no substitute for in-person, face-to-face visits with
        physical contact and the benefits these bring to residents. In-person visits should
        be prioritised where possible.

  care partners

        also evolved to allow a specified relative, family member or friend to visit in order to
        perform an enhanced caring role. This role was defined and referred to differently
        across the UK, with terms such as ‘care partner’ or ‘essential care giver’ being

         used.94 This Report adopts the term ‘essential carer’. This role allowed for support
         of vulnerable residents in everyday tasks – such as eating, drinking, washing, taking
         medication and exercising – to overcome social isolation.95

4.46. The essential carer role, or that of a single named essential visitor, had been
      discussed and contemplated at the outset of the initial restrictions in England and
      Scotland but not introduced.96 In England, this was because clinical guidance at the
      time focused on the importance of reducing footfall in care homes.97 In Scotland,
      guidance published on 13 March 2020 suggested that less-frequent visiting by a
      named relative could be considered. However, visiting was subsequently suspended
      on 26 March 2020 and only permitted in exceptional circumstances – for example,
      essential visits for those with dementia who were experiencing distress.98

4.47.    The essential carer role was eventually incorporated into the guidance across all
         four nations. Guidance in Scotland dated 25 June 2020 introduced “essential”
         and “designated” visitors, with designated visitors being allowed to visit residents
         outdoors or indoors depending on the wider societal restrictions.99 Advice on
         15 September 2021 provided for a named nominated visitor who, with agreement,
         could provide care.100 Welsh guidance dated 28 August 2020 allowed residents
         to nominate a designated visitor and a deputy who could visit indoors, provided
         the care home was not experiencing a Covid-19 outbreak.101 Guidance published
         in Northern Ireland on 22 September 2020 recommended one face-to-face visit
         per week by one person and introduced the concept of a “care partner”, unless
         healthcare services were at a significant risk of being overwhelmed.102 From 8 March
         2021, guidance in England enabled every resident to nominate a “single named
         visitor” to have regular visits, subject to testing and infection prevention and control
         measures, and separately an “essential care giver” for some.103 This followed
         lobbying in November 2020 and February 2021 from organisations representing

                relatives, carers and providers, calling for the creation of a ‘key visitor’ or essential
                care giver role.104

      4.48. While limiting the number of essential carers put strain on family relationships and
            undoubtedly placed a significant responsibility on essential carers, allowing them
            to visit would have had a very positive impact on the residents and the care they
            received.105

      4.49. Tensions however arose from the operation of the guidance alongside wider societal
            restrictions in each nation. In September 2020, for example, soon after the essential
            carer role was introduced in Wales, broader legal restrictions led to the suspension
            of designated visits in many areas.106 Guidance in Scotland and Northern Ireland
            envisaged that the role could be suspended in times of outbreak or high levels of
            infection and transmission.107

      4.50. Later guidance in 2021 provided for an individualised assessment of risk at the care
            home level. For example, there was a presumption in England that essential carer
            visits should continue in the event of an outbreak and Scottish guidance allowed a
            named visitor to visit a resident in their room during a managed Covid-19 outbreak.108

      4.51.     Some guidance lacked clarity about which residents could have an essential carer
                and what the role entailed. For example:

                • The March 2021 guidance in England stated that an “essential care giver” was
                   available to “residents with the highest care needs”, without any further
                   definition.109
                • The Independent Care Homes Scotland group explained: “What might be an
                   ‘essential visit’ or ‘meaningful contact’ in one care home might not be the same
                   in another.”110
                • Professor Fu-Meng Khaw, National Director of Health Protection and Screening
                   Services and Executive Medical Director of Public Health Wales from June 2021,
                   accepted that the key role of family members as care givers or essential carers
                   was not “clearly defined in the initial stages”.111

                                                                  Chapter 4: Visiting restrictions in care homes




         • Similar challenges relating to the definition of a ‘care partner’ were reported
             by the Belfast Health and Social Care trust, which explained that it was open
             to interpretation by care home managers, resulting in confusion.112

4.52. There were also complaints in Northern Ireland about inconsistent implementation
      of the guidance and, separately, from providers of care who considered that they
      were not able to facilitate implementation of the care partner scheme safely, or that
      it was an unreasonable demand. This led to “frustration” on the part of Robin Swann
      (Minister of Health for Northern Ireland from January 2020 to October 2022 and
      from February to May 2024). Further guidance on the role was issued in November
      2020 following further consultation, with measures put in place to improve
      implementation.113

4.53. In a future pandemic, visits from essential carers should be available to all residents
      where possible and should be distinct from considerations regarding general visiting.
      There was a failure during the Covid-19 pandemic to implement this as soon as
      possible. Had there been better engagement with staff, care home residents and
      loved ones in pandemic planning, the UK government and devolved administrations
      would have better understood, at the outset of the pandemic, the vital role of family
      members and loved ones as essential carers. Even if uncertainty about transmission
      and risks in the early part of the pandemic meant immediate introduction of the
      role was not possible, pre-pandemic planning might have allowed for clarification of
      the role, quicker implementation at an appropriate time, and may have significantly
      reduced the harm caused by isolation.

4.54. In March 2022, the Scottish Health and Social Care Standards (which set out what
      people should expect when using health, social care or social work services in
      Scotland) were amended to allow residents to nominate relatives or friends to
      visit in person and to be involved in care during an outbreak.114 The Care Reform
      (Scotland) Act 2025, known as ‘Anne’s Law’, is now in force in Scotland, along with
      accompanying regulations and a statutory code of practice. Among other visiting
      provisions discussed below, Anne’s Law requires care homes to identify at least one
      person as an ‘essential care supporter’ with a legal presumption that suspension of
      such visits is likely to cause serious harm to the resident’s health or wellbeing.115 The
      document entitled Anne’s Law Code of Practice explains:

      4.55. This legal duty to identify an essential care supporter for every care home resident
            in Scotland is a positive step. The UK government, Welsh Government and Northern
            Ireland Executive should consider the introduction of equivalent guidance or
            regulatory duties and standards.


      Healthcare visits
      4.56. While the initial restrictions did not prevent healthcare professionals from entering
            care homes, UK-wide guidance strongly discouraged in-person routine or ad hoc
            visits unless they were essential.117 Primary care GPs, nurses and other healthcare
            professionals reduced the frequency with which they visited people in care homes
            (and at home).118 For example:

               • Between 25 March 2020 and 30 June 2020, the Welsh Government stated
                   that GPs did not have to conduct routine visits, patient reviews within 28 days
                   of admission, unscheduled care or medication reviews, and were to reduce all
                   non-essential visits.119
               • The Public Health Agency (Northern Ireland) reported that during the first wave
                   of the pandemic, 42% of residents’ needs were not met or only partially met by
                   external healthcare professionals.120


                     “I was refused in-person GP appointments for a period of over two years.”121

                      Person with care and support needs, Wales, Every Story Matters

               varied success. The Care Inspectorate in Scotland and Healthcare Improvement
               Scotland carried out targeted inspections of care homes, which considered the
               physical, wellbeing and healthcare needs of residents from May 2020, but clinical
               support was still not provided consistently.122 Only 4 of 29 local authorities in

         Scotland reported that visits by healthcare professionals had not been limited
         between March 2020 and June 2022.123 In contrast, by June 2020, providers in
         Wales described an improving situation, with increased in-person visits. Enhanced
         services (including weekly ward rounds, multidisciplinary teams and patient reviews)
         were directed in July 2020 – although the Inquiry heard in relation to one Welsh care
         home that it continued to face challenges getting a GP to attend in person in 2021.124

4.58. An acceleration of some measures contained in the long-term NHS Enhanced Health
      in Care Homes programme was announced in England in May 2020 to provide
      support to care homes with the Covid-19 response. This provided for: weekly
      ‘check‑ins’ of care homes, personalised care and support plans for care home
      residents, remote monitoring of residents with suspected or confirmed Covid-19, and
      the identification of a named clinical lead.125 From 1 October 2020, further measures
      were introduced, including a more comprehensive requirement to carry out weekly
      ‘home rounds’ for care home residents.126 There was, however, significant local
      variation to implementation, with some areas reporting it working well and others
      less so.127

4.59. Ms Whately recalled receiving reports showing that having access to a named GP
      “didn’t make a difference with some care homes … so I suspect that there was
      variability in how it was implemented”.128

4.60. Technology was also used to facilitate access to healthcare, with a significant
      proportion of healthcare consultations being carried out remotely during
      the pandemic.129 Some care homes reported positive experiences of remote
      consultations, particularly with regard to medication requests or in reducing the risks
      of transmission from in-person visits.130 Remote consultations, however, were not
      suitable or appropriate for all patients.131 For some residents, remote consultations
      were challenging and affected their mental health as they struggled to engage,
      became frustrated or considered that they were not being heard, understood or

               seen.132 Much of the burden fell on care workers to facilitate remote GP consultations,
               to explain symptoms over the phone, and sometimes to provide treatment.133 Care
               home workers described feeling as though they had taken on the role of doctors
               and nurses, without the proper training or necessary qualifications.134 One care
               home manager noted the resultant lack of proper assessment and subsequent
               impact on diagnosis and treatment: “We were taking basic observations … but we
               were not able to listen to someone’s chest, a key observation in diagnosing a chest

      4.61.    Similarly, Professor Banerjee explained the importance of in-person multidisciplinary
               assessments for people with dementia. The absence of assessments meant that:

                    “[T]here was a cohort of individuals … whose diagnosis was missed, and we still
                      are reaping the problems that come from that, and a cohort of people whose
                      diagnosis was delayed.”136

               As a result, it was likely that less accurate, lower-quality diagnoses were made.137

      4.62. Residents also had other conditions that were difficult to manage without in-
            person specialist care and treatment or rehabilitative support.138 For example, visits
            from district and specialist nurses, podiatrists and physiotherapists were severely
            disrupted during the pandemic, as many professionals were redeployed to work in
            hospitals or critical care, and there was no adequate remote alternative.139

      4.63. Many residents experienced a deterioration in their physical and mental health.
            The nature and severity of health conditions worsened as activity and movement
            decreased and there was less or unavailable support, diagnosis and treatment,
            including pain relief.140

      4.64. There were potentially avoidable hospital admissions resulting from GPs not being
            “prepared to visit a care home”.141 This placed even greater pressures on those
            providing care and the hospitals.142 Where they were unable to access healthcare
            services, staff and residents reported feeling “unsupported and exposed”.143

4.65. Planning for a future pandemic should facilitate continued access to healthcare,
      except in truly exceptional circumstances. Visiting policies should also allow for
      healthcare professionals to visit and include practical measures to minimise the risk
      of transmission by the provision of adequate PPE. While remote consultations may
      be appropriate in some circumstances, these should not replace in-person visits
      where these can be facilitated without undue risk.

4.66. On 6 April 2024, the Care Quality Commission’s fundamental standard on visiting
      was introduced in England (known as regulation 9A). The provisions mandate
      that, even during exceptional circumstances like a pandemic, visits by healthcare
      professionals and social workers should be permitted if a delay would be detrimental
      to the resident.144 The Inquiry welcomes this change and encourages its adoption
      across the other nations of the UK.


Visiting out
4.67.    Leaving care homes for social visits or healthcare appointments (known as ‘visiting
         out’) is also an important part of residents’ lives.145 Explicit guidance on visits out,
         however, was not issued in Scotland until September 2020, and in England, Wales
         and Northern Ireland until December 2020.146

4.68. The issue had been raised earlier in England, with Public Health England advising
      the Department of Health and Social Care in July 2020 that enabling visits out
      would require further consideration given the different level of risks posed.147 This
      was followed on 6 August 2020 by further advice with a recommendation to publish
      visiting out guidance as soon as possible.148

4.69. Throughout 2020, the applicable guidance across the four nations required residents
      to isolate for 14 days upon their return to care homes. With the exception of the
      Scottish guidance, the requirement for isolation was also included in the specific
      visiting out guidance when introduced, with no consideration of individual needs,
      circumstances or risk.149

=== PAGE 143 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      4.70.     The lack of guidance resulted in an assumption on the part of some care providers
                that visiting out was not permitted. In England, this had the consequence that some
                residents “did not leave their care homes or even their rooms for months on end”,
                with a severe impact upon their mental and physical health.150 Even when guidance
                expressly included reference to visiting out, the wider societal restrictions in place
                throughout the winter of 2020/21 combined with ongoing restrictive visiting out
                guidance meant that residents remained confined to their care homes.151

      4.71.     The effect of a lack of visiting out was described by an anonymous caller to the Care
                Rights UK advice line in April 2021:

                    “Grandma has not been able to leave her care home for over a year now.
                      She has dementia and prior to the lockdown she was very fit and active.
                      She just wants to go for a walk with us. Although she is not one to complain,
                      she tells me ‘they won’t let me out, it’s like a prison’. She asks me ‘will I be stuck
                      in here for the rest of my life?’ saying she would rather end it all now.
                     I can’t bring myself to take Grandma out knowing she would have to endure the
                      suffering of isolation afterwards. When the home had an outbreak, she had to
                      quarantine in her room, which had a grave impact on her mental wellbeing.
                      She became withdrawn and took to her bed, seeing no reason to get up.”152

      4.72. The Inquiry recognises that the situation was evolving and that the protection of all
            residents of care homes was a primary concern. However, the delay in providing
            effective guidance on visits out caused great distress and harm to residents and their
            loved ones. In a future pandemic, guidance must address such visits from the outset,
            with clear and appropriate advice on balancing the risk of infection against risk of
            harm.

      4.73.     The Inquiry notes that in England and Wales, regulations introduced on 6 April 2024
                provide that people residing in care homes must not be discouraged from taking
                visits out of the care home.153 Similar support and provision is made within Anne’s
                Law, the regulations and Code of Practice which apply to visits to and by care home
                residents in Scotland.154 This is to be welcomed. The Northern Ireland Executive
                should introduce equivalent duties on adult care home providers.

      Scottish Government, 31 March 2026 (https://www.gov.scot/publications/annes-law-code-practice-care-home-services-visits-care-
      home-residents-scotland-regulations-2026-31-march-2026/documents; INQ000660427)

=== PAGE 144 ===
                                                                   Chapter 4: Visiting restrictions in care homes




Post-pandemic developments in respect of visiting
4.74.     The fundamental role played by visitors has subsequently been recognised and
          reflected in regulations and guidance across the UK.

4.75. In Scotland, Anne’s Law has ensured that visits can only be suspended where
      essential to prevent a risk to life, health or wellbeing.155 Where visiting has been
      suspended, care homes are under an obligation to take all reasonable steps to
      eliminate or substantially mitigate the risk so that visits can resume as soon as
      practicable. Visiting must be allowed to continue at end of life, where a resident
      has suffered a significant deterioration in physical or mental condition, or where
      suspension is causing, or is likely to cause, serious harm to the resident’s health or
      wellbeing, and that harm outweighs the risk. Care homes are required to notify the
      Care Inspectorate when they suspend visits into or out of the home.156

4.76.     In England and Wales, regulations were introduced on 6 April 2024, to ensure that
          visits must be facilitated for people receiving care or treatment in a care home,
          hospital or hospice, unless there are exceptional circumstances (regulation 9A
          of The Health and Social Care Act 2008 (Regulated Activities) Regulations 2014).157
          Breaches may result in regulatory action including loss of registration.158

4.77.     Guidance produced by the UK Health Security Agency in April 2024, in the context
          of regulation 9A, sets out the following guiding principles:

          • contact with relatives and friends is “fundamental to care home residents’ health
             and wellbeing”;159
          • restrictions should only be imposed if a visit would “pose a significant risk to the
             health or wellbeing” of residents “which cannot be mitigated”;160
          • any such restrictions should be “time limited, proportionate to each specific
             outbreak and risk based”; and
          • the wellbeing of residents should be prioritised when visiting restrictions are
             contemplated.161

      Module 6: Care sector




      4.78. In March 2026, the Department of Health and Social Care published a review
            of regulation 9A of The Health and Social Care Act 2008 (Regulated Activities)
            Regulations 2014. The review noted that the regulation has had a “positive effect
            in clarifying expectations for providers, reinforced best practice and prompted
            organisations to review their processes”.162 The review did, however, identify that:

                    “[M]ore needs to be done to address gaps in awareness and understanding,
                      variation in decision making, and weaknesses in monitoring and enforcement
                      to ensure that the intentions of Regulation 9A are fully realised in practice.”163

               The regulation is a welcome first step but requires further work, which the UK
               government has already recognised.

      4.79.    More work is also necessary in Northern Ireland. The Care Standards for Nursing
               and Residential Homes (dated December 2022) provide that “residents can have
               visitors at any reasonable time”.164 The language of a ‘reasonable time’ is likely to be
               too vague to assist in a future pandemic. The Northern Ireland Executive should, as
               a minimum, introduce equivalent duties to those in England, Scotland and Wales for
               adult care home providers.165


      The impact of visiting restrictions
      4.80. Visiting restrictions had immediate and, in some cases, devastating effects on
            residents, their families and those providing care. Professor Vic Rayner, Chief
            Executive Officer of the National Care Forum from 2016, told the Inquiry:

                    “[I]solation caused by restrictions on visits from loved ones is intrinsically harmful
                      and we have heard over and over again the extreme anguish this caused … for
                      staff, families and residents.”166

               The Technical Report on the COVID-19 Pandemic in the UK – produced in 2022 by
               the UK’s Chief and Deputy Chief Medical Officers, the UK Chief Scientific Adviser
               and the National Medical Director, NHS England – recorded that visiting restrictions

Impact on residents
4.81.    The impact of visiting restrictions for weeks and months on residents was profound,
         causing considerable trauma and long-term ramifications.168 For many, the absence
         of visits from relatives meant that the care home no longer felt like their home.169
         Residents and relatives alike talked about care homes becoming prisons.170 A
         profound sense of isolation developed, with some residents reporting that they felt
         abandoned.171

4.82. The Inquiry heard evidence that residents living in care homes thrived on the routine
      of visits.172 The implementation of visiting restrictions resulted in disruption, confusion
      and distress, particularly for those residents who could not understand why the
      restrictions were in place.173 For some residents, this manifested in challenging or
      unusual behaviour, particularly for people with dementia and people with learning
      disabilities.174


              “They were very up and down emotionally. Very confused. Didn’t really
                understand … if one was, like, stressed, or off their baseline, it would then
                antagonise the other one and they would become stressed. Then it was
                about trying to calm them down and stuff.”175

              Support worker, Every Story Matters


4.83. Alternative forms of visiting also presented their own challenges and caused
      distress.176

4.86. As a result, family members expressed a feeling of lost time and connection with
      their relatives who had dementia.183 Professor Banerjee told the Inquiry that it was
      likely that had people with dementia retained access to their main family carer,
      their quality of life would have been better, and the decline in their illness may have
      been mitigated. He explained that they would have been happier, more content
      and ultimately required less care.184 This observation applies more broadly to all
      residents.

4.87.    The relationship between visits and emotional and physical health was encapsulated
         by the experience of one contributor to Every Story Matters of visiting their relative.


              “She could not understand why she could only see me only through the
                window … she stopped eating because she was depressed by life without
                visitors, and very brief care visits from the staff.”185

               Bereaved family member of a care home resident, Scotland, Every Story
               Matters


4.88. Professor Chris Hatton and Professor Richard Hastings, expert witnesses on
      learning disabilities, explained that feelings of abandonment for people with learning
      disabilities were exacerbated because there was “no sense of when things might
      ever come back”.186 They also described how “people’s worlds became incredibly
      small”, resulting in a long-lasting impact on residents with learning disabilities and a
      reduction in self-reported quality of life.187

4.89. When visits were reinstated, relatives were often shocked at the extent of the
      deterioration in their loved ones. Out of respondents to a survey conducted by
      John’s Campaign, 80% noted deterioration in their relatives’ physical or mental
      health.188 The resumption of in-person visits also resulted in distress.

      Impact on families and loved ones
      4.90. Restrictions also affected family members and loved ones. Feelings of guilt at being
            unable to visit and support relatives were common.190


                     “My mother’s death in the care home – alone and confused about why her
                       only child appeared to have abandoned her. It absolutely kills me to think
                       about it. I felt, and still feel, angry, powerless and distraught.”191

                     Daughter of a care home resident, England, Every Story Matters


      4.91.    In some cases, the limited cognitive abilities and lack of understanding of some
               residents meant that they “simply did not understand the significance of what was
               going on”, and so the restrictions affected family and loved ones more keenly than
               the resident.192

      4.92. Where visits were permitted, they were often limited to a short period of time,
            which contributed to anxiety and upset and prevented visitors from performing their
            essential caring role.193 As Jane Wier-Wierzbowska, a member of Covid 19 Bereaved
            Families for Justice UK, said about her mother:

                    “It was heartbreaking not to be able to touch her and hold her and be able to
                     explain what was happening. I could see an escalation of her deterioration …
                     [it was] deeply distressing.”194

               Ms Wier-Wierzbowska described her experience as “an open-ended trauma, a
               wound that won’t close”.195

4.93. Physical barriers also caused distress to some family members.


               “Seeing my relative in this room divided by a Perspex screen was just
                 dreadful, not being able to physically touch him, or speak to him properly.
                 It was just distressing. It was almost worse than not seeing him at all.”196

               Loved one of a care home resident, England, Every Story Matters


4.94. Professor Banerjee stated that visiting restrictions led to a “growth of anger in family
      carers” who felt that they had missed out on important and limited quality time
      with their loved ones.197 Family carers also reported a loss of trust in the care home
      system and the government.198 The loss of trust was in part because of concerns that
      hidden harms, abuse and deterioration in a resident’s condition would go unnoticed,
      with a lack of reassurance that measures were in place to identify these issues.199
      This was compounded in some circumstances by a lack of communication from the
      care home as to their relatives’ wellbeing, with updates sometimes being limited to
      generalities that the resident was “‘okay’ or ‘comfortable’”.200 However, examples
      of good practice were also noted, such as online portals (where photos could be
      shared and updates provided) or messaging platforms – as are typically used by
      early years care providers and schools.201 Where visiting restrictions are necessary
      in the future, it is essential that communication between residents, providers and
      relatives is facilitated to provide reassurance.


Impact on people providing care
4.95. The imposition of strict visiting restrictions also significantly affected those providing
      care to residents in a care home.

4.96. Staff took on an enhanced role with additional responsibilities, alongside caring for
      residents who were mentally and physically affected by the restrictions.202 For many,
      relationships developed beyond the normal carer role in the absence of visits from
      relatives.

            stricter lockdowns of the care home. Some families expressed frustration about
            the delayed implementation of government-announced changes to visiting policies
            while care homes worked out logistics and risk assessments. She explained that “the
            tough work of mediation” fell on care workers.208

            and burnout”.209

=== PAGE 152 ===
                                                                Chapter 4: Visiting restrictions in care homes




Planning for a future pandemic
Balancing of harms
4.100. The introduction and continuation of visiting restrictions involved balancing the need
       to protect vulnerable residents – from the potentially fatal risk of Covid-19 infection
       – with the impact of restrictions on residents, their families and care workers, as set
       out above.210

4.101. Views about restrictions among residents, their relatives and staff varied. Some
       people considered that visits to care homes should not take place due to the risks
       of infection. Others considered that the harms caused by the resulting isolation were
       too great and outweighed the benefits.211 The views of someone approaching the
       end of life might be very different from a young person with care needs.

4.102. Many witnesses acknowledged that it was difficult to balance the benefits and harms
       of visiting restrictions and noted that there was no ideal solution.212 As subsequently
       noted in the Technical Report on the COVID-19 Pandemic in the UK, reducing the risk
       of transmission of Covid-19 in care homes “involved some of the most complex trade-
       offs of risk to individuals of any part of the pandemic”.213

4.103. The overwhelming imperative at the outset of the pandemic was to protect life.
       This necessitated the introduction of the visiting restrictions. It was, however, clear
       from at least early April 2020 that visiting restrictions were likely to be required for
       prolonged and unprecedented periods of time.214

4.104. As the Covid-19 pandemic progressed, for many, the harms caused by the absence
       of visits outweighed the potential risks of infection. The risk of harm from restricting
       visits and consequent isolation was obvious from the outset, not least because
       isolation and loneliness were known and established risk factors in mortality.215
       The extent of that harm was however significantly worsened by the length of
       time that visiting restrictions were necessary. Much of this harm could have been
       mitigated with planning and innovation to enable a swifter introduction of remote,
       window and outdoor visits, and the use of temporary structures and practical
       adaptations in care homes (such as screens).

INQ000613603_0198-199 para 672; INQ000587254_0064-0065 para 237; Vaughan Gething 15 July 2025 80/14-21;
