=== PAGE 153 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      4.105. The pandemic has laid bare the importance of visiting and the catastrophic impact
             of visiting restrictions for many. It has also highlighted the need for clear guidance
             from the UK government and devolved administrations as to the principles to be
             applied in deciding whether to introduce visiting restrictions. The absence of such
             guidance resulted in inconsistencies across care homes and placed care homes in
             invidious positions in seeking to balance the risk to their residents, causing inevitable
             confusion and distress.

      4.106. Had there been detailed guidance setting out the core principles governing visiting
             restrictions from the outset, the difficulties encountered might have been more
             readily managed, with the result that less harm was caused.


      Guidance and decision-making
      4.107. In a future public health emergency, decision-makers should start from the
             presumption that the harm caused by anything other than short-term isolation to
             residents may well be as significant, grave and devastating as the risks posed by the
             infection. Visiting guidance should be issued that considers the impact of isolation
             on those living in care homes, particularly on people with dementia, cognitive
             and communication disabilities, and on those at end of life. The guidance should
             be created with input from government departments, healthcare bodies, local
             authorities and those impacted by the guidance.

      4.108. It should set out the guiding principles and provide advice based on the type of
             pathogen and level of transmission and infection (when available), along with other
             relevant factors such as availability of PPE, testing and vaccines. Guidance and
             restrictions should be reviewed regularly at national, local and care home levels and
             they should be relaxed as soon as circumstances allow.

      4.109. Guidance should draw a distinction between more general, in-person visits and
             those providing essential care. Visits by essential carers should be maintained
             throughout any pandemic, unless the circumstances make it impossible or the risk is
             simply too great. Where possible, in-person visits with wider family and loved ones
             should also be planned for, facilitated and promoted. The individual circumstances
             of each care home mean that workable alternatives to in-person visiting should also
             be considered in advance.

      4.110. This guidance should also recognise that the decisions about visiting individual
             residents should be made by care home managers who are informed by the
             principles set out – alongside local guidance, including that from directors of public
             health or their equivalents. Decisions on how to facilitate visiting safely should rest
             with the individual care homes, who can take into account the circumstances of their
             settings as well as local infection rates. As the principles set out in the guidance
             issued on 5 March 2021 in England stated:



140

=== PAGE 154 ===
                                                                Chapter 4: Visiting restrictions in care homes




             “Each care home is unique in its physical layout, surrounding environment and
              facilities. Residents vary in their needs, health and current wellbeing. Care home
              managers are best placed to decide how their care home can best enable
              visiting in line with this guidance and in a way that meets the needs of their
              residents both individually and collectively.”216

4.111.   Finally, there is a need for greater understanding of the levels of risk and trade-offs
         that care home residents and their relatives are prepared to withstand to protect
         themselves. Some individuals may prefer to prioritise their quality of life and ability
         to be with their relatives over the enhanced (and, in some cases, extreme) risk of
         premature death or serious illness. Others may wish to preserve life at all costs.
         Care home providers should be prepared to have these conversations with residents
         and their loved ones, where appropriate, and ascertain wishes and feelings. Families
         and loved ones’ views may align or be different from those of the resident. Issues
         of capacity are likely to arise and create further complexities if such an exercise
         is embarked upon during a pandemic. The views of residents and relatives will
         inevitably not be determinative – not least given the wider risks to other vulnerable
         residents and staff in the care home and the likelihood of competing wishes in the
         same care home or the same family. Views may also change. They will nonetheless
         be an important factor to be weighed in the balance and must be taken into account.

4.112. The Inquiry recommends that clear guidance for the implementation of visiting
       restrictions in care homes in a future pandemic, taking into account all these factors,
       is developed and published by the UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh
       Government and Northern Ireland Executive.




216 Guidance on Care Home Visiting, Department of Health and Social Care, 5 March 2021 (INQ000325310_0003); see also
Claire Sutton 29 July 2025 47/16-48/23

=== PAGE 155 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




            Recommendation 4: Visiting restrictions
            The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
            Ireland Executive should publish guidance for the implementation of visiting
            restrictions in care homes in a future pandemic.
            The guidance should be led by the following core principles:

            • Measures applied should be the least restrictive possible, both in terms of
               severity and the length of time for which they apply.

            • Residents should have the right to in-person visitors, except in the most grave
               or exceptional of circumstances.

            • Restrictions should be decided upon and applied at the most local level
               possible, based on local risk assessments and considering individual
               circumstances.

            • Communications with the public, care home residents and loved ones must
               clearly explain the measures in place and their purpose.
            People who work in and use social care should be closely involved in developing
            this guidance.

Chapter 5: Regulation
and safeguarding

=== PAGE 157 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      Introduction
      5.1.      Ensuring that adults are enabled to live a life free from abuse or neglect “cannot be
                achieved by any single agency”.1 As the Local Government Association explained:
                “Safeguarding adults is everybody’s business. We all have a role to play in
                ensuring that everyone in our communities is treated with dignity and respect.”2
                Local authorities and councils, regulators, care providers and workers, healthcare
                professionals, families and friends provide complementary and overlapping
                oversight, enabling the identification, escalation and addressing of safeguarding
                concerns.

      5.2.      During the Covid-19 pandemic, the role of regulators in keeping adults who use
                social care safe came under some scrutiny. The regulators in each nation of the UK
                play a central role and share the same aims: to ensure the provision of safe, high-
                quality care, maintain standards and drive improvement.3

      5.3.      This chapter focuses on the decision taken by the regulators to suspend routine in-
                person inspections in care homes in March 2020 and the subsequent move to a risk-
                based inspection programme. It also considers the impact of the Covid-19 pandemic
                on registration and enforcement activities, and the extent to which regulators were
                successful in their core function of safeguarding.


      The social care regulators
      5.4.      Adult social care is independently regulated in each of the four nations of the UK:
                in England, by the Care Quality Commission;4 in Scotland, by the Care Inspectorate;5
                in Wales, by Care Inspectorate Wales;6 and in Northern Ireland, by the Regulation
                and Quality Improvement Authority.7 Although responsibilities vary between the
                regulators, each is responsible for regulation of care homes and providers of
                domiciliary care (care provided in people’s own homes).8

=== PAGE 158 ===
                                                                           Chapter 5: Regulation and safeguarding




5.5.      Regulation is achieved primarily through:

          • the use of robust registration processes;
          • ongoing assessment, which includes undertaking both routine and responsive
              (or intelligence-led) inspections alongside one another; and
          • clear, progressive and proportionate enforcement.
5.6.      Across the UK, registration serves as the foundation of regulation, requiring
          providers to meet specific standards and legal requirements before they can
          operate. Robust registration processes are therefore central to effective regulation,
          providing initial assurance and quality control of providers of regulated adult
          social care. Regulators may grant or refuse registration applications. To reflect the
          importance of ensuring safe, high-quality care, operating a care setting or service
          without registration with the relevant regulator is a criminal offence.9

5.7.      Inspections often form part of registration processes and are also conducted
          subsequently to assess ongoing compliance with regulations as well as the quality
          of care delivered. Inspections, whether routine or risk-based, gather evidence to
          evaluate whether providers meet the required standards and identify areas for
          improvement. Inspections are also used to determine whether enforcement actions –
          such as improvement notices, penalties or registration cancellations – are necessary
          to address risks and protect service users.10

5.8.      Enforcement is aimed at improving the quality of care services and safeguarding
          individuals from harm. It ensures that providers meet fundamental standards of
          quality and safety. Each regulator uses enforcement powers to address breaches
          of regulations and to hold providers accountable for failures that put users of care
          services at risk.11


Regulatory approaches during the pandemic
5.9.      As a consequence of the Covid-19 pandemic, and in accordance with its own
          priorities, each regulator significantly adapted its ordinary regulatory processes
          from March 2020.

=== PAGE 159 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      Northern Ireland
      5.10.    Regulation in Northern Ireland faced its own particular challenges. The Inquiry was
               told that the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority entered the Covid-19
               pandemic with significant challenges, including “dysfunctional” relations between
               its board members, its management team and the Department of Health (Northern
               Ireland).12 This dysfunctionality was compounded by the department’s decision on
               or about 18 March 2020 to redeploy the authority’s senior leadership staff to other
               areas of the Covid-19 response (initially temporarily, but none of them returned).13
               The authority explained that this had a significant impact on its work, in particular on
               its core functions of registration, regulation and inspection in relation to care homes
               and domiciliary care.14

      5.11.    On 18 June 2020, the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority’s board
               resigned, citing concerns about the conduct of the Department of Health (Northern
               Ireland).15 A subsequent independent report questioned the legal authority of
               the department to redeploy staff and found that, notwithstanding the unique
               circumstances brought about by the pandemic, the department’s actions “showed
               a lack of respect to the [authority] and its Board”.16 It concluded that the board’s
               resignation, along with the redeployment of senior staff, left the authority lacking the
               full strategic leadership and oversight to effectively manage its normal business.17
               The department’s actions undoubtedly undermined the authority’s independence
               and its ability to fulfil its statutory function in the time of a public health emergency.18


      Registration
      5.12.    During the pandemic, each regulator sought to be flexible in its approach to
               registration and to relax some of the pre-pandemic regulatory requirements.
               This was in order to support the adult social care sector in its Covid-19 response
               and enable additional capacity to be created without being delayed by the normal
               registration or variation processes. Registration provisions were altered, or a
               pragmatic approach was adopted, to allow temporary registration arrangements
               for new care services or to allow existing providers to vary their services.19 The aim
               of quicker registration was to increase the number of care providers and, in turn,
               increase the number of available bed spaces. Allowing variations to services meant

         a provider could expand the number and type of residents for whom it was allowed

5.13.    Despite these steps, the Inquiry heard some evidence that there were significant
         delays in registration.20 For example, in Northern Ireland, the redeployment of
         senior managerial staff (discussed above), as well as internal governance and
         staffing issues at the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority, had a material
         impact on registration in the early stages of the pandemic.21 The initial processing
         of applications was also slower, as processes to enable identities to be verified by
         video call took time to develop and implement.22

5.14.    During the pandemic, initial regulatory adjustments provided flexibility and support
         to help secure and expand capacity during the critical acute phase. Delays in
         registration, however, had implications for users of care who faced the risk of harm
         in the absence of available services.


Inspections
5.15.    Prior to the Covid-19 pandemic, each regulator undertook routine monitoring and
         inspection of adult social care. The regulators broadly followed the same processes
         for inspections, which involved information-collation and inspection-planning, the
         inspection itself, feedback and the publication of a report.23 Inspections took various
         forms, including full inspections (a comprehensive inspection evaluating all key
         areas), focused inspections (a targeted inspection typically triggered by identified
         concerns) and thematic inspections (examining a specific issue, such as medication
         management). Inspections were normally unannounced and in person.24

5.16.    Regulatory requirements provided for maximum intervals between inspections.
         Inspection intervals varied from annual inspections to between 6 and 30 months
         – depending on the regulator, the service being provided, and other factors such
         as the outcome of previous inspections, ratings and the length of registration.25
         In England, where care homes were previously rated as ‘good’ or ‘outstanding’,
         they were to be inspected within 30 months. For care homes rated ‘inadequate’,
         a follow-up inspection took place within 6 months, and newly registered services
         were typically inspected between 6 and 12 months from the date of registration.26
         In Wales, the timing of routine inspections depended on the nature of the service

               combined with an assessment of whether the service required a routine, early or
               priority inspection. Intervals ranged from 6 to 18 months.27 The regulatory regime
               in Northern Ireland provided for care homes to be inspected a minimum of twice
               within a 12-month period and domiciliary care agencies to be inspected annually.28
               Regulations in Scotland required annual inspections for care homes and support
               services providing personal care or domiciliary care.29

      5.17.    In addition to routine inspections, each regulator conducted responsive inspections
               based on intelligence and potential risk of harm, and services could be inspected at
               any time.30 Inspections supplemented other forms of reporting – for example, annual
               returns from providers to the Care Quality Commission.31

      Decisions to suspend routine inspections

      5.18.    In early March 2020, the Care Quality Commission in England and the Care
               Inspectorate in Scotland scaled back inspection activity, by cancelling or postponing
               planned and routine inspections, and by moving to a risk-based approach. These
               decisions came at a time when – given the pressures facing the health and social
               care sector and public health advice to minimise footfall in care homes – various
               organisations (predominantly healthcare organisations, including the British Medical
               Association and the NHS Confederation) were calling for a pause to inspections
               generally.32

      5.19.    Initially, it was anticipated that these measures would be temporary and time-
               limited.33 As at 10 March 2020, Care Inspectorate Wales intended to continue
               with planned inspections, although this was subject to daily review.34 In Northern
               Ireland, on 13 March, a direction was sought from the Department of Health
               (Northern Ireland) to reduce the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority’s
               inspection activity.35

      5.20. The position evolved quickly. By mid-March 2020, each of the regulators had
            suspended routine inspections of care homes; but inspections were still to be
            carried out where there were concerns or reports of potential harm or abuse.36
            Each regulator also decided that a risk-based approach to inspections – based on
            proactive identification of high-risk services and factors influencing levels of risk,

         rather than a fixed period of time – was appropriate.37 Each regulator operated a
         risk-based regulatory framework prior to the pandemic. The approach during the
         pandemic adapted and built upon this.38

5.21.    The decisions to suspend routine inspections were taken primarily on the following
         grounds:

         • the potential risk of transmission by inspectors to residents, those in receipt of
             care and staff through in-person inspections (including the risks of introducing
             Covid-19 into care homes and potential onward transmission);
         • the potential risk of infection to the inspector; and
         • the need to provide support to care providers, including by allowing them to
             concentrate on providing care in challenging circumstances (without the focus on
             a potential inspection).39

5.22. The decisions to stop routine inspections were supported by public health
      advice at the time and agreed or supported by the UK government and devolved
      administrations.40 It was not without precedent. For example, in 2018, as a result of
      respiratory illnesses and influenza, routine inspections for certain healthcare services
      were suspended by the Care Quality Commission and replaced with risk-based
      inspections.41 However, as with the suspension of visiting (discussed in Chapter 4:
      Visiting restrictions in care homes), previous suspensions of routine inspections were
      for short periods of time.42

5.23. Each regulator acknowledged the tension between maintaining the safety of
      care recipients through routine in-person inspections, the pressures placed upon
      providers and the significant risk that inspections would spread Covid-19, which
      could lead to serious illness or death. Mary Cridge, Director of Adult Social Care at
      the Care Quality Commission from July 2022, told the Inquiry:

              “Our intent was always to balance the value to be gained from a full physical
                inspection with the risk posed by inspectors moving between services, alongside
                the recognition that every provider was operating an ‘exceptional’ service.”43

         Gillian Baranski, Chief Inspector of Care Inspectorate Wales from October 2016,
         explained that it was:

                “appropriate to enable local authorities and to providers to give them the space
                 and the opportunity to concentrate on looking after people in their care, rather
                 than worrying about a potential inspection”.44

  5.24. Significant concerns were, however, raised about the lack of oversight that resulted
        from the suspension of routine inspections. The Relatives and Residents Association
        (now Care Rights UK, a charity focused on promoting the rights of older people using
        care services) wrote to the Care Quality Commission explaining that residents felt
        “totally abandoned”.45 Scottish Care described the stopping of inspections by the
        Care Inspectorate as creating “a degree of suspicion and distrust”.46 Eddie Lynch,
        Commissioner for Older People for Northern Ireland from June 2016 to December
        2024, described the decision of the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority
        to cease inspections as leaving “care homes to their own devices save for telephone
        advice and self-reported monitoring”.47 Mr Lynch considered that a single inspector
        entering a care home would have been a “reasonable risk”.48

  5.25. In practice, however, an individual inspector was responsible for more than one care
        home or service.49 Professor Sir Michael McBride, Chief Medical Officer for Northern
        Ireland from September 2006, told the Inquiry:

                “[T]here were also significant risks with individual care home inspectors, who
                  individually inspected 30 to 40 care homes, moving from care home to care
                  home in terms of introducing infection. So it was about controlling the risks that
                  we could control.”50

  5.26. The extent to which inspectors may have posed a transmission risk and the ways
        in which risks could have been mitigated remain unclear. While the benefits of an
        inspection undertaken by a single additional visitor would potentially outweigh
        any additional risk, there were risks associated with spreading Covid-19 between
        care homes. In such a scenario there may be mitigation measures that can be put
        in place – for example, single rather than multiple inspectors on an inspection,
        isolation periods between visits to different care homes, or testing prior to in-
        person inspection, and the provision of personal protective equipment (PPE) where
        available.51 Potential measures to mitigate the risks of transmission should be
        considered in future planning.

  5.27.     Given the risk of death or serious illness for many residents, the initial decision
            to suspend inspections was a reasonable one. At that stage, however, it was not
            envisaged that restrictions would last as long as they did, or that there would be no

         return to routine in-person inspections for the duration of the pandemic. The risks
         of harm from failures in safeguarding – including unsafe infection prevention and
         control (IPC) practices, abuse or other failings such as inadequate end-of-life care
         – were significant. Alternatives to inspections were not in place and so had to be
         developed during the course of the pandemic.

Move to a support function

5.28. Following the suspension of routine inspections, each regulator sought to develop
      policies, processes, tools and guidance to underpin a risk-based approach to
      inspections, along with new assurance approaches that deliberately limited on-site
      activity. Along with the focus on risk-based regulatory activity in care homes, each
      regulator prioritised and established additional support functions to assist care
      providers.

5.29. In Northern Ireland, the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority established
      a Services Support Team and the Care Inspectorate in Scotland established a
      response team to act as a central resource. These teams were intended to answer
      questions and to provide and explain guidance and advice.52 Similarly, in England,
      the Care Quality Commission’s inspection team was to provide guidance and
      advice to care providers and act as a conduit.53 Care Inspectorate Wales explained
      that it sought to provide support and gain feedback to assist in understanding the
      pressures across Wales and those placed upon services, including the identification
      of any specific problems.54

5.30. In providing support, each regulator saw its role as having changed fundamentally.55
      Ms Baranski explained that, in Wales, “at that stage, it seemed more important that
      we should be checking in and providing support, rather than our normal regulatory
      oversight”.56 In relation to England, the Care Quality Commission explained at
      the time that its role would shift to “acting as a source of intelligence and conduit
      between health and social care”.57

5.31.    The Inquiry heard that the provision of support was broadly welcomed by the sector,
         including some people in receipt of care.58 Joanne Sansome, a member of Disability
         Action Northern Ireland (a charity working with disabled people in Northern Ireland),
         told the Inquiry that the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority “acted as a
         good source of the information and advice which was not always available through
         published guidance”.59

  5.32. In Northern Ireland, however, the support came at the inevitable expense of other
        regulatory activities. For example, on 11 May 2020, Mr Lynch wrote to Robin Swann,
        Minister of Health for Northern Ireland from January 2020 to October 2022 and
        from February to May 2024. While noting the value of the new advisory role of
        the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority, Mr Lynch raised concerns
        and sought assurances that, notwithstanding the repurposing of the authority,
        alternative measures were nonetheless in place to ensure care standards and safety
        in care homes.60 Briege Donaghy, Chief Executive of the Regulation and Quality
        Improvement Authority from July 2021, explained that the authority’s support role,
        combined with the ceasing of routine regulatory activity, fundamentally altered its
        routine regulatory purpose.61 She observed that it is possible for the authority both to
        support and inspect, and that this “shouldn’t be at the expense of inspections”.62 The
        provision of support should not impede oversight or safeguarding functions where
        capacity and safety allow. Had there been adequate emergency plans in place, the
        regulators might have been better able to provide effective support and regulatory
        oversight and safeguarding.

  5.33. Common to each regulator was the introduction of supportive check-in calls and
        additional contact with providers of adult social care. From 30 March 2020, Care
        Inspectorate Wales instituted weekly (fortnightly from 22 April 2020) check-in calls.63
        The Care Inspectorate in Scotland increased contact to weekly or sometimes daily
        calls, depending on individual risk and support needs.64 In Northern Ireland, the
        Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority’s Service Support Team provided
        support and guidance to providers both by making proactive contact and in
        response to providers contacting them.65 Later, on 4 May 2020, the Care Quality
        Commission introduced the Emergency Support Framework in England. Calls under
        the framework were similarly intended to be supportive conversations between the
        registered manager and the inspector and used to inform ongoing assessment of the
        provider and next steps, including whether to inspect.66

  5.34. Although this contact was intended initially primarily to be supportive, as the
        pandemic progressed, the calls took on more of an oversight and regulatory
        function. For example, in August 2020, the Care Inspectorate Wales calls were
        replaced by monthly “monitoring calls”, which were described as more closely
        resembling regulatory activity.67 The Care Inspectorate in Scotland used the calls to
        carry out scrutiny checks and progressed to the use of ‘Near-Me’ video consultation,
        which enabled observation and examination of the care home’s environments,

         systems and practices.68 From June 2021, the Care Quality Commission instituted
         Direct Monitoring Activity calls in England, a form of assessment accompanying an
         expansion of its monitoring approach “to build capacity to inspect where higher risk
         [was] identified”.69

5.35. Some care providers reported finding these calls to be of minimal use. Julie
      Parkinson, manager of a domiciliary care agency, said that she received one call from
      her Care Quality Commission inspector, which she considered “to be neither helpful
      nor supportive”, with no “tangible support” or acknowledgement of things done
      well.70 Helen Hough, former care home owner and member of Covid-19 Bereaved
      Families for Justice Cymru, told the Inquiry that, although she had weekly calls,
      Care Inspectorate Wales “did little to assist”.71

5.36. Other care providers found the calls to be more useful. The Regulation and Quality
      Improvement Authority, in its Lessons Learned Report, highlighted that regular
      check-in telephone calls were among the strengths of its pandemic response in
      Northern Ireland.72 Some providers of care considered that the Services Support
      Team in Northern Ireland provided “valuable assistance”.73 Members of the
      Homecare Association reported a high level of support for domiciliary care from
      the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority.74 The Care Quality Commission
      and Care Inspectorate Wales also received positive feedback.75

5.37.    Kevin Mitchell, Executive Director of Assurance and Improvement at the Care
         Inspectorate in Scotland from August 2015, explained that the contacts and calls:

              “would never do all that an inspection would do, but they certainly, I think, were
                as much as we could do in the circumstances … they would never replace an
                inspection”.76

         This was echoed by the Care Quality Commission. Ms Cridge told the Inquiry that,
         while the calls under the Emergency Support Framework provided some insight, this
         was not comparable to the understanding gained from an inspection.77 The use of
         calls was one of the only realistic alternatives available to the regulators once routine
         inspections were suspended.

      Module 6: Care sector




      A risk-based approach to inspections

      5.38. After the initial decision to suspend in-person routine inspections, all four regulators
            moved to systems for identifying and prioritising higher-risk services and harm, with
            in-person inspections where considered necessary. These processes developed and
            were refined throughout the pandemic, as more comprehensive and enhanced forms
            of risk assessment were introduced.78

      5.39. In England, alongside the Emergency Support Framework model, the Care Quality
            Commission initially introduced a ‘decision to assess process’ for cases concerning
            harm, abuse, or human rights breaches, which could bypass the framework in cases
            of “extreme risk”.79 This process was used until July 2020, when it was replaced
            by the Care Quality Commission with a set of ‘Principles and Triggers’ for entering
            a care home during lockdown easing. One principle was the increased use of
            on-site assessments and inspections as the prevalence of Covid-19 decreased.
            The Care Quality Commission subsequently introduced a number of different
            approaches designed to improve the way it monitored care homes, as well as a
            more comprehensive form of risk assessment with improved modelling.80

      5.40. In Scotland, from early March to 19 June 2020, the Care Inspectorate required care
            services to notify it of potential and confirmed cases of Covid-19 and of staffing
            levels, to enable oversight and the deployment of support and resources.81 From
            mid-April 2020, it also developed an enhanced system of assurance assessment
            and a framework so that, by 1 May 2020, an initial assessment of each care home in
            Scotland had taken place.82 In order to reduce the risk of transmission, inspection
            visits were only permitted “where absolutely necessary, and on a risk assessed
            basis”.83 Further tools were developed, along with enhanced monitoring procedures
            for homes experiencing an outbreak.84 The requirement to seek agreement from the
            directors of public health caused conflict on occasion between them and the Care
            Inspectorate, initially prevented an inspection of a care home considered to be in “a
            critical position”, and resulted in the need for significant negotiations and discussion
            in urgent situations.85

      5.41.    From May 2020, Care Inspectorate Wales introduced a system to enable inspectors
               to look at care settings with Covid-19 outbreaks and identify higher-risk services that
               might need increased monitoring and an inspection.86 Care Inspectorate Wales also

         published guidance on its approach to independent scrutiny and assurance.87 In the
         event that it was not satisfied with the provider’s response and actions, the provider
         was referred to a Securing Improvement and Enforcement Panel for consideration of
         subsequent action.88

5.42. In Northern Ireland, in August 2020, the Regulation and Quality Improvement
      Authority introduced a risk assessment tool to guide decision-making on inspection
      type. Factors such as a significant outbreak of infection, a decrease in available
      workforce and any information received indicating poor IPC practices were all
      matters that may have led to on-site inspections being undertaken.89

5.43. The changes to the routine inspection regime caused concern and confusion about
      whether all in-person inspections had been stopped during the pandemic and over
      the role to be played by the regulators.90 It is vital for public confidence that changes
      to inspection activity during a future pandemic are clearly communicated to the
      public, and that the rationale is clearly explained.

Infection prevention and control inspections

5.44. The central importance of IPC was reflected in the number of concerns raised with
      regulators in the first few months of the pandemic.91 This included, for example,
      concerns about staff working while unwell, staff movement, poor infection control
      practices, social distancing and isolation practices, and access to and use of PPE.92

5.45. In May 2020, the Scottish Government announced arrangements for multi-
      disciplinary teams to scrutinise and support care homes. This included IPC measures
      and provided for joint Care Inspectorate and Healthcare Improvement Scotland
      inspections.93 Jeane Freeman (Cabinet Secretary for Health and Sport in the Scottish
      Government from June 2018 to May 2021) told the Inquiry that the role of the Care
      Inspectorate changed, with a focus on inspections to support IPC.94 Care homes
      were potentially subject to multiple inspections by different agencies – for example,
      from the Care Inspectorate and, separately, an NHS-established inspection team.95
      One Scottish care home manager reported experiencing suicidal feelings, owing to
      the pressures on them that increased following such a visit.96 Scottish Care told the

      5.46. In August 2020, in England, the Care Quality Commission carried out a thematic
            review of 300 care homes where the care home was considered to have managed
            well. The aim was to identify good IPC practice.98 The thematic review found
            that very few care homes were not managing well and required a more in-depth
            inspection, and the Care Quality Commission was assured, or somewhat assured,
            of effective IPC in more than 90% of care homes across the areas of inspection.99

      5.47.    During the first half of August 2020, in response to concerns raised about the

               inspections of care homes. The commission recorded in its September 2020 report
               that, in a number of them, it was not fully assured of effective IPC measures (see

                                                                        Chapter 5: Regulation and safeguarding




5.48. Some IPC policies contained out-of-date information as they had not been updated
      since 2019 (or since the early stages of the pandemic), which posed a risk to the
      staff and residents of the care home. Some care homes lacked risk assessments for
      people from Black and ethnic minority groups and from others who were at higher
      risk; and, where there were risk assessments, these had not been actioned. In some
      care homes, PPE was not worn, or was worn incorrectly, and there were limited
      supplies of it, resulting in staff not changing their masks.100 It is likely that similar
      problems were present during the earlier months of the pandemic, with the resulting
      risk and harm to care home residents and staff.

5.49. From September 2020, in Northern Ireland, the Regulation and Quality Improvement
      Authority also conducted a series of IPC-focused inspections.101 From October 2020,
      in England, the Care Quality Commission adopted a new policy of automatically
      inspecting any care home with credible information about IPC problems, regardless
      of its rating.102

5.50. The Inquiry heard some evidence that, in Scotland, the emphasis on IPC created
      friction and conflict between care providers and regulators, with significant
      deterioration in working relationships and morale.103 Donald Macaskill, Chief
      Executive of Scottish Care, attributed this to:

         • the decision to establish inspections focused solely on IPC;
         • new scrutiny requirements;
         • the submissions by the Care Inspectorate of the inspection reports to the Scottish
             Parliament before they were provided to the care home; and
         • the failure to engage fully with the sector when developing and implementing new
             criteria for IPC inspections and disseminating information about the changes.

         He told the Inquiry that there was inadequate consideration of the practical
         challenges for care homes, such as the need for on-the-job training and the wide
         variations in settings, staff experience and knowledge.104 As with the move to a
         support function more generally, there was a tension between the provision of
         support by regulators for IPC measures and their seeking to perform a regulatory
         oversight and enforcement role.

5.51.    Mr Macaskill explained that, prior to the pandemic, the regulatory model of the Care
         Inspectorate in Scotland involved engagement and collaboration. However, he said
         that the experience of providers during the pandemic was one increasingly focused

               on scrutiny, which had a negative impact on the improvement in quality seen prior to
               the pandemic.105

      5.52. More broadly, it was acknowledged that many decisions and pieces of guidance
            were often issued without the involvement of the care sector or those using care.
            For example, not all decisions to cease inspections followed consultations – Care
            Inspectorate Wales did not consult.106 In England, the Care Quality Commission
            observed that the lack of consultation made various changes for the sector difficult
            to understand, follow and implement. The commission acknowledged that the
            resulting pressure placed on the sector could not be underestimated; it considered
            that, without co-production of advice and guidance, there was a risk that the
            guidance would not be effective.107 Consultation with stakeholders is desirable
            where possible.

      Remote inspections

      5.53. Where inspections were undertaken, each regulator sought to utilise or pilot
            technology to conduct them, either remotely or as part of a blended approach
            (a remote inspection followed by a site visit).108

      5.54. Each regulator told the Inquiry that remote or blended inspections were no substitute
            for those undertaken in person. Ms Cridge underscored the value of in-person
            inspections and the insight that is obtained from them. She explained:

                    “There are the sights, the sounds, the smells, frankly … an opportunity to observe
                     care being delivered, to get a sense of the place, of the atmosphere, to observe
                     the interactions between staff and those receiving care.”109

               Mr Mitchell told the Inquiry that, although there were benefits to be drawn from
               the use of remote technology, the Care Inspectorate in Scotland concluded that
               inspections in Scotland should be carried out on-site to the extent possible.110
               Ms Baranski was “not convinced” of the utility of remote inspections in Welsh adult
               care homes (or domiciliary care).111 In Northern Ireland, blended inspections were
               considered to be time-consuming and ineffective, with the Regulation and Quality
               Improvement Authority reporting that care homes found difficulties in facilitating
               them.112 Consequently, blended inspections were phased out.113

                                                      Chapter 5: Regulation and safeguarding




5.55. There were also significant challenges with the use of technology in adult social care
      and in care homes. For some people receiving care, however, the use of remote
      inspections may have conferred additional benefits over and above in-person
      inspections. Ms Sansome explained that, when inspectors met with residents in the
      doorways of their rooms, this afforded little privacy, and staff and residents were
      unable to provide confidential feedback. In her view, the use of remote technology
      would have been more effective as it would have enabled residents to speak freely
      from inside their own rooms.114 However, some residents may not have been able to
      use the technology at all or may have required assistance from staff.

5.56. When inspections of domiciliary care services were carried out, the sufficiency
      and utility of inspections remained ambiguous. For example, in Northern Ireland,
      the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority’s inspections of domiciliary care
      providers were limited to the operating office of the provider.115 In England, the Care
      Quality Commission similarly piloted remote inspections of domiciliary care agency
      offices.116

5.57.     In-person inspections should be prioritised and facilitated wherever possible.
          However, in a future public health emergency, the risks of harm arising from in-
          person inspections may be so great that remote inspections are the only alternative
          option. It is imperative that regulators are able to hear from residents, staff and
          care providers first-hand, undiluted and unmoderated. There is a need, therefore,
          to embed the use of technology across the adult social care sector, to the extent
          possible, to develop a level of familiarity and skill.

Testing and inspections

5.58. The Inquiry heard some evidence about limited Covid-19 testing eligibility for
      inspectors. Testing was unavailable to Care Inspectorate Wales until June 2020.
      At that point, inspectors were advised to request a test either through the UK
      government website or their local health board.117 In England, throughout 2020,
      staff at the Care Quality Commission only had access to the same type and level
      of testing available for members of the public. For example, on 14 August 2020, it
      was announced that Care Quality Commission inspectors did not meet the criteria
      for weekly asymptomatic testing. Concerns were raised, not least with regard to
      their potential to “become ‘super spreaders’ as they [were] likely to be visiting
      more than one home in a day”.118 Significant engagement with the Department of
      Health and Social Care followed before access to regular asymptomatic testing was
      introduced, when weekly polymerase chain reaction (PCR) testing became available
      in December 2020.119 In Scotland, it was not until 11 January 2021 that there was a

      Module 6: Care sector




               requirement for all inspectors to test negative prior to entering care homes for older
               people.120

      5.59. Testing during a pandemic is an essential tool in enabling inspections and, in
            turn, ensuring the safeguarding of those in receipt of care. Where testing capacity
            is sufficient and inspections are possible, consideration should be given to the
            prioritisation of inspectors conducting in-person inspections. Similar considerations
            apply to inspectors regarding designation as key workers and prioritisation for PPE
            and vaccinations. Without access to testing and PPE, the risk of transmitting an
            infectious disease cannot be mitigated or removed. The UK government, devolved
            administrations and regulators will need to consider and make choices about
            balancing the relative risks and resources. Advance pre-pandemic planning is
            essential.

      Post-pandemic inspection activity

      5.60. The suspension of routine inspections saw a dramatic drop in the number of
            inspections across the UK, but each regulator subsequently resumed its inspection
            activity in accordance with its regulatory approach.

      5.61.    Inspection numbers in England and Northern Ireland remained significantly below
               pre-pandemic volumes, attributed in part to a lasting shift towards risk-based
               regulatory models, along with a backlog of regulatory activity (see Table 5).121
               In Northern Ireland, the requirement of twice-annual inspections was not met,
               although the regulatory requirement was not changed. The Regulation and Quality
               Improvement Authority told the Inquiry that this was in part because the pandemic
               acted as a catalyst towards the adoption of a risk-based approach.122 The data
               indicate that Wales and Scotland eventually returned to, or surpassed, pre-pandemic
               numbers of inspection. These totals, however, often included repeat visits to the
               same services – sometimes up to four times – to ensure that improvements had
               been made.123

Enforcement
5.62. As with registration, there was a ‘light touch’ approach to enforcement. For example,
      on 12 March 2020, Ian Trenholm (Chief Executive of the Care Quality Commission
      in England from 2018 to 2024) wrote to Matt Hancock (Secretary of State for Health
      and Social Care from July 2018 to June 2021), setting out the commission’s approach
      to enforcement. Mr Trenholm explained that the Care Quality Commission did
      not anticipate undertaking significant enforcement action during the pandemic.
      He confirmed that the commission would, however, act to keep people safe if
      intelligence indicated serious harm or abuse.124

5.63. Across England, Wales and Northern Ireland, each regulator consequently decreased
      its level of enforcement action during 2020. The number of regulatory actions
      undertaken by the Care Quality Commission in England reduced by just under 60%,
      from 7,522 in 2019 to 3,298 in 2020.125 Similarly, the number of quality improvement
      plans issued by the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority in Northern
      Ireland and priority action notices issued by Care Inspectorate Wales saw notable
      decreases in the year ending 31 March 2021.126 These decreases were most likely a
      result of the different approach to enforcement, competing priorities and the general
      reduction in regulatory activity, particularly inspections.

124 INQ000525082_0002
125 INQ000567487
126 INQ000475143_0046-0047 Table 8; INQ000569773_0046-0047 Table 11

=== PAGE 175 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




  5.64. By contrast, in Scotland, enforcement notices significantly increased in the years
        ending March 2021 and March 2022, before decreasing in the years ending March
        2023 and March 2024.127 The causes of this are not clear but may, in part, be a
        consequence of new procedures adopted by the Care Inspectorate immediately
        prior to the pandemic, following a review of enforcement activity. Although rollout of
        the revised procedures was put on hold as a consequence of the need to train staff
        and the impact of Covid-19, the Care Inspectorate was able to use the period during
        the pandemic to ‘test’ them.128

  5.65. Enforcement is a powerful tool in the regulatory framework, central to both the
        protection of those receiving regulated adult social care and the promotion of
        improvements in the quality of care. The Inquiry heard limited evidence of the extent
        to which enforcement powers were utilised by each regulator or affected by the
        pandemic. It is, therefore, unclear whether problems were identified but not subject
        to enforcement actions, and whether the lack of enforcement affected safety and the
        risk of harm.


  Prioritisation of care homes
  5.66. The vulnerability of care home residents to serious illness or death as a
        consequence of Covid-19 infection, and the fact that care homes were considered
        to be high-risk environments for the spread of infection – together with the potential
        for undetected harm – meant that care homes were, in some instances, prioritised.
        This was to the detriment of domiciliary care, where there were fewer inspections
        and less support offered.129

  5.67.     Dr Jane Townson, Chief Executive of the Homecare Association from May 2019,
            said she considered that domiciliary care was neglected in the focus on other
            services.130 The Care Quality Commission was described by one provider as being
            “completely AWOL [absent without leave] and provided absolutely no help or advice”
            to domiciliary care providers.131 By June 2024, 60% of domiciliary care services in
            England either had never been assessed by the Care Quality Commission or had
            a rating that was between four and eight years old.132 Dr Townson thought that, as
            at May 2025, there remained outstanding safeguarding issues that had not been
            investigated.133 Ms Cridge accepted that the commission could have done more for
            domiciliary care providers.134

5.68. It is crucial to ensure that domiciliary care is not neglected and that planning
      incorporates robust measures to be established by each regulator to ensure
      support, standards of care and safety for people in receipt of care in their own
      home, wherever circumstances allow.


Risk of harm
5.69. There were significant concerns across the adult social care sector that the
      decision to reduce regulatory activity, and, in particular, limit routine and in-person
      inspections, undermined effective safeguarding and oversight.135

5.70. In April 2020, the Association of Directors of Adult Social Services expressed
      alarm that inspections could be stopped at the point when, in its view, monitoring
      was more important than ever. It pointed to the need to consider the rise
      in infections, deaths, staffing shortages, and increases in the abuse of older
      and disabled people.136

5.71.    Professor Vic Rayner, Chief Executive Officer of the National Care Forum from
         2016, considered that the move to a risk-based model resulted in oversight being
         withdrawn from adult social care services throughout the pandemic.137 This was
         a view shared more widely by organisations including Care Rights UK, John’s
         Campaign and the Patients Association, who felt that the Care Quality Commission
         “abdicated responsibility”.138 They considered that instead of a proper balance being
         struck, “those needing care were abandoned in the name of infection control”.139

5.72. The absence of routine regulatory inspections was compounded by the suspension
      of in-person visits from family, loved ones, and health and other care workers,
      which would otherwise also play an additional supervisory and oversight role.
      Unsurprisingly, there was a significant reduction in safeguarding referrals –
      particularly in the initial period when routine regulatory inspections were paused and
      lockdown restrictions were at their most severe.140


Foreseeability
5.73. The Inquiry heard that, as restrictions eased, there was a recognised likelihood of
      an increase in safeguarding concerns and identification of harm that had previously
      gone unseen. The UK government, for example, was aware that Hourglass, a charity
      that runs a helpline for older people who are victims of abuse, had experienced
      a rise in calls of at least 30% during the first lockdown. This included a sharp rise

           in reports of neglect, particularly in care homes.141 The Department of Health and
           Social Care was concerned about an increase in safeguarding referrals as lockdown
           restrictions began to ease and professionals recommenced contact.142 Similar
           concerns were expressed by the Local Government Association, the Association of
           Directors of Adult Social Services and the National Independent Safeguarding Board
           Wales (which provides support to and advises safeguarding boards, reports on
           safeguarding arrangements and makes recommendations to ministers of the Welsh
           Government).143

  5.74.    Regulators also envisaged increases in safeguarding concerns. On 1 July 2020, the
           Care Quality Commission told Helen Whately MP, Minister of State for Social Care at
           the Department of Health and Social Care from February 2020 to September 2021,
           that a “spike” in cases involving safeguarding issues was likely, as services that had
           been unable to cope were identified. While Ms Whately agreed with the decision
           to cease routine inspections, she “did not agree that this was done at the risk of
           neglect/abuse to residents”.144

  5.75. Ms Whately told the Inquiry that she received no sense of the scale of the problem
        or of how many safeguarding concerns were likely to be uncovered – it was a “black
        box or hole”.145 She knew that there was a risk of neglect, but this was the first time
        she had been formally told of it happening.146 Ms Whately was also informed of one
        care home that had been closed as a result of a lack of safety and basic failures.147

  5.76. Subsequent analysis by the Local Government Association recorded that reported
        safeguarding concerns in England dropped markedly during the initial months of
        the pandemic, and below those of the comparable period in 2019, before returning
        to – and then exceeding – normal levels in July 2020. The pattern of decreases in
        safeguarding concerns during periods of restrictions, and increases when restrictions
        were relaxed, was replicated across the various waves of the pandemic in England.148
        The Local Government Association recorded concerns that safeguarding issues
        were not being identified and were under-reported.149 It noted that the nature and
        type of safeguarding enquiries broadly remained the same, with the most prevalent
        relating to neglect. There was, however, a notable variance between domiciliary care
        and that in care homes. Enquiries related to risk in an individual’s own home had
        “increased markedly”, while those in care homes had decreased.150

=== PAGE 178 ===
                                                                        Chapter 5: Regulation and safeguarding




5.77.    The suspension and subsequent limiting of in-person inspections is likely to have
         contributed to both the under-reporting and under-identification of safeguarding
         issues. The pattern of reporting of safeguarding concerns, alongside the comparative
         differences from the years preceding the pandemic, also provides some evidence
         for there having been delays in the recognition and identification of safeguarding
         issues.151 Delays may have resulted in more severe consequences for those receiving
         care.152

5.78. A particular concern was an increase in ‘closed cultures’, defined by the Care
      Quality Commission as poor cultures that can lead to harm (including human rights
      breaches) such as abuse.153 Evaluations of the commission’s Emergency Support
      Framework as well as later risk assessment frameworks identified concerns about
      their effectiveness. In particular, they identified concerns with the utility of remote
      monitoring methods in identifying closed cultures, along with the ability of closed
      cultures to develop in those circumstances.154 The commission’s evaluation of its
      Closed Cultures Project described the pandemic as creating a “hotbed” in which
      closed cultures could develop and thrive.155 Approximately 19% of a sample analysis
      of Care Quality Commission inspections up until 14 July 2020 raised closed cultures
      as an issue.156

5.79. The risk of significant harm (to residents in care homes and people using adult
      social care) going unchecked in the absence of the normal regulatory safeguards
      – combined with the lack of external oversight from other organisations, visitors
      and loved ones – was subsequently borne out when inspections resumed and the
      number of inspections increased.


Harm and concerns
5.80. The Care Quality Commission completed 17 in-person inspections in England
      between 17 March and 17 June 2020. It conducted a further 51 inspections in adult
      social care services by 14 July 2020.157 Upon a sample analysis, the most common
      initial reason for inspection was where there were safeguarding concerns (39.47%).
      The most common theme to emerge was “lack of oversight/poor governance”,
      with impacts on safety, service delivery and IPC practice.158 The most commonly

            That included identifying cases of “extremely poor and unsafe care, with people’s

            August 2020 onwards, but these were still considerably below pre-pandemic

  5.81.     In Scotland, on-site inspections resumed on 4 May 2020 as a result of serious
            concerns arising with regard to one specific care home. The same care home was
            subsequently reinspected on 12 and 13 May 2020. The concerns related to the
            lack of use of PPE, incorrect advice regarding the use of such equipment, hygiene,
            contaminated food, and management and leadership oversight. Enforcement action
            was subsequently taken, and in due course the care home was sold and registration
            transferred to NHS Scotland. Legal proceedings were then concluded by the Care
            Inspectorate.162 On 14 May, plans were finalised to inspect a further six care homes
            over the weekend of 15 to 17 May 2020, again as a consequence of significant
            concerns being raised. Of the six homes, those concerns were confirmed in one.
            The identified issues related to IPC practice, general cleanliness and hygiene,
            fluids and risk of dehydration, and leadership. In the remaining five homes, no
            major concerns were identified, with some noted as providing good or excellent
            practices.163

  5.82. Despite the number of inspections significantly reducing throughout the pandemic,
        the number of priority action notices issued by Care Inspectorate Wales in response
        to non-compliance with regulations saw a significant increase, indicating potentially
        unsafe practices and the pressures on care homes.164

  5.83. Grave harm was uncovered in one particular care home in England. In June 2019, the
        care home was rated as ‘requiring improvement’, and it was found to have breached
        regulations relating to safe care and treatment and good governance. This resulted
        in conditions being placed on its registration.165 When inspected in May 2020,

        to hospital. A further 14 residents were identified as being dehydrated, of which 8

  Regulatory activity in future public health emergencies
  5.87.    While the initial suspension of on-site and routine inspections was a reasonable
           response, it was fraught with difficulties. The introduction of supportive calls –
           and the subsequent evidence that these could not be, and were not, the same
           as inspection – highlighted a critical gap in assurance during a period of extreme
           pressure. Regulatory authorities adapted existing models and created new
           approaches to assessing risk, but much of this work came weeks or months after the
           initial decisions to suspend routine inspections were taken. It involved substantial
           work at a time when the adult social care system was under immense pressure.
           The time for doing this work was before the pandemic. Better planning is required.

  5.88. During the initial months of the Covid-19 pandemic, the lack of adequate oversight
        meant external checks on care homes and domiciliary care providers were reduced
        – heightening the risk to users of care. The extent of the risk of harm to users of care
        (and staff) and of actual harm that went undetected is difficult to ascertain. However,
        it is clear that harm resulted, and the risk of harm in a future public health emergency
        is real. While some aspects of regulatory activity may necessarily be scaled back,
        safeguarding activity must be prioritised.

  5.89. It was recognised that regulatory activity and inspection were critical – especially in
        the event of a public health emergency – and should be continued where possible.174
        The Care Quality Commission observed that, in England, in-person inspections
        were integral and should be protected.175 The Regulation and Quality Improvement
        Authority in Northern Ireland stressed that, during a crisis, regulatory activity was
        more important, not less.176

  5.90. There is no substantive alternative to on-site inspections, which provide “a rich
        tapestry of information [and are] certainly not a box-ticking exercise”.177 Intelligence
        and ongoing close contact can, however, play an important role in being able
        to respond flexibly where circumstances do not allow for on-site inspections.178
        Ms Donaghy suggested that:

                “[R]ather than an abrupt end to inspection, [the regulators] should leverage up
                  the intelligence-based approach, reducing the scheduled [inspections], so that
                  there’s a portfolio of inspection approaches available.”179

=== PAGE 182 ===
                                                                     Chapter 5: Regulation and safeguarding




5.91.    The Care Quality Commission was similarly clear that intelligence – from people
         in receipt of care, their loved ones, the staff and care providers – is invaluable.180
         However, where care homes or services are effectively closed, intelligence-gathering
         – as seen in the decrease in safeguarding reports during periods of greater
         restrictions – falls away.

5.92. The benefits of on-site inspection do nonetheless need to be balanced against the
      potential risks that inspections might pose. There may be situations where on-site
      inspections cannot be safely undertaken, due to the risks to the people using care,
      staff and inspectors simply being too great.181

5.93. The Inquiry therefore recommends that regulators take steps to ensure that they
      have practical and adaptable plans and protocols to maintain regulatory oversight
      and to enable inspections – in some form – to continue to take place in future
      pandemics. These protocols should be guided by the following core principles:

         • individual risk assessments to determine whether on-site inspections are safe and
             proportionate, considering the geographical area and care setting in question;
         • regulators to work closely with individual providers to find, wherever possible,
             bespoke solutions that enable inspections to take place;
         • the identification of clear channels of communication between care providers and
             regulators that can be maintained at all times during a future pandemic; and
         • the involvement of care providers, staff, people who use care services, their
             families and loved ones in developing the protocols.


         Recommendation 5: Maintaining regulatory oversight of care quality
         and safety in a public health emergency
         The Care Quality Commission in England, Care Inspectorate Wales, the Care
         Inspectorate in Scotland and the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority
         in Northern Ireland should each develop adaptable protocols for carrying out
         on-site, hybrid and remote inspections in a future public health emergency.
         These protocols should be guided by the core principle of continuing on-site
         inspections where possible. They should also clearly identify processes to
         minimise:

         • the risk of transmission when on-site inspections are carried out; and
         • the risk of safeguarding issues going unidentified where remote or hybrid
             inspections are carried out.
         Regulators should regularly review these protocols to ensure that they remain
         practical and effective.

Chapter 6: The impact on
unpaid carers, care workers

=== PAGE 184 ===
                           Chapter 6: The impact on unpaid carers, care workers and care providers




Introduction
6.1.      The impact of the Covid-19 pandemic on people working in adult social care cannot
          be overstated. Millions of unpaid carers and care workers showed extraordinary
          dedication and compassion as they worked tirelessly caring for people who
          were very unwell or dying, often with limited resources or support. Without the
          support and care of family and friends during the pandemic, health and social care
          services could have been quickly overwhelmed.1 One care worker told the National
          Association of Care and Support Workers in a survey: “We did what was needed to
          be done. If care workers had withdrawn … the most vulnerable people would not
          have survived. We did not have a choice.”2

6.2.      For many care workers in England, Scotland and Wales, this came at a considerable
          risk to their own health and lives. The data suggest that they had a significantly
          higher risk of dying from Covid-19 than the general population or people in other
          occupations.3

6.3.      The Covid-19 pandemic also created a multitude of challenges for the thousands
          of social care providers (the organisations that deliver social care). Many needed
          emergency funding due to increasing costs, and support with workforce shortages
          that jeopardised their existence and ability to deliver care.

6.4.      This chapter considers the impact of the Covid-19 pandemic on the people and
          organisations delivering social care and the support available to them. It also
          examines the issues with collecting data about the number of care workers
          who died.


The impact of the pandemic on unpaid carers
The number and identification of unpaid carers
6.5.      The Covid-19 pandemic highlighted the importance of being able to identify unpaid
          carers to provide them with information and to ensure that they could access
          support. It was (and remains) difficult, though, to ascertain the number of people
          providing unpaid care. Census data from 2021 and 2022 suggest approximately
          5.8 million people were providing unpaid care in the UK. However, many people do
          not self-identify as a carer and so would not necessarily be counted in the census

2    INQ000518420_0060

causesofdeath/bulletins/coronaviruscovid19relateddeathsbyoccupationenglandandwales/deathsregistereduptoandincluding
20april2020; INQ000503378); INQ000514105_0009 para 30; INQ000273805_0012 para 16. In Northern Ireland, the proportion
of deaths in the health and social care workforce was lower than in the general population. However, occupation stated on a death
certificate should be interpreted with caution, as it is self-reported and may not reflect the deceased’s occupation at the time of
death; for example, they may have been retired, unemployed or in a different job.

           data.4 There is no register of unpaid adult carers held in any of the four nations
           of the UK.

  6.6.     Carers UK estimated that the Covid-19 pandemic could result in an additional
           4.5 million people providing care.5 An increase in people’s social care needs,
           concerns about letting social care professionals into homes for fear of infection
           and the reduction in the availability of social care services are likely reasons for
           this growth.6

  6.7.     Problems identifying unpaid carers meant that the Department of Health (Northern
           Ireland) had to send a leaflet about the support available to unpaid carers to every
           household. According to Robin Swann, Minister of Health for Northern Ireland from
           January 2020 to October 2022, it would help with disseminating information in future
           if there was a central register of unpaid carers that healthcare providers could add to
           and unpaid carers could apply to join.7

  6.8.     In England, records held by the Department for Work and Pensions, local authorities
           and GPs helped to identify unpaid carers, but the data from the latter two sources
           were not comprehensive.8 Helen Whately MP, Minister of State for Social Care at the
           Department of Health and Social Care from February 2020 to September 2021, said
           that the UK government had limited knowledge about who was an unpaid carer.9
           In August 2020, the Carers Advisory Group encouraged NHS England (supported by
           the UK government) to implement a requirement that all GP practices have a system
           for identifying carers within three months.10 There is no evidence to suggest that this
           was implemented.11

  6.9.     In Wales, work had begun before the pandemic to enhance unpaid carers’ and
           healthcare professionals’ knowledge about what a caring role entails and their right
           to support. A similar campaign was launched in Scotland in December 2020.12

  6.10.    There should be better systems across the UK to identify unpaid carers in order to
           improve communication and access to support for them in a future pandemic. The
           UK government’s Pandemic Preparedness Strategy published in March 2026 has
           committed to improving the identification of unpaid carers in England through digital

Personal protective equipment for unpaid carers
6.11.     Most unpaid carers in the UK had to buy their own personal protective equipment
          (PPE), but the increased cost of PPE (where it was available) meant that many could
          not afford it.15 Unpaid carers were concerned that not wearing PPE might increase
          their risk of becoming unwell with Covid-19 and prevent them from carrying out their
          caring role.16 This caused understandable concern and anxiety. An unpaid carer said:

              “I think as unpaid carers we should be given some level of support in the
               provision of PPE – although I appreciate that there is currently a shortage –
               what would happen if, as an unpaid carer, I was taken ill?”17

6.12.     From 17 March 2020, Health and Social Care trusts in Northern Ireland were
          encouraged to provide unpaid carers with PPE where it was required.18


               “[ W]e were provided with masks and gloves … that didn’t cost us anything
                 and … buffered the problem [of not being able to find PPE].”19

                Unpaid carer, Northern Ireland, Every Story Matters


6.13.     From 26 April 2020, unpaid carers in Scotland could access free PPE from a local
          hub; eligibility was based on need.20 Unpaid carers in Wales might have been eligible
          to receive free PPE through their local authority from around the end of June 2020
          if: the person being cared for or someone in their household had symptoms of
          Covid-19; the individual being cared for or the carer was shielding; or the carer could
          not access PPE through their usual routes.21 Unpaid carers providing “hands on care”

  Module 6: Care sector




           in Northern Ireland should have been provided with disposable gloves and aprons
           from 3 August 2020.22

  6.14.    During a pilot programme between December 2020 and January 2021, some unpaid
           carers in England were offered free PPE by the Department of Health and Social
           Care. However, not many unpaid carers used the service and, when they did, the
           demand could be met by stocks of PPE already held by local authorities.23 Issues
           with identifying unpaid carers are likely to have contributed to the low uptake.24
           From 9 February 2021, unpaid carers in England who cared for a person outside their
           household could access free PPE from their local authority. However, yet again, there
           was limited uptake of this offer. PPE was considered by carers’ organisations as less
           of a priority at this time due to the vaccine rollout and access to tests. In addition, the
           department’s adult social care PPE team suggested that local authorities and local
           resilience forums might not have been promoting the offer of free PPE to unpaid
           carers for fear of unmanageable demand.25

  6.15.    The millions of unpaid carers who could not afford to source their own PPE still
           needed to be able to access it to protect themselves and the people for whom they
           cared during the pandemic. Unpaid carers are very likely to need PPE during a future
           pandemic; therefore, it should form part of pandemic planning. Unpaid carers should
           be able easily to access information about how to obtain and use PPE.


  Support for unpaid carers
  6.16.    Unpaid carers needed support to assist them with changes to the type of care they
           were required to provide and to manage the increase in financial and emotional
           pressure they felt.

  Clinical care support

  6.17.    Difficulties accessing health and social care services meant that, throughout the
           Covid-19 pandemic, many unpaid carers had to provide more complex and clinical
           care for which they were not prepared.26 In April 2020, a survey of more than 5,000
           unpaid carers in the UK found that 24% were helping more with medication and 11%
           were providing more medical care, such as administering injections and changing
           dressings.27 Six months into the pandemic, unpaid carers reported to Carers UK that

               to do any activities … I am trying to work full time from her home whilst caring for
