6.18.    Unpaid carers responding to a survey by Carers UK described how they became
         the sole carer for their family member. One explained how her husband (who used
         a wheelchair) usually had two care workers visit their home three times a day, but
         when these visits stopped, she became responsible for the entirety of his care.
         Another said:

              “I have cancelled Mum’s care calls, four a day, two care workers, to reduce
               the amount of people entering Mum’s home. I have moved in with her, for
               the shielding period, and I’m providing the care she needs.”31

Financial support

6.19.    Financial pressures on unpaid carers increased, as they had to buy PPE and their
         household bills rose.32 Research by Carers UK estimated that 21.5% of people
         providing unpaid care to someone outside their own household experienced some
         degree of financial strain in April and May 2020.33 In some cases, the impact could
         be life-changing. Responding to a survey by Carers UK, one unpaid carer reported:
         “[W]ith my [disabled] son home full-time my household bills have trebled.” Another
         said: “[M]y savings that I have spent 30 years trying to build up for retirement are
         now vanishing to pay the bills.”34

  Module 6: Care sector




  6.20. In England, Wales and Scotland, additional financial support was made available to
        unpaid carers but not all were eligible to receive this.35 Difficulties with identification
        meant there were no direct payments for unpaid carers in Northern Ireland.36

  Respite for unpaid carers

  6.21.    Respite care provides an essential break for unpaid carers. During the Covid-19
           pandemic, respite care in the form of community day services, activities or overnight
           care closed or reduced their services. Family and friends were also less able to
           provide support to unpaid carers.37 As a result, many unpaid carers provided care
           for prolonged periods of time without a break, often affecting their own wellbeing.38
           This led to a significant number of unpaid carers experiencing or being on the brink
           of burn-out.39


                 “At one point, I was probably only getting about four hours sleep a day for
                   months and it just became the norm. I thought I was all right, but I was
                   absolutely exhausted.”40

                 Unpaid carer living with the person they cared for, Northern Ireland,
                 Every Story Matters



                 “The burden of working maybe 14 or 15 hours a day, seven days a week and
                   caring for my parents who were both shielding, I became burned out. One
                   day, I just started to cry.”41

                 Unpaid carer, Northern Ireland, Every Story Matters


  6.22. Unpaid carers described feeling anxious and afraid about what would happen in
        the event that they could no longer provide care to their loved ones.42 One carer,
        six months into the pandemic, said:

                “I have struggled hugely being with my husband 24/7 unable to have a break
                 from caring. There are times I think I can’t go on, but of course I have to.
                 Sometimes I don’t want to wake up in the morning.”43

=== PAGE 190 ===
                          Chapter 6: The impact on unpaid carers, care workers and care providers




6.23. According to a survey of directors of adult social services in England, 64% said that
      Covid-19 had led to an increase in the number of individuals presenting with social
      care needs to local authorities due to carer breakdown, sickness or unavailability
      between March and May 2020.44 In the summer of 2020, unpaid carers in England
      said that the most urgent issue was the reopening of services for respite.45

6.24. There was also concern about the potential impact of subsequent outbreaks of
      Covid-19. A report by the Association of Directors of Adult Social Services in October
      2020 identified that:

              “[D]uring a second or subsequent wave, energy levels may not be as great and
                respite services may be even more important … they may also be needed
                during, rather than after, the wave.”46

         The report suggested that ongoing review and consideration of the need for respite
         for unpaid carers was necessary.47

6.25. Some alternative support was available. Just over half of local authorities in England
      offered telephone welfare checks, counselling services and practical support to
      unpaid carers.48 In Scotland, from May 2020, unpaid carers could access a digital
      wellbeing hub that was established for people working or volunteering in health and
      social care services.49 A Carer ID card was used by 42,000 unpaid carers in Northern
      Ireland from June 2020, which, among other things, provided proof of carer status.50
      Guidance for unpaid carers in Wales in August 2020 encouraged use of online and
      telephone support services.51 While some unpaid carers may have benefited from the
      additional support, many were unable to access it and it was not always targeted to
      their needs.52

6.26. There was some funding to restart or expand respite and support services for unpaid
      carers. For example:

         • The Scottish Government established a £500,000 fund for local carer
             organisations in April 2020 to transition to online working. Additional funding to
             expand support was granted in January 2021 and in January and April 2022.53

=== PAGE 191 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




           • From around June 2020, the Welsh Government provided Carers Wales with
               £50,000 to extend its mental health support for unpaid carers. Additional funding
               was granted in April and September 2021.54
           • The Department for Digital, Culture, Media and Sport granted £500,000 to the
               Carers Trust at the start of 2021 (matched by a charitable foundation), which was
               used to support almost 7,000 unpaid carers to access breaks and respite.55
           • Funding could be accessed by community and voluntary sector organisations
               supporting unpaid carers in Northern Ireland from September 2021 to May 2023.56

  6.27.    Despite the additional funding and alternative support, unpaid carers reported
           that they were unable to take a break and their mental health deteriorated, which
           threatened their ability to continue to provide care. UK-wide surveys undertaken
           by Carers UK during the first half of 2021 found that 72% of unpaid carers still had
           not had a break from their caring role since the start of the pandemic. In addition,
           74% reported that their caring role had left them feeling exhausted and 35% felt
           unable to continue with their caring responsibilities.57 In November 2021, a review in
           England noted that unpaid carers’ needs had continued to increase at the same time
           as access to support had reduced, including the suspension or reduction of respite
           services. The need for unpaid carers to have a break was identified again.58

  6.28. The Independent Review of Adult Social Care in Scotland (known as the Feeley
        Review), commissioned by the Scottish Government and published in February
        2021, found that carers needed better, more consistent support to carry out their
        caring role, with regular breaks.59 The Care Reform (Scotland) Act 2025 requires
        local authorities to ensure that carers can have a break if they cannot do so on their
        own.60 This is a positive development.

  6.29. The reduction in respite services during the Covid-19 pandemic added to the
        extraordinary pressures on unpaid carers. Many felt abandoned and unsupported.
        Pandemic planning must consider how social care services can continue to support
        unpaid carers and, where temporary closures are necessary, how unpaid carers will
        be able to access alternatives. Reopening respite services should be prioritised.

=== PAGE 192 ===
                          Chapter 6: The impact on unpaid carers, care workers and care providers




         Improvements to the identification of unpaid carers will ensure that, during a future
         pandemic, there can be direct communication and targeted support.


The impact of the pandemic on care workers
6.30. Care workers witnessed and were personally affected by the impact of the Covid-19
      pandemic on the vulnerable people they supported.


Staff moving into care homes
6.31.    During the pandemic, some care workers moved into the care home they worked
         in.61 A care home manager said:

              “I moved into the home as staff were either getting Covid or were too scared to
               come into work. I was here 24 hours a day and it was absolutely draining,
               mentally and physically. But we had no choice.”62

         A care home owner in Wales described 39 members of staff moving into the care
         home for 12 days during the early stages of the pandemic to deal with the lack of
         PPE and testing.63

6.32. This had an impact on care workers’ mental health and wellbeing and on their family
      members.64 Reverend Charlotte Hudd worked as a nurse in a care home. When 95%
      of the staff were absent, she moved into the home and provided 24/7 clinical care
      for 10 days to 20 residents with complex needs or Covid-19, some of whom were
      approaching the end of life. While a single resident would usually receive one-to-one
      care with five or six nurses caring for the other residents, staff shortages meant that
      they had to:

              “prioritise nutrition, continence -- and so they were seeing even less of us. That
               was really hard. And I would hear at night sometimes, the person next door to
               my room crying … ‘Where is everybody?’ That was quite distressing.”65

         Reverend Hudd’s experience led to her suffering post-traumatic stress disorder.66

6.33. The fact that some care workers felt they had little choice but to move into the care
      home in which they worked demonstrates their commitment and dedication to the
      people for whom they were caring.

=== PAGE 193 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




  Witnessing death
  6.34. The number of people dying in care homes during the Covid-19 pandemic placed
        enormous strain on care workers, who often had to provide end-of-life care to
        multiple residents at the same time.67 For example, one care home lost 19 residents
        from a dementia unit (two-thirds of its residents) in two weeks; and there was a
        point where four residents died on one day. Another care home lost almost half of
        its residents in three weeks, some of whom had lived in the home for many years.
        The manager of the home said that the residents who died “had become part of
        our family so it was devastating for my team”.68 A care home worker described 30
        residents dying and, during one shift, 5 residents were all close to the end of life.
        The staff went between them to check that they were comfortable, clean and still
        breathing.69

  6.35. Many care workers, understandably, experienced deep distress when people they
        cared for died.70


               “Staff were sitting on video calls with families, saying their last goodbye to
                their loved ones. That was horrendous … staff were crying with the residents,
                because it was just so emotional for them. They were holding their hands
                until they died.”71

               Care home worker, Northern Ireland, Every Story Matters



               “It was horrible, we were the ones sat there, holding their hand as they were
                dying. We were the ones ringing family to say, ‘Your mum’s passed away this
                morning.’”72

               Domiciliary care worker, England, Every Story Matters


  6.36. Visiting restrictions meant it was common for care home staff to have to contact a
        resident’s loved ones to inform them that the resident was dying or had died. This
        had a lasting impact:

              “I will never forget having to ring a relative to tell her that her mother had died
               and she just screamed at me. Every time that I ring a relative to deliver bad
               news, I still think of that phone call.”73

              “I was talking to families I barely knew – calling to introduce myself and then on
               the next call saying, ‘your mum is taking her last breaths’. It’s something that you
               never get over.”74

6.37.     Care staff also experienced the deaths of colleagues. A care home manager said:

              “It was one of the hardest things I have ever done, getting staff together in the
               middle of this massive challenge to tell them that one of their colleagues, who
               was really highly thought of, had passed away … I never dreamed I would have
               to go through the awful experience of telling my team that one of their
               colleagues had died a second time.”75

6.38. The experience of Covid-19 for so many care workers was summarised by one
      contributor to the Inquiry’s listening exercise, Every Story Matters.


               “I feel sad for the ones we lost and that they didn’t get the end of life care they
                deserved, I feel sad for the good staff we lost because they were scared of
                getting Covid, my heart breaks for the families that didn’t get to say goodbye
                and I’m sad for me being stuck with the memories of the worst things I’ve
                ever had to endure with barely any support.”76

                Senior care worker in a residential care home, Wales, Every Story Matters


6.39. Caroline Abrahams, Charity Director of Age UK from 2012, said that it must have
      been an “absolutely shattering experience” for care workers to have to look after so
      many people who were dying.77 The scale of death and suffering witnessed by social
      care staff during the Covid-19 pandemic was unlike anything they had seen before.
      Many were alone with someone in their last few moments of life.


Additional responsibilities
6.40. Not all care homes or domiciliary care providers (which provide a range of services
      to support an individual in their own home) typically provide clinical or end-of-life
      care. However, during the pandemic, an absence or reduction in visits by healthcare
      professionals meant that care workers took on additional clinical responsibilities.

                 “I felt like we were on our own, health professionals didn’t want to know,

  6.41.    Many care workers had not received training for the clinical or ‘delegated’ tasks that
           they were asked to do, which included wound care and providing insulin injections.80
           Access to training and support from healthcare professionals was inconsistent during
           the pandemic.81 A care home manager in Scotland said that district nurses provided
           training and were available on the phone if required.82 Julie Parkinson, manager of a
           domiciliary care agency, said:

                “I felt we received a very poor response from GP services, which were
                 responding to a crisis of their own but failed to consider those of us working in
                 the community. I feel that this made life extremely and unnecessarily difficult
                 for us.”83

  6.42. Undertaking delegated tasks increased the burden on care workers and, without
        proper training or support, the risk of a poor outcome for users of care services.84
        However, regular contact between healthcare professionals and care workers
        provided an opportunity to raise clinical concerns and receive advice.85

  6.43. In July 2020, Skills for Care (the independent workforce development and planning
        body for social care in England) developed two short guides to support care workers
        undertaking delegated tasks; it issued further guidance in 2021 which set out
        the conditions for delegating a task. Since the pandemic, guiding principles have

         been developed.86 Despite this, Skills for Care said that there remains a lack of
         infrastructure, incentives and clinical capacity to support care workers to safely carry
         out delegated tasks if required to do so again.87 While the progress on training in
         this area is welcome, there need to be sufficient numbers of nurses and clinical staff
         working in adult social care (and healthcare) to direct and supervise care workers to
         carry out delegated tasks.

6.44. Care home managers and staff also had to keep abreast of and implement guidance
      provided to the adult social care sector. This guidance was updated frequently
      and sometimes at short notice as scientific understanding of Covid-19 developed.
      The Inquiry was told how it was difficult to keep up with the changes while also
      responding to other challenges presented by the pandemic.88

6.45. In recognition of the additional responsibilities and longer hours worked during the
      pandemic, care workers became eligible for a one-off payment of £500 in Scotland
      in late 2020 and in Northern Ireland in early 2021.89 In Wales, care workers were
      eligible to receive payments of £500 in June 2020 and March 2021.90 Although the
      payment had a negative impact on some care workers’ benefits and not all received
      it promptly, it was an important way of recognising their invaluable contribution
      during the pandemic.91

6.46. While the UK government did not make a one-off payment to care workers in
      England, some providers used other funding to pay them a bonus.92 The Care
      Workers’ Charity said that the UK government’s decision showed a lack of
      recognition, contributed to widespread feelings of neglect and was a disincentive
      to staying in or joining the sector.93


Support for care workers’ mental health and wellbeing
6.47.    The impact of the Covid-19 pandemic on the mental health and wellbeing of many
         people working in social care was significant and, for some, long-lasting.94 Analysis

  6.48. Some social care staff felt unable to continue in their role. A manager of a domiciliary
        care agency said:

                “[T]he trauma was so overwhelming that some of these remarkable individuals
                  chose to leave the care profession, unable to overcome the emotional toll of
                  what transpired.”96

  6.49. The UK government and devolved administrations provided some psychological and
        wellbeing support for social care staff:

           • The Care Workforce app launched in England in May 2020. It closed in March
               2021 due to low uptake, the costs involved and issues with compliance.97
           • A wellbeing helpline was available in Scotland from July 2020, the Workforce
               Specialist Service (psychological support) launched in February 2021, and funding
               to support the wellbeing of the health and social care workforce was announced
               in June 2021.98
           • In July 2020, there was a programme in Northern Ireland to introduce ‘rainbow
               rooms’ (dedicated reflection spaces for care home staff), and resources with
               information and advice on health and wellbeing were delivered to care homes.99
           • A Samaritans helpline for health and social care workers in Wales was accessible
               from August 2020.100

  6.50. Professor Vic Rayner, Chief Executive Officer of the National Care Forum from 2016,
        said that the services and resources provided by the Department of Health and
        Social Care and NHS England were not always suitable for the sector, as they were
        created for clinical settings and NHS staff, who were not facing the same issues as
        care workers.101 Additionally, some care workers did not have an NHS email address
        to be able to access the resources.102 The fragmented nature of the workforce also
        made it difficult to communicate with care workers about the support available,
        which limited its effectiveness.103

  6.51.    The mental health support available in England and Scotland for social care staff
           during the pandemic has been described by representatives in the care sector as

6.52. Voluntary and independent organisations acted quickly to provide vital support.106
      Mental health charities operated a 24/7 telephone and text support service for all UK
      front-line and key workers – ‘Our Frontline’ – from April 2020 to November 2021.107
      Specific support for care home managers was available in Wales (from Age Cymru,
      Care Home Cymru and others) and in England (from Skills for Care) from November
      2020 to February 2021.108 Skills for Care said that the text support service in England
      was described as “useful” and “relevant”.109

6.53. Some migrant workers were fearful that seeking professional help could have an
      effect on their employment and visa status. Kanlungan (a charity consisting of several
      Filipino and South-East and East Asian community organisations) therefore offered
      mental health support to migrant workers in the social care sector.110

6.54. Many care workers felt the impact later in the pandemic or after it had ended, at
      a time when support was no longer available, as one contributor told Every Story
      Matters.111


              “[The] mental challenge that I faced, as things wound down, was as a result of
                what we all went through … we thrived off of the emergency of having to deal
                [with it] and respond to things [then], but it took its toll some years later.”112

               Registered manager, England, Every Story Matters


6.55. For some, the impact was particularly severe, leading to post-traumatic stress and
      suicidal thoughts. The Inquiry was told of care workers who died by suicide.113

6.56. The impact and ongoing effects of the Covid-19 pandemic on the social care
      workforce demonstrate the need for governments to plan for better psychological
      and emotional support for care workers in future to help mitigate the risk of more
      people leaving the profession.

=== PAGE 199 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




  The impact of the pandemic on care providers
  and local authorities
  6.57.   In addition to coping with the emergence of a novel, highly transmissible virus, care
          providers also had to contend with additional workforce shortages and increased
          costs.

  6.58. Although a number of measures were introduced to manage staff shortages and
        increase capacity in the workforce, there were very real concerns that providers
        would have to close or would have insufficient resources to deliver care to those who
        needed it. This would have increased the pressure on healthcare providers, unpaid
        carers and community organisations.


  Viability of care providers during the pandemic
  6.59. Prior to the Covid-19 pandemic, across the UK, the adult social care sector had
        experienced the closure of services and shortages of care provision.114 Care homes
        and domiciliary care providers therefore encountered a perfect storm during the
        pandemic as the number of people being cared for and income declined, at the
        same time as outgoings increased.115

  6.60. The decline in the number of people using adult social care services is likely to
        have been caused by the increase in care home deaths and fewer people entering
        care homes due to fear that they may become infected or would be prevented from
        seeing their loved ones.116 For example:

          • Occupancy rates in care homes in England declined from 87% in April 2020 to
              between 79% and 82% in August 2020, and to between 76% and 81% by August
              2021.117
          • Some care homes in Wales were operating below 50% occupancy during the
              pandemic (the majority needed approximately 90% occupancy to be viable).118
          • In Scotland, occupancy rates in care homes reduced from 89.9% in December
              2019 to 83.2% in December 2020, and improved only marginally to 84.3% by
              December 2021.119
          • Several care homes in Northern Ireland experienced a significant decline in the
              number of residents and had problems filling beds.120

                          Chapter 6: The impact on unpaid carers, care workers and care providers




6.61.    There were also concerns about the viability of domiciliary care providers. UK-wide
         analysis by the Homecare Association in April 2020 estimated that domiciliary care
         providers could experience a loss of income of 15% and incur additional costs of 22%
         due to PPE and staffing, for example.121 In June 2020, the Welsh Government noted
         the immediate concern of representatives in the sector about the impact of reduced
         demand for domiciliary care.122 As at 10 July 2020, almost 4,000 domiciliary care
         packages in Northern Ireland had been stopped as a result of the pandemic.123

6.62. The UK government and devolved administrations took steps at different times to
      provide additional funding to support care homes and domiciliary care providers with
      the decline in demand.

6.63. In Northern Ireland, an income support guarantee was available to care homes
      from 17 March 2020 to the end of July 2021 and, in response to feedback from the
      sector about additional costs, a lump sum payment for care homes was announced
      by the Department of Health (Northern Ireland) on 27 April 2020.124 On 23 March
      2020, representatives of domiciliary care providers in Northern Ireland requested
      urgent support from the Chief Medical Officer and the Department of Health
      (Northern Ireland) following a considerable number of cancellations.125 Funding for
      domiciliary care providers was made available from 5 May to 31 October 2020, with
      conditions.126

6.64. Local authorities in Wales could use the Local Government Hardship Fund from the
      end of April 2020 to support care homes that were unable to operate fully after a
      Covid-19 death, but it did not apply to all types of funded care until 5 August 2020.127
      This led one Welsh care home to consider closure in July 2020.128 Care home
      managers had different experiences accessing the funds. One manager described
      how the widening of the fund in August 2020 saved them and the care home
      group.129 Another manager said:

              “Financially crippled by loss of residents, unable to receive money from
               government as [local authority] won’t release any money to us, homes will go
               under as already struggling because of underfunding of social care before
               Covid, now we don’t stand a chance.”130

=== PAGE 201 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




               Funding for domiciliary care providers in Wales was announced at the start of August
               2020.131

      6.65. In response to concerns from the sector, the Scottish Government made financial
            support (also referred to as ‘sustainability payments’) available to social care
            providers from 12 May 2020. Some care homes that were under-occupied as a direct
            result of the pandemic could claim additional payments.132

      6.66. Many local authorities in England paid domiciliary care providers based on planned
            activity (rather than in arrears) from April 2020, which the Homecare Association
            described as “pivotal” in sustaining these services during the pandemic.133 The
            Infection Control Fund in England (which operated from 15 May 2020 to 31 March
            2022) was intended to help all care providers stay financially viable when they had
            reduced occupancy. However, grants from the fund had to cover many additional
            costs, including infection prevention and control measures.134

      6.67.    The Homecare Association described how they felt that access to additional funding
               favoured care homes. Smaller providers were particularly vulnerable to closure, as
               they did not have the financial protection of some larger organisations.135 The impact
               in England was seen during 2021, when the demand for domiciliary care returned at
               the same time as staff vacancies increased and there were significant problems with
               recruitment. Domiciliary care providers reported that demand had exceeded supply,
               as some were unable to continue with their existing workload or take on new clients.
               In August 2021, only 70% of 843 domiciliary care providers in England were able to
               meet the existing needs of care recipients.136

      6.68. Financial arrangements governing the amount of funding that care providers receive
            for each individual in their care are complex and outside the scope of this Report.
            However, given that the UK government and devolved administrations knew before
            the pandemic that the care sector was in a precarious position, it was foreseeable
            that a significant and sudden increase in costs and a decline in demand could
            threaten the viability of many care providers who were already struggling.


      Problems accessing funding for care providers
      6.69. The purpose, timing and conditions attached to additional funding for care providers
            varied across the UK. Generally, funding was intended to cover additional costs

        or lost income due to Covid-19. Funding for the adult social care sector is also
        considered in Chapter 3: Protective measures. Wider funding is examined by the
        Inquiry in Module 9: Economic response.

6.70. Covid-19 funding packages were provided by the four governments, with restrictions
      or conditions on how it could be spent, to local authorities and trusts to distribute to
      the sector.137 While the funding differed, there were nationwide and local issues that
      care providers consistently experienced:

        • Funds were slow to reach some parts of the sector and the providers most in
            need.138 A care home owner in Northern Ireland said that financial support from
            the Health and Social Care trust (via the government and Public Health Agency)
            came too late.139 The Independent Health and Care Providers (Northern Ireland)
            said that the funding processes were “at times slow and time-consuming”.140
        • There were inconsistencies between local authorities as to the proof
            of expenditure care providers were required to submit to access funds.141
            Each of the 22 local authorities in Wales had different processes and conditions.142
        • Some requests from local authorities and trusts for proof were also
            disproportionate. A provider in Wales had to provide 60 payslips to evidence
            a spend of £2,000.143 Reporting and gathering the evidence was a significant
            administrative burden for providers when resources were already stretched,
            and absorbed some of the funds.144
        • Nationwide restrictions about how the funds could be spent did not always
            correspond to the care providers’ needs at the time.145 In England, funds could
            not be used to purchase high-efficiency particulate air (HEPA) filters until the end
            of 2021.146

6.71.   From December 2020, Scottish Government funding was focused on supporting
        providers with additional costs incurred by the pandemic and was intended to be
        more targeted and less administratively burdensome.147 However, a lack of capacity
        to process claims led to some payments being delayed by over a year, particularly
        during later pandemic peaks.148

=== PAGE 203 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




  6.72. While the funding had to respond to the needs of the sector, the delays in decisions
        at a national level to extend funding (including extensions only being made very
        close to, or after, the funding period had ended) prevented care providers and
        local authorities from being able to undertake long-term planning.149 Providers
        in England described funding coming to an end as akin to approaching a “cliff
        edge”.150 The National Care Forum said that it was “unhelpful, insufficient, inefficient
        and bureaucratic” for the funding to be drip-fed to the sector, and the Association
        of Directors of Adult Social Services agreed that the funding arrangements were
        problematic.151

  6.73. Additional funding was a lifeline for many care providers, but there was a lack of
        advance planning by the UK government, Welsh Government, Scottish Government
        and Department of Health (Northern Ireland) about the financial support that
        might be needed to remain sustainable and to meet increasing costs caused by a
        pandemic. As a result, financial packages were developed in response to concerns
        from the sector about the ability to remain open. The barriers and challenges
        providers encountered in trying to access the funds could have been mitigated
        by advance planning and better engagement with the sector.

  6.74.   Michelle Dyson (Interim Director General of Adult Social Care at the Department
          of Health and Social Care from September 2020, permanent from May 2021 to
          July 2025) told the Inquiry that the UK government now has the ability to deliver
          funding directly to care providers, without the need to go through a local authority.152
          This should help improve access to funding during a pandemic for care providers
          in England. The devolved administrations are encouraged to explore options for
          the distribution of targeted funding to different parts of the social care sector.


  Workforce capacity during the pandemic
  6.75. At the outset of the pandemic, high levels of staff vacancies and turnover in the
        social care sector meant that the workforce was fragile and lacking resilience; it
        therefore struggled to cope with the additional demands caused by the pandemic.153

  6.76. Despite the decline in occupancy in some care homes, pre-existing staff shortages
        and an increase in workforce absences during the pandemic led to concerns that
        providers would have insufficient workforce capacity to deliver care.154 For example,
        in March 2020, care providers in England reported that 15% to 25% of their workforce
        was absent for reasons related to Covid-19.155

                         Chapter 6: The impact on unpaid carers, care workers and care providers




6.77.    Many care homes relied on agency staff to increase staff capacity. Although agency
         staff provided valuable support, they were more expensive for providers and
         often needed training and supervision so could not always fill the gap caused by
         absences.156 There were also insufficient numbers of agency staff.157

6.78. Initiatives to increase the number of people available to work in adult social care
      included the following:

         • A social care workforce appeal in Northern Ireland between April 2020 and the
             spring of 2021 placed 900 people in temporary positions in social care, social
             work, allied health, pharmacy and psychology.158
         • A recruitment portal was operated by the Scottish Social Services Council from
             14 April 2020 to 1 February 2022. Only 40 people had been employed when it
             closed.159
         • In August 2021, the Welsh Government funded a social care recruitment campaign
             that led to a 27% increase in job applications. It is not clear whether the advice in
             October 2021 to continue the funding was followed.160

6.79. The Department of Health and Social Care aimed to increase the adult social care
      workforce in England by 20,000 within three months of 15 April 2020. As part of this
      initiative, it funded ‘Join Social Care’, a recruitment hub and online training platform.
      The platform launched in May 2020 and, by June 2020 (the last point at which data
      were collected by the department), 471 people had completed the training.161 The
      department had no way to assess whether the target of 20,000 workers was met.162

6.80. The Workforce Capacity Fund (considered in Chapter 3: Protective measures) led
      to the recruitment of almost 40,000 staff in England.163 When the fund ceased at
      the end of March 2021, the Department of Health and Social Care did not apply for
      additional funding because it understood that there was reduced pressure on care
      homes and that fewer staff were testing positive for Covid-19.164 However, during
      the summer of 2021, there was an increase in workforce absences, due in part to
      stress, anxiety and depression. By September 2021, the National Care Forum and
      Homecare Association reported an immediate workforce crisis due to large-scale
      absences and people leaving the sector.165 This was attributed to a combination of:

  Module 6: Care sector




                 “total burn out and exhaustion from the last few months, growing levels of mental
                   health, poverty amongst the workforce triggering departures for better paid
                   work”.166

  6.81.     The Department of Health and Social Care requested urgent funding from the UK
            government to support local authorities and care providers to retain and enhance
            capacity in the workforce and, in October 2021, the Workforce Recruitment and
            Retention Fund was introduced.167 However, the department’s own analysis
            concluded:

                 “The funds did not have a measurable positive or negative impact on overall
                  recruitment during the funded period (although they may have had an impact
                  later on). Feedback highlighted that the lead-in time or fund period was too short
                  to have a tangible impact on recruitment activities.”168

  6.82. Although the various recruitment initiatives and additional funding provided some
        short-term solutions to ongoing staff recruitment and retention issues, they did not
        help to maintain permanent staff to make the workforce more resilient later in the
        pandemic or during a future pandemic.169

  6.83. It is likely that additional people will be needed to work in the adult social care sector
        in a future pandemic. Plans should be developed by the Department of Health and
        Social Care, Welsh Government, Scottish Government and Department of Health
        (Northern Ireland) in conjunction with local authorities, trusts and the sector to
        ensure that capacity can be increased at pace.


  The use of easements in England, Wales and Scotland
  6.84. During the Covid-19 pandemic, local authorities in England, Wales and Scotland
        were expected to comply with their legal duties to carry out needs and financial
        assessments for people who may require social care and to provide that care and
        support.170 However, where pressure on the workforce and the level of demand
        meant that they could not do so, local authorities could implement measures known
        as ‘easements’.

  6.85. Introduced by legislation in England, Scotland and Wales from the start of April 2020,
        easements allowed local authorities to streamline resources and prioritise care for

          to 200 new assessment referrals each week and 20% of its staff were unavailable.172
          Easements were intended to be used only when absolutely necessary and full
          services were to be restored as soon as it was reasonably practicable to do so.173

6.86. Easements were not introduced in Northern Ireland, as Health and Social Care trusts
      were permitted from 26 March 2020 to interpret, with some flexibility, the guidance
      on assessments and reviews.174

6.87.     In April 2020, representatives in the care sector raised concerns that it was unclear
          how easements should be implemented, leaving decisions open to interpretation
          and potential legal challenge.175 Local authorities that used easements received
          interest and some criticism from the media and representative groups.176

6.88. In practice, easements were rarely used. In Scotland, between four and six local
      authorities used easements at any one time up until they were suspended on
      30 November 2020.177 Easements were not used by any local authority in Wales; they
      were suspended in December 2020.178 In England, 8 out of the 152 local authorities
      used easements. They had not been used for over a year when they expired in July
      2021.179

6.89. Before the Covid-19 pandemic, local authorities in England could use existing
      flexibilities in the Care Act 2014 to ensure that they complied with their legal

           duties.180 This could include conducting assessments virtually or rescheduling

           with which they were familiar, rather than using easements.181 It is not possible to

  6.90. Since 1 April 2023, in England, the Care Quality Commission has had a duty to
        inspect and monitor the way in which local authorities exercise their responsibilities
        under the Care Act 2014 and to publish a report of the assessment.183 This has
        potential to improve the oversight and accountability of its implementation.184

  6.91.    Easements may be required in a future pandemic and, in conjunction with the adult
           social care sector, should be prepared for in advance. This will help to ensure that
           there is more transparency and greater understanding about when it is appropriate
           to use easements. It is also important to monitor and record where easements
           are used to target support in those areas and ensure that a full service resumes
           promptly.


  Vaccination as a condition of deployment
  6.92. In December 2020, vaccines for Covid-19 became available across the UK and front-
        line care workers were in a priority group.185 Although there was widespread support
        for the vaccination programme in the care sector, vaccine uptake by care workers
        in care homes was initially below the level that government scientific advisers
        recommended was needed to result in a sustained reduction in positive tests.186

  6.93. In response to the low uptake, on 16 June 2021, the UK government announced
        that a mandatory Covid-19 vaccination policy would be introduced in care homes.
        The policy came into effect from 11 November 2021 and required anyone working
        in a Care Quality Commission registered care home in England to have two doses
        of a Covid-19 vaccine unless medically exempt.187 On 9 November 2021, the UK
        government stated its intention to extend the policy to all care workers working in

6.94. In October 2021, the Department of Health and Social Care acknowledged that
      introducing the vaccination as a condition of deployment risked exacerbating the
      workforce pressures in adult social care.191 The department had predicted that
      approximately 37,000 care workers could leave the sector. According to the Capacity
      Tracker (which recorded data on the social care sector in England), the workforce fell
      by 19,300 between July and December 2021. It is not possible to know the number
      of staff who left or were dismissed as a result of the policy, as the reasons for
      workforce departures were not systematically collected.192

6.95. The Inquiry heard that some staff left their job as a result of the policy. For example:

         • A manager of a domiciliary care agency said that care workers resigned because
             they were unhappy about the policy.193
         • One care home lost 3 of its 51 members of staff when the policy was introduced
             and another lost 5.194
         • Five of the eight care home managers who participated in research commissioned
             by the Cabinet Office said staff had left due to the policy.195
         • A care home manager told Every Story Matters that half of their care workers left,
             some of whom had worked at the home for years.196

6.96. Preparing for and implementing the policy and managing workforce shortages
      created an additional financial and administrative burden for care providers and
      managers at a time when resources were already strained.197

  6.97.     The Workforce Recruitment and Retention Fund was intended, among other things,
            to reduce workforce pressures, including those caused by the vaccination as a
            condition of deployment policy.200 However, the fund could not mitigate the distrust
            and division the policy had fostered. There was particular concern in the sector that
            the initial policy did not apply to all staff working in care settings or to NHS staff.201
            Sir Sajid Javid (Secretary of State for Health and Social Care from June 2021 to July
            2022) accepted that the policy would have contributed to workforce pressures but
            maintained that it was the right policy at the time.202

  6.98. While the rationale for mandatory vaccination was to save lives and protect the
        vulnerable, the policy was introduced at a time when the social care sector was
        already experiencing staff shortages and low morale. It undoubtedly increased the
        pressure on care providers and led to social care staff leaving the sector. As noted in
        the Inquiry’s Module 4 Report, a focus on increasing vaccine confidence among the
        social care workforce in areas of low uptake would have been more effective.203


  Mortality data in the adult social care workforce
  6.99. There was a greater risk of Covid-19 mortality for the social care workforce than for
        the general population, due to difficulties preventing transmission of infection in
        care homes and the close physical contact needed when providing care. In addition,
        many care workers often work in multiple care settings and so were more likely to be
        exposed to the virus. These factors meant that it was foreseeable that the social care
        workforce would be vulnerable to mortality from a pandemic virus.204

                          Chapter 6: The impact on unpaid carers, care workers and care providers




6.100. Despite the evident vulnerability of the workforce, prior to and during the pandemic,
       the data on the number of care workers who died from Covid-19 were not centrally
       recorded and the available data were not always accurate or comprehensive.


Recording occupation in mortality data during the pandemic
6.101. Data about deaths of care workers play an important role in understanding the
       severity and transmission of a virus and ensuring that the workforce is protected.
       During the Covid-19 pandemic, mortality data by occupation were collected in two
       ways. The first relied on the information provided when a death was registered and
       the second required social care providers to report the death. There were important
       caveats to the data collected through both methods.

Registration of deaths

6.102. The UK’s statistics agencies – National Records of Scotland, the Office for National
       Statistics, and the Northern Ireland Statistics and Research Agency – collected data
       on deaths involving Covid-19 by occupation by relying on the information provided
       when the death was registered.205 However, in England, Wales and Northern Ireland,
       the deceased’s occupation was not always recorded on a death certificate and,
       where it was, it was not always up to date.206

6.103. At the outset of the pandemic, the statistics agencies’ analysis of mortality rates
       by occupation was not available. As the pandemic progressed, these data became
       available but were not consistently reliable or comparable:

          • On 11 May 2020, the Office for National Statistics first published provisional data
             on registered deaths by occupation. They showed that in England and Wales, up
             to 20 April 2020, there had been 98 care worker deaths. The data were published
             only twice more up to January 2021.207 Separate figures for Wales were first
             published on 26 June 2020.208
          • On 17 June 2020, National Records of Scotland first published a monthly analysis
             of registered deaths, which showed that care worker death rates between March
             and May 2020 were 25 per 100,000, compared with 20 per 100,000 for all
             occupations.209

               • The Northern Ireland Statistics and Research Agency first published a count

      Notification by care providers

      6.104. At the outset of the pandemic, care providers, trusts and health boards were not
             required or asked to report the deaths of care workers. This changed at different
             times in each nation and notifications of deaths had to be provided to the regulator
             or relevant government department.

      6.105. From 17 April 2020, care providers in Scotland were requested to notify the Care
             Inspectorate of a staff Covid-19 death.211 Providers also had to report confirmed or
             presumed Covid-19 deaths to the Crown Office and Procurator Fiscal Service from
             21 May 2020 (applied retrospectively).212

      6.106. Health boards and care home providers in Wales were asked to report care home
             worker deaths to Public Health Wales from 23 April 2020, but this was not mandatory
             and did not capture all deaths. Public Health Wales said that, as the pandemic
             progressed, the data became less complete and their accuracy could not be
             guaranteed.213

      6.107. From 12 May 2020, Health and Social Care trusts in Northern Ireland were requested
             to report the number of care workers who had died with or from Covid-19 to the
             Department of Health (Northern Ireland) on a daily basis.214 However, there was no
             way of ensuring that all trusts provided the data and, when they did, the data were
             not verified or collated.215

      6.108. In late May 2020, the Department of Health and Social Care asked care providers
             in England to notify them by email of a staff Covid-19 death.216 Joanna Killian,
             Chief Executive of the Local Government Association from March 2024 to July
             2026, said that they were concerned about the email being missed and no action
             being taken.217 Ms Whately recalled that the Department of Health and Social Care
             obtained estimates of the number of deaths or data about mortality in the workforce
             through a compensation scheme.218 This led to significant discrepancies in the

6.109. Developments in the routine collection of reliable data on Covid-19 deaths in the
       adult social care workforce were slow, inconsistent and inadequate across the UK.
       This meant that the number of deaths in the workforce and any potential increased
       mortality risks were not identified as quickly as they could have been.


Recording ethnicity in mortality data during the pandemic
6.110. Data recording the ethnicity of people who die with a pandemic virus are key to
       understanding whether it has a disproportionate impact on a specific ethnic group.
       This is particularly important for the adult social care workforce, where a significant
       proportion of workers are from an ethnic minority group.220 For example, in London,
       67% of the adult social care workforce was from an ethnic minority background (in
       April 2020) and in England it was 22% (according to data from 2020/21).221

6.111.   Throughout the pandemic, mortality rates were higher in ethnic minority groups
         than in the White British population in the UK.222 A review of disparities in the risk
         and outcomes of Covid-19 by Public Health England found that people from ethnic
         minorities may be more exposed to the virus and are more likely to be diagnosed
         through their occupation and living conditions.223

6.112. In June 2020, Public Health England recommended mandatory collection of
       “comprehensive and quality” ethnicity data, including on death certificates, and that
       these data should be “readily available to local health and care partners to inform
       actions to mitigate the impact of COVID-19 on BAME [Black, Asian and minority
       ethnic] communities”.224 Public Health England considered that this could help to
       make a significant difference to the lives and experiences of these communities.

6.113. The Frontline Migrant Health Workers Group said that mortality data on migrant
       care workers would have been crucial to understand the impact of the pandemic
       on this group and encouraged better collection of such data in future.225 The Local

                Government Association advocated for local authorities to have access to mortality

      6.114. Since the start of 2012, the ethnicity of the deceased person has been collected, on
             a voluntary basis, when a death is registered in Scotland.227 The Office for National
             Statistics linked data from the 2011 census and, later, the 2021 census in England with
             death certificates to obtain some information about the ethnicity of people who died
             from Covid-19.228

      6.115. In Northern Ireland, only the country of birth was (and continues to be) requested on
             death certificates. The Northern Ireland Statistics and Research Agency considered
             that this information could be more meaningful than ethnicity data due to the small
             ethnic minority population in Northern Ireland. However, it acknowledged that place
             of birth did not equate to ethnicity.229

      6.116. As part of Public Health Wales’s data collection during the pandemic, ethnicity was
             requested, but, for reasons unknown, it was unavailable for one in four reported
             deaths.230 In its combined Report relating to Modules 2, 2A, 2B and 2C, the Inquiry
             noted that in Wales there were no datasets allowing for any meaningful comparison
             of the impact of the Covid-19 pandemic on occupation or ethnicity.231

      6.117. During the pandemic, there was insufficient collection of ethnicity and occupation
             data on death certificates in the UK. As a result, the increased Covid-19 mortality
             risk among care workers in a particular ethnic group was not identified as quickly
             as it could have been. Death certificates in England and Wales now record ethnicity
             if it has been self-declared in medical records.232 Despite some improvements to
             the collection of ethnicity data, across the UK, comprehensive and consistent data
             collection is needed to understand the impact of a pandemic on ethnic minority
             groups.233

                          Chapter 6: The impact on unpaid carers, care workers and care providers




Future collection of mortality data for the social care workforce
during a pandemic
6.118. The fragmented nature of the adult social care sector is one of the primary barriers
       to the collection of data. Care providers also had to respond to many additional
       requests for data during the pandemic, which have been documented throughout
       this Report. Notwithstanding this, it is important to have data about the deaths
       of care workers to ensure that the workforce is protected and to understand the
       severity and transmission of the virus. The Trades Union Congress, the Royal College
       of Nursing and the Local Government Association agreed that the collection of
       mortality data for care workers needs to be improved.234

6.119. The Public Health Agency (Northern Ireland) said that mortality data for social care
       staff could be available in future by linking the workforce register with healthcare
       records and that it would be a useful source of information to understand infections
       in care workers and to inform guidance.235 The collection and publication of these
       data could also be one way of showing that care workers are valued and recognised
       (see Chapter 8: Recognition and protection).

6.120. In its Module 3 Report, the Inquiry recommends the development of mechanisms
       to improve the collection and publication of data about the deaths of healthcare
       workers during a pandemic and that such data should be comparable across the
       four nations of the UK.236 There should be equivalent data collected for the adult
       social care sector. The Inquiry recommends that these data be collected during a
       pandemic. Any mechanism for collecting the data must be simple and quick, and not
       impose a disproportionate burden on care providers.


         Recommendation 6: Collection of data about deaths of adult social
         care workers during a pandemic
         The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
         Ireland Executive should develop mechanisms to collect, analyse and publish data
         systematically about the deaths of adult social care workers during a pandemic.
         The data must be comparable across the four nations of the UK.
         The mechanisms should be developed with the adult social care workforce
         regulators, public health agencies and employers of care workers.

Chapter 7: Deaths and end

=== PAGE 216 ===
                                                             Chapter 7: Deaths and end of life




Introduction
7.1.   The vulnerability of people using adult social care was widely known before the
       Covid-19 pandemic. In general, the age and underlying health conditions of adult
       social care users mean that they have a higher risk of serious illness and death
       from respiratory diseases than the general population. This resulted in a significant
       increase in the number of people who died in care homes and at home during the
       Covid-19 pandemic. This chapter examines the delay in establishing and publishing
       the number of Covid-19 deaths in people who used adult social care services.

7.2.   While social care staff went to extraordinary efforts to provide compassionate end-
       of-life care, some people did not experience a dignified death. Many people died
       alone without the comfort or support of their loved ones in their final days and last
       moments. Bereaved families still question the circumstances of their loved ones’
       deaths and feel guilty for not being present. Their grief was compounded by the
       Covid-19 restrictions, which prevented them from giving their loved one the funeral
       they would have wished for and deserved.

7.3.   There were also concerns that decisions on Do Not Attempt Cardiopulmonary
       Resuscitation (DNACPR) were made for people using adult social care services
       without proper consultation or a personalised assessment. This chapter considers
       how advance care planning and DNACPR decision-making might be improved in
       adult social care in the future. It also looks at the changes to end-of-life care and
       whether the social care workforce had the necessary training and skills.


Understanding deaths in adult social care
7.4.   At the start of the Covid-19 pandemic, there were concerns about the ability to
       access accurate data on Covid-19 deaths of care home residents and of people
       receiving domiciliary care who died at home.

7.5.   The importance of these data cannot be overemphasised. Comprehensive and
       reliable mortality data enable decision-makers to understand the true impact and
       severity of a pandemic. The data can inform decisions and guidance about how and
       when to protect and support people who are vulnerable to a pandemic virus, such as
       those using adult social care services. They can also be used to evaluate the impact
       of interventions such as expedited discharges from hospital to a care home.


Mortality data for care homes
7.6.   During the pandemic, 43,088 care home residents died with Covid-19 mentioned
       on their death certificate, comprising:

               • 4,272 in Scotland;
               • 1,961 in Wales; and
               • 987 in Northern Ireland.            1

               Covid-19 in the early months of the pandemic and again from late 2020 to early 2021
               (see Figure 3).2 Covid-19 was the second leading cause of death in care homes in all
               four nations of the UK in 2020.3

7.8.      There was no central mechanism for collecting data about care home residents who
          died of Covid-19. Mortality data were obtained from three different sources:

          • regulators who were notified of the death of a care home resident by care
              providers;4
          • statistics agencies who used information provided when a death was registered;
              and
          • reporting systems and reports that were developed during the pandemic by
              public health agencies in Wales, England and Northern Ireland in response to
              an identified need for more timely mortality data to monitor trends in Covid-19
              infection.5

7.9.      The utility of the data, however, was limited by issues with their timeliness and the
          different ways in which they were collected.

Timely access to data

7.10.     The UK government and devolved administrations could not access comprehensive
          mortality data for care home residents for some weeks after the first registered
          Covid-19 care home death, and it was months before the data distinguished between
          Covid-19 deaths and deaths from other causes.

7.11.     The first Covid-19-related care home deaths were registered in mid-March 2020
          in England, Wales and Scotland and in the final week of March 2020 in Northern
          Ireland.6 As they did not have access to mortality data for care homes at this time,
          Helen Whately MP (Minister of State for Social Care at the Department of Health and
          Social Care from February 2020 to September 2021) explained that this contributed
          to feeling as if they “were operating in the dark about the extent of the pandemic in
          care homes”.7

7.12.     The Care Inspectorate in Scotland provided, initially, a daily summary of Covid-19
          deaths in care services to the Scottish Government from mid-March 2020.8 From
          June 2020, care home providers were required to report a confirmed or suspected

               Covid-19 death to the Care Inspectorate within 24 hours, which the Care Inspectorate

      7.13.    From 26 March 2020, Health and Social Care trusts in Northern Ireland were
               requested to report to the Public Health Agency (Northern Ireland) any deaths
               occurring within 28 days of a positive Covid-19 test, with an option to report the
               place of death.10 However, Robin Swann (Minister of Health for Northern Ireland
               from January 2020 to October 2022 and from February to May 2024) said that
               he only received these data up to 19 April 2020 and that they were not specific
               to care homes.11

      7.14.    The data for England and Wales provided by the Office for National Statistics
               included the place of death from 7 April 2020.12 While these data were
               comprehensive, a delay between the death occurring and being registered
               (usually seven days) meant that some deaths were not included in the weekly
               figures until 11 days afterwards.13

      7.15.    In England, the delay in the registration of deaths, and the fact that care providers
               did not always promptly report deaths during the pandemic, created a discrepancy
               in the figures provided to the UK government and, in April 2020, under-reporting
               of care home deaths by the Care Quality Commission.14 Ms Whately said that it was
               not until 9 April 2020 that she received the “correct” data from the Care Quality
               Commission showing care home (and domiciliary care) deaths.15 Ms Whately
               described being “extremely concerned” by the data, adding:

                   “[ W]hilst it was not possible at this early stage to be able to say that all of those
                     additional deaths were as a result of Covid-19, the spike in deaths was so stark
                     that I could only conclude that the pandemic had contributed significantly to the
                     increased death rate.”16

      7.16.    Care Inspectorate Wales developed a care home deaths report that was available
               from 15 April 2020 and shared daily with the Welsh Government from 29 April 2020.17
               It was not until 9 November 2020 that the report separated Covid-19 deaths and
               deaths from other causes.18 Health boards in Wales were asked (but not required)
               to report Covid-19-related deaths of care home residents to Public Health Wales
               from 23 April 2020 and the data were provided daily to the Welsh Government and

          health boards.19 However, Public Health Wales told the Inquiry that, as the pandemic
          progressed, the data it received became less complete and more varied as health
          boards adopted different systems and methods for reporting, which might have
          contributed to under or over-reporting.20

7.17.     The Northern Ireland Statistics and Research Agency published weekly data about
          registered Covid-19-related deaths by place, including care homes, from 24 April
          2020.21 The Department of Health (Northern Ireland) asked for more frequent
          reporting in May 2020 but the Northern Ireland Statistics and Research Agency was
          unable to do so for a number of reasons, which included the time it took to register
          deaths and analyse the data as well as a lack of resources.22

7.18.     If the UK government and devolved administrations had been able to access
          comprehensive data about Covid-19 deaths among care home residents from early
          March 2020, it could have informed the timing of policy decisions and guidance on
          matters such as infection prevention and control, testing and personal protective
          equipment. The Local Government Association, the Commissioner for Older People
          for Northern Ireland, the Welsh Local Government Association and the National Care
          Forum agreed that the data could have informed policy and helped providers and
          local authorities to protect care home residents.23

Differences in data collection

7.19.     It is not possible to compare data across the UK or produce a UK-wide figure for care
          home resident deaths, due to the significant differences in the way mortality data
          were collected and the definitions adopted in adult social care.24 Across the UK, it
          was not always clear that different measures were used to calculate the number of
          deaths, which was difficult for those relying on the data.25

7.20.     In any event, some care home residents died in hospital, not in the care home.
          While the data collected by the statistics agencies for England, Wales and Northern
          Ireland identified care home residents who died in hospital, the data for Scotland
          did not.26 The Public Health Agency (Northern Ireland) reported 501 care home
          deaths, which did not include people who died in a location other than a care home

                or those who died with a clinical diagnosis of Covid-19. The Public Health Agency

      Future collection of mortality data for care home residents

      7.21.     The Public Health Agency (Northern Ireland) considers that the collection, sharing
                and linking of data during a pandemic needs to be improved in order to understand
                the impact of the virus and to inform public health advice.28 Professor Fu-Meng
                Khaw (National Director of Health Protection and Screening Services and Executive
                Medical Director of Public Health Wales from June 2021) told the Inquiry that a single
                point of registration and collection of mortality data would be helpful, particularly
                during the early stages of a pandemic, when it is critical to be able to monitor how
                serious and infectious a virus could be.29

      7.22.     At the start of the Covid-19 pandemic, policy decisions to protect vulnerable people
                living in care homes could not be informed by timely, accurate data. Although there
                were some improvements in the collection of mortality data in adult social care as
                the pandemic progressed, it was vital that national and local decision-makers had
                early access to real-time, reliable and comparable data to understand and monitor
                the effect of Covid-19 in care homes.

      7.23.     Each of the UK government, Welsh Government, Scottish Government and Northern
                Ireland Executive should develop a single, consistent measure for daily reporting of
                deaths in care homes from a pandemic virus. It should be possible to compare the
                data across the UK. It is also important to have clear definitions with an explanation
                about the different collection methods and any limitations to the data. This will help
                to ensure that the data are readily available and easily understood in future.


      Mortality data for people using domiciliary care services
      7.24.     Between late March to late April 2020 and early January to mid-February 2021,
                the number of people dying at home increased and stayed above average after
                the pandemic peaks had subsided.30 In England and Wales, there were more
                than 59,000 more deaths at home between March 2020 and 28 May 2021 (a 39%
                increase from the 2015 to 2019 average). The King’s Fund (an independent charity)
                said that these data strongly suggest that the large number of people dying at home
                would likely have died in hospital outside of a pandemic.31 In Scotland, the proportion

          of deaths at home increased from 25.6% (10,433) in 2019 to 31.4% (14,538) in 2020.32
          Similarly, in Northern Ireland, excess deaths at home (the difference between the
          actual and expected number of deaths) accounted for 77.9% of the total excess
          deaths between March 2020 and December 2022.33

7.25.     Domiciliary care providers in Scotland and England are required to notify the
          regulator when a person to whom they provide care dies.34 However, during the
          pandemic, the data were not comprehensive or always accessible. For example,
          providers only had to report it if the person died while they were receiving care.35
          In England, between 10 April and 9 June 2020, there were 6,523 deaths of recipients
          of domiciliary care; this was more than double the three-year average. Of these
          deaths, 819 (12.6%) involved Covid-19.36 The data were only published (in England)
          on two occasions (15 May and 3 July 2020).37

7.26.     The majority of deaths at home in the four nations of the UK were not described
          as related to Covid-19, in part due to the lack of testing early in the pandemic.
          Therefore, the number of deaths at home due to Covid-19 is likely to be higher
          than reported.38

7.27.     Mortality data about people receiving domiciliary care during a pandemic are
          vital to inform policy decisions on protective measures for people using social
          care services and the staff delivering care, including the distribution of resources
          and support for end-of-life care. Each of the UK government, Welsh Government,
          Scottish Government and Northern Ireland Executive must establish mechanisms
          to collect mortality data for people who use domiciliary care services and die at
          home from a pandemic disease. Care Inspectorate Wales and the Regulation and
          Quality Improvement Authority in Northern Ireland should consider introducing a
          requirement on domiciliary care providers to report the death of a person using
          their services from a pandemic virus.

      Module 6: Care sector




      Advance care planning in adult social care
      7.28.     Advance or anticipatory care planning involves discussing and recording an
                individual’s preferences for future medical care. This may include how and where
                they want to be cared for and treatments that they would or would not want.39

      7.29.     Guidelines on advance care planning produced in 2018 by the National Institute
                for Health and Care Excellence (often referred to as NICE) state that input from a
                clinician may be appropriate in some circumstances. Therefore:

                    “Skilled practitioners need to be able to have sensitive conversations with
                     people in the context of a trusting and collaborative relationship, and provide
                     the person with clear and accessible information to help them make these
                     important decisions.”40

      7.30.     Discussions about plans for advance care should be conducted at a pace that suits
                the individual and involve their loved ones (where possible).41


      Advance care planning during the Covid-19 pandemic
      7.31.     Pressures on the adult social care and healthcare sectors meant that it was more
                difficult to have sensitive conversations about advance care at a suitable pace during
                the pandemic. As a result, there were concerns that people had or may have been
                rushed into making a decision.42 A consultant in palliative medicine in Scotland said
                that, while it was important for advance care planning to be done quickly during
                the pandemic, “it wasn’t always done at the right time, right place, or by the right
                person”.43

      7.32.     Professor Stephen Barclay, expert witness on end-of-life care, said that there was
                evidence that hurried discussions had created a misunderstanding about the reason
                for advance care plans (some people thought that it was because of their age or
                health, or because of rationing) and that the quality of these conversations had
                deteriorated.44

      7.33.     Visiting restrictions meant that some discussions about advance care did not happen
                in person or with family members present.45 A person with care and support needs

7.34.    Additionally, not everyone received the support or information they needed to make
         an informed plan. The Care Quality Commission was told that, in England:

              “[O]lder people, people with dementia and people with a learning disability …
                were not supported to the extent they needed to be in advance care planning
                conversations, or given the information they needed in an accessible way.”47

         These factors understandably created confusion and distress for the individual, their
         loved ones and clinicians during a period of heightened anxiety.48

7.35.    Professor Barclay and fellow expert witness on end-of-life care Professor Katherine
         Sleeman said that people’s experience during the pandemic demonstrated the
         importance of discussions about advance care taking place in non-pandemic times,
         so that a plan is already in place. They reported a care home manager’s view that the
         lack of advance care planning before the pandemic had created “chaos, confusion,
         and obstacles that caused undue suffering”.49

7.36.    Advance care planning should be encouraged outside of a pandemic; greater public
         engagement on this topic will lead to a better understanding of its importance.

Personalised planning for advance care

7.37.    On 1 April 2020, a statement by the Royal College of General Practitioners,
         British Medical Association, Care Quality Commission and Care Provider Alliance
         stressed that:
