                “The importance of having a personalised care plan in place, especially for older
                 people, people who are frail or have other serious conditions has never been
                 more important than it is now during the COVID-19 Pandemic.”50

            While the statement was a reminder to health and social care professionals that
            advance care plans must continue to be made on an individual basis according
            to need, this did not always happen during the pandemic.

  7.38.     The Recommended Summary Plan for Emergency Care and Treatment (ReSPECT)
            is used in England and Scotland to complement the advance care planning process.
            It enables an individual and their clinician to establish which treatments (including
            cardiopulmonary resuscitation or CPR) would and would not benefit the patient. It
            can be completed in a hospital, care home or the community. A ReSPECT form may
            be used to record the outcome of the discussion.51

  7.39.     There were examples of the ReSPECT process not being followed during the
            pandemic, such as forms completed without proper engagement or a personalised
            assessment.


                 “These calls for the ReSPECT forms were done all over the phone and to see
                   residents that we care for being told that they weren’t going to get any
                   treatment because of their age, or disabilities or anything, it was just
                   heartbreaking. That was the most traumatic.”52

                 Care home worker, England, Every Story Matters



                 “October 2020, there were just blanket ReSPECT forms done for everybody.
                   They would literally just fill these forms out for the person and automatically
                   decide, so everybody over a certain age was automatically … deemed, to not
                   be worth reviving … every ReSPECT form had no comments, or no signatures
                   from residents.”53

             “[ W]here no decision has previously been made during advanced planning
               discussion (including the failure of relatives to return complete documents) and
               no completed document is returned within a suitable time frame, a best interests
               decision may well be made by the clinicians involved in your/your relatives care
               if you/they become unwell.”55

7.41.    Prior to and during the pandemic, there was no mandatory training on advance
         care planning for all social care workers in England, Scotland and Northern Ireland.
         This remains the case. In Wales, domiciliary care workers providing direct care were
         required to have, or be working towards, a qualification that included training about
         advance care planning. This training was not mandatory for residential care home
         workers providing direct care in Wales until October 2022.56

7.42.    In England, Scotland and Northern Ireland, additional guidance and resources
         were made available to support the workforce in advance care planning during the
         pandemic.57 However, in a report in January 2021, the Northern Ireland Assembly’s
         Committee for Health noted that some care home staff said they had felt pressure
         to lead a conversation on this topic and needed more training and medical input. It
         recommended training in advance care planning for relevant professionals.58

7.43.    The Care Quality Commission recorded in March 2021 that there was an evident
         risk of inappropriate decision-making and unsafe care or treatment if health and
         care staff were not fully informed about advance care planning.59 In March 2022,
         Universal Principles for Advance Care Planning was jointly published by a number
         of health and social care organisations and was intended for anyone involved in
         discussions about advance care and implementing a plan. It also emphasised
         the importance of training and education for health and social care professionals
         undertaking such discussions.60 The Inquiry endorses the calls for training.

7.44.    Some of the problems encountered with advance care planning during the pandemic
         may have been avoided had the health and social care professionals involved in the
         discussions received appropriate training. This would have equipped them with the
         necessary knowledge and skills to engage with people in a sensitive way.

      Module 6: Care sector




      Do Not Attempt Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation
      (DNACPR) decisions
      7.45.     Whether a person should undergo CPR in the event that their heart stops beating
                is a clinical decision that must be made by a healthcare professional. Ideally,
                the decision should be made in advance with the individual as part of a holistic
                discussion about their wishes for future care and treatment.61 They may be supported
                by loved ones and care workers. CPR can be effective if someone is otherwise fit
                and healthy, but it is unlikely to be successful and may cause more harm than good
                for people with a progressive terminal condition, a serious life-limiting illness or
                physical frailty.62

      7.46.     Where a person’s preference is not to undergo CPR, or a clinician considers that
                CPR would not be appropriate, a DNACPR form should be completed (on paper
                or digitally) and placed on their medical file.63

      7.47.     Since 2001 and during the pandemic, UK-wide professional guidance on DNACPR
                decision-making has stated that:64

                • DNACPR decisions should be based on an individualised assessment of the
                    patient’s health and that “[b]lanket policies that deny CPR or apply CPR to groups
                    of people, for example to all patients in a hospice, nursing home or particular
                    hospital ward, or to people above or below a certain age, are unethical and
                    probably unlawful”;65
                • communication with the patient, and (unless they have requested otherwise)
                    those close to them, must be clear, accurate, honest and timely;66 and
                • a DNACPR decision applies only to CPR and does not equate to a decision
                    to decline any other element of care or treatment (‘do not treat’).67

7.48.     The Inquiry heard that, during the pandemic, some DNACPR decisions were not
          based on a personalised assessment, were made without proper consultation with
          the individual or their loved ones and were wrongly interpreted as decisions not
          to treat.


‘Blanket’ DNACPR decisions for groups of people
7.49.     Early in the pandemic, a number of sources, including the media, reported concerns
          that DNACPR decisions were being applied to groups of people in adult social care
          (often referred to as ‘blanket’ DNACPRs) based on age, disability or health conditions
          and not on an individualised clinical assessment.68

7.50.     The Inquiry also heard examples of GPs adopting a blanket approach to DNACPR
          decisions:

          • Helen Hough, former care home owner and member of Covid-19 Bereaved
              Families for Justice Cymru, said that GPs made a DNACPR decision for all of their
              patients in mid-March 2020. In two cases, the residents’ families contacted the GP
              and the DNACPR was removed.69
          • A bereaved family member told Every Story Matters what they observed: “As
              lockdown started being discussed, the priority of the GPs for the care homes was
              to move all residents onto DNACPRs. At no point was there any discussion on best
              ways to support or treat residents with Covid. Blanket DNACPRs rather than
              asking ‘how will we treat people?’”70
          • A care home worker in Wales told Every Story Matters: “Our local doctor put a
              blanket DNACPR on all his patients to stop them taking up beds in the hospital
              which families contested.”71
          • The Care Quality Commission reported that one GP surgery in England sent
              letters to care homes for people with learning disabilities about making blanket
              DNACPR decisions. The letters were later retracted.72
          • One individual with care and support needs in Wales told Every Story Matters
              that she was “still shaken to the very core of my being, that they imposed
              ‘Do Not Resuscitate’ notices on those of us with a significant disability or
              over a certain age”.73

      Module 6: Care sector




      7.51.    There are no formal records of the number of DNACPR decisions that were made
               during the pandemic. Evidence submitted to the Care Quality Commission by 2,048
               adult social care providers in England showed that the number of people in these
               settings with a DNACPR in place increased from 16,876 to 26,555 between 17 March
               2020 and December 2020. Of those providers, 119 (6%) felt that people in their care
               had been subject to blanket DNACPR decisions, which the Care Quality Commission
               described as concerning. In its final report, published in March 2021, the Care Quality
               Commission recorded: “[W]e heard evidence from people, their families and carers
               that there had been ‘blanket’ DNACPR decisions in place.”74

      7.52.    The high number of reports of blanket DNACPR decisions led to concerns that there
               might have been national instructions to adopt this approach, but the UK government
               and devolved administrations said that they did not authorise such a policy.75
               The Care Quality Commission found no evidence of any instruction and neither has
               the Inquiry.76 Nevertheless, there were many reports of their inappropriate use.77
               DNACPR decisions made during the pandemic based on a person’s age, disability
               or health conditions, or without a personalised assessment to consider the person’s
               wishes and feelings, caused considerable distress and confusion for the individual,
               their loved ones and staff. This was unacceptable and must not happen again.

      Response to concerns about DNACPR decisions during the pandemic

      7.53.    On 1 April 2020, the Royal College of General Practitioners issued a UK-wide joint
               statement with the British Medical Association, the Care Provider Alliance and the
               Care Quality Commission. The statement emphasised that it was essential that
               DNACPR decisions be made on an individual basis, in consultation with the patient
               (or family members where the patient lacked capacity) and as part of advance care
               planning.78 Similar communications to the health and social care sectors followed in
               April 2020 from the UK government, Welsh Government and Scottish Government
               and from the Commissioner for Older People for Northern Ireland.79

      7.54.    In June and September 2020, the Department of Health (Northern Ireland) issued
               guidance highlighting the need for a DNACPR decision to be made with a GP and

7.55.    Concerns also continued to be raised elsewhere in the UK. For example, in
         November 2020, the Scottish Government had to provide materials on DNACPR
         decisions to Age Scotland to help it respond to queries and direct people to advice
         and support.82 The message that a DNACPR decision should be individualised
         had to be repeated in March 2021 by NHS England and the Welsh Government.83
         The repeated messaging on the need for personalised assessments suggests
         that there remains a gap in medical training.

Reviews of DNACPR decisions during the pandemic

7.56.    In October 2020, the Department of Health and Social Care commissioned the
         Care Quality Commission to review how DNACPR decisions had been made
         during the pandemic. The Care Quality Commission analysed the implementation
         of DNACPR guidance, including in primary care and care homes in England.84 Its
         interim report, published in November 2020, found that there had been confusion
         and miscommunication about the application of DNACPRs and that the providers
         appeared to be overwhelmed. The Care Quality Commission said that there was
         evidence of unacceptable and inappropriate DNACPR decisions being made at the
         start of the pandemic, but, following a quick response to the issue, there was no
         evidence to suggest that it had continued on a widespread basis. The Care Quality
         Commission said that all care providers should ensure that any DNACPR decision
         had been made appropriately.85

7.57.    Care Inspectorate Wales recorded in its annual 2020/21 report that there had
         been blanket DNACPR decisions made early in the pandemic, which it described
         as extremely concerning.86 It did not investigate individual reports of blanket
         DNACPRs, but Gillian Baranski, Chief Inspector of Care Inspectorate Wales
         from October 2016, said that it would have taken action if it had become an
         issue in the care sector.87

      Module 6: Care sector




      7.58.    Jeane Freeman, Cabinet Secretary for Health and Sport in the Scottish Government
               from June 2018 to May 2021, told the Inquiry:

                    “I do not recall any instances where there did in fact appear to have been
                     a blanket use of DNACPRs [in Scotland] but there were some instances
                     where a DNACPR decision appeared on a patient record where neither
                     patient or family were aware.”88

               The Care Inspectorate in Scotland said that it was unaware of specific allegations
               being made about DNACPR decisions, so it did not carry out an investigation.89

      7.59.    In Northern Ireland, Mr Swann said that he was aware that two trusts (the Belfast
               and the Western Health and Social Care trusts) had regularly reviewed DNACPRs.90
               The latter told the Inquiry that it was unable to comment on DNACPR decisions in
               care homes.91

      7.60.    Briege Donaghy, Chief Executive of the Regulation and Quality Improvement
               Authority in Northern Ireland from July 2021, said DNACPRs were “not an issue
               raised with us very much”.92 In preparation for the Inquiry, the Regulation and Quality
               Improvement Authority searched its records from between March 2020 and March
               2021, but none of the 8,500 issues raised with it related to DNACPR decisions in
               care homes.93

      7.61.    Some Core Participants asked the Inquiry to recommend an investigation
               into DNACPR decisions made during the pandemic.94 The Inquiry has carefully
               considered the request, recognising that these concerns may reflect the distress that
               individuals, families and communities experienced. However, such an investigation
               would require a manual review of the hospital and care home records of each
               individual. Given the demands on health and social care services, this would be
               a disproportionate use of limited resources, in the Inquiry’s view. However, the
               Inquiry acknowledges the importance of these concerns and the need for continued
               scrutiny, learning and appropriate safeguards in relation to DNACPR decisions.


      Communications about DNACPR decision-making
      7.62.    While it may be appropriate – particularly during a pandemic – to encourage people
               to consider and discuss their future care and treatment, including DNACPR decisions,
               some of the communications sent during the pandemic failed to raise the topic of

         DNACPR in a thoughtful and sensitive manner. One letter from a GP surgery to care

7.63.    There were also occasions where people felt pressured to agree to a DNACPR.97
         Respondents to a Care Rights UK survey in 2023 described their feelings about
         making a DNACPR decision during the pandemic:

              “Coerced to comply.”

              “I felt my brother was forced to sign documents with respect to my mother.”

              “[I] was advised this was in mum[’s] best interest … felt like I didn’t know enough
                about it and just accepted I should do it. Felt guilty afterwards and wasn’t sure
                I made the right decision.”98

7.64.    There were communications about DNACPR that came without warning.99 Catherine
         Griffiths, a member of Covid-19 Bereaved Families for Justice Cymru, received a call
         from a GP early in the pandemic about her 86-year-old father who lived in a care
         home. He had dementia but was otherwise in good physical health. Ms Griffiths said
         that the GP had only just met her father and told her that a DNACPR should be in
         place. She described this experience as “brutal”.100

7.65.    Susan Lyons (a member of John’s Campaign, Care Rights UK and the Patients
         Association) told the Inquiry about two letters she had received from a GP asking
         about her daughter, Sarah’s, CPR preferences. Sarah was in her twenties at the time
         and has learning difficulties. Mrs Lyons said that she was incandescent when she
         received the first letter early in the pandemic, and Sarah did not agree to a DNACPR.
         She described feeling shock and horror when she received a second letter in
         October 2021.101

      Module 6: Care sector




      7.66.    The Scottish Government sent a letter to the social care sector on 13 March 2020
               that encouraged anticipatory care plans to be put in place “for as many residents
               as possible (and ideally all residents)”.102 It also said that, where appropriate, there
               should be a DNACPR decision that is communicated to residents and carers.103 GPs
               in Scotland telephoned their patients who lived in care homes to discuss DNACPR
               (in the context of anticipatory care plans). Some residents felt anxious that this was
               an indicator of access to intensive care being restricted.104 In response to concerns,
               on 10 April 2020, the Scottish Government clarified that it was not a requirement
               to discuss DNACPR during anticipatory care planning.105 Ms Freeman, who signed
               the 13 March letter, said that the wording was based on the presumption that those
               reading the letter were familiar with anticipatory care planning, but she accepted that
               it could have been interpreted more widely than intended.106

      7.67.    Family members who were not included in discussions about DNACPR recounted
               feeling fearful about what could be happening without their knowledge, which was
               compounded by restrictions on visiting and reduced contact.107


                     “The GP asked for a DNACPR to be in place, my dad knew about this and the
                       possible consequences, he wanted to live, he didn’t want one. Then I found
                       out the GP had visited again unannounced with a DNACPR request and they
                       never mentioned it to me.”108

                     Bereaved family member, Scotland, Every Story Matters


      7.68.    The Care Quality Commission report in March 2021 noted that, during the pandemic,
               some DNACPR decisions in England had been made remotely without consulting
               the individual and that discussions had occurred at short notice. It said that this
               reduced the extent to which the decisions were personalised and contributed to
               misunderstandings as to what DNACPR meant.109

               decisions being part of a wider discussion about advance care planning.110

Improving DNACPR discussions and decisions
7.70.     Following its review of DNACPR decisions in England, the Care Quality Commission
          concluded in March 2021 that “a lack of training and support for staff, and how
          confident they were in holding these conversations, has impacted on the quality
          of people’s experiences”.112 It recommended that:

              “clinicians, professionals and workers must have the knowledge, skills
               and confidence to speak with people about, and support them in, making
               DNACPR decisions”.113

7.71.     To achieve this, the Care Quality Commission suggested that clear and consistent
          training, standards and guidance be developed in England.114 From September 2021,
          social care workers could access resources from Skills for Care on best practice
          for DNACPR decision-making, including effective communication strategies and
          adopting a person-centred approach.115 Since November 2023, the Care Quality
          Commission has followed a Single Assessment Framework that requires care
          providers in England to demonstrate how care users’ rights are being protected in
          relation to DNACPR. The framework is also intended to help providers to manage
          and maintain effective oversight of DNACPR decisions.116 This is a welcome
          development that has the potential to improve practice in this area. The regulators
          in Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland are encouraged to consider adopting a
          similar approach.


DNACPR decisions interpreted as ‘do not treat’
7.72.     The UK-wide professional guidance on DNACPR decision-making explicitly stated
          that a DNACPR decision applies only to CPR and does not equate to a decision to
          decline any other care or treatment.117

=== PAGE 235 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




  7.73.     In surveys undertaken by the Care Quality Commission in December 2020, 44% of
            the 544 people who responded said that someone they cared for or a relative had
            experienced unfair or discriminatory treatment while making a DNACPR decision
            during the pandemic.118 In one case, a care home resident in Wales, who had
            Covid-19 symptoms and a DNACPR in place, was told by a GP or paramedic that he
            would not be taken to hospital. The care home said that the GP who saw the resident
            did not put support or pain relief in place to ease suffering and, when the resident
            died, within 24 hours, he was described as “struggling to breathe and in effect slowly
            suffocating to death”.119

  7.74.     Some DNACPR decisions were interpreted by healthcare professionals as a decision
            not to take a person to hospital or not to provide them with treatment (‘do not
            treat’).120 This was unacceptable, particularly at a time when people were fearful
            that medical decisions were being made due to limited resources and rationing.121


  End-of-life care for people in adult social care
  7.75.     End-of-life care is intended to help people to live as well as possible during the last
            months or year of their life and to die with dignity. However, during the pandemic, not
            all adults in social care were able to access end-of-life care and the workforce did
            not have sufficient training and support to deliver the care required. There were also
            issues with supplies of medication for people at the end of life. Experts described
            the Covid-19 pandemic as a “stress test” for end-of-life care.122


  Changes to end-of-life care during the pandemic
  7.76.     Routine and systematic collection of data on the number of people receiving end-
            of-life care in the UK was not undertaken before or during the Covid-19 pandemic.123
            Professor Barclay explained that this lack of data meant that governments had a
            reactive approach to end-of-life care, when it needed to be proactive.124 For example,
            initially there was very little, if any, focus on how end-of-life care would continue to
            be provided in social care settings and how to manage the symptoms associated
            with dying from Covid-19. This may have contributed to equipment, medicine
            and staff shortages that led to many people not receiving the care they needed
            and dying with avoidable symptoms and distress.125

                                                                                    Chapter 7: Deaths and end of life




7.77.     While 99.56% of GP practices in England reported that they kept a register of
          patients who needed end-of-life care and support in 2022/23, it is not known if the
          data are comprehensive or accurate. This data collection does not exist in Scotland,
          Wales or Northern Ireland.126 The use of registers is encouraged and could be used
          to prepare for the allocation and distribution of resources for end-of-life care in future
          pandemics.

End-of-life care at home

7.78.     More people than usual, some of whom were very unwell, required end-of-life care
          at home during the Covid-19 pandemic. At the same time, visiting restrictions meant
          that there were fewer in-person health and social care visits.127 The responsibility
          for providing care and medication at the end of life fell on many unpaid carers and
          family members.128 Professor Barclay described how extremely distressing it could
          be to administer drugs to a loved one who was close to the end of life and who died
          shortly after: “I know, as a doctor, [it] is emotionally very challenging. What it must be
          like for a family carer I think is very different.”129

7.79.     During a 2021 survey by Marie Curie (a UK end-of-life charity), three-quarters of
          bereaved carers reported that a loved one had died at home without all of the care
          and support they needed. Further, 76% of bereaved carers felt that they had not
          been offered all of the care and support they required.130


               “I remember when Daddy was dying and thinking, ‘Why are we here doing this
                 on our own? Why is there not someone coming in and helping us? We don’t
                 know what we’re doing. We don’t know how to look after a man dying.’”131

                Bereaved family member, Northern Ireland, Every Story Matters

      7.80.     Where support from health and social care professionals was available, it made
                a significant difference.


                     “We had the support of the district nurses which was fantastic. They were
                       coming around every day for the first couple of weeks, advising us and giving
                       medications [including ‘just in case’] end of life medication which would ease
                       her suffering [if needed]. I did feel we were pretty well supported.”133

                      Bereaved family member, Wales, Every Story Matters


      7.81.     The voluntary sector plays a key role in supporting people who are providing
                end-of-life care to someone at home. For example, Marie Curie extended its free
                telephone support lines to anybody affected by death and bereavement during the
                pandemic.134 The health and social care sectors need to work with the voluntary
                sector to develop future support and information on this topic for unpaid carers and
                family members.135 This is vital at a time of increased anxiety and distress and will
                help to ensure that people who receive end-of-life care at home during a pandemic
                have the best possible chance of experiencing a dignified death.

      End-of-life care in care homes

      7.82.     Many care homes do not usually provide nursing or medical care. In the UK, more
                than 70% of care homes are residential and rely on specialist services and
                primary care to deliver and advise on clinical matters, including end-of-life care.
                Between March and May 2020, care home deaths in the UK increased rapidly by
                220% (from 2,451 to 7,911).136 Care workers had to take on responsibility for providing
                end-of-life care, including alleviating distressing symptoms, supporting recovery and

          anticipating the end of life, for the first time, without the required knowledge and
          skills.137 This made it crucial that they could access external support.138

7.83.     Care home staff went to extraordinary lengths to ensure that residents received
          compassionate care at the end of life. A care home manager described how a care
          worker would lie in bed with a resident to provide comfort when they were dying.139
          Ms Hough said that staff in her care home would sit with a dying resident throughout
          the night and always worked to ensure that they had the necessary medication and
          equipment to provide a dignified death.140

7.84.     A bereaved family member responding to a survey by the Patients Association
          described the care that their relative received:

              “[T]he care home staff did not leave my relative’s side during the last 24 hours of
                their life; they made sure someone was with them at all times. The compassion
                shown was second to none.”141

7.85.     In a 2021 study, care home managers (the majority of whom worked in England)
          reported difficulties delivering high-quality, personalised end-of-life care during the
          pandemic, particularly when there were staff shortages. The study also found that
          managers who received advice from GPs and specialists felt able to support their
          staff to provide end-of-life care. However, the remote delivery of healthcare services
          during the pandemic increased the burden on care home workers and reduced
          access to support. Remote consultations were not always a suitable alternative and,
          in some instances, care homes lacked the technology to facilitate them.142 Professor
          Barclay described his experience of conducting ward rounds via remote technology:

              “[G]oing from room to room … I found it really distressing that there was a patient
                who was, from what I could see on the screen, in the very final few hours of life,
                and there was the family next to them, but I couldn’t be there.”143

7.86.     It is highly likely that care home workers will be required to provide more end-of-
          life care in a future pandemic. To do so, they must be properly equipped with the
          necessary knowledge and skills. The health and social care sectors must work
          together to ensure that care workers delivering end-of-life care in care homes
          can continue to access support, guidance and information (see Chapter 4: Visiting
          restrictions in care homes).

      Module 6: Care sector




      Access to emergency end-of-life care outside care homes

      7.87.     During the pandemic, some people living in care homes were not taken to hospital
                for end-of-life care when they should have been or would have been previously.144

      7.88.     Judith Kilbee, a member of Scottish Covid Bereaved, described how her father’s
                physical health deteriorated at the start of May 2020 following a Covid-19 diagnosis.
                She recalled how upsetting it was to hear that the care home was told by 111 (a
                telephone service providing advice about non-life-threatening illness or injury) that
                a resident testing positive for Covid-19 would not be taken to hospital and to order
                an ‘end-of-life pack’.145

      7.89.     Some GPs sent letters to care home residents confirming that it would not be
                appropriate to take them to hospital if they had Covid-19.146 In Scotland, for example,
                one letter said:

                    “We would plan to continue to nurse our patients … and not to transfer them
                      to hospital. Sadly if they are so unwell as to require breathing support from
                      a ventilator it is unlikely that given their frailty they would survive.”147

      7.90. Decisions to restrict care home residents’ access to end-of-life care in hospital during
            the pandemic caused great distress and upset. This decision must always be based
            on a clinical assessment of the individual’s condition and treatment needs.


      Medication at the end of life
      7.91.     At the end of life, medication can be prescribed to ease pain and anxiety and
                reduce nausea. It may be placed in an individual’s private home or in a care home,
                to be used if needed. This practice, known as anticipatory prescribing, significantly
                increased during the pandemic due to concerns that people might die from Covid-19
                in distress before medication could be obtained.148 Professor Barclay said that this
                led to shortages of medication and it came very close to running out.149

      7.92.     Medication shortages are likely to have led to people receiving inadequate symptom
                control or pain relief at the end of life.150

              “Sometimes, you’d go to a patient that was struggling with their breathing.
                We didn’t have the medications, because there was [a] short supply, because
                they were going to the hospitals … So, we were running out of medications,
                so patients were passing away in pain, struggling.”151

               Community nurse, England, Every Story Matters


7.93.    At the start of the pandemic, there were strict rules to prevent medication
         being repurposed (medication prescribed for one patient cannot be used by
         or administered to another patient).152 While this ensured patient safety, some
         medication went to waste at a time when supplies were limited. Caroline Abrahams,
         Charity Director of Age UK from 2012, described the impact on care home residents
         and staff:

             “[It] led to people dying in care homes not in a dignified way, not in a pain-free
               way, and it put the professionals working there … very often not medically
               qualified people … in a horrible, horrible position when they had a lot of people
               there who needed those drugs, they weren’t able to share them from one person
               to another.”153

7.94.    At different times in April and May 2020, the rules temporarily changed in the UK
         to allow end-of-life medications to be repurposed in care homes.154 Although the
         temporary measures were not regularly used in practice, Professor Barclay described
         the changes as “an outbreak of common sense” and said they relieved some of the
         pressures on care homes.155

7.95.    While some action was taken to ensure that access to end-of-life medications
         continued, it was inevitable that a virus with a high mortality rate, such as Covid-19,
         would significantly increase demand for these medications and, without proper
         planning, this could lead to medication shortages.156 For this reason, each
         government should develop plans to ensure, as far as possible, that, during a
         pandemic, there continues to be access to medications commonly given as part
         of end-of-life care.

=== PAGE 241 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      Training on end-of-life care for health and social care
      professionals
      7.96. It is difficult to judge when a person is at the end of their life and in need of end-
            of-life care, particularly for older people and those who are more frail. A GP or
            consultant is best placed to make a prognosis.157 However, during the Covid-19
            pandemic, they were not always available and the task fell to others.

      7.97.     A 2021 study found that care home managers and clinical leads were more confident
                in delivering end-of-life care where they had received training or had established
                ways of providing this type of care. A residential care home manager said:

                    “[ W]e had the experience of [delivering palliative and end-of-life care] in the home
                      to begin with, and training … I think if we hadn’t … had that prior we would really
                      have struggled.”158

                Some care home leaders reported that the training they received was inadequate,
                not tailored or not sufficiently timely.159

      7.98.     Health and social care professionals were signposted to resources, developed
                during the pandemic, on delivering and supporting end-of-life care.160 For example,
                in March 2020, Marie Curie, Hospice UK, the Royal College of Nursing and the Royal
                College of General Practitioners published a Covid End of Life Community Charter
                with ethical guidelines for providing care to people affected by Covid-19.161


      Post-pandemic developments in training
      7.99.     There have been developments in end-of-life care training in Wales, Scotland and
                Northern Ireland since the pandemic, but not all training is mandatory or applies
                to all health and social care professionals working in adult social care settings.
                There is no evidence of developments being made in England. Such developments
                include the following:

                • Since October 2022, residential care home workers in Wales have been
                    required to have or be working towards a qualification that includes training
                    on end-of-life care.162

          • The All Wales Competency Framework for Adult Palliative and End of Life Care
              2025 provides the competencies required of healthcare professionals to deliver
              end-of-life care but does not specifically apply to social care staff.163
          • The Palliative Care Education Framework was updated in 2025 for health and
              social care workers in Scotland and includes ‘care around dying’. It is not
              mandatory.164
          • The Social Care Workforce Strategy 2025–2035 (Northern Ireland) includes an
              ambition for a mandatory qualification, with a module on end-of-life care, for all
              newly registered social care workers. While the qualification has been available
              since September 2024, it is not mandatory for all care workers.165

7.100. Professors Barclay and Sleeman recommend that end-of-life care be mandatory in
       training for all health and social care professionals. They suggest that the training
       include communication skills and how staff can help people to access support.166
       The Association for Palliative Medicine of Great Britain and Ireland agreed that a
       healthcare workforce with more knowledge and training on end-of-life care would
       ensure better care during a future pandemic.167 Helen Wildbore (Director of Care
       Rights UK from 2019 to 2025) said:

              “[L]ooking to the future, we need proper training on end-of-life care for care
                workers … we need to embed that last year of life as standard practice, and we
                need to ensure that people’s choices and autonomy around how they want to
                end their lives are respected.”168

          The need to improve training on end-of-life care was also supported by the
          Royal College of Nursing, Covid-19 bereaved groups, John’s Campaign, Care
          Rights UK, the Patients Association, Scottish territorial health boards and the
          Care Professional Organisation (formerly known as the National Association
          of Care and Support Workers).169

=== PAGE 243 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




  7.101. Two reports published in November 2024 advocated for more education and training
         for health and adult social care staff in these areas. The Coalition of Frontline Care
         urged the UK government to “[m]ake a radical change in the care for older people
         nearing the end of life, by investing in training and support for the frontline health
         and care workforce”.170 The National Confidential Enquiry into Patient Outcome
         and Death (an independent body aiming to improve the quality of healthcare)
         recommended that end-of-life care be a core competency during training for
         patient-facing health and social care staff.171

  7.102. The Covid-19 pandemic demonstrated the need for training in a number of matters
         relating to end of life. The training should be tailored to the different roles and
         responsibilities that health and social care professionals have during advance care
         planning and DNACPR decision-making.172

  7.103. People nearing the end of life are entitled to dignity in death and their loved ones
         are entitled to know that they received the best care possible. Whether discussing
         advance care plans or DNACPRs or providing end-of-life care in the final days and
         hours of an individual’s life, a more knowledgeable and confident adult social care
         workforce will help ensure that there is high-quality end-of-life care.


            Recommendation 7: Mandatory end-of-life care training for health
            and social care professionals
            The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
            Ireland Executive should establish mandatory training for health and social care
            professionals looking after people in the adult social care sector who are nearing
            the end of life.
            The training should be developed in conjunction with health and social care
            regulators and provide, at a minimum:

            • an understanding and enhanced knowledge of how to deliver high-quality
               end-of-life care as well as the support available; and

            • communication skills to be able confidently to conduct and document
               conversations about advance care and, where appropriate to the professional’s
               role, decisions on Do Not Attempt Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (DNACPR).

=== PAGE 245 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      Introduction
      8.1.     Millions of people will require care as an adult at some stage of their life, whether
               provided in their own home, a care home or both. It is unsurprising, therefore, that
               adult social care has been described as “a vital public service that’s the backbone
               of communities”.1

      8.2.     However, the Inquiry heard that, while the healthcare systems in the four nations
               of the UK have significant public visibility, people do not think about social care
               until they actually need it.2 The adult social care workforce has been described as
               invisible because much of its work is undertaken behind closed doors in people’s
               homes and in care homes.3 As a result, the sector feels that it is a “Cinderella”
               service, with healthcare services prioritised at the expense of adult social care.4

      8.3.     These feelings were evident, if not exacerbated, during the Covid-19 pandemic.
               Cathryn Williams, Chief Executive of the Association of Directors of Adult Social
               Services (a membership organisation for local authority adult social care leaders
               in England) from January 2015 to July 2024, said: “[O]riginally ‘clap for carers’
               became ‘clap for the NHS’, and I think that led to everybody feeling less valued.”5
               The Inquiry’s listening exercise, Every Story Matters, heard from domiciliary
               care workers.


                     “The NHS were not the only heroes. The quiet, underpaid, overworked,
                       undervalued, overlooked domiciliary care workers were and still are
                       [heroes too]!”6

                       Domiciliary care worker, England, Every Story Matters


      8.4.     A registered nurse in a care home in Scotland told the Scottish Covid-19 Inquiry:
               “Social care staff felt a bit put out because it was all clapping for NHS … The NHS
               were like the ones who saved us and we were just getting on with it.”7

      8.5.     As a result, the contribution that adult social care workers in both care homes
               and domiciliary care (delivered in a person’s home) make to society often goes
               unrecognised and is undervalued. This chapter considers the role of the adult social
               care workforce, which is large and varied – comprising, among others, care home
               and domiciliary care workers, registered managers and registered nurses.
               It considers the arguments for and against introducing a voluntary workforce

         register for care home and domiciliary care workers in England; registration already
         exists in Northern Ireland, Scotland and Wales.

8.6.     This chapter also examines the statements made by some ministers that decisions
         in the early months of the pandemic were taken to protect care homes and that
         vulnerable individuals had been at the forefront of their decisions.


Recognition of the adult social care workforce
8.7.     As outlined in Chapter 1: Overview of the adult social care sector, there were a large
         number of vacancies in the adult social care workforce at the outset of and during
         the Covid-19 pandemic. The shortages have often been attributed to poor working
         conditions, including zero-hours contracts, low pay and limited opportunities for
         career development. These systemic issues mean that social care is not always
         seen as an attractive career option.8

8.8.     The Covid-19 pandemic highlighted and exacerbated challenges that already existed
         with identifying, contacting, retaining and recruiting people to work in adult social
         care. By September 2021, an “immediate crisis” was identified in England due to staff
         absences and workers leaving the profession.9 In addition, Professor Sube Banerjee,
         Dr Nicolas Farina and Dr Ben Hicks (expert witnesses on the care and treatment of
         people with dementia and their families) explained that care workers were exposed
         to traumatic events during the pandemic, such as high death rates of people using
         care, which could contribute to workforce losses.10 Chapter 6: The impact on unpaid
         carers, care workers and care providers considers these matters in greater detail.

8.9.     A number of witnesses spoke of the urgent need for better recognition and
         professionalisation of the adult social care workforce. For example, the Trades Union
         Congress recommended that higher levels of respect and recognition and greater
         access to formal professional qualifications would reduce vacancies and turnover.11
         Introducing a workforce register for care workers in both care homes and domiciliary
         care in England is one way to improve professionalisation, recognition, retention and
         future emergency responses in adult social care.

Benefits of registration for care workers
8.11.     A number of organisations, interest groups and individuals closely involved in the
          work of the adult social care sector support the introduction of a workforce register
          for England. They believe a register would help to professionalise the sector by
          supporting people working in social care to develop their careers through education,
          training and qualifications. This, in turn, should help to improve recruitment into and
          retention of the workforce, which will ensure that it is more stable and resilient in a
          future pandemic.

8.12.     Caroline Abrahams, Charity Director of Age UK from 2012, explained that providing
          social care is a highly skilled task undertaken by people who are hugely committed
          to their work. She said that care work deserves to be professionalised, which likely
          requires registration.13 Dr Jane Townson (Chief Executive of the Homecare
          Association from May 2019), Mary Cridge (Director of Adult Social Care at the Care
          Quality Commission from July 2022) and Professor Vic Rayner (Chief Executive
          Officer of the National Care Forum from 2016, a membership organisation for not-for-
          profit social care and support providers) recognised the benefits that registration
          could bring.14

8.13.     The Nuffield Trust (an independent health and social care think tank) commented
          that, although it was “early days”, registration in Northern Ireland was having a
          positive impact on both the workforce and retention.15 It said the growing interest in

  Module 6: Care sector




           professionalisation (including registration) in England is based on the rationale
           that it would attract new entrants, support workforce retention by offering more
           opportunities for career progression and help to develop a strong professional
           identity for social care workers, facilitated by registration and adherence to common
           professional standards.16

  8.14.    The Nuffield Trust reviewed the use of workforce registers in the UK and
           internationally. It concluded that registration and consistent access to training and
           development opportunities in social care could help to address inequalities in career
           progression and earnings and attract people from under-represented groups.17
           A number of other professions, such as nurses, have registration requirements.18
           Social workers have had a professional register and a protected title (to be a ‘social
           worker’ one must be qualified and registered) across the UK since the early 2000s.19
           The Nuffield Trust considered that professional registration has “instilled greater
           public awareness of social workers as professionals”.20

  8.15.    However, there are possible disadvantages to a register, the most significant of
           which is a potential reduction in the number of people willing to work in the adult
           social care sector.


  Risk of loss to the workforce
  8.16.    There is some evidence that the introduction of a register for adult social care
           workers could risk the loss of some of the workforce. However, none of the concerns
           were expressed to the Inquiry as definitive reasons against the introduction of
           registration and, in parts of the UK where registers are operating, the concerns
           are being addressed.

  8.17.    Joanna Killian, Chief Executive of the Local Government Association from March
           2024 to July 2026, was concerned that an onerous bureaucratic registration system
           would force people out of the market. However, she emphasised the importance of
           engagement with the sector. She said that, if registration recognised those working
           in adult social care as a strong and capable profession, the Local Government
           Association would endorse it.21 Provided any system devised for registration
           is simple and easy to access and use, this should not therefore prove to be a
           major hurdle.

                                                                            Chapter 8: Recognition and protection




8.18.    Francesca Humi, who gave evidence on behalf of the Frontline Migrant Health
         Workers Group, raised concerns that a registration requirement of care workers with
         “really precarious immigration status … would prevent a lot of people from entering
         the sector”.22 This is an undoubted risk, but, as Ms Humi acknowledged: “[C]are
         should be regulated in a way that improves health outcomes for both patients and
         workers.”23 Furthermore, the risk could be mitigated by making a register voluntary
         at the beginning and only made mandatory once the workforce had adapted to it
         and seen the benefits of it.

8.19.    The Nuffield Trust highlighted concerns that fees and training requirements could act
         as barriers to existing and potential workers joining the register. However, in light of
         the limited available data on staff turnover broken down by profession, it explained
         that it is unclear whether the introduction of registration in other nations has in fact
         exacerbated existing levels of turnover for care workers specifically. The Nuffield
         Trust cited evidence from Wales that some registration fees for social care remained
         outstanding by March 2021, but it also noted that, while some people left the register
         in Wales in 2020/21, staff turnover levels in Wales compared “quite favourably” with
         those in England in 2022.24 The barrier may not be insurmountable.

8.20. A report in Scotland from 2019 outlined that approximately one in five care providers
      had suspended staff because they had not achieved registration qualifications.
      However, there was also evidence that almost a quarter of care homes and more
      than 1 in 10 domiciliary care providers had avoided suspending workers through
      positive engagement with regulatory and inspection colleagues, usually by agreeing
      extensions of time to complete qualifications.25 This concern is therefore being
      addressed by collaborative working.


Wider benefits of a workforce register during a pandemic
8.21.    The existence of workforce registers in other professions and registers of adult social
         care workers in the devolved nations had a number of important benefits during the
         response to Covid-19.

8.22. Early in the pandemic, the nursing register allowed the Nursing and Midwifery
      Council to identify nurses who had recently left the register as a potential resource to
      increase capacity in the NHS.26 Likewise, in Scotland, the register for adult social care
      workers meant that the Scottish Social Services Council had access to people who
      had recently left the register to ask if they would return. According to Kevin Mitchell,

            Executive Director of Assurance and Improvement at the Care Inspectorate in

  8.23. In Northern Ireland, Professor Sean Holland (Chief Social Work Officer in the
        Department of Health (Northern Ireland) from July 2010 to October 2022) said that
        the existence of a register during the pandemic meant that the department could
        communicate with the workforce and that learning resources could also be shared
        directly with the workforce.28 The register in Wales was used alongside healthcare
        records to analyse the impact of the Covid-19 pandemic on 15,725 domiciliary care
        workers. This included collating how many workers had tested positive for, or died
        from, Covid-19 and how many received care for a mental health diagnosis.29
        Registers for adult social care were also used in the devolved nations to implement
        continuing professional development for care workers on topics such as infection
        control.30

  8.24. By contrast, in England, the pandemic exposed a number of difficulties that could
        have been addressed by the existence of a workforce register. The UK government
        had no direct route of communication or contact with the social care workforce.31
        There was also no central record of care workers’ qualifications, training or
        experience, which meant that it was not possible to track who had completed
        infection prevention and control training.32 As Helen Whately MP (Minister of State
        for Social Care at the Department of Health and Social Care from February 2020
        to September 2021) summarised, there were:

                “around one and a half million people looking after extremely vulnerable people
                 … we don’t know who that workforce is, we have no way of contacting them,
                 and we don’t know what their qualifications are”.33

  8.25. The lack of data about care workers likewise made workforce planning in the
        sector more difficult.34 Matt Hancock, Secretary of State for Health and Social Care
        from July 2018 to June 2021, said that a workforce register would have assisted
        the government during the pandemic, including in relation to matters such as the
        distribution of personal protective equipment (PPE) and sick pay.35

  8.26. A review by the Nuffield Trust in May 2023 identified that, without a register, local
        authorities did not have an accessible source of information to identify the social
        care workforce. This was problematic during community outbreaks and led to delays
        in implementing infection control measures, such as rapid tracing of close contacts

          who worked in social care. As a result, “transmission of infections via staff from the
          community was not always acted on quickly”.36

8.27.     The practical benefits that a workforce register in England could have during a future
          pandemic include:

          • the rapid identification of recent leavers from the sector who may be willing
              to return to help with the pandemic effort;37
          • ensuring that people with particular skills and experience are deployed in areas
              where they are most needed;38
          • an improved understanding of how many workers work across multiple care
              settings;39
          • more timely, widespread and direct dissemination of information, advice
              and support to care workers;40
          • the rapid identification of the adult social care workforce, so that they can
              be given priority access to vaccinations where necessary;41 and
          • ensuring that training and professional development is kept up to date
              and transportable, so that the information could move with the care worker
              to any new employer.42

8.28. Clear and timely data and information on the workforce in the adult social care
      sector are particularly useful when decisions need to be taken quickly in response
      to a rapidly changing situation. Importantly, such data and information can also help
      decision-makers to assess and understand the impact of their decisions. Workforce
      registers can also facilitate communication directly with the sector, to ensure that it
      has the most up-to-date information.

8.29. The idea of an adult social care workforce register is nothing new. It has been
      explored by the UK government a number of times since 2008 and calls for
      registration grew during the pandemic. In 2021, the Department for Work and

      Module 6: Care sector




               Pensions’ In-Work Progression Commission called for a central body to register
               care workers, to manage their training and ongoing professional development.43

      8.30. The size of the workforce in England means significant planning and resources
            would be required to implement a mandatory register.44 On behalf of the Department
            of Health and Social Care, in April 2021, Skills for Care (an independent strategic
            workforce development body for adult social care in England) explored the
            development of a voluntary register to provide the UK government with a method for
            contacting adult social care workers directly. Skills for Care advised that it could be a
            first step towards registration, with some long-term benefits if “accompanied by clear
            incentives and long-term investment”.45 On 8 April 2021, the Workforce Advisory
            Group (established by the Department of Health and Social Care and bringing
            together representatives from the sector) expressed uncertainty about whether the
            workforce would engage with a register if it was not voluntary.46 It is not clear if any
            further work on a voluntary register took place during 2021.

      8.31.    An approach that works in the devolved nations may not necessarily work in
               England, given, for example, the larger workforce. There are also concerns that
               registration may disincentivise some people from joining the profession or could
               lead to some workers leaving. However, the Inquiry concludes that the benefits
               of registration set out above outweigh this potential harm, particularly in the context
               of a pandemic. It agrees with those who suggest that one way to avoid the risk of
               people leaving the profession would be to start by introducing a voluntary register.
               If there is proper engagement with the sector in developing such a register, some
               of the risks may be mitigated.

      8.32. As explained by the Nuffield Trust, beginning with a voluntary scheme and phasing
            in a mandatory scheme (as in Wales and Northern Ireland) might help to reduce the
            risk of existing staff leaving. The Nuffield Trust stated that support from employers,
            regulators and trade unions can help to ensure that staff understand their
            responsibilities and reduce anxiety about registration.47 The National Care
            Association said that the sector may feel encouraged to move towards mandatory
            registration once the benefits, such as for data collection, are clear.48

            representing adult social care, has been established by Skills for Care.49 The group

          considered that workforce registration is “key to the public recognising the sector as
          the professionals that they are, important for public safety and for development of
          people working in social care”.50 However, it noted that there is insufficient evidence
          from other countries of the impact of registration on the workforce and, due to the
          size of the workforce in England, “the cost-benefit argument is not clear enough
          yet”.51 The group announced that it would gather evidence on the impacts and
          potential models of a social care workforce registration scheme during a 2025
          scoping exercise.52

8.34. The Care Professional Organisation (formerly known as the National Association
      of Care and Support Workers) has now developed an independent voluntary Care
      Professional Register for England.53 The register is open to all care professionals who
      work in England. The registration process is described as straightforward and takes
      just a few minutes to complete. Each registered user receives a unique PIN and
      digital registration badge.54 The register displays a registered care worker’s full
      name, their unique registration PIN, the date of their registration and their current
      registration status (either active or inactive). Other personal data are not displayed.55
      Paul Featherstone (Chair and Founder of the National Association of Care and
      Support Workers from March 2016) explained that one of the aims of the voluntary
      register is to professionalise the care worker role:

               “[ W]e see it as a statement of their professionalism, that they take accountability
                 for their actions … it raises the profile of care working as a profession, alongside
                 the individual care worker … it gives a certain prestige … to the work that we
                 do.”56

8.35. Accordingly, the Inquiry recommends that the UK government should implement
      a voluntary register for adult social care workers in England as a step towards
      improving professionalisation, recognition and retention of the workforce.
      A workforce register is likely also to be of great assistance in future emergency
      responses. For example, in a future pandemic, it would provide a better
      understanding of staff movement in the sector and it may help with initiatives to
      expand the workforce. It would also allow for rapid identification of the workforce
      and greater communication with the sector, and may assist in the delivery
      of additional training.

=== PAGE 255 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      8.36. The design of the register should consider the most appropriate funding model.
            Broadly speaking, registration fees could be funded by individual workers, their
            employers or the government. In most professions, the individual worker pays the
            fees (eg doctors, nurses and social workers). However, this would place an extra
            burden on adult social care workers in what is already a relatively low-paid
            profession and could deter new entrants to the profession. The UK government
            should consider the impact that registration fees may have on retention and
            recruitment and consider which funding model can best minimise those risks.

      8.37.     The Inquiry heard a range of views as to which organisation, body or agency should
                be responsible for a workforce register in England. There was a consensus that it
                must be reliable and respected.57 The Inquiry recommends that the UK government
                work with the sector and those organisations and groups most closely involved
                in the delivery of adult social care, both to inform the development of the register
                and to determine the most appropriate body or organisation to operate it. As is
                established best practice, where possible and appropriate, users of care should
                also be consulted. The UK government should work with the Care Professional
                Organisation, Skills for Care and the Workforce Strategy Oversight Executive Group
                to understand and build upon the work that has already been undertaken. The UK
                government may also find it beneficial to learn from the experiences of introducing
                registration requirements in the devolved administrations.

      8.38. Mandatory registration of care workers in Northern Ireland and Wales was phased
            in over the course of two to three years. The number of domiciliary and care home
            workers in England is much larger (see Chapter 1: Overview of the adult social care
            sector). Once the voluntary register has been operating for an extended period of no
            more than five years, a cost–benefit analysis of the impact of voluntary registration
            should be conducted to help decision-makers determine whether registration
            should be made mandatory. This will also allow time to consider whether the UK
            government wishes to introduce the professional regulation (ie of qualifications
            and conduct) of workers in adult social care.




      57   Caroline Abrahams 16 July 2025 35/1-4; Helen Whately 17 July 2025 139/24-140/1


242

=== PAGE 256 ===
                                                       Chapter 8: Recognition and protection




       Recommendation 8: A voluntary workforce register for adult
       social care workers
       The UK government should implement a voluntary workforce register for adult
       social care workers in England, in order to strengthen the professionalisation
       and recognition for those working in the care sector. This should be developed in
       conjunction with social care staff, providers, commissioners and oversight bodies.
       This should formalise the ongoing work to improve workforce progression and
       retention and any learning from registration schemes in Northern Ireland, Scotland
       and Wales.
       The design of the register should consider:

       • the most appropriate body or agency to operate the register, including
          processing applications, data-sharing and publication;

       • the benefits and requirements of joining the register, including the best means
          to incentivise membership; and

       • the funding model that will support the establishment and operation
          of the register.
       The voluntary register should be implemented within two years of the publication
       of this Report.
       Once the voluntary register has been operating for no more than five years, the
       UK government should undertake a full cost–benefit analysis of the workforce
       register and review whether membership should become mandatory.



Workforce strategies and wider reform
8.39. While workforce registers have been identified as one way of improving
      professionalisation, recognition and retention of the adult social care profession,
      wider issues relating to recruitment, training opportunities and career progression
      must be addressed. Without wider reform, issues with retention and encouraging
      new individuals to join the workforce are likely to remain.

8.40. Although recommendations about wider reform are outside the scope of this Inquiry,
      an effective response to a future pandemic will depend on governments addressing
      the systemic issues that affect the long-term resilience of the adult social care sector.
      Longer-term demographic changes will also require consideration of fundamental
      questions about how services are organised and funded to ensure that they remain
      sustainable and can meet future needs. The work that has already commenced in
      recognition of this across the UK, in the form of workforce strategies and legislative
      change, should be continued. Such steps will help to build a resilient workforce.

      8.41.     In March 2022, the Scottish Government announced a National Workforce Strategy
                for Health and Social Care in Scotland. The strategy recognises:

                     “[S]ustained actions are required from planning for and attracting into the
                       workforce through to support and development of our workforce, supporting
                       and delivering Recovery, Growth and Transformation of our workforce.”58

                The strategy requires health boards, local authorities, and health and social care
                partnerships to submit three-year workforce plans. The aim is to improve capacity
                planning and the quality of workforce data so that gaps and pressures can be more
                easily identified.59

      8.42. The Skills for Care 2024 ‘A Workforce Strategy for Adult Social Care in England’
            included a number of recommendations for retaining and attracting more people into
            the social care workforce. Some of its recommendations are directed at the sector
            itself, while others require government action (such as additional funding).60

      8.43. In June 2025, Skills for Care confirmed that more than half of its recommendations
            were either complete or in progress.61 While there has been no formal response
            from the UK government, some recommendations that involve government input
            are currently being implemented. For example, following collaboration between
            the Department of Health and Social Care and Skills for Care, the Care Workforce
            Pathway was launched in January 2024. This pathway marks:

                     “the first time the care sector has seen a universal career structure setting out
                       the knowledge, skills, values and behaviours required to deliver high-quality,
                       personalised care and support”.62

8.44. Skills for Care described the aims of the pathway:

              “to help address recruitment and retention issues by providing a shared vision
                for the workforce and a recognised platform to support career progression
                and individual learning, making the sector a more attractive place to work”.63

8.45. The Inquiry encourages the UK government to consider the remaining
      recommendations of ‘A Workforce Strategy for Adult Social Care in England’ to
      identify what further action it can take to address outstanding issues in the adult
      social care workforce.

8.46. In 2024, the Welsh Government set out the second phase of its 10-year workforce
      strategy (announced in October 2020) with the Social Care Workforce Delivery Plan
      2024 to 2027. It recognises: “[T]here is much more to do to ensure our workforce
      feel valued and has the best possible support available.”64 Improving the wellbeing
      of the workforce is integral to the delivery plan. The plan also commits the Welsh
      Government to developing frameworks to improve recruitment and retention in the
      sector and a training ‘passport’ that travels with the individual. It acknowledges a
      need for complete information about the workforce and more sophisticated
      modelling to support workforce planning.65

         access to qualifications and development opportunities.67 The strategy has been
         recognised by the Northern Ireland Social Care Council (which maintains the register)
         as an opportunity to “formally recognise the social care workforce’s development
         and learning and value them in a way we never have before”.68

      8.49. Urgent action is required across the UK to recognise the important role played by all
            those who work in adult social care. Care workers should no longer feel as though
            they operate behind closed doors. It is a workforce that deserves to be valued and
            respected by all of society at all times – not just in an emergency.


      The protection of care home residents and staff
      Messaging

      The rhetoric

      8.50. Ministers in each of the four nations understood from early in the pandemic
            that Covid-19 had the greatest impact on older people (see Chapter 3: Protective
            measures). They also knew of the risks of serious illness and death posed to those
            residing in adult care homes in particular. Decision-makers in each nation said that
            they tried to prioritise care homes in their decisions during the pandemic.

      8.51.    On 3 March 2020, Mark Drakeford (First Minister of Wales from December 2018
               to March 2024) told the Senedd (the Welsh Parliament) that the impact of the virus
               was more significant among older people and people with other conditions that
               compromised their immune systems. Mr Drakeford acknowledged that those
               individuals were found in greater concentrations in care homes and said that the
               Welsh Government was “very alert” to the particular challenges in Wales and was
               “taking action already to make sure that people at that front line have all the best
               information and are able to respond to the spread of this virus”.70

      8.52. On 13 March 2020, Vaughan Gething (Minister for Health and Social Services in the
            Welsh Government from May 2016 to May 2021) announced a framework of actions
            in Wales, including a policy of expedited hospital discharge. The briefing he received
            on 13 March said that arrangements to ensure system readiness were needed to
            protect vulnerable groups and that the:

                   “overriding priority is to ensure: health protection; the safeguarding of vulnerable
                    groups; staff welfare; to ensure NHS and social care preparedness; and systems
                    resilience”.71

8.53. Commenting on the decision to introduce the framework of actions, Mr Gething said:

              “As ever, the most vulnerable in Wales were at the heart of the decision-making
                … We were mindful that Wales has a larger older population than the other home
                nations. This was also reflected in the age profiles within the health and social
                care workforce. The measures announced were taken directly to protect those
                vulnerable groups.”72

         When asked about this comment, Mr Gething said that “whole-society choices” were
         made to protect the most vulnerable, where, in managing the risks, it was necessary
         to understand the balance of harm in every choice that was made.73

8.54. At a meeting of the Scottish Cabinet on 3 March 2020, it was noted that coronavirus
      had “a disproportionately serious effect on those in their 70s, 80s and over”.74
      Jeane Freeman (Cabinet Secretary for Health and Sport in the Scottish Government
      from June 2018 to May 2021) said that, given that the virus put individuals in receipt
      of adult social care at the greater risk of harm, it was her primary objective to
      minimise the number of infections and deaths of people in the sector.75

8.55. Professor Sir Michael McBride, Chief Medical Officer for Northern Ireland from
      September 2006, said that there was an awareness of the risk posed to care home
      residents from Covid-19 “at an early stage”.76 On 8 April 2020, handwritten notes
      from a meeting of the Northern Ireland Executive recorded Robin Swann (Minister
      of Health for Northern Ireland from January 2020 to October 2022 and from
      February to May 2024) informing the Executive that care homes had been
      prioritised from the outset.77

      8.59. Mr Hancock said that, while his use of the phrase ‘protective ring’ was:

                    “clearly a piece of rhetoric, we really did do our best to take all of the actions
                     we could to protect those in care homes from the virus in incredibly difficult
                     circumstances”.84
