         He told the Inquiry: “[W]hat I said is that we had tried. It was not possible to protect
         as much as I would have wanted.”85 Mr Hancock reflected:

              “[I]t was a form of words rather than the substance … what you should look at
                is the policy support that was put in place from the centre … and … the other
                options available, given the clinical advice I had at the time, were worse.”86

8.60. Mr Hancock’s statement may well have been a piece of rhetoric, but his choice
      of words did not reflect what was happening in care homes in the first weeks and
      months of the pandemic. Much of the action to which Mr Hancock referred did not
      take place from the start. For example, testing all people discharged from hospital
      into care homes only began in mid-April 2020 and the Infection Control Fund was
      introduced in mid-May 2020. The delivery of infection prevention and control
      training by the NHS also took place in May 2020. By the time Mr Hancock made this
      statement, thousands of people had died in care homes in England. Between 10 April
      (the first day on which data were collected using the Care Quality Commission’s new
      method of identifying deaths involving Covid-19) and 8 May 2020, the Care Quality
      Commission had received notification of 12,409 deaths involving Covid-19.87

8.61.    Professor Sir Jonathan Van-Tam, Deputy Chief Medical Officer for England from
         October 2017 to March 2022, said a “ring is a circle without a break in it”.88 He said
         that “if you want to achieve that, you have to close off the three routes of ingress”
         to care homes, which he identified as new or returning admissions from hospital and
         the community, visitors and care home workers.89 He said that this required more
         testing, more resources for isolating individuals in both hospitals and care homes,
         and far more stringent policies on care home workers moving in and out of homes.90
         During the Inquiry’s Module 2 hearings, Mr Hancock accepted that the protective
         measures put in place in England did not constitute an unbroken circle, stating that
         it is “quite clear from the evidence that Professor Van-Tam is right”.91

8.62. Ms Killian said that the “notion of a ‘protective ring’ was known to be simply not
      true”.92 She said this “caused deep concern and distress for families, as well as for
      sector professionals seeking to perform their duties”.93 Ms Abrahams was of the view
      that “promises of a ‘protective ring’ around care homes did not materialise” in the
      early stages of the pandemic in either policy or practice.94 She referred, for example,

  Module 6: Care sector




           to the fact that there was no published strategy for the sector in England until mid-
           April 2020, no routine collection of real-time data and a lack of access to PPE.95

  8.63. While significant measures were introduced to protect care homes later in the
        pandemic, it cannot be said that care homes were protected from the outset in any
        nation of the UK. As outlined in the Inquiry’s combined Report relating to Modules 2,
        2A, 2B and 2C, the pandemic generally was not taken seriously enough in the UK
        until it was too late, and February 2020 was a lost month.96 The same can be said in
        respect of adult social care.


  Lack of protection

  Control and understanding

  8.64. Entering the pandemic, the UK government and devolved administrations had
        limited oversight, control and understanding of the adult social care sector. It was
        also known that the sector was likely to face serious consequences from a pandemic.
        Despite this, there was a woeful lack of planning for the impact of a pandemic on the
        sector. Had there been established and adequate mechanisms to collect capacity
        data fully and routinely, early decisions – including the expedited discharge of
        hospital patients, which led to many admissions to the care sector – could have
        been better informed. Importantly, the impact on the sector may have been more
        fully understood. Early guidance for care homes did not consider the practical
        realities of the ways in which care is delivered.

  Testing

  8.65. Testing in adult social care was essential for the early implementation of effective
        infection prevention and control measures in care homes, to try to prevent entry
        of the virus into care homes or to mitigate its effects once it arrived. While the
        scientific advice on the value of asymptomatic testing evolved during the pandemic,
        the reality is that limited testing capacity led to delays in testing people discharged
        from hospitals to care homes and wider symptomatic and asymptomatic testing
        of staff and residents.

  Discharge from hospitals to care homes

  8.66. There were concerns that healthcare settings were prioritised at the expense of
        care homes in respect of both hospital discharges and PPE. When the expedited
        discharge policies were introduced in each nation, there were no contingency plans
        in place to accommodate Covid-19-positive or symptomatic residents who could not
        be cared for in their intended care home. This was in spite of a general awareness
  95 INQ000509808_0024-0025 paras 63-65; Caroline Abrahams 16 July 2025 11/3-21
  96 Modules 2, 2A, 2B, 2C: Core decision-making and political governance, UK Covid-19 Inquiry, November 2025, Vol I, Chapter 2
  (https://covid19.public-inquiry.uk/documents/module-2-full-report)

=== PAGE 264 ===
                                                        Chapter 8: Recognition and protection




          among decision-makers and advisers in each nation that some care homes would
          be unable to implement effective isolation policies. Care homes were instead
          encouraged to accept people who had not been tested and felt pressure to accept
          those who were symptomatic or Covid-19-positive to free up hospital capacity.
          Isolation of all people admitted from hospital, a key protective measure, was
          not advised in England, Wales and Northern Ireland until mid to late April 2020.

Personal protective equipment

8.67.     In the first weeks and months of the pandemic, PPE was prioritised for acute
          healthcare settings. While each government took steps to provide free PPE to care
          homes during the pandemic, the stark reality is that, during the early weeks and
          months of the pandemic, care homes simply did not have access to enough PPE
          to keep both staff and residents safe.

Support for isolation

8.68. For workers on low pay, financial support during isolation is highly significant.
      The Inquiry heard that some care workers may have felt the need to work when
      they were unwell (including with symptoms of Covid-19) or when they should have
      been isolating, simply to make ends meet. Some were scared to test, fearing a
      positive result that would require them to stay off work. Across the UK, it took time
      to introduce funding to ensure that care staff received full pay if they were required
      to isolate. There was a particular delay in introducing this funding in Wales. Some
      workers experienced difficulties in obtaining the funding that was introduced.

Healthcare

8.69. Ms Abrahams said that there were some naive assumptions by the UK government
      about how well equipped care homes were to deal with people who had both care
      and medical needs.97 In particular, from March to May 2020, healthcare professionals
      reduced the frequency with which they attended care homes, with guidance issued
      that healthcare visits should take place only if they were necessary or essential.
      There is evidence that a number of care home residents were left with unmet health
      needs. Initially there was very little, if any, focus on how end-of-life care would be
      provided in care homes and how the symptoms associated with dying from Covid-19
      could be managed. Many people did not receive the care they needed and died with
      avoidable symptoms and distress.




97   Caroline Abrahams 16 July 2025 11/21-24


                                                                                               251

=== PAGE 265 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




  Resilience

  8.70. In respect of Northern Ireland, Professor McBride said that, in hindsight, more could
        have been done in the lead-up to the pandemic to improve the resilience of the
        sector and more could have been done during the pandemic to protect care
        homes.98 Both could have – and should have – been done across the UK.


  Protection in the future
  8.71.    The allocation of resources and public money is a matter for policy-makers, and the
           broader reform of the adult social care sector is not for this Inquiry to determine.

  8.72. However, during the Covid-19 pandemic, adult social care staff and the people to
        whom they provided care bore the appalling cost of being an unrecognised and
        unprotected part of society. Care home residents in particular faced a high risk
        of serious harm from the virus, isolation from family and friends, and distressing and
        lonely deaths. Yet, particularly in the first few months of the pandemic, they were
        simply not protected.

  8.73. The UK government and devolved administrations must develop plans now for any
        future pandemic response to ensure that this never happens again. As noted by the
        UK’s Chief Medical Officers and the Chief Scientific Adviser in their Technical Report
        on the COVID-19 Pandemic in the UK, published on 1 December 2022, care home
        residents are very likely to be at high risk of serious disease in any future respiratory
        disease epidemic.99 The UK government and devolved administrations must improve
        their understanding of the sector and consult those providing care in, working in
        and using the care sector. The recommendations in this Report are intended as a
        package of measures that should be introduced to ensure that the impact of a future
        pandemic on the adult social care sector is properly considered in advance, so that
        greater protection can be ensured right from the start.

Appendix 1: The background
to this module and the

=== PAGE 267 ===
  Module 6: Care sector




  Background
  A1.1.     The Right Honourable Boris Johnson MP, Prime Minister from July 2019 to
            September 2022, formally established the UK Covid-19 Inquiry in June 2022 to
            examine the preparations for and response to the Covid-19 pandemic in the UK
            and to learn lessons for the future. In December 2021, he appointed The Right
            Honourable the Baroness Hallett DBE, a retired judge of the Court of Appeal,
            as its Chair.

  A1.2.     On 28 June 2022, the Prime Minister issued the final Terms of Reference for the
            Inquiry, establishing it under the Inquiries Act 2005.1 The Inquiry formally opened
            on 21 July 2022 to:

                 “examine, consider and report on preparations and the response to the pandemic
                  in England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland, up to and including the
                  Inquiry’s formal setting-up date, 28 June 2022”.2

  A1.3.     To ensure a full and focused examination of the wide range of issues covered in the
            Terms of Reference and to produce regular reports, the Inquiry’s investigation has
            been divided into sections or ‘modules’. Each module gathers evidence, designates
            Core Participants and has both preliminary hearings (at which decisions about the
            procedure for the conduct of its investigations and public hearings are made) and full
            public hearings where evidence is heard. Details of public hearings are published by
            the Inquiry.3

  A1.4.     The Inquiry’s public hearings concluded on 5 March 2026. Five investigation reports
            have already been published (see Table 7).




  1    See ‘Covid-19 Inquiry Terms of Reference’, UK Covid-19 Inquiry, 20 July 2022 (https://covid19.public-inquiry.uk/documents/
  terms-of-reference), which includes translations of the Inquiry’s Terms of Reference; for the Inquiries Act 2005, see https://www.
  legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/2005/12/contents. A separate inquiry is taking place in Scotland, which will evaluate areas where policy was
  devolved to the Scottish Government, as set out in its Terms of Reference. The UK Covid-19 Inquiry works with the Scottish Covid-19
  Inquiry to avoid duplication of work where possible.
  2    ‘Covid-19 Inquiry Terms of Reference’, UK Covid-19 Inquiry, 20 July 2022 (https://covid19.public-inquiry.uk/documents/terms-of-
  reference)
  3    For further information, see ‘Structure of the Inquiry’, UK Covid-19 Inquiry, no date (https://covid19.public-inquiry.uk/structure-of-
  the-inquiry)

          recommendations in each report are based on an objective assessment of the
          totality of the evidence received by the Inquiry.

  Module 6: Care sector




            emotional and social needs of adults and older people. This support can be provided
            in a variety of settings, including in people’s own homes, in care homes or in a
            number of other places such as day centres or supported housing. For the purposes
            of Module 6, the Inquiry focused on care homes, domiciliary care and unpaid care.


  Outline of Scope
  A1.8.     As described in its Outline of Scope, Module 6 focused primarily on the
            consequences of government decision-making for those living and working in
            the adult social care sector.8 In particular, the matters examined encompassed:

            • the impact of the pandemic on people’s experience of the adult social care sector;
            • the structure of the adult social care sector and the key bodies involved in the UK
                government and devolved administrations at the start of and during the pandemic;
            • the key decisions made by the UK government and devolved administrations in
                respect of the adult social care sector, including the decisions relating to the
                discharge of people from hospitals into adult care and residential homes in the
                early stages of the pandemic;
            • the management of the pandemic in adult care and residential homes;
            • the use of Do Not Attempt Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (DNACPR) and
                communication with recipients of care and their loved ones about the recipient
                of care’s condition and treatment, including discussions and decisions about
                DNACPRs;
            • changes to the regulatory inspection regimes in the adult social care sector;
            • deaths related to infection by Covid-19, including the deaths of recipients of care
                and staff; and
            • infection prevention and control measures for those providing care in the home,
                including by unpaid carers.


  Core Participants
  A1.9.     In accordance with rule 5 of the Inquiry Rules 2006 and the Inquiry’s Core
            Participant Protocol, the Chair designates a number of Core Participants –
            individuals, organisations or institutions with a specific interest – in each module.9
            Core Participants have enhanced rights in the Inquiry process, including receiving
            disclosure of documents, being represented, making legal submissions and
            suggesting lines of enquiry. They are also able to apply to the Inquiry for funding
            to cover legal and other costs.

  Public access to Inquiry proceedings
  A1.11. In keeping with its public nature and the Chair’s commitment to conduct the Inquiry
         in as open and transparent a manner as possible, arrangements were made for
         the hearings to be accessible to all who wished to follow them. The hearings were
         broadcast via livestream on the Inquiry’s website or its YouTube channel (where they
         remain accessible) and members of the public were able to watch the hearings in
         person.11


  Evidence gathering
  A1.12. In Module 6, the Inquiry issued 186 requests for evidence pursuant to rule 9
         of the Inquiry Rules 2006, to 184 organisations and individuals. It received and
         considered 177 witness statements and 11,831 documents, containing 103,020 pages.
         The Inquiry also reviewed and disclosed material provided for other modules and
         deemed relevant to its Scope. In total, 17,196 documents, comprising 235,291 pages,
         were disclosed to Core Participants.

  A1.13. The Inquiry received statements from a sample of 14 individual care homes chosen
         from a variety of care homes across the UK, taking into account factors including
         geographical spread, size and demography. This enabled the Inquiry to gain a
         broader understanding of the experience of those working in care homes and of the
         impact of the Covid-19 pandemic on providers of adult social care across the UK.

  A1.14. The Inquiry also sent requests to a number of individuals from Core Participant
         groups who were particularly affected by the Covid-19 pandemic for evidence about
         the impact it had on them. Those contacted included recipients of adult social
         care, bereaved families, charity groups, workers’ organisations and professional
         membership organisations. The Inquiry received 22 such statements, all of which
         have been published.


  Every Story Matters
  A1.15. As set out in the Terms of Reference, the Inquiry regards it as critical to its work to
         listen to and carefully consider the experiences of bereaved families and others who
         have suffered hardship or loss as a result of the pandemic. The Inquiry has done this

  11 ‘Care Sector (Module 6) – Public Hearings’, UK Covid-19 Inquiry, no date (https://covid19.public-inquiry.uk/hearings/care-sector-

         in a number of different ways, including through its listening exercise, Every Story
         Matters, the largest public engagement exercise by a UK public inquiry. It heard from
         thousands of people about their experiences of the pandemic.

A1.16. Stories were shared with the Inquiry through a webform (including accessible
       versions), a series of events across the UK and interviews with under-represented
       groups.12 Those aged 18 or older were invited to share as much or as little of their
       pandemic experience as they felt able, without the formality of giving evidence or
       attending a public hearing. More than 58,000 stories were shared with the Inquiry.
       The sharing of these experiences has helped the Inquiry to understand events
       and their impact and has aided the development of recommendations that could
       reduce suffering in the future. Experiences shared with the Inquiry were analysed
       and reports – called records – have been produced highlighting the themes that
       emerge, and telling people’s stories in their own words. Every Story Matters records
       have been used in evidence in relevant modules of the Inquiry.13 The record relating
       to Module 6 has been published as Every Story Matters: Adult Social Care Sector.
       This has been referenced in this Report and has been published on the Inquiry
       website.14

       requests for documents or witness statements pursuant to rule 9 of the Inquiry
       Rules 2006.15 This should be read with the Inquiry’s Protocol on the Redaction
       of Documents, which details the approach to the redaction of documents for the
       purposes of both disclosure to Core Participants and publication.16

       to Core Participants in full, subject to any redactions applied in accordance with the

       the public are able to view documents provided to the Inquiry and attend Inquiry
       hearings.18 All documents shown on screen during the course of the hearings appear
       in the YouTube recording of the evidence and are published on the Inquiry’s website
       at the end of each day. The witness statements of those who give evidence each day

Terminology and references
A1.25. The nature of the subject matter means that the evidence considered by the
       Inquiry contains technical and specialist language, which the Inquiry has tried to
       minimise in this Report. A number of witnesses and documents also used a range
       of abbreviations and acronyms. To avoid any confusion and to assist the reader, the
       Inquiry has set out names and other key phrases in full in this Report; a detailed
       glossary is also included at Appendix 2.

A1.26. Some terminology that is particularly key to understanding this Report is listed below
       for ease of reference.


Key terminology
A1.27. The virus that causes the coronavirus disease known as Covid-19 is SARS-CoV-2.
       However, where this specificity is not necessary, in accordance with the practice of
       the World Health Organization, the Inquiry uses ‘Covid-19’ to refer to both the virus
       and the disease.

A1.28. Although the first Covid-19 patients in the UK were announced on 31 January
       2020 and the outbreak was not characterised by the World Health Organization
       as a pandemic until 11 March 2020, for clarity this Report refers to the time period
       beginning with the arrival of Covid-19 in the UK as the ‘Covid-19 pandemic’.

A1.29. The Covid-19 pandemic required action by both the UK government and devolved
       administrations. Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland each have a legislature
       and executive elected by their own electorates (referred to in this Report as the
       ‘devolved administrations’). Although each devolution settlement is different, each
       administration is responsible for a range of matters, including health, education
       and transport. England has no legislature of its own – instead, the UK Parliament
       legislates on both UK-wide, ‘reserved’ (ie not devolved) issues such as defence and
       foreign affairs and legislates for England on issues devolved to other nations. The UK



                                                                                                265

=== PAGE 279 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




                Parliament also, at times, legislates for other groupings – for example, in England and
                Wales on issues of justice.

      A1.30. The UK government is responsible for all aspects of government policy in England.
             At the time of the Covid-19 pandemic, the Department of Health and Social Care was
             responsible for policy on health and adult social care matters in England (and on a
             UK-wide basis for a few issues that are not otherwise devolved). Prior to January
             2018, the Department of Health and Social Care was known as the Department of
             Health – for clarity, the department is referred to throughout this Report using its
             current name.

      A1.31. ‘The NHS’ is the term used to refer collectively to the publicly funded national
             healthcare systems in England, Scotland and Wales, comprising the NHS in England,
             NHS Scotland and the NHS in Wales (also known as NHS Wales). In Northern Ireland,
             the publicly funded healthcare system is Health and Social Care (Northern Ireland),
             with health and social care integrated under a single framework. In this Report, the
             term ‘healthcare systems in the four nations’ is used to describe the healthcare
             systems across the UK.

      A1.32. Prior to the pandemic, responsibility for responding to public health challenges in
             Scotland was divided between a number of different bodies. As part of the Public
             Health Reform programme, Public Health Scotland was launched on 1 April 2020.
             It brought together three legacy bodies: NHS Health Scotland, Health Protection
             Scotland (part of NHS National Services Scotland) and the Information Services
             Division (also part of NHS National Services Scotland). As a result, at an early
             stage of the pandemic, all staff and functions of those legacy bodies transferred
             to Public Health Scotland, with limited exceptions (the Antimicrobial Resistance
             and Healthcare Associated Infection function and a number of corporate services
             staff from NHS Health Scotland that remained in NHS National Services Scotland).
             Accordingly, questions relating to the public health response in Scotland were
             directed to both Public Health Scotland and NHS National Services Scotland.

      A1.33. References to ‘R’ in public health communications during the Covid-19 pandemic
             – and references to ‘R’ in this Report – are generally references to Rt: the average
             number of people that a single infected person passes a virus on to at a particular
             time point, taking into account current levels of immunity and the extent of social
             mixing.25


      References
      A1.34. References such as ‘Caroline Abrahams 16 July 2025 21/4-5’ or
             ‘INQ000572015_0025 para 82’ in the footnotes of this Report relate to material that
             is available on the Inquiry’s website.26

      25 INQ000252450_0013 para 2.15
      26 ‘What is the UK Covid-19 Inquiry?’, UK Covid-19 Inquiry, no date (https://covid19.public-inquiry.uk)

A1.35. The transcripts of the Inquiry’s hearings are referenced by person, hearing date, and
       internal page and line numbers. For example, ‘Caroline Abrahams 16 July 2025 21/4-5’
       refers to the evidence of Caroline Abrahams on 16 July 2025, page 21, lines 4 to 5.

A1.36. Documentary evidence is referenced by the document’s number and, where
       relevant, page and paragraph numbers. For example, ‘INQ000572015_0025
       para 82’ refers to document INQ000572015, page 25, paragraph 82.

A1.37. Publicly available documents are listed in the footnotes with both their wider
       internet and Inquiry website links. For example:

          Care Home Census for Adults in Scotland: Statistics for 2015 – 2025, Public
          Health Scotland, 28 October 2025, page 11 (https://publichealthscotland.scot/
          media/35666/2025-chc-report_final.pdf; INQ000660214).

Term (including acronym) Description
Adult social care         The adult social care sector provides care in a range of settings,
                          including in adult care homes and in people’s own homes.
                          The type of care provided includes help with personal tasks
                          and nursing care.
Advance care plan         An umbrella term for a document in which an individual records
                          their priorities and decisions about future medical care and
                          treatment, in the event of a medical emergency or critical,
                          life-threatening illness.
Asymptomatic              Having an infection but not showing any symptoms.
Asymptomatic testing      Testing for those without symptoms.
British Medical           A trade union and professional body for doctors and medical
Association               students in the UK.
Cabinet Office            A ministerial UK government department, supported by 29
                          agencies and public bodies. It supports the Prime Minister,
                          ensures the effective running of government and takes the lead
                          in certain critical policy areas.
Cardiopulmonary           The process of attempting to restart a patient’s heart in the
resuscitation (CPR)       event that it stops pumping (ie a cardiac arrest).
Care home                 An institution providing accommodation and personal care for
                          people who are no longer able to live independently.
Care Inspectorate         The regulator for all health and social care, early learning and
                          childcare services in Scotland. The legal name of the Care
                          Inspectorate is Social Care and Social Work Improvement
                          Scotland.
Care Inspectorate Wales   The regulator for all social care and childcare services in Wales.
Care Quality Commission The regulator for all health and social care services in England.
Care Rights UK            A charity focusing on the rights of people in care.
Chief Medical Officer     A qualified medical practitioner, the most senior government
                          adviser on health matters, and the professional head of all
                          directors of public health in local government and the medical
                          profession in government. There is a Chief Medical Officer
                          for each of England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland.
                          The Chief Medical Officer for England is the UK government’s
                          chief medical adviser.
Chief Nursing Officer     The Chief Nursing Officer for England provides clinical and
                          professional leadership for all nurses and midwives in England
                          (with the exception of public health and adult social care
                          nurses), including the 350,000 nurses and midwives who work
                          for the NHS and who make up the largest group of the total
                          NHS workforce.

infectious disease       • can transmit in the community and may be difficult to detect

                         • typically has a high case fatality ratio (the percentage of

                           or no prophylactic or therapeutic drugs; and
                         • therefore requires an enhanced individual, population and
                           system response.

                         epidemics or pandemics.
Integrated care          An integrated care partnership is a statutory joint committee
partnerships             in England formed between the NHS and local authorities,

      Term (including acronym) Description
      Lateral flow test         A type of molecular test to detect an active infection. These
                                tests do not require processing in a laboratory and results are

      Local Government          A national membership body for local authorities in England
      Association               and Wales.

                                Wales for emergency preparedness and cooperation between
                                agencies. Their main purpose is to ensure that local responders
                                are able effectively to act on the duties imposed upon them
                                under the Civil Contingencies Act 2004.
      Lockdown                  A mandatory stay-at-home order, a legal prohibition placing
                                blanket restrictions on the whole population (apart from

                                of a disease.

      syndrome (MERS)           coronavirus (MERS-CoV).

                                It was known by its current name from July 2024, but also from
                                January 2018 to September 2021. From May 2006 to January
                                2018, it was known as the Department for Communities and
                                Local Government, and from September 2021 to July 2024 as
                                the Department for Levelling Up, Housing and Communities. It
                                is referred to in this Report by the correct name for the relevant
                                time period or, for references that span name changes, by its
                                current name.
      Mitigation                A strategy aiming to use limited but effective interventions to
                                delay and minimise the peak of waves of infections and to
                                reduce their size. The goal is primarily to spread pressure on
                                the healthcare system over a longer time period, while
                                accepting that a similar number of people will become infected
                                eventually.
      Modelling                 Statistical models are tools for analysing complex data,
                                identifying patterns, and informing evidence-based decision-
                                making where direct measurement is not possible. Figures from
                                modelling are estimates only, and different statistical models
                                will produce different outcomes. These estimates are not based
                                on direct observations. They are therefore limited by the
                                underlying assumptions used in the models and are subject to
                                uncertainty.

Term (including acronym) Description
Personal protective        Equipment that minimises exposure to hazards. In health and
equipment (PPE)            social care, it ranges from basic items such as aprons, gowns
                           and disposable gloves to specialised items such as face shields
                           and respirator masks.
Polymerase chain           A specialised laboratory method used to increase the amount
reaction (PCR) test        of DNA or RNA in a sample so that there is enough to test it.
                           PCR tests are used to detect RNA in samples from people to
                           establish if the samples contain SARS-CoV-2 virus.
Pre-symptomatic            When a person has been infected with a disease but no
                           symptoms have yet appeared.
Prevalence                 A measurement that expresses the proportion of people who
                           have a disease at or during a given time period. Prevalence
                           rates for disease are calculated by dividing the number of cases
                           by the total number of people in the sample.
Prime Minister of the UK   The leader of the UK government, ultimately responsible for the
                           policy and decisions of the UK government.
Priority groups            Groups of people identified by the Joint Committee on
                           Vaccination and Immunisation who are given the vaccine before
                           it is available for the general population.
Public health              The science and art of preventing disease, prolonging life and
                           promoting health through organised efforts of society.
Public Health Agency       Established under the Health and Social Care (Reform) Act
(Northern Ireland)         (Northern Ireland) 2009. Responsible for the improvement of
                           health and social wellbeing, reducing health inequalities in
                           Northern Ireland, health protection and service development.
Public Health England      An executive agency of the Department of Health and Social
                           Care until it was replaced by the UK Health Security Agency
                           and Office for Health Improvement and Disparities in October
                           2021. It was responsible for all aspects of public health.

      Term (including acronym) Description
      Vaccine                  Vaccines train the immune system to recognise a pathogen and
                               to produce antibodies to defend the body from it at the next
                               encounter.
      Variant                  As a virus replicates, it can accumulate mutations. A version of
                               the virus with these mutations is called a ‘variant’. Mutations
                               may facilitate transmission or impact the effectiveness of
                               vaccines, treatments or immunity resulting from previous
                               infection.
      Ventilation              The process of introducing fresh air into indoor spaces while
                               removing stale air.
      Virus                    A parasitic infectious agent that replicates only inside the cells
                               of an organism.
      Vivaldi 1 study          A study by University College London, the Office for National
                               Statistics and Public Health England which ran in May and June
                               2020 and surveyed 5,126 care homes for those aged over 65 in
                               England. Survey responses were then linked to Covid-19 test
                               results.
      Vulnerable groups        Groups within the population who were particularly susceptible

=== PAGE 294 ===
Appendix 3: List of
recommendations

=== PAGE 295 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      Chapter 1: Overview of the adult social care sector
            Recommendation 1: Improved pandemic planning for the adult social
            care sector
            The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
            Ireland Executive should each establish clear arrangements for pandemic
            planning in adult social care. Those arrangements should facilitate the production
            of pandemic plans for adult social care at the government, local and provider
            levels.
            Plans at a government level should identify:

            • the roles and responsibilities of each government in relation to adult social care
               during a pandemic;

            • the requirements for local-level plans, including the roles and responsibilities
               of local authorities, Health and Social Care trusts, and health and social care
               partnerships in producing those plans;

            • the requirements for provider-level plans;
            • how and by whom the adequacy of local and provider-level plans will
               be assured;

            • how bed capacity and staffing levels will be tracked in care homes and
               domiciliary care; and

            • how the adult social care sector will be represented in the pandemic planning
               of the four governments.
            Government pandemic plans for adult social care should be regularly reviewed
            and tested as part of the pandemic response exercises recommended in the
            Inquiry’s Module 1 Report (Recommendation 6).




282

=== PAGE 296 ===
                                                     Appendix 3: List of recommendations




Chapter 2: Care home admissions, testing
and isolation
   Recommendation 2: Enable the safe and timely isolation of care
   home residents, including following discharge from hospital
   The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
   Ireland Executive should ensure that pandemic plans for adult social care contain
   practical steps rapidly to scale up isolation facilities for care home residents in a
   future pandemic.
   As a minimum, plans should:

   • establish mechanisms to record systematically the availability of isolation
     facilities in care homes in each nation and keep these up to date;

   • identify settings that can be used in a future pandemic to isolate and meet the
     needs of residents, including those who need to be discharged from hospital
     into a care home; and

   • establish guidance for how these settings can be run to ensure high-quality,
     safe care and effective infection prevention and control.




                                                                                           283

=== PAGE 297 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      Chapter 3: Protective measures
            Recommendation 3: Financial support to adult social care staff to
            self-isolate or to limit working across multiple sites
            The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
            Ireland Executive should put in place financial support schemes that should be
            made available and that can be implemented at the start of a future pandemic:

            • to protect adult social care staff against financial loss as a result of compliance
               with mandatory self-isolation rules; and

            • to ensure that adult social care providers and staff can comply with rules or
               guidance restricting the number of sites at which adult social care staff may
               work during a pandemic.
            The following principles should apply:

            • There should be clear and straightforward guidance about eligibility criteria,
               application processes and payment arrangements for the support schemes.

            • Information about the introduction of support schemes and how adult social
               care staff and providers can receive support should be communicated clearly
               and made available in accessible formats.

            • Mechanisms should be in place to monitor the effectiveness of the support
               schemes.
            These financial support schemes should be designed in partnership with
            representatives from across the adult social care sector.




284

=== PAGE 298 ===
                                                    Appendix 3: List of recommendations




Chapter 4: Visiting restrictions in care homes
    Recommendation 4: Visiting restrictions
    The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
    Ireland Executive should publish guidance for the implementation of visiting
    restrictions in care homes in a future pandemic.
    The guidance should be led by the following core principles:

    • Measures applied should be the least restrictive possible, both in terms
      of severity and the length of time for which they apply.

    • Residents should have the right to in-person visitors, except in the most grave
      or exceptional of circumstances.

    • Restrictions should be decided upon and applied at the most local level
      possible, based on local risk assessments and considering individual
      circumstances.

    • Communications with the public, care home residents and loved ones must
      clearly explain the measures in place and their purpose.
    People who work in and use social care should be closely involved in developing
    this guidance.



Chapter 5: Regulation and safeguarding
    Recommendation 5: Maintaining regulatory oversight of care quality
    and safety in a public health emergency
    The Care Quality Commission in England, Care Inspectorate Wales, the Care
    Inspectorate in Scotland and the Regulation and Quality Improvement Authority
    in Northern Ireland should each develop adaptable protocols for carrying out
    on-site, hybrid and remote inspections in a future public health emergency.
    These protocols should be guided by the core principle of continuing on-site
    inspections where possible. They should also clearly identify processes
    to minimise:

    • the risk of transmission when on-site inspections are carried out; and
    • the risk of safeguarding issues going unidentified where remote or hybrid
      inspections are carried out.
    Regulators should regularly review these protocols to ensure that they remain
    practical and effective.


                                                                                        285

=== PAGE 299 ===
      Module 6: Care sector




      Chapter 6: The impact on unpaid carers, care workers
      and care providers
            Recommendation 6: Collection of data about deaths of adult social
            care workers during a pandemic
            The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
            Ireland Executive should develop mechanisms to collect, analyse and publish data
            systematically about the deaths of adult social care workers during a pandemic.
            The data must be comparable across the four nations of the UK.
            The mechanisms should be developed with the adult social care workforce
            regulators, public health agencies and employers of care workers.



      Chapter 7: Deaths and end of life
            Recommendation 7: Mandatory end-of-life care training for health
            and social care professionals
            The UK government, Scottish Government, Welsh Government and Northern
            Ireland Executive should establish mandatory training for health and social care
            professionals looking after people in the adult social care sector who are nearing
            the end of life.
            The training should be developed in conjunction with health and social care
            regulators and provide, at a minimum:

            • an understanding and enhanced knowledge of how to deliver high-quality
               end-of-life care as well as the support available; and

            • communication skills to be able confidently to conduct and document
               conversations about advance care and, where appropriate to the professional’s
               role, decisions on Do Not Attempt Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (DNACPR).




286

=== PAGE 300 ===
                                                    Appendix 3: List of recommendations




Chapter 8: Recognition and protection
    Recommendation 8: A voluntary workforce register for adult social
    care workers
    The UK government should implement a voluntary workforce register for adult
    social care workers in England, in order to strengthen the professionalisation
    and recognition for those working in the care sector. This should be developed in
    conjunction with social care staff, providers, commissioners and oversight bodies.
    This should formalise the ongoing work to improve workforce progression and
    retention and any learning from registration schemes in Northern Ireland, Scotland
    and Wales.
    The design of the register should consider:

    • the most appropriate body or agency to operate the register, including
      processing applications, data-sharing and publication;

    • the benefits and requirements of joining the register, including the best means
      to incentivise membership; and

    • the funding model that will support the establishment and operation of the
      register.
    The voluntary register should be implemented within two years of the publication
    of this Report.
    Once the voluntary register has been operating for no more than five years, the
    UK government should undertake a full cost–benefit analysis of the workforce
    register and review whether membership should become mandatory.




                                                                                         287
